## 1.3. Eligibility to the centralised procedure

The applicant Minoryx Therapeutics S.L. submitted on 27 June 2025 an application for marketing authorisation to the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for Nezglyal (Leriglitazone), through the centralised procedure falling within the Article 3(1) and point 4 of Annex of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The eligibility to the centralised procedure was agreed upon by the EMA/CHMP on 14 November 2024.

The applicant applied for the following indication:

Nezglyal is indicated for the treatment of male adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) patients 2 years and older with cerebral ALD (cALD).

## 1.4. Legal basis and dossier content

## The legal basis for this application refers to:

Article 8.3 of Directive 2001/83/EC - complete and independent application

The application submitted is composed of administrative information, complete quality data, and nonclinical and clinical data based on applicant's own tests and studies and bibliographic literature substituting/supporting certain test(s) or study(ies).

## 1.7. Applicant's request(s) for consideration

## 1.7.1. Accelerated assessment request

The applicant requested accelerated assessment in accordance to Article 14 (9) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. The CHMP did not agree to the applicant's request for an accelerated assessment as not all criteria for an accelerated assessment were met. This was based on the issues and uncertainties that were identified regarding part of the target indication, i.e., above 12 years with Gd+ positive lesions. It was concluded that the submitted package does not allow for the fulfillment of the calendar of an accelerated procedure.

The applicant requested consideration of its application for a marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances in accordance with Article 14(8) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004. See section 9.6.2.1 of this document.

## 1.7.3. New active substance status

The applicant requested the active substance Leriglitazone contained in the above medicinal product to be considered as a new active substance (NAS), as the applicant claimed that it is not a constituent of a medicinal product previously authorised within the European Union.

Furthermore, the applicant also requested leriglitazone to be considered as a new active substance in comparison to pioglitazone previously authorised in the European Union as Actos, as the applicant

claimed that leriglitazone differs significantly in properties with regard to safety and/or efficacy from the already authorised active substance.

The applicant withdrew their claim for new active substance during the procedure.

Nezglyal is indicated for the treatment of Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2 to 12 years with non Gadolinium (Gd) enhancing lesions (i.e. Gd negative) in brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), with a Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1.

## 2. Introduction

## 2.1. Therapeutic context

Cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD), is a rare (estimated birth incidence of 1 in 17,000 worldwide (male and female) and the most devastating phenotype of adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD), a rare genetic disorder linked to the X-chromosome caused by mutations of the ABCD1 gene that result in loss of function of the encoded ALD protein (ALDP), an ABCD transporter located in the peroxisomal membrane. Deficiency of the ALDP impairs peroxisomal β-oxidation of very-long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs) leading to its accumulation in plasma and tissues, particularly the brain, spinal cord, and adrenal glands impairing their physiological functions.

cALD is characterised by the presence of brain lesions that, once progressive, can lead to a rapid neurological decline, vegetative state and death. All male ALD patients older than 2 years are at risk for developing cALD. The pathophysiology of cALD is associated with increasing inflammation, along with demyelination, axonal damage and BBB disrupt, which is detected through magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), the only a marker for disease progression, as currently there are no validated tests or biomarkers that can predict cALD onset and progression in ALD patients and contribute to establish the diagnose. It is generally recognised that cALD reflects a continuum of lesion development, lesion evolution, and progressive disease. Brain lesion growth precedes clinical decline and death in cALD patients. Lesions can be classified as Arrested cALD (clinically stable with lesions remaining Gd negative and without showing significant growth for a minimum period of 6 months) or Progressive cALD (when the brain MRI shows significant growth of Gd negative lesions, appearance of new lesions or when lesions become gadolinium positive (Gd positive), even if a patient is still asymptomatic).

The progression from Gd negative to Gd positive lesions represents not only a radiological change but a point of no return after which the median survival is less than 4 years.

Children have a higher rate of disease progression compared to adults. Only in about 10% of boys the disease will arrest compared to up to 40% in adults.

Currently no pharmacological therapies are approved for cALD patients to reduce and/or prevent the onset of progressive disease. In the EU, haematopoietic stem-cell transplantation (HSCT) is the only potential treatment option for progressive cALD (typically with Gd positive lesions), which may halt disease progression in children. However, the window of opportunity to treat patients and the outcome is poor in advanced disease (Engelen 2022), along with limitation of suitable donors, engraftment failure and severe side effects (Raymond 2019) and high mortality.

It can be concluded that there is an unmet medical need for the treatment of this condition.

## 2.2. Aspects of development

Leriglitazone is currently not licensed in any region.

Leriglitazone has an orphan drug designation for the treatment of ALD and Friedreich's Ataxia, both in the EU and the US.

The proposed indication for Leriglitazone is the treatment of male adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) patients 2 years and older with cerebral ALD (cALD).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A paediatric investigational plan was submitted to and approved by the EMA, which included agreement on the pivotal study NEXUS in paediatric cALD patients (P/0330/2024).

A previous application for a conditional marketing authorisation for NEZGLYAL was submitted on 29 July 2022 but for a different indication from the current one: 'treatment of cerebral progression and myelopathy in male patients with ALD'. At that point the ADVANCE trial was the pivotal study and it did not meet its primary endpoint of six-minute walk test for adrenomyeloneuropathy (AMN) in adult ALD patients.

The CHMP considered, on 25 January 2024, by consensus that the safety and efficacy of Nezglyal in the proposed indication had not been properly or sufficiently demonstrated and issued a negative opinion on granting the approval of the medicinal product.

The Applicant also requested a re-examination of the opinion with a changed indication 'treatment of male ALD patients aged 2 years and older with brain lesions to delay progression of cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD)'. The CHMP confirmed the initial grounds for refusal.

With the completion of the positive NEXUS study, the Applicant hereby submitted the new application.

The sponsor submitted a request for an accelerated assessment pursuant to Article 14(9) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004, for an application for marketing authorisation for NEZGLYAL, for the treatment of male adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) in patients 2 years and older with cerebral ALD (cALD). The CHMP concluded that the submitted package did not allow for the fulfilment of the calendar of an accelerated procedure (see 1.7.1.).

Leriglitazone has been investigated in six completed and ongoing studies in healthy volunteers and in paediatric and adult patients. Also, a significant compassionate use allowed data generation.

One hundred and fifty cALD paediatric and adult patients have been included in the development programme for leriglitazone in the completed NEXUS (23) and ADVANCE (33) studies and in the cALD compassionate use programmes (94, 71 from formal programmes under ANSM and BfArM approved protocols).

Additionally, two further studies are on-going: i) CALYX, a placebo controlled randomised study in adult cALD men with Gd positive lesions, and ii) a Phase I study in healthy volunteers to evaluate drug-drug interactions including evaluation of food effect using the commercial preparation of the product.

## 2.3. Description of the product

Leriglitazone (brand name: Nezglyal), is an active metabolite of the thiazolidinedione (TZD) pioglitazone, and a selective peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma (PPARγ) agonist that crosses the blood-brain barrier (BBB). It is believed to have an antioxidant and neuroprotective activity in the central nervous system (CNS) by modulating critical pathways involved in cALD progression by decreasing neuroinflammation, enhancing BBB integrity, promoting myelination and improving mitochondrial function (Rodríguez-Pascau et al., 2021).

Nezglyal is an immediate release, oral suspension containing leriglitazone 13.7 mg/mL (equivalent to 15 mg/mL leriglitazone hydrochloride), and packed in a 12 mL oral syringe. The proposed starting dose is 10 mL once daily which should be increased to 12 mL after 1 month in order to achieve a target leriglitazone plasma exposure of 200 µg·h/mL (±20%) at steady state.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.4. Inspection issues

## 2.4.1. Good manufacturing practice (GMP) inspection(s)

Satisfactory GMP status is confirmed for manufacturing sites involved in the manufacturing process. A valid GMP certificate for finished product manufacturer is available in EudraGMP database.

No inspection required.

## 2.4.2. Good laboratory practice (GLP) inspection(s)

No inspection required.

## 2.4.3. Good clinical practice (GCP) inspection(s)

No for-cause inspection required. However, a routine GCP inspection (Case ID: EMA/IN/0000293052) for the clinical study MT-2-02 was conducted. No specific concerns were identified by the assessment at the time of adoption of the inspection request. General triggers have been used in the choice of this dossier and the sites involved in line with the guideline 'Points to consider for assessors, inspectors and EMA inspection coordinators on the identification of triggers for the selection of applications for 'routine' and/or 'for cause' inspections, their investigation and scope of such Inspections'.

The following sites were inspected: Site #1 (trial site 6020) Hospital Sant Joan de Déu, Passeig De Sant Joan De Deu 2, Esplugues De Llobregat, 08950 Barcelona, Spain; and Site #2 Minoryx Therapeutics S.L., Calle D'Ernest Lluch 32 Tcm 3, 08302 Mataro, Barcelona, Spain.

The integrated inspection report (10-02-2026) refers that compliance with GCP and applicable regulations were verified, in particular where it had impact on the validity of the data or the ethical conduct of the study. The inspection focused on the verification of selected efficacy and safety data reported in the Marketing Authorisation Application (MAA), and primary and secondary objectives for all trial participants (since the size sample was very limited).

At the inspection of Investigator site #1 (trial site 6020 Hospital Sant Joan de Deu), in Barcelona, Spain, there were one (01) major and eight (08) minor findings. At the inspection of Sponsor site #2 (Minoryx Therapeutics S.L.), Mataró, Barcelona, Spain, there were ten (10) major and two (02) minor findings. Critical finding was detected during Sponsor inspection on clinical conduct related with longitudinal quality control review of the MRI images that was performed under uncontrolled conditions, but, upon further investigation and impact assessment that concluded that primary endpoint was not compromised by this issue, it was downgraded to major finding. So, no final critical findings were detected at Investigator and Sponsor inspection.

Despite some of the findings during inspection being considered relevant, there are a few of them that were site specific, and those are not expected to affect the overall clinical trial. Most of the findings detected in the sponsor inspection were process related, and, even though, are deemed to have potentially impacted the full study, no indication of actual impact on either participant's safety, rights or wellbeing was identified.

Regarding data integrity, despite the inspection noted non-GCP compliances that have affected the quality of the data, the impact assessment carried out by the Sponsor noted no impact to the primary endpoint and the secondary outcome of the LOES score after the cross check from the MRI listings from the CSR against the specific audit trails from the databases. Overall, the trial was conducted in an ethical manner.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The inspectees have in general acknowledged the findings in their responses to the individual IRs and the CAPAs performed or planned have been taken into account in the final assessment on the quality and acceptability of the data and recommendation by the inspection team. The responses and actions described are considered, in general, as adequate.

The IIR concludes that data quality is acceptable and the trial data has been considered as trustworthy following GCP and ethical standards, and therefore data can be used in support of the Marketing Authorisation Application submitted to the EMA for Nezglyal by Minoryx Therapeutics S.L.

## 3. Quality aspects

## 3.1. Introduction

The finished product is presented as an oral suspension containing 13.7 mg/mL of leriglitazone as active substance.

Other ingredients are sorbitol (E420), microcrystalline cellulose (E460), carmellose sodium (E466), saccharin sodium (E954), sodium benzoate (E211), sodium citrate (E331), citric acid monohydrate (E330), strawberry flavour, citric acid monohydrate 0.2M solution, and purified water.

The product is available in a hydrolytic class III amber glass bottle with a white polyethylene childresistant cap as described in section 6.5 of the SmPC.

The pack includes a polyethylene oral dosing syringe of 12 mL graduated every 0.5 mL.

## 3.2. Active substance

## 3.2.1. General information

The chemical name of the active substance is 5-[[4-[2-[5-(1-hydroxyethyl)-2pyridinyl]ethoxy]phenyl]methyl]-2,4-thiazolidinedione hydrochloride (1:1) corresponding to the molecular formula C19H20N2O4S.HCl. It has a relative molecular mass of 408.90 g/mol and the following structure:

Figure 1: Active substance structure

<!-- image -->

## 3.2.2. Manufacture, characterisation, and process controls

## Description of manufacturing process and process controls

The active substance is manufactured by one manufacturing site. Evidence of GMP compliance has been provided in the QP declaration.

The active substance is synthesized in five main chemical synthesis steps using well defined starting materials with acceptable specifications.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Reprocessing procedures may be applied to the active substance or any relevant intermediate or processing step, in the extent and in the cases prescribed by ICH Q7. In such cases, reprocessing procedures will be managed under the manufacturer's QMS.

Adequate in-process controls are applied during the synthesis. The specifications and control methods for intermediate products, starting materials and reagents have been presented.

## 3.2.1. Characterisation and impurities

Potential, structurally related organic impurities have been provided along with structures, origin, control and levels observed. No degradation products have been detected.

The control strategy of residual solvents used in active substance synthesis is summarised and considered acceptable. The risk assessment on elemental impurities content is not provided, instead control of seven elemental impurities (Class 1 and 2a) is included in the specification of active substance with acceptance criteria set according to ICH Q3D, option 1 for oral route. Carryover of several reagents is sufficiently addressed.

The information provided on the evaluation of potentially genotoxic impurities was considered sufficient. All the structures considered in mutagenicity assessment, along with the results of the in silico evaluation and the relevant ICH M7 classification, are provided. All potential impurities including reagents, intermediates, starting materials and potential impurities arising from starting material synthesis are included. The control strategy for potentially genotoxic impurities was discussed and implemented in line with the ICH M7 guideline. For all compounds, the risk associated with their carry over to the final active substance was demonstrated to be negligible and the relevant control strategy adequate for assuring the quality and safety of the active substance.

A nitrosamines risk evaluation was performed taking into account the requirements of ICH M7, EMA, EU and FDA guidelines and covers all possible sources. The risk was determined to be very low, and no analytical testing was requested.

The characterisation of the active substance and its impurities are in accordance with the EU guideline on chemistry of active substances.

Potential and actual impurities were well discussed with regards to their origin and characterised.

The chemical structure of the active substance was elucidated by a combination of elemental analysis, Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy (FT-IR), nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy (NMR), ultraviolet-visible absorption spectrophotometry (UV-VIS), accurate mass determination by high performance liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (HPLC-MS).

The solid state properties of the active substance were measured by powder X-Ray diffraction (PXRD), single crystal X-Ray diffraction (SCXRD), differential scanning calorimetry (DSC), thermogravimetric analysis (TGA) and differential scanning calorimetry (DSC).

The active substance is a slightly hygroscopic white to off-white powder, practically insoluble in acetonitrile, slightly soluble in ethanol, sparingly soluble in methanol. In acidic aqueous solutions, it is slightly soluble, while at pH 7 and pH 8 it is insoluble.

Leriglitazone hydrochloride has two asymmetric carbon atoms, resulting in two chiral centres. The ratio of the stereoisomers is controlled.

The active substance has defined polymorphic form.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.2.2. Control of active substance

The active substance specification includes tests for appearance (visual), identification (IR, HPLC), polymorphic form (XRPD), equivalent purity (water and solvent free) (titration), assay (HPLC), related substances (HPLC), stereoisomers (HPLC), residual solvents (GC-HS), water content (Ph. Eur.), elemental impurities (ICP-OES/MS), residue on ignition, sulphated ash (Ph. Eur.), microbial tests (Ph. Eur.), and particle size distribution (laser diffraction).

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and non-compendial methods appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for testing has been presented.

## Batch analysis

Batch analysis data of the active substance are provided. The results are within the specifications and consistent from batch to batch.

## 3.2.3. Stability

The retest period in the proposed container is justified by the available stability data.

Stability data from a number of batches of the active substance used in clinical studies and for registration purposes from the proposed manufacturer stored in the intended commercial package for up to 36 months under long term conditions (25 °C ± 2 °C / 60% RH ± 5% RH) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 °C ± 2 °C / 75% RH ± 5% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided. Since the synthesis of clinical batches maintains the same overall route of synthesis of commercial scale, the clinical batches are considered as representative of commercial batches, and thus the presented stability data can be used for re-test period evaluation

The following parameters were tested: appearance, assay, specified impurities, unspecified impurities and total impurities, water content, stereoisomers, particle size distribution, microbiological quality, and polymorphic form. The analytical methods used were the same as for release and are stability indicating.

Based on the presented stability results, the active substance is stable under long term and accelerated conditions.

Forced degradation studies and studies at stressed conditions have been also performed on one batch, considering the effects of photo-degradation, temperature/humidity, oxidation, and different pHs.

The active substance showed no degradation under the ICH Q1B applied light stress conditions. It is not negatively impacted by light stress condition according to ICH requirement.

The active substance showed (almost) no degradation under the other stressed conditions like temperature/humidity, hydrogen peroxide and acidic stress conditions.

Taking into consideration the results of the stability studies, the active substance does not require any special storage conditions.

The stability results indicate that the active substance manufactured by the proposed supplier is sufficiently stable. The stability results justify the proposed retest period of 48 months without storage conditions in the proposed container.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3. Finished medicinal product

## 3.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

## Description of the product

The finished product is presented as a white, homogeneous oral suspension containing 13.7 mg/ml of leriglitazone as active substance.

## Pharmaceutical development

The aim of the pharmaceutical development was to develop an immediate release oral dosage form as oral suspension in multidose packaging allowing flexible dosing. The oral suspension is contained in a glass bottle capped with child resistant closures supplemented with a 12 ml oral syringe. The bottle is filled with 100 ml of oral suspension.

The suitability of the pharmaceutical form and composition for use in paediatric patients is considered sufficiently justified in accordance with guideline EMA/CHMP/QWP/805880/2012.

The properties of the active substance and the choice of the excipients have been satisfactorily described. The studies used to investigate the compatibility between the active substance and the excipients selected for the formulation of the finished product are provided.

The active substance does not show hygroscopicity, has a defined melting point and a defined crystal form. The particle size distribution is consistent batch to batch. Both polymorphic form and particle size distribution are controlled in the active substance specification.

A variation in the polymorph form in the finished product may represent a potential impact in certain finished product attributes, especially in its dissolution rate and extent, and in sedimentation tendency. However, these finished product attributes have demonstrated to remain unaltered in the oral suspension, both at release and during stability studies (accelerated and long-term conditions). QbD elements QTPP and risk assessment have been defined. Based on QTPP together with product knowledge from early clinical studies, critical quality attributes (CQAs) related to the product which have an impact on quality, safety, or efficacy have been identified and defined.

Formulations used in clinical phase 1 and clinical phase 2/3 registration studies differs. An EU Phase I clinical study utilized a non-flavoured formulation with minimum excipients to achieve immediate solubility, suspension, and stability. The final formulation used in the clinical phase 2/3 and proposed for commericalization was developed to support the QTPP for conventional immediate-release oral form and improved the characteristics for patient acceptability. The excipients chosen for development of the finished product are common excipients used in suspension dosage forms. The relationship between excipient characteristics and finished product CQAs has been demonstrated through risk assessment and discussed in detail to justify the selected concentrations and grades.

All excipients are well known pharmaceutical ingredients, and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. requirements except the strawberry flavour and the citric acid monohydrate for which in house specifications are used. There are no novel excipients used in the finished product formulation.

The formulation development included studies to investigate viscosity, pH, optimization of organoleptic characteristics, dissolution, particle size stability, preservative efficacy, homogeneity, syringe dosage test, and impact of thermal cycling.

The following dissolution conditions for routine analysis have been defined. The experiments performing deliberate variations of composition of the formulation and of attributes of active substance (change in particle size) to show discriminatory power of the method showed that these modifications had an impact on the dissolution profiles, showing a decrease in dissolution rate. However, dissolution results were within the proposed specification limit for routine control. A complete and fast dissolution of leriglitazone was observed and in accordance with EMA Reflection paper on dissolution specification for generic solid oral immediate release products with systemic action, it may not always be possible to detect any differences in dissolution behaviour after meaningful changes in the formulation, material specifications and/or manufacturing parameters and the method is considered to be adequate without further justification of the discriminatory power.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Manufacturing process development

Manufacturing process development has been described and is based on assessment of the CQAs, and process by FMEA as well as product and process knowledge. No design space is proposed. The control strategy is based on fixed production parameter settings, in process controls and product testing after bulk manufacture and after filling/packaging. Apart from the manufacturing process scale-up, the manufacturing process of the finished product remained unchanged throughout the product development.

## 3.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

The finished product is manufactured by one manufacturing site.

Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been provided for all sites involved in the manufacturing, testing and batch release of the finished product.

The manufacturing process consists of 4 main steps: preparation of suspension, mixing, filling and packaging. The process is considered to be a standard manufacturing process.

A holding time is proposed for commercial manufacturing, which will be assessed during validation.

As it is a standard manufacturing process, no validation data is provided. A process validation protocol is included and considered acceptable.. Based on the information provided to date, it can be inferred that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner.

The in-process controls are adequate for this type of manufacturing process. No holding time is proposed during manufacturing.

## 3.3.3. Control of Finished product

The finished product release specifications include appropriate tests for this kind of dosage form: appearance (visual), sediment (visual), identification of active substance (HPLC, UV), identification of sodium benzoate (HPLC, UV), assay of the active substance (HPLC), assay of sodium benzoate (HPLC), related substances (HPLC), total impurities (HPLC), stereoisomers (HPLC), dissolution (Ph. Eur.), pH (Ph. Eur.), viscosity (Ph. Eur.), uniformity of mass of delivered doses (Ph. Eur.), deliverable volume (USP), and microbial content (Ph. Eur.).

The finished product is released on the market based on the above release specifications, through traditional final product release testing.

Based on an evaluation of the release and stability data from clinical and registration batches manufactured to date, three specified impurities have been identified as potential related substances of the active substance.

Individual specified and unspecified impurities are limited to ≤ 0.2% corresponding to the identification and qualification threshold for impurities in a finished product with a maximum daily dose ≤ 2 g/day. In accordance with ICH Q3B, the proposed limits are appropriate and justified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The limit for total impurities is specified in line with the currently available release and stability data.

Impurity levels at batch release from representative batches were provided. Impurities were below quantitation limits, and unspecified impurities were present at levels ≤ 0.1%. These results showed that the finished product is highly pure with respect to related substances. Furthermore, in stability studies, the specified, and unspecified individual impurity levels remained at or below 0.2%.

The elemental impurities risk assessment was performed in line with ICH Q3D(R1) Guideline for Elemental Impurities according to component-based approach, using data collected from suppliers and evaluation of contribution of utilities (including water), manufacturing equipment and the container closure system. According to the product assessment, there is no level of elemental impurities above control thresholds, therefore testing of elemental impurities is not required in the specification.

A risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities in the finished product has been performed (as requested) considering all suspected and actual root causes in line with the 'Questions and answers for marketing authorisation holders/applicants on the CHMP Opinion for the Article 5(3) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 referral on nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products' (EMA/409815/2020) and the 'Assessment report- Procedure under Article 5(3) of Regulation EC (No) 726/2004- Nitrosamine impurities in human medicinal products' (EMA/369136/2020). Based on the information provided, it is accepted that there is no risk of nitrosamine impurities in the active substance or the related finished product. Therefore, no specific control measures are deemed necessary.

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for testing has been presented.

## Batch analysis

Batch results have been provided for various batches including development, clinical and stability batches, all manufactured according to the proposed commercial formulation. Results comply to the specifications valid at the time of testing. For final formulation batches manufactured with active substance from the proposed manufacturer, results are presented confirming the consistency of the manufacturing process and its ability to manufacture to the intended product specification.

## 3.3.4. Stability of the product

The shelf life of 4 years without special storage conditions in the proposed container, as stated in the SmPC, is justified by the available stability data.

## Container closure system

The finished product is packaged in hydrolytic Type III amber glass bottles with high density polyethylene (HDPE) screw child resistant closures.

Suitability of proposed packaging and dosing materials was demonstrated by thermal cycling studies, compatibility studies (including migration/interaction studies, stability) and a glass delamination study. In relation to the oral syringe provided with the finished product, a copy of the EC Certificate was provided demonstrating conformity with Directive 93/42/EEC.

The proposed type of container closure system is suitable for the intended product. Bottles and closures are sufficiently described, including technical drawings, and adequate specifications are provided. Compliance with Commission Regulation (EU) No 10/2011 is stated for all packaging components in contact with the finished product.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

An extractable study was performed to investigate the extractable profile of the bottle caps in organic solvents (polar and apolar) and aqueous media. Extractables were only detected in the apolar organic extract. Many compounds were detected above the toxicological threshold but only one of these was also found in stressed samples of the finished product.

## Stability data

Stability data of finished product up to production scale used in clinical and stability studies, stored under long term conditions (25 °C ± 2 °C / 60% RH ± 5% RH) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 °C ± 2 °C / 75% RH ± 5% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided. The batches of medicinal product are identical to those proposed for marketing and were packed in the primary packaging proposed for marketing.

Samples were tested for appearance, sediment, leriglitazone assay, sodium benzoate assay, related substances, total impurities, stereoisomers, dissolution, pH, viscosity, uniformity of mass of delivered doses, and microbial content. The analytical procedures used are stability indicating.

The finished product is physically, chemically, and microbiologically stable under long term and accelerated conditions.

In order to demonstrate the stability of the product after the first opening, an in-use stability study was performed on two leriglitazone oral suspension batches (one from the end of shelf life). The test simulated the use of the product in practice taking into consideration the use of the graduated oral dosing syringe. The results demonstrated that there is no risk regarding microbiological contamination or physical and chemical degradation of the finished product for a maximum of 66 days at 25 °C.

A photostability study was conducted according to ICH Q1B Photostability Testing of New Drug Substances and Products guideline on one batch. The results demonstrated that light exposure has no impact on the physical and chemical characteristics of the finished product.

An accelerated degradation study was performed on one batch to identify the likely degradation products of the finished product. The degradation testing included the effect of temperature, photolysis, oxidation as well as the effect of acidic or alkaline conditions. The accelerated degradation study performed shows that the product is sensitive to alkaline treatment, generating several impurities peaks, slightly sensitive to oxidative, acid and light stress conditions and relatively stable to heat.

Based on available stability data, the proposed shelf-life of 4 years without special storage conditions as stated in the SmPC (section 6.3) are acceptable. After the first opening, the in-use shelf life is 66 days stored below 25 °C.

## 3.3.5. Adventitious agents

No excipients derived from animal or human origin have been used.

## 3.4. Discussion and conclusions on chemical, pharmaceutical and biological aspects

Information on development, manufacture and control of the active substance and finished product has been presented in a satisfactory manner.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The results of tests carried out indicate consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in clinical use.

The quality of this product is considered to be adequate. Physicochemical and biological aspects relevant to the uniform clinical performance of the product have been investigated and are controlled in a satisfactory way.

Not applicable.

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## 4.1. Introduction

Nezglyal oral suspension (INN: leriglitazone; company code: MIN-102, as hydrochloride) is novel brain penetrant, full and selective peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma (PPARγ) agonist. Nezglyal oral suspension is administered orally once daily. Each mL contains 13.7 mg leriglitazone (equivalent to 15 mg of leriglitazone hydrochloride). The proposed indication for Nezglyal is the treatment of male adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) patients aged 2 years and older with cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD).

The nonclinical programme for leriglitazone sponsored by Minoryx Therapeutics is broad and ICH-compliant, covering pharmacology, pharmacokinetics, metabolism, distribution, safety pharmacology, toxicology, and reproductive/developmental studies across several species, supplemented by literature data on pioglitazone as a mechanistically related glitazone. Primary pharmacology demonstrated selective PPARγ activation and CNS penetration with activity in disease-relevant in vitro and in vivo models, including cALD models, while secondary pharmacology and safety pharmacology studies addressed offtarget activities and potential organ system effects in line with ICH S7A/S7B. Absorption, bioavailability, tissue distribution, metabolism, and excretion were extensively characterised, with M3 identified as the main metabolite, and DDI risk evaluated through CYP and transporter interaction studies supported by PBPK modelling. Repeated-dose GLP toxicology studies in rats, dogs, and mice up to 9 months characterised the systemic toxicity profile, while juvenile rat studies addressed paediatric use, and reproductive/developmental studies in rats and rabbits supported broader indications. Genetic toxicology testing revealed some in vitro signals of mutagenicity, which were de-risked by negative in vivo followup assays; a completed 2-year rat carcinogenicity study showed no carcinogenic potential, and a mouse study is ongoing. The stereoisomeric composition of leriglitazone and its rapid interconversion were investigated, with data supporting development of the racemic mixture.

## 4.2. Analytical methods

Various methods were used to quantify pioglitazone, leriglitazone, and M3 in biological samples from nonclinical studies. Most early studies used protein precipitation (with acetonitrile) and fit-for-purpose LC-MS/MS methods. Subsequent methods employing LC-MS/MS were developed and validated for more recent studies to quantify pioglitazone, leriglitazone and M3 in mouse, rat, dog and rabbit plasma. The validated methods are considered acceptable for their intended purpose based on selectivity, accuracy, precision, LLOQ, range/linearity, extraction recovery and stability.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

Leriglitazone (MIN-102) is a selective PPARγ agonist that has been characterised in a range of in vitro and in vivo systems. Transactivation assays demonstrated potent activation of PPARγ with no appreciable effect on PPARα or PPARδ. Its main metabolite (M3) and stereoisomers displayed similar activity, confirming that the pharmacological effect is not limited to the parent compound. In cellular systems, leriglitazone induced adipogenesis in human preadipocytes and protected cortical neurons against VLCFA-induced toxicity, with both effects abolished by PPARγ antagonists, supporting a receptor-mediated mechanism.

In vivo studies in rats confirmed target engagement, with increases in adiponectin levels in plasma and CSF, although the magnitude of the effect and the exposure margins relative to projected human therapeutic concentrations were not always clearly delineated. The compound demonstrated CNS penetration, with higher brain/plasma ratios compared with pioglitazone, thus overcoming one of the main limitations of earlier PPARγ agonists.

Mechanistic investigations showed that leriglitazone attenuates neuroinflammation by inhibiting NF-κB activation and reducing pro-inflammatory cytokines such as IL-1β, IL-6, TNFα and IFNγ. Protective effects were reported in neurons, astrocytes, oligodendrocytes and microglia, including reduced VLCFAinduced cytotoxicity, enhanced phagocytosis of myelin debris, stimulation of oligodendrocyte precursor cell proliferation and differentiation, and improved survival of mature oligodendrocytes. In vivo, in models including Abcd1 and Abcd1/Abcd2 knockout mice, experimental autoimmune encephalomyelitis, cuprizone intoxication and ICV-LPS, leriglitazone prevented or attenuated axonal degeneration, glial activation, BBB disruption, demyelination and functional decline.

In summary, the nonclinical pharmacology package provides a coherent body of evidence indicating that leriglitazone is a selective, CNS-penetrant PPARγ agonist with anti-inflammatory, neuroprotective and pro-remyelinating effects in disease-relevant models.

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

The Applicant has conducted a reasonably broad set of secondary pharmacology investigations to characterise potential off-target activities of leriglitazone, complemented by in vitro assays in cellular models of neuronal injury.

To determine binding selectivity, leriglitazone was tested at a concentration of 10 µM in a panel of 55 proteins, including receptors, ion channels, and transporters. There was no meaningful inhibition (&gt; 50% at 10 µM) of any of the targets.

The enzymatic inhibition studies focused on monoamine oxidases. Using recombinant enzymes and a fluorescent kynuramine substrate, leriglitazone inhibited MAO-B with an IC₅₀ of 0.822 ± 0.062 µM and an IC₈₀ of 3.495 ± 1.198 µM, while no inhibition was observed at MAO-A. The results were reproducible across independent experiments and consistent with data obtained for the racemic mixture and individual diastereomers. For comparison, pioglitazone showed somewhat greater potency (IC₅₀ ~0.16 µM), whereas deprenyl (selegiline) displayed markedly higher potency (IC₅₀ ~0.018 µM). Thus, leriglitazone is a weak MAO-B inhibitor, approximately 20-fold less potent than classical MAO-B inhibitors used in Parkinson's disease.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In cellular models, the Applicant investigated dopaminergic neuroprotection using primary rat mesencephalic neurons exposed to MPP⁺). Leriglitazone produced concentration-dependent neuroprotection across 3 nM to 1 µM, with significant effects on both survival of tyrosine hydroxylasepositive (TH⁺) neurons and preservation of neurite networks. The maximal effect was observed at 30 nM. The PPARγ antagonist T0070907 (1 µM) completely abolished the protective effect at 30 nM, demonstrating a clear PPARγ-dependent mechanism. The dopaminergic antagonist zuclopenthixol (100 nM) had no effect, indicating that the benefit was not mediated through D1/D2 receptor activity. At 100 nM in the presence of T0070907, neurite protection was attenuated but TH⁺ neuron survival remained significantly improved, suggesting either incomplete antagonism or an additional mechanism.

The secondary pharmacology of the major human metabolite M3 was evaluated, including a broad offtarget panel and dedicated MAO A and MAO B inhibition assays. The results do not indicate clinically relevant off-target activity at anticipated exposure levels.

## 4.3.1.3. Safety pharmacology

Safety pharmacology studies of leriglitazone were conducted in accordance with the ICH S7A core battery, addressing cardiovascular, central nervous system, and respiratory functions.

In vitro cardiovascular assessment included a GLP hERG assay in transfected HEK cells, where leriglitazone inhibited the tail current by 11.8%, 22.8%, 32.1%, and 43.6% at 10, 30, 100, and 300 µM, respectively. Inhibition was only partially reversible on washout. The estimated IC₅₀ was &gt;300 µM (112 µg/mL), more than 250-fold above the anticipated human unbound Cmax (1.16 µM). A GLP study in isolated canine Purkinje fibres showed that leriglitazone up to 10 µM had no significant effect on action potential parameters, while at 30 µM a minor reduction in APD₅₀ was observed at low stimulation frequency, without changes in APD₇₀ or APD₉₀. Leriglitazone was classified as group C in the TDPscreen database, indicating no risk of torsadogenic potential.

In vivo CNS evaluation consisted of a GLP Irwin screen in female Sprague Dawley rats given single oral doses of 25, 65, or 170 mg/kg. No treatment-related effects on behaviour or body temperature were detected. The positive control clonidine induced the expected changes, validating the assay.

Respiratory function was assessed in conscious female Wistar rats by plethysmography after single oral doses of 25, 50, or 150 mg/kg. Leriglitazone produced a dose-dependent increase in peak inspiratory flow, significant at all doses at 30 minutes post-dose, with persistence to 120 minutes at the highest dose. Decreases in inspiratory time were also observed at 50 and 150 mg/kg. At 150 mg/kg, PenH (airway resistance index) was significantly increased at 180-240 minutes. However, as there were no accompanying changes in expiratory parameters or minute volume, these findings were not interpreted as adverse. The NOAEL was identified as 150 mg/kg.

In vivo cardiovascular assessment in telemetered Beagle dogs (single oral doses of 3, 12, and 50 mg/kg) revealed hypersalivation at 3 mg/kg and vomiting at 50 mg/kg. At 12 and 50 mg/kg, animals exhibited a disruption of the normal nychthemeral heart rate cycle, with blunting of nocturnal heart rate reduction, associated with shortening of PQ, PR, QT, and QT shift intervals. These findings were not consistent with a direct electrophysiological effect, given the Purkinje fibre data, but were considered related to effects on heart rate regulation. The NOAEL was defined as 3 mg/kg.

Overall, leriglitazone did not show significant adverse effects on hERG current, cardiac action potential, CNS function, or respiratory function at exposures well above those anticipated clinically.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.1.4. Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

No dedicated pharmacodynamic interaction programme was presented for leriglitazone, which is acceptable. No clinically meaningful pharmacodynamic interactions are expected based on target selectivity for PPARγ and the absence of robust effects at a broad off-target screening panel.

## 4.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

Leriglitazone absorption and pharmacokinetics were characterised extensively through in vitro, animal and clinical studies. Permeability assays using Caco-2 cells indicated moderate to high apical-tobasolateral transport (&gt;3 × 10⁻⁶ cm/s, mostly ~20 × 10⁻⁶ cm/s), suggesting complete intestinal absorption, which was confirmed in humans with an absolute oral bioavailability exceeding 90%. This places leriglitazone in BCS Class II (low solubility, high permeability). This is consistent with rapid and extensive absorption across species. In mice and rats, oral bioavailability was generally high (≈8590%), with Tmax between 0.5 and 1 h and low clearance, while exposure was mostly dose-proportional and without meaningful accumulation, except for slightly higher exposures in females and some accumulation of metabolite M3. In long-term rat studies, systemic exposure increased proportionally and was sustained without major sex differences, although in carcinogenicity studies females showed slight accumulation. In rabbits, pregnant animals displayed proportional increases in exposure to parent drug and lower exposures to M3, without accumulation. In dogs, oral bioavailability was very high (~96%), absorption was rapid (Tmax ~1 h), and exposure was proportional at low to moderate doses but supra-proportional at the highest dose; M3 levels remained much lower than those of the parent, though administration of M3 itself led to back-conversion into leriglitazone with complete bioavailability. In humans, Phase 1 studies confirmed rapid absorption (Tmax ~2 h) and high oral bioavailability (&gt;90%), with moderate accumulation at steady state. Overall, leriglitazone is rapidly and extensively absorbed, with bioavailability above 85% in all species studied, predictable doseexposure relationships, and limited accumulation, supporting its development for clinical use.

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

Leriglitazone is highly bound to plasma proteins in all species tested, with unbound fractions of about 3-5% in humans and rats, somewhat higher in rabbits and dogs (up to ~15%), and much lower in mice (≈30% free fraction), suggesting generally strong protein binding except in mouse plasma. Its main metabolite M3 also shows high plasma protein binding across species.

Tissue distribution studies with radiolabelled leriglitazone in rats demonstrated rapid and extensive distribution to multiple organs, especially liver, kidney, gastrointestinal tract, urinary bladder, lung and adipose tissue. Importantly, leriglitazone crosses the blood-brain barrier, with brain-to-plasma exposure ratios of about 0.1-0.2 in rodents, and unbound brain-to-plasma partition coefficients (Kp,uu,brain) above 1 in dedicated studies, supporting preferential central nervous system distribution. No mechanistic basis for CNS penetration (passive diffusion vs active transport) was presented.

Spinal nerves showed the highest exposure within the nervous system, while brain and spinal cord exposures were lower. M3 was generally not detected in brain tissue.

Reported brain concentrations are total concentrations measured by LC-MS/MS after protein precipitation, whereas unbound brain concentrations were estimated using species-specific brain tissue binding data and used, together with unbound plasma fractions, to calculate Kp,uu,brain. Leriglitazone showed low unbound plasma fractions in humans and rats, but higher in mice, while unbound brain fractions were similar in rodents. In mice and rats, calculated unbound brain concentrations were in the low hundreds of nM range at efficacious doses, with Kp,uu,brain generally around 0.03-0.3. In rats, calculated unbound brain concentrations were broadly consistent with measured CSF concentrations, supporting CSF as a surrogate of unbound CNS exposure. In humans, CSF concentrations measured in study MT-1-01 (188 and 332 ng/mL at 135 and 270 mg and about 2% of total plasma concentrations, yielding an estimated Kp,uu,CSF of 0.6-0.7. Despite interspecies differences in Kp,uu values, the absolute unbound CNS concentrations are within a similar range across species at efficacious exposures, supporting translational PK/PD relevance.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Radioactivity was also observed in melanin-containing structures such as the uveal tract and pigmented skin, with concentrations higher than in non-pigmented skin, although levels declined over time, suggesting reversible binding rather than strong accumulation. By 24 hours after dosing, most tissues contained less than 1 µg equivalents/g of radioactivity, and only traces remained at 168-336 hours, mainly in the eye and pigmented skin. No placental transfer studies were performed, consistent with the indication limited to males.

The indication for leriglitazone targets male paediatric patients ≥2 years with cALD. Thus, a dedicated juvenile CNS distribution study has been completed in rats. Leriglitazone was administered orally at 25 mg/kg to 3-, 5-, 7-, and 10-week-old rats, with plasma, brain, CSF, and spinal cord sampled up to 12 hours post-dose. No major or systematic age-related differences were observed in plasma exposure or in CNS distribution, with brain-to-plasma and CSF-to-plasma AUC ratios comparable across age groups, including the youngest group considered developmentally relevant to the intended paediatric population. Together with paediatric clinical experience in 23 cALD patients showing plasma exposures within the expected therapeutic range and no CNS-related safety concerns, these data are presented as adequately supporting the assessment of CNS exposure in the target paediatric population.

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

Leriglitazone undergoes metabolism primarily through oxidation and conjugation, with metabolite M3 identified as the main circulating metabolite across species. In rats, mass balance studies following [¹⁴C]-leriglitazone showed that, in plasma, leriglitazone accounted for about 60% of total radioactivity and M3 for about 34% one-hour post-dose, with additional minor sulphated metabolites detected at later time points. In urine, no single metabolite exceeded 10% of total radioactivity and neither leriglitazone nor M3 were detected. In faeces and bile, multiple metabolites were observed, two of which each accounted for more than 10% of the dose, while unchanged parent accounted for about 3%; M3 was absent from excreta.

In vitro studies with human hepatocytes confirmed that leriglitazone is relatively stable, with ~76% remaining after 120 minutes. M3 was less stable (40% remaining), but together parent and M3 accounted for over 98% of drug-related material. Twenty-one other metabolites were detected but each at trace levels (&lt;0.2%). When M3 was incubated separately, it converted back to leriglitazone, supporting in vivo findings of interconversion. In mouse hepatocytes, extensive metabolism was observed, with 36 metabolites detected, although most were at very low abundance except M3 (~25% of total peak area).

Phenotyping experiments in human liver microsomes indicated that CYP2C8 and CYP3A are the main oxidative enzymes, with CYP2C8 being primarily responsible for formation of M3. Other oxidative metabolites such as M102, M103, and M41 were also identified, formed by CYP2C8 and CYP3A isoforms. Conjugation pathways include glucuronidation via UGT2B7 (to M21) and UGT1A4 (to M45), and sulfonation via SULT1A2 and related enzymes (to M28).

Overall, the metabolism of leriglitazone is predictable, with M3 as the only major metabolite in humans and animals, extensive overlap across species, and no unique human-specific metabolites detected.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

Excretion of leriglitazone was evaluated in vitro using human sandwich-cultured hepatocytes and in vivo through radiolabelled mass balance studies in rats. In hepatocyte assays (B-Clear® system), leriglitazone showed minimal biliary excretion, with a low biliary excretion index (ranging between ~2% and 20% depending on concentration) and very low calculated in vitro biliary clearance values (0.0003-0.0008 µL/min/mg protein), suggesting that direct biliary secretion is a minor clearance pathway. There is an apparent inconsistency between in vitro and in vivo excretion data that requires clarification, as mass balance studies in bile duct-cannulated rats indicated that biliary excretion is a major route of elimination (~74% of dose). However, in bile-duct cannulated rats, about 74% of the administered radioactivity was recovered in bile, predominantly as metabolites, while unchanged leriglitazone accounted for only ~3% of the dose. The rat findings therefore do not contradict the in vitro human data, but rather indicate that biliary elimination in rats is mainly a route for metabolites, not parent compound. This interpretation is supported by the human mass balance study, in which only 2.8% of the dose was recovered in faeces as unchanged drug after intravenous administration, indicating that biliary excretion of unchanged leriglitazone is also negligible in humans.

In rats, a single oral dose of [¹⁴C]-leriglitazone (18 mg/kg) resulted in essentially complete recovery of administered radioactivity (&gt;99%) within 7 days. In non-cannulated rats, excretion was rapid, with 87% of the dose eliminated within 24 hours. Faecal elimination predominated, accounting for ~68% of the dose over 168 hours, most of it in the first 24 hours. Urinary elimination accounted for ~32% of the dose, with ~3% recovered in cage washings; only trace amounts (&lt;0.2%) remained in carcasses.

In bile duct-cannulated rats, excretion was mainly via bile, with ~74% of the dose recovered over 48 hours, indicating substantial biliary elimination and suggesting enterohepatic circulation of drug-related material. In this model, urinary excretion represented ~22% of the dose and faecal excretion ~5%. Carcass residues were low (~1%). Total recoveries in both models exceeded 99.9% of the administered dose.

No data on excretion into milk are available, and the applicant considered such studies unnecessary because the indication is restricted to male patients.

## 4.3.2.5. Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

The interaction potential of leriglitazone and its main metabolite M3 with human transporters and CYP enzymes has been extensively characterised in vitro and further assessed through PBPK modelling.

Transporter studies showed that leriglitazone is a substrate of the efflux transporters BCRP and MDR1, while M3 is transported only by BCRP. M3 inhibits BCRP (IC₅₀ 1.25 µM), but estimated unbound exposures are low: ~0.3 µM systemically and in enterocytes, and 0.048-0.144 µM intracellularly in hepatocytes (via Kp,uu). These correspond to ≤24% and 4-12% of the IC₅₀, respectively. The Applicant therefore considers the risk of intestinal or hepatic BCRP-mediated DDIs to be low, though a clinical rosuvastatin DDI study is planned to confirm this.

Leriglitazone did not inhibit MDR1, and neither leriglitazone nor M3 are considered substrates of OATP1A2, OATP1B3, or OATP2B1; however, M3 may be a substrate of OATP1B1. Both leriglitazone and M3 inhibited OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 in vitro, but PBPK modelling and coproporphyrin biomarker studies in humans excluded a clinically relevant effect. Leriglitazone was also shown to inhibit OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, OAT3, and MATE1, while M3 inhibited these same transporters plus MATE2-K; in all cases, risk assessment confirmed negligible potential for clinical DDIs.

CYP enzyme interaction studies demonstrated that leriglitazone is metabolised mainly by CYP2C8 and CYP3A4, with some contribution of CYP2C9. In vitro inhibition studies indicated a low risk of clinically relevant inhibition for all CYPs, including CYP2C8 and CYP2C9, as confirmed by static and PBPK modelling. Both leriglitazone and M3 showed very low potential to inhibit CYPs at clinically relevant concentrations. CYP induction studies detected weak induction of CYP2B6, CYP1A2, and CYP3A4 in vitro at high concentrations; however, PBPK models predicted no clinically meaningful induction.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

PBPK modelling confirmed negligible risk of DDIs through CYP inhibition or induction, negligible risk for transporter-mediated interactions, and excluded clinically relevant interactions with sensitive probe substrates (midazolam, theophylline, rosuvastatin). A small residual uncertainty remains regarding intestinal BCRP inhibition by leriglitazone, but the risk is considered low.

Thus, leriglitazone and M3 show in vitro interactions with several transporters and weak CYP effects, but integrated modelling and biomarker data support a negligible risk of clinically relevant pharmacokinetic DDIs, except for a low but unexcluded potential for intestinal BCRP inhibition.

## 4.3.2.6. Other pharmacokinetic studies

The applicant did not perform other pharmacokinetic studies.

## 4.4. Toxicology

## 4.4.1. Single-dose toxicity

No dedicated single dose toxicity studies have been conducted.

## 4.4.2. Repeat-dose toxicity

Repeated dose toxicity studies comprised a total of 9 studies, all with administration by once daily oral gavage. The main studies were conducted in rats and dogs, with treatment during up 26 and 39 weeks, respectively. In addition, studies were also conducted in mice to determine the dose levels to be used in a carcinogenicity study.

GLP compliant studies in rats included studies with 4-, 13- and 26-weeks treatment duration. In these studies, the leriglitazone tested dose levels were 25, 100 and 150 mg/kg/day, in the 4-week study, 25, 50 and 100 mg/kg/day, in the 13-week, and 12.5, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day, in the 26-week study. In addition, both the 4- and 13-week studies have also included groups of animals given pioglitazone as a comparator. The 13- and 26-week studies included recovery periods of 4 and 8 weeks, respectively.

GLP compliant studies in dogs included studies with 13- and 39-weeks treatment duration, with recovery periods of 4 and 13 weeks, respectively. Leriglitazone was tested at 0.6, 2.5 and 10 mg/kg/day, in the 13-week study, and at 0.2, 0.6, 2.5 and 10 mg/kg/day, in the 39-week study.

Regarding the studies to determine dose levels to be used in the mouse carcinogenicity study, the mouse GLP compliant study was a 13-week study, with administration of leriglitazone at 12.5, 25, 50 and 100 mg/kg/day. The study has not included a recovery period.

Targets of toxicity as identified in the chronic toxicity study in rats included the haematopoietic system (including decrease in RBC), heart (increase in weight), adipose tissue (adipose changes observed in epididymis, ovaries, mesenteric tissue, and hibernating gland, including lobular formation, reduced size of adipocytes, fibroplasia and macrovesicular change; subcutaneous masses composed of thickened areas of white fat), mammary gland (atrophy/feminisation in males and lobulo-alveolar hyperplasia in females), pituitary gland (vacuolation in pars distalis and dilated sinusoids) and ovary (atrophy and disruption of the oestrous cycle). A subcutaneous malignant sarcoma was found in one male given the high dose. Five early deaths among males were attributed to leriglitazone. There was evidence of partial or complete recovery in most of the affected tissue. Following the recovery phase, mean absolute heart were still increased. The NOAEL was set at 12.5 mg/kg/day.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Targets identified in chronic toxicity study in dogs included the hematopoietic system (decreased in RBC), heart (increase in weight, hypertrophy/hyperplasia of the mesothelium lining portions of the epicardium), adipose tissue (steatopathy of white fat), liver (increase in weight, extramedullary haematopoiesis), rectum (atrophy in the perianal sebaceous glands), mammary gland (squamous metaplasia), ovaries (corpora lutea cysts). There was evidence of partial or complete recovery in most of the affected tissue. Following the recovery phase, mean absolute heart were still increased. The NOAEL for males and females were set at 0.6 and 0.2 mg/kg/day, respectively.

Effects observed in mice were generally consistent with those observed for rats and dogs.

Based on comparative systemic exposures to unbound leriglitazone and leriglitazone combined with metabolite M3, safety margins for patients based on animal exposure at the NOAEL in chronic toxicity studies are low to null. At the maximum tested doses, in rats, these margins were 3.96 and 4.7-times, respectively, the target exposure in adult patients; in dogs, 2.89 and 2.63-times, respectively.

## 4.4.3. Genotoxicity

The potential genotoxicity of leriglitazone was tested in four assay types, Ames test, mouse lymphoma assay, in vivo rat micronucleus assay and, as a follow-up study, in vivo rat Comet assay (with assessment of DNA damage in liver and duodenum). All definitive studies were GLP compliant.

The in vitro assays were conducted with and without metabolic activation. In the mouse lymphoma assay, the treatment periods were of 3 hours, with or without metabolic activation, and 24 hours, without metabolic activation. Both in vivo studies used male and female animals and treatment during 2 consecutive days, with administration by oral gavage. In the micronucleus assay, leriglitazone was administered at doses up to 500 and 1250 mg/kg/day in males and females, respectively; in the Comet assay, at doses up to 1500 and 2000 mg/kg/day, respectively.

The mouse lymphoma assay revealed an increase in mutant frequency (small colonies) following the 24-h treatment period. No mutagenicity was observed neither with 3 hours treatment in the mouse lymphoma assay nor in the other conducted genotoxicity assays.

## 4.4.4. Carcinogenicity

The Applicant has completed a long-term carcinogenicity studies in mice and rats. In the mouse study, leriglitazone was administered by oral gavage, at 10, 30 and 100 mg/kg/day, in males, and at 5, 15 and 50 mg/kg/day, in females. The high doses were selected based on data from 13-week GLP oral toxicity study in CD-1 mice.

The mouse carcinogenicity study revealed no carcinogenicity. Non-neoplastic microscopic findings were present in the heart and lungs of males and in the bone marrow, spleen, and adipose tissue of both sexes, and were consistent with known class effects of PPAR-γ agonists. A test article-related increase in mortality occurred in males administered 100 mg/kg/day, leading to early termination of this group in Week 95.

At the maximum tested doses, systemic exposures to unbound leriglitazone in combination with metabolite M3 were up to 32.9-fold the maximum clinical exposure.

In the rat carcinogenicity study, leriglitazone was administered by oral gavage, at 1, 2.5 or 8 mg/kg/day, in males, and 1.25, 4 or 12.5 mg/kg/day, in females. The high doses were selected based on the finding in heart weight increase in the repeated dose toxicity studies in rats.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The study revealed no carcinogenicity. In addition, there were no increase in mortality, or leriglitazonerelated clinical signs, effects on body weight, haematology, macroscopic or microscopic findings at necropsy. All differences in organ weight parameters, statistically significant or not, including in the heart, were considered to be consistent with normal variation.

At the maximum tested doses, systemic exposures to unbound leriglitazone or leriglitazone in combination with metabolite M3 in rats were identical or lower than target exposure in patients.

## 4.4.5. Developmental and reproductive toxicity

Reproductive toxicity studies included studies on fertility, embryo-foetal development and juvenile animal studies.

## Fertility and early embryonic development

Studies on fertility and early embryonic development comprised two GLP-compliant studies in rats, one with treatment of males only and the other of females only.

In both studies, leriglitazone was administered by oral gavage at dose levels of 12.5, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day, starting at 2 weeks pre-mating. In the study with treatment of females, at the high dose (50 mg/kg/day), there was a decrease in the number of live embryos, attributed to a slight reduction in corpora lutea and fewer implantation sites, as well as increases in the incidence of pre- and postimplantation loss. The NOAEL for males and females were set at 50 and 25 mg/kg/day, respectively.

None of the studies included toxicokinetic analysis.

## Embryo-foetal development

Studies on potential effects on embryo-foetal development were conducted in rats and rabbits, with the pivotal studies (GLP-compliant) preceded by the respective dose range finding studies. In the pivotal studies, leriglitazone was administered at 5, 12.5 or 30 mg/kg/day in rats, and at 5, 15 or 50 mg/kg/kg/day, in rabbits. Both studies included toxicokinetic analysis.

In both studies, the maternal NOAEL was set at the maximum tested dose. The development NOAEL was set at the mid tested dose, based on the observation of decreased foetal body weights.

Based on comparative AUC for unbound fraction, at the developmental NOAEL in rats and rabbits, systemic exposure to both leriglitazone and to combined leriglitazone and M3 metabolite were lower than the target systemic exposure in patients; at the maximum tested doses, only slightly higher.

## Pre- postnatal development

No pre- and postnatal development studies have been conducted.

## Juvenile animal studies

Studies in juvenile animals comprised two studies in rat, a dose range finding study and a GLP compliant 10-week study, with a 4-week recovery period. In the pivotal study, rats received leriglitazone by oral gavage at 12.5, 25 or 50 mg/kg/day, starting at postnatal day 22. In addition to standard repeated dose toxicity endpoints, the animals were also examined for long bone growth and bone mineral density, development and behavioural observations, reproductive performance, and sperm analysis.

Effects observed were generally consistent with those observed in adult animals, including, among others, increase in heart weight and changes in adipose tissue. There were no adverse developmental findings, as assessed by long bone growth, bone mineral density, learning and memory, auditory function, motor activity, sexual development, and reproductive performance.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Nevertheless, in the absence of historical control data for bone density, the relevance for humans of the observed minimal to slight decreases in bone density was not excluded.

The NOAEL was set at 25 mg/kg/day, based on changes in adipose tissue and death of one male at 50 mg/kg/day. At the NOAEL, systemic exposure (AUC) to unbound leriglitazone or leriglitazone combined with M3 were only slightly higher than target exposure in adult patients; at the 50 mg/kg/day, approximately, 3 times higher.

## 4.4.6. Toxicokinetics and exposure margins

Except for the fertility and early embryonic development studies, all pivotal in vivo toxicological studies have included toxicokinetic data for leriglitazone. In addition, except for two repeated dose toxicity studies in rats - 4- and 13-weeks duration -, all of these have also included toxicokinetic data for the metabolite M3.

Except for the in vivo micronucleus assays, all pivotal studies with toxicokinetic analysis have also included analysis of plasma samples from control (vehicle) animals. With one exception, leriglitazone and, applicable, metabolite M3, concentrations were below the bioanalytical limit of quantitation in all plasma samples analyzed from control animals. The one exception, in the 4-week rat repeated dose toxicity study, was considered to represent ex vivo contamination.

Upon administration of leriglitazone, systemic exposure to leriglitazone and its metabolite M3 increased with dose, and there were no consistent differences between genders nor an accumulation following repeated dose. Exposure to metabolite M3 was lower than to leriglitazone in mice, dogs and rabbits. In rats, exposures to metabolite M3 and leriglitazone were there similar. M3 is the main metabolite of leriglitazone and is pharmacologically active.

Except for when considering unbound fraction in mice, systemic exposures in the animal studies were generally lower or only slightly higher than the indicated target systemic exposure in patients.

## 4.4.7. Local tolerance

No dedicated local tolerance studies of leriglitazone have been conducted.

## 4.4.8. Other toxicity studies

No other toxicity studies have been conducted, except for a negative Ames test with a potential impurity (dimethylglyoxime), with the respective study report located in the quality sections of the dossier.

## 4.4.9. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

The Environmental Risk Assessment (ERA) provided by the Applicant follows the Guideline on the Environmental Risk Assessment of Medicinal Products for Human Use (CHMP/SWP/4447/00, Rev 1, 2024).

An ERA Phase I was conducted to assess the environmental risk associated with Nezglyal, an oral suspension at 13.7 mg/mL, indicated for male patients with adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2 years and older with cerebral ALD (cALD).

Relevant endpoints, methods used and results obtained were discussed, and study results are summarised in Table 5.
