<!-- image -->

Phase I was conducted to evaluate environmental exposure. Log Dow values determined experimentally using the Shake Flask Method (Test OECD Guideline 107) at three pH levels were -0.97 at pH 1.0, 2.21 at pH 5.5, and -0.57 at pH 10.0, all below the trigger value of 4.5. Consequently, Leriglitazone is not classified as a persistent and bioaccumulative substance. Therefore, no further screening for persistence, bioaccumulation, and toxicity (PBT) assessment is necessary.

Following EMA guidelines, the Applicant's Fpen refinement relies on prevalence data for the orphan disease ALD within the European Union, which is 0.4 per 10,000 people. This information comes from the European Orphanet Report Series, 'Prevalence of Rare Diseases: Bibliographic Data - June 2013, Number 1,' corresponding to 3.5 cases per 100,000 individuals. For the current prevalence, the 2014 Orphanet Report Series provides detailed data, although the disease itself is not explicitly mentioned.

The refined PEC surface water value is 0.00328 µg/l, which is below the action limit established by EMA.

Based on the values obtained for Refined PECsurfacewater and log Dow at three environmentally relevant pHs, which are below the action limits defined by EMA, no Phase II assessment is required.

Therefore, it can be assumed that the approval of Nezglyal, an oral suspension at 13.7 mg/mL, is unlikely to pose a risk to the environment when used as prescribed in patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant applied suitable precautionary and safety measures to inform patients and healthcare professionals, mitigate environmental risks, and promote environmental protection, as outlined in EMEA/CHMP/SWP/4447/00, 2024.

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

## Pharmacology

## Primary Pharmacodynamics

A comprehensive panel of pharmacology studies has been conducted by the applicant to substantiate the proposed MoA of leriglitazone. Leriglitazone demonstrated comparable activity toward human and rodent PPARγ receptors, with EC50 values of 10.3 μM and 12.5 μM, respectively, while no pharmacological activity was observed toward PPARα or PPARδ, confirming the selectivity of leriglitazone for PPARγ. The in vivo epimerisation of leriglitazone at the C2 chiral centre is rapid and leads to a stable configuration that is species-dependent: E1 predominates in rodents, whereas E2 predominates in humans and dogs. Since both diastereomers E1 and E2 exhibited similar activity toward the human PPARγ receptor (EC50 of 13.5 and 18.3 μM, respectively), species-dependent differences in pharmacological activity are not expected. Although binding to the dog PPARγ receptor has not been directly assessed, the 100% sequence homology with the human receptor suggests comparable binding.

The MoA of leriglitazone has also been studied in a cell-based model, where markers of adipogenic differentiation and metabolism were used as indirect read-outs of PPARγ agonism. The PPARγ antagonist GW9662 reduced leriglitazone-induced lipid accumulation, expression of the PPARγ engagement marker FABP4, adiponectin mRNA, and PPARγ mRNA, supporting that these effects are mediated through PPARγ activation. Similar observations were made in rats, where leriglitazone administration significantly increased adiponectin concentrations in plasma and CSF from the lowest leriglitazone dose tested (6 mg/kg/day for 7 days).

Additional confirmatory studies of PPARγ agonism were performed in an ALD in vitro model using primary cortical neurons injured with VLCFA (C26:0, cerotonic acid) alone or in combination with LPS. At 100 nM, leriglitazone significantly improved neuronal survival and neurite network integrity and reduced microglial activation following C26:0-induced damage.

A comprehensive panel of non-clinical studies has been submitted to support the efficacy of leriglitazone on cALD-relevant pathological processes, including neuroinflammation, BBB disruption and permeability, demyelination, axonal damage, and neurodegeneration.

Neuroinflammation in performed pharmacology studies were induced using VLCFA cerotonic acid or LPS. In rat spinal cord motor neurons exposed to cerotonic acid, leriglitazone attenuated NF-κB pathway activation. EAE and ICV-LPS mouse models were employed as relevant non-clinical systems, as both recapitulate key pathological features of cALD, including neuroinflammation and demyelination. In the EAE model leriglitazone reduced a dose-dependent reduction in severity score, and in ICV-LPS model, it significantly decreased IFN-γ, IL-1β, IL-6, TNF-α, as well as CD68+ and GFAP+ brain areas. Cytokine profiles varied with time, indicating that the effects of leriglitazone were highly time dependent. Additional in vitro studies further supported anti-inflammatory findings. Leriglitazone significantly reduced TNF-α secretion in THP-1 cells and decreased TNF-α mRNA expression in monocyte-derived macrophages from AMN patients following LPS stimulation. Similarly, in human microglial HMC3 cells, leriglitazone markedly suppressed IL-1β and IL-18 secretion in response to LPS-induced inflammation. In a co-culture BBB model consisting of rat cortical neurons, human brain microvascular endothelial cells, and astrocytes, leriglitazone improved neurite network integrity, enhanced astrocyte survival, and reduced microglial activation following injury with cerotonic acid or cerotonic acid/LPS. These protective effects were observed both under normal conditions and after ABCD1 gene silencing in endothelial cells, indicating that leriglitazone retains activity irrespective of ABCD1 status. The ability of leriglitazone to penetrate the BBB and exert neuroprotective effects even when the barrier remained intact was demonstrated. Furthermore, leriglitazone decreased monocyte adhesion to ABCD1 knock-down endothelial cells at 100 μM (THP-1 cells) and 1 μM (primary PBMCs). As ABCD1 deficiency is associated with increased monocyte infiltration into the brain, this finding suggests that leriglitazone may reduce immune cell trafficking across the BBB. However, since migration assays were not performed, the evidence is considered supportive but not confirmatory. Leriglitazone has demonstrated protective and restorative effects on demyelination across a range of experimental models. In vitro, leriglitazone (≥0.5 µM) enhanced microglial capacity to phagocytose myelin debris which is linked to tissue repair and neuroprotection. In oligodendrocyte precursor lineage at healthy conditions, leriglitazone promoted precursor cell proliferation (≥1 nM), stimulated differentiation into mature oligodendrocytes (10-30 nM), and improved myelination at concentrations of 30 nM and above. Under healthy conditions, leriglitazone also supported neuronal network development, increasing neurite elongation (100-300 nM) of primary neurons. In injury models, leriglitazone maintained its protective profile. In LPS-induced brain inflammation, 100 nM leriglitazone significantly improved both neurite network elongation and myelination of rat central neurons. In the cuprizone model of oligodendrocyte death, pre-treatment with leriglitazone preserved MBP expression at 10-30 nM, promoted neurite elongation at ≥1 nM, and enhanced myelination at 10-30 nM. Similarly, in an in vitro model of vincristine-induced peripheral neuropathy, leriglitazone protected Schwann cells, as shown by increased MBP expression at 1-30 nM and improved neurite network integrity at 3-30 nM. In vivo studies further confirmed these findings. In C57BL/6J mice exposed to cuprizone, pretreatment with 75 mg/kg/day leriglitazone followed by continued administration during demyelination significantly increased the density of myelinated axons. A similar protective effect was observed when leriglitazone was administered alongside cuprizone, although this benefit was not maintained when rapamycin was added to the regimen. Taken together, these results demonstrate that leriglitazone protects against demyelination, supports glial cell survival, facilitates remyelination, and enhances neuronal network integrity across both in vitro and in vivo models. Cerotonic acid has been used to induce axonal damage and neurodegeneration in cALD in vitro models. While some of the performed studies employed both lignoceric acid (C24:0) and cerotonic acid, the presented data focuses on leriglitazone effects achieved in cerotonic acid induced injury. The applicant justifies that cerotonic acid is a more appropriate VLCFA for ALD models given its accumulation as C26:0-lysophosphatidylcholine in ALD patients, stronger induction of ROS and cell death compared with lignoceric acid. This justification is considered acceptable. In rat spinal cord motor neurons and astroglia, leriglitazone demonstrated protective effects against cerotonic acidinduced injury at 3-30 nM concentrations. Pre-treatment and co-incubation with leriglitazone preserved neuronal viability, reduced ROS production, decreased oxidative stress as assessed by methionine sulfoxide reductase expression, and improved ATP levels. In oligodendrocytes, which are particularly vulnerable to VLCFA toxicity, leriglitazone at 10-30 nM significantly improved oligodendrocyte survival. Overall, leriglitazone improved the survival of motor neurons, astrocytes, and oligodendrocytes after cerotonic acid-induced toxicity, although cell type-specific differences in susceptibility were noted.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The ABCD1 knock-out mouse accumulates VLCFAs and develops progressive biochemical, histological, and functional alterations that resemble AMN. Although it does not reproduce the cerebral pathology of cALD, it is a relevant model to investigate mechanisms of disease and evaluate therapeutic effects of leriglitazone. The applicant claimed that leriglitazone efficacy in AMN non-clinical models has been assessed because clinical trials involve patients with AMN and cALD. In ABCD1 knock-out mousederived primary neuronal cultures, leriglitazone (30-300 nM) increased the number of surviving spinal cord motor and cortical neurons after cerotonic acid-induced toxicity, reduced cortical ROS (300 nM), and enhanced neurite network integrity (10-300 nM). In vivo, short-term administration of leriglitazone in ABCD1 knock-out mice (7 days, twice daily) produced dose-dependent increases in plasma adiponectin levels (significant at ≥50 mg/kg/day), consistent with PPARγ activation. Furthermore, leriglitazone upregulated Nrf1 and SOD2 gene expression, suggesting enhanced mitochondrial biogenesis and antioxidant activity, and reduced spinal cord expression of proinflammatory cytokines IL-1β and TNFα at 50 mg/kg/day.

In a ABCD1/ABCD2 double knock-out model, which develops earlier and more pronounced AMN-like pathology, leriglitazone demonstrated both functional and neuropathological benefits. Motor performance in rotarod assays improved at 110 mg/kg/day (month 16), at 75-110 mg/kg/day (month 18), and at 110 mg/kg/day (month 20), while balance beam outcomes were variable and significance was reached only at 17 mg/kg/day (month 20). Neuropathological evaluation confirmed significant reductions in axonal swelling (APP marker) and microglial activation (MAC-3 marker) at 110 mg/kg/day. Taken together, the non-clinical evidence demonstrates consistent neuroprotective activity of leriglitazone in cerotonic acid-induced toxicity models in vitro and in both single and double ABCD1-deficient mouse models.

In summary, the MoA and efficacy of leriglitazone are supported by a comprehensive set of in vitro and in vivo studies. The data consistently confirm that leriglitazone acts as a PPARγ receptor agonist. Its therapeutic potential in the proposed cALD indication is mediated by penetration of the BBB, reduction of neuroinflammation, promotion of remyelination, and preservation of neuronal and glial cell survival. In all models, leriglitazone has been administered as a pre-treatment prior to injury induction in both in vitro and in vivo studies, which raises questions about its effectiveness in reducing an already existing injury. Nevertheless, its protective effects when used as a pre-treatment have been consistently demonstrated. In section 5.1 of the SmPC, the proposed mechanism of action is considered adequately substantiated and supported by the available data.

## Secondary pharmacodynamics

Binding selectivity of leriglitazone was tested at a concentration of 10 μM in a panel of 55 proteins, including receptors, ion channels, and transporters and no meaningful inhibition (&gt; 50% at 10 μM) towards any of the tested targets was observed.

Leriglitazone did not demonstrate inhibitory potential toward MAO-A up to 30 µM but showed inhibitory activity toward MAO-B (IC₅₀ = 0.82 µM). Functional studies demonstrated that its neuroprotective effects are PPARγ-dependent and independent of dopaminergic or MAO-B mechanisms. In ALD models, efficacy is linked to anti-inflammatory, mitochondrial, and neuroprotective pathways consistent with PPARγ activation. While a minor adjunctive role of partial MAO-B inhibition cannot be excluded, it is not considered clinically meaningful. This is further supported by a safety profile consistent with PPARγ agonists and lacking features typical of MAO-B inhibition.

## Safety pharmacology

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Safety pharmacology studies have been performed in line with ICH S7A requirements. Leriglitazone safety on cardiovascular system has been assessed in vitro and in vivo; CNS and respiratory system have been evaluated in in vivo.

Based on cardiovascular safety studies, leriglitazone shows no relevant risk for QTc prolongation, with hERG inhibition occurring only at concentrations &gt;300 µM, which is &gt;250-fold above the highest anticipated leriglitazone exposure (steady state Cmax,u - 1.16 μM). In dog Purkinje fibers a decrease in APD50 was observed at 30 μM leriglitazone, however, no effects were detected on APD70, APD90, or on early or delayed after-depolarisations at any concentration tested, thereby supporting the absence of a QTc prolongation risk. In conscious dogs, oral administration of leriglitazone at 12 and 50 mg/kg altered the nychthemeral heart rate cycle with secondary changes in conduction and repolarisation parameters, while no cardiovascular effects were observed at 3 mg/kg. Thus, the NOAEL was set at 3 mg/kg. The corresponding Cmax,u (considering total Cmax of 4720 ng/mL and unbound fraction of 0.143) is 1.6-fold higher than the proposed clinical Cmax,u of 1.16 μM at 180 mg (considering total Cmax of 12046 ng/mL, unbound fraction of 0.036). Although the safety margin is considered low, effects on cardiovascular parameters are known class effects of PPARγ agonists.

In rats, leriglitazone showed no effects on behaviour or body temperature at 170 mg/kg in the Irwin's test. Respiratory studies identified only minor, non-adverse changes in breathing pattern, with a NOAEL of 150 mg/kg orally, indicating no safety concerns for CNS or respiratory function.

## Pharmacokinetics

## Analytical method validation

An LC/MS/MS method was validated for the quantification of leriglitazone and its main metabolite M3 in mouse, rat, rabbit, and dog plasma - the species used in toxicokinetic studies. In mouse plasma, the validated calibration ranges were 50.0-50,000 ng/mL for leriglitazone and 20.0-20,000 ng/mL for M3. In rabbit plasma, the ranges were 100-100,000 ng/mL for leriglitazone and 10.0-10,000 ng/mL for M3, while in dog plasma the validated ranges were 20.0-20,000 ng/mL for leriglitazone and 10.010,000 ng/mL for M3. In rat plasma determination of leriglitazone and M3 has been validated within a range of 50.0 to 50000 ng/mL and 20.0 to 20000 ng/mL. In another validation study the range was determined to be 30.0 to 30000 ng/mL for leriglitazone and 20.0 to 20000 ng/mL for M3 in rat plasma. The validated methods for detection of leriglitazone in animal plasma were considered acceptable for their intended purpose based on selectivity, accuracy, precision, LLOQ, range/linearity, extraction recovery and stability.

To test leriglitazone formulations used in GLP toxicokinetic studies, HPLC-UV and HPLC methods have been validated.

## Absorption

## Single-dose administration

The absorption of leriglitazone in single-dose studies was assessed by IV and oral administration in mouse rat and dog.

Following intravenous administration, leriglitazone exhibited low systemic clearance in both mice (11.79 mL/min/kg vs. liver blood flow of 90 mL/min/kg) and rats (2.63 mL/min/kg vs. 55 mL/min/kg), with terminal half-lives of 1.79 h and 5.6 h, respectively. The Vss was moderate in mice (Vss = 1.26 L/kg, approximately twice total body water), indicating moderate tissue distribution, whereas in rats Vss was 0.73 L/kg, close to total body water, suggesting more limited distribution. Following oral administration, leriglitazone was rapidly absorbed, with Tmax values of 0.5-1 h in mice, 0.5-2 h in rats, and 1-4 h in dogs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Leriglitazone exerts high oral bioavailability - 85% in mouse, 86% in rat, 96% in dog. In vivo data are in line with in vitro data from Caco-2 permeability study in which the permeability of leriglitazone was approximately 20 × 10⁻⁶ cm/s indicating that the soluble dose is expected to be fully absorbed through the intestinal wall in vivo.

After single administration leriglitazone exposure increases with increasing dose in all non-clinical species.

In mice M3 showed low clearance (20.65 mL/min/kg), a half-life of 2h, and a Vss of 2.73 L/kg, around 4-fold higher than total body water, suggesting extensive distribution. In beagle dogs a complete oral bioavailability was demonstrated for M3.

## Repeated-dose administration

Repeated-dose administration studies have been conducted in mice, rats, dogs, as well as in juvenile rats and pregnant rats and rabbits.

Across species and study designs, leriglitazone demonstrated generally dose proportional increase in systemic exposure, high oral bioavailability, and rapid absorption. M3 exposure typically increased with dose but remained lower than for leriglitazone. In general, no significant sex differences nor accumulation of leriglitazone were observed across the tested non-clinical species. M3 accumulation was observed in rats at 26-weeks, however it was not confirmed in other non-clinical species.

Leriglitazone can form two types of diastereomers - E1 and E2. In rodents, E1 is the predominant form, whereas in dogs and humans, E2 is more prevalent. According to primary pharmacology studies both E1 and E2 show potency comparable to leriglitazone at the PPARγ receptor, thus the difference in predominant diastereomer across species is not considered to have toxicological or clinical relevance.

## Distribution

Leriglitazone exerts high plasma protein binding in human, rat, rabbit and dog, while lower plasma protein binding was observed in mouse. The free leriglitazone fraction at 1-100 μM test concentration was 4.9 - 5.2% for rat, 4.1-6.9% in rabbit, 9.4-14.3% in dog, 33-36% for mouse and 3-4.3% in human plasma. No concentration dependency was observed, thus the average values of free leriglitazone in rat and human were used for DDI and safety margin determination - 5.1% and 3.6%, respectively.

The unbound fraction of M3 at concentrations 0.5 and 5 μM was 1.9% and 1.8% in human plasma, 2.7% and 3.2% in rat plasma, 3.6% and 4% in rabbit plasma and 5.7% and 6.9% in dog plasma and 4.9% and 7.9% in mouse plasma.

Leriglitazone and M3 do not extensively bind to cellular fraction of blood or erythrocytes as confirmed by Kblood/plasma and KRBC/plasma values &lt; 1.

Reported brain concentrations are total concentrations measured by LC-MS/MS after protein precipitation, whereas unbound brain concentrations were estimated using species-specific brain tissue binding data and used, together with unbound plasma fractions, to calculate Kp,uu,brain. Leriglitazone showed low unbound plasma fractions in humans and rats, but higher in mice, while unbound brain fractions were similar in rodents. In mice and rats, calculated unbound brain concentrations were in the low hundreds of nM range at efficacious doses, with Kp,uu,brain generally around 0.03-0.3. In rats, calculated unbound brain concentrations were broadly consistent with measured CSF concentrations, supporting CSF as a surrogate of unbound CNS exposure. In humans, CSF concentrations measured in study MT-1-01 (188 and 332 ng/mL at 135 and 270 mg) corresponded to approximately 509-898 nM and about 2% of total plasma concentrations, yielding an estimated Kp,uu,CSF of 0.6-0.7. Despite interspecies differences in Kp,uu values, the absolute unbound CNS concentrations are within a similar range across species at efficacious exposures, supporting translational PK/PD relevance.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A dedicated juvenile CNS distribution study has been completed in rats. Leriglitazone was administered orally at 25 mg/kg to 3-, 5-, 7-, and 10-week-old rats, with plasma, brain, CSF, and spinal cord sampled up to 12 hours post-dose. No major or systematic age-related differences were observed in plasma exposure or in CNS distribution, with brain-to-plasma and CSF-to-plasma AUC ratios comparable across age groups, including the youngest group considered developmentally relevant to the intended paediatric population. Together with paediatric clinical experience in 23 cALD patients showing plasma exposures within the expected therapeutic range and no CNS-related safety concerns, these data are presented as adequately supporting the assessment of CNS exposure in the target paediatric population.

Only male rats were enrolled in tissue distribution study which is considered acceptable considering the intended use only in male cALD patients. In albino rats, the highest concentrations of radioactivity were observed in blood and in elimination-associated organs (gastrointestinal tract, urinary bladder, liver, and kidney), as well as in lung and spinal nerves. By 168 hours post-dose, radioactivity was no longer detectable in most tissues. In partially pigmented rats, the greatest concentrations were found in the gastrointestinal tract and urinary bladder, with notable levels also in the liver, kidney cortex, blood, lung, and spinal nerve. At 168 hours post-dose, measurable radioactivity persisted only in the large intestine and caecum contents, the uveal tract, and spinal nerve. Radioactivity was also detected in melanin-containing tissues, with higher concentrations in pigmented skin. The interaction with melanin was reversible, as concentrations declined steadily in all melanin-containing tissues. Low levels of leriglitazone remained detectable in the uveal tract (0.179 μg equivalents/g) at 168 hours. No dedicated phototoxicity study was conducted, but this is considered acceptable given the demonstrated reversibility of melanin binding and the decline in uveal tract concentrations.

Placental transfer was not studied, which is considered acceptable considering the intended explicit use in male cALD patients.

## Metabolism

Leriglitazone metabolism was assessed in vitro in human and mice hepatocytes and human liver microsomes. The main metabolite was M3 accounting for 22% and 25% of share of the total combined LC/MS peak in human and mice, respectively. Other metabolites were present at very low levels.

M3 metabolism was studied in human hepatocytes and the main M3 metabolite was leriglitazone accounting for 71% share of the total LC/MS peak area. Other metabolite abundance was low.

CYP2C8 and CYP3A are suggested as the key oxidative enzymes involved in the metabolism of leriglitazone, with CYP2C8 being mainly in charge of the formation of the main metabolite, M3. Based on the available studies, it is concluded that the conversion of M3 to leriglitazone is not mediated by CYP enzymes, whereas the reverse conversion of leriglitazone to M3 is CYP-dependent. The metabolism of M3 is likely catalysed by aldoketo reductase, with additional evidence suggesting possible involvement of CYP reductase and carbonyl reductase.

UGT and SULT enzymes were shown to possess a minor route of leriglitazone elimination and given the low abundance of these metabolites it is considered acceptable to omit additional clinical characterisation for the interaction with these enzymes.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In vivo metabolism of leriglitazone was characterized in humans, rats, and dogs. Across all species, M3 was the major metabolite, representing 25-31% of plasma exposure in rats, 7-11% in dogs, and 21-25% in humans. Therefore, both rat and dog are considered relevant toxicology species, as their biotransformation pathways are comparable to those in humans. It is claimed that no unique human metabolites were identified, however according to data summarized in Pharmacokinetics tabulated summary metabolites M102, M48, M49, M50, M51, M53, M55, M56 were only present in human and were not identified in non-clinical species. Since the abundance of these metabolites in human is very low, they are not considered clinically relevant, and no concerns are raised as it is not considered to affect B/R.

## Excretion

In the sandwich-cultured human hepatocytes the biliary clearance of leriglitazone was determined to be 0.0003 and 0.0008 μL/min/mg protein at 10 and 1 μM respectively, which is considered to be low.

In a mass balance study in non-cannulated rats faecal route was the dominant excretion pathway accounting for 68% of the recovered dose at 168h, while urine accounted for 32%. In BDC rats biliary route was the predominant excretion pathway with 74% of the dose recovered after 48h in bile, while 22% were recovered in urine. In cannulated and non-cannulated rats recovery of leriglitazone was complete.

Taken together, the in vitro data from human sandwich-cultured hepatocytes suggest minimal biliary excretion, whereas the in vivo mass balance studies in BDC rats indicate that biliary elimination is the major route of excretion. However, in bile-duct cannulated rats, about 74% of the administered radioactivity was recovered in bile, predominantly as metabolites, while unchanged leriglitazone accounted for only ~3% of the dose. The rat findings therefore do not contradict the in vitro human data but rather indicate that biliary elimination in rats is mainly a route for metabolites, not parent compound. This interpretation is supported by the human mass balance study, in which only 2.8% of the dose was recovered in faeces as unchanged drug after intravenous administration, indicating that biliary excretion of unchanged leriglitazone is also negligible in humans.

Excretion in milk has not been studies which is considered acceptable due to intended use only in male cALD patients.

## Drug-drug interactions

PK DDI have been studied in line with ICH M12 and EMA DDI guideline requirements.

Leriglitazone and M3 were determined to be a reversible inhibitor of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9. The determined IC50 values for leriglitazone 63-fold exceed the proposed clinical Cmax of 1.16 μM at 180 mg dose and thus the possible interaction at clinical exposure is considered negligible.

The risk of leriglitazone for clinical DDI through CYP1A2 and CYP3A4 induction was evaluated using a PBPK modelling approach and confirmed to be negligible.

Leriglitazone at 150 μM and 175 μM inhibited BCRP-dependent prazosin transport by 57.0% and 29.9%, respectively. Although this could indicate that leriglitazone is BCRP inhibitor, in the absence of a concentration dependent response the IC50 could not be calculated.

Leriglitazone and M3 are substrates of the BCRP efflux transporter and only leriglitazone is substrate of MDR1 (P-gp) efflux transporter. The high oral bioavailability of leriglitazone demonstrated in nonclinical and clinical studies indicates that intestinal absorption is not significantly compromised. However, based on the PK DDI risk assessment performed by the applicant the potential for inhibition of BCRP at the level of the intestine by leriglitazone cannot be completely ruled out. The information that leriglitazone is a substrate of the efflux transporters BCRP and MDR1, and that intestinal BCRP inhibition is not known, is included in section 4.5 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

M3 inhibits BCRP (IC₅₀ 1.25 µM), but estimated unbound exposures are low: ~0.3 µM systemically and in enterocytes, and 0.048-0.144 µM intracellularly in hepatocytes (via Kp,uu). These correspond to ≤24% and 4-12% of the IC₅₀, respectively. The Applicant therefore considers the risk of intestinal or hepatic BCRP-mediated DDIs to be low, though a clinical rosuvastatin DDI study is planned to confirm this.

Clinical relevance of other detected leriglitazone interactions with uptake transporters were concluded to be negligible based on basic model calculations and PBPK modelling approach.

## Toxicology

The toxicity of leriglitazone was investigated in a comprehensive battery of in vitro and in vivo studies. The package of toxicological studies conducted with leriglitazone included studies on repeated dose toxicity, genotoxicity, carcinogenicity and reproductive toxicity. No single-dose toxicity studies were performed. This is acceptable. The toxicity profile following repeat dosing of leriglitazone was characterised in mice, rats and dogs and was consistent with that observed for other members of the PPARy agonist class.

Except for the fertility and early embryonic development studies, all pivotal in vivo toxicological studies have included toxicokinetic data for leriglitazone. In addition, except for two repeated dose toxicity studies in rats (4- and 13-weeks duration), all of these have also included toxicokinetic data for the metabolite M3. Except for the in vivo micronucleus assays, all pivotal studies with toxicokinetic analysis have also included analysis of plasma samples from control (vehicle) animals. With one exception, leriglitazone and, as applicable, metabolite M3, concentrations were below the bioanalytical limit of quantitation in all plasma samples analyzed from control animals. The one exception, in the 4-week rat repeated dose toxicity study, was considered to represent ex vivo contamination.

Upon administration of leriglitazone, systemic exposure to leriglitazone and its metabolite M3 increased with dose, and there were no consistent differences between genders nor an accumulation following repeated dose. Exposure to metabolite M3 was lower than to leriglitazone in mice, dogs and rabbits. In rats, exposures to metabolite M3 and leriglitazone were there similar. M3 is the main metabolite of leriglitazone and is pharmacologically active.

All pivotal studies were GLP compliant.

Further detailed information and discussion on the different study types are included below.

## Single dose toxicity

No dedicated single dose toxicity studies have been conducted. This is acceptable.

## Repeated dose toxicity

Repeated dose toxicity studies comprised a total of 9 studies, all with administration by once daily oral gavage. The main studies were conducted in rats and dogs, with treatment during up 26 and 39 weeks, respectively. In addition, studies were also conducted in mice, with treatment durations up to 13 weeks.

Rats were selected as the relevant toxicology species because they demonstrated the main adverse effects associated with the administration of PPARγ agonists in humans. Dogs were selected as the non-rodent species in according to the previous information collected with other PPARγ agonists. Mice were selected to support the carcinogenicity study dose selection.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

GLP compliant studies in rats included studies with 4-, 13- and 26-weeks treatment duration. In these studies, the leriglitazone tested dose levels were 25, 100 and 150 mg/kg/day, in the 4-week study, 25, 50 and 100 mg/kg/day, in the 13-week, and 12.5, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day, in the 26-week study. In addition, both the 4- and 13-week studies have also included groups of animals given pioglitazone as a comparator. The 13- and 26-week studies included recovery periods of 4 and 8 weeks, respectively.

GLP compliant studies in dogs included studies with 13- and 39-weeks treatment duration, with recovery periods of 4 and 13 weeks, respectively. Leriglitazone was tested at 0.6, 2.5 and 10 mg/kg/day, in the 13-week study, and at 0.2, 0.6, 2.5 and 10 mg/kg/day, in the 39-week study.

Regarding the studies to determine dose levels to be used in the mouse carcinogenicity study, the mouse GLP compliant study was a 13-week study, with administration of leriglitazone at 12.5, 25, 50 and 100 mg/kg/day. The study has not included a recovery period.

Targets of toxicity as identified in the chronic toxicity study in rats included the haematopoietic system (including decrease in RBC), heart (increase in weight), adipose tissue (adipose changes observed in epididymis, ovaries, mesenteric tissue, and hibernating gland, including lobular formation, reduced size of adipocytes, fibroplasia and macrovesicular change; subcutaneous masses composed of thickened areas of white fat), mammary gland (atrophy/feminisation in males and lobulo-alveolar hyperplasia in females), pituitary gland (vacuolation in pars distalis and dilated sinusoids) and ovary (atrophy and disruption of the oestrous cycle). A subcutaneous malignant sarcoma was found in one male given the high dose. Five early deaths among males were attributed to leriglitazone. There was evidence of partial or complete recovery in most of the affected tissue. Following the recovery phase, mean absolute heart were still increased. The NOAEL was set at 12.5 mg/kg/day.

Targets identified in chronic toxicity study in dogs included the hematopoietic system (decreased in RBC), heart (increase in weight, hypertrophy/hyperplasia of the mesothelium lining portions of the epicardium), adipose tissue (steatopathy of white fat), liver (increase in weight, extramedullary haematopoiesis), rectum (atrophy in the perianal sebaceous glands), mammary gland (squamous metaplasia), ovaries (corpora lutea cysts). There was evidence of partial or complete recovery in most of the affected tissue. Following the recovery phase, mean absolute heart were still increased. The NOAEL for males and females were set at 0.6 and 0.2 mg/kg/day, respectively.

Effects observed in mice were generally consistent with those observed for rats and dogs.

Leriglitazone caused similar adverse effects across all three species tested and were described as a class effect of PPARγ agonists occurring secondary to oedema-induced haemodilution. The main haematology findings were reduced RBC, WBC count and decreased haemoglobin concentration. In rats, these effects occurred following treatment with 50 and 100 mg/kg/day of leriglitazone for 3 months and at 50 mg/kg/day (both sexes) and at 25 mg/kg/day (females only) for 6 months. In dogs these effects were observed following treatment for 9 months at 10 mg/kg/day.

The effects on the liver and spleen generally consisted of an increase in liver weight, accompanied by hepatocellular hypertrophy, focal necrosis and increased hepatocyte mitosis. The increase in spleen weight correlated with extramedullary haematopoiesis. No microscopic correlation was evident with the observed changes in liver enzymes and proteins. Changes in the liver were reversible.

An increase in heart weight in a dose-dependent manner was observed in studies with mice (at doses ≥ 25 mg/kg/day), rats (at doses ≥ 12.5 mg/kg/day) and dogs (at doses ≥ 2.5 mg/kg/day). In rats, myocarditis and brown fat in the aorta with macrovesicular changes were also observed. In dogs, hypertrophy/hyperplasia of the mesothelial cells in the heart was observed. The increased heart weight persisted after the recovery period. Cardiac hypertrophy is a known class effect of PPARγ agonists. Fat deposits and changes in adipose tissue were observed in rats treated with either leriglitazone or pioglitazone. These changes occurred at exposures below the clinical exposure, with brown fat deposits in the aorta occurring at the lowest dose. Proliferation of adipose tissue (increased deposition of white and brown fat and fatty infiltration of tissues) is a known class effect of PPARγ agonists. Subcutaneous masses were not reported in dogs. In rats, several effects on the reproductive organs were observed. These included follicular cysts, atretic follicles, a reduced number of corpora lutea, and disruption of oestrous cycle. These effects were observed in both the leriglitazone and pioglitazone treatment groups in a 3-month study. In addition, ovarian atrophy, squamous metaplasia of the uterus and senescent mucification in the vagina were observed after 6 months of dosing in female rats. Decreased corpora lutea were also observed in dogs. However, these findings are not relevant to the currently proposed target population of males only. No effects on fertility were observed in a dedicated fertility study. Increased weight and vacuolation of cells in the pars distalis in males were observed in male rats, while dilated sinusoids and diffuse hyperplasia were observed in the pituitary glands of female rats. These effects occurred at exposures just above the clinical exposures in males and females. These effects were reversible following a recovery period. Changes to the adrenal glands (vacuolation/hypertrophy of the zona glomerulosa) were observed in female rats in the 3-month study and in both sexes in the 6-month study. Changes to the adrenal glands were also observed in a toxicity study in dogs. However, the findings observed in rat studies in the form of increased weight, vacuolation of cells and diffuse hyperplasia with both leriglitazone and pioglitazone were broadly similar and likely reflect disturbances in endocrine homeostasis secondary to PPAR gamma agonism. No such effects have been reported in dogs, suggesting that they can be rat specific. Moreover, similar changes have not been observed in humans. Based on the available non-clinical and clinical data, therefore, it is considered unlikely that leriglitazone at therapeutic doses will produce clinically meaningful hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis disturbances, and the clinical relevance of these findings is minimal. Furthermore, the HPA axis is clinically monitored in ALD patients, and adrenal insufficiency is treated with corticosteroid replacement therapy. Toxicokinetic evaluations were conducted in mice, rat and dog studies. Based on comparative systemic exposures to unbound leriglitazone and leriglitazone combined with metabolite M3, safety margins for patients based on animal exposure at the NOAEL in chronic toxicity studies are low to null. At the maximum tested doses, in rats, these margins were 3.96 and 4.7-times, respectively, the target exposure in adult patients; in dogs, 2.89 and 2.63-times, respectively. The toxicological effects observed for leriglitazone were considered to be in accordance with those expected for PPARγ agonists and observed for pioglitazone groups. This is accepted. Information on repeated dose toxicity studies in section 5.3 of the SmPC is considered adequate. Genotoxicity The potential genotoxicity of leriglitazone was tested in four assay types, Ames test, mouse lymphoma assay, in vivo rat micronucleus assay and, as a follow-up study, in vivo rat Comet assay (with assessment of DNA damage in liver and duodenum). All definitive studies were GLP compliant. The in vitro assays were conducted with and without metabolic activation. In the mouse lymphoma assay, the treatment periods were of 3 hours, with or without metabolic activation, and 24 hours, without metabolic activation. Both in vivo studies used male and female animals and treatment during 2 consecutive days, with administration by oral gavage. In the micronucleus assay, leriglitazone was administered at doses up to 500 and 1250 mg/kg/day in males and females, respectively; in the Comet assay, at doses up to 1500 and 2000 mg/kg/day, respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Leriglitazone was negative in the bacterial mutagenesis assay. The mouse lymphoma assay revealed an increase in mutant frequency (small colonies) following the 24-h treatment period. Moreover, the size of the mutant colonies indicated that the effect was due to clastogenicity. No mutagenicity was observed after 3 hours treatment.

Both in vivo rat studies were negative following sufficient exposure of the animals.

The positive result in the mouse lymphoma assay was attributed to cytotoxicity, and it was concluded that, taken together, the results from the in vitro and in vivo studies do not suggest that leriglitazone poses a genotoxic risk in patients. This is agreed.

Information regarding genotoxicity in section 5.3 of the SmPC is considered acceptable.

## Carcinogenicity

The Applicant has completed long-term carcinogenicity studies in mice and rats. Both studies are GLP compliant. The carcinogenic potential of leriglitazone in Sprague Dawley (Crl:CD[SD]) rats was assessed over periods of 86 weeks for females and 102 weeks for males, following the administration of 1, 2.5, or 8 mg/kg/day for males and 1.25, 4, or 12.5 mg/kg/day for females, by oral gavage. The high doses were selected based on the finding in heart weight increase in the repeated dose toxicity studies in rats.

The rat carcinogenicity study was terminated early; all surviving females were euthanized at week 86 due to the reduced number of female controls, meeting the criteria for early termination. All surviving males were euthanized on week 102 due to the reduced number of males administered 8 mg/kg/day. No leriglitazone-related effects were noted on clinical observations, palpable masses, food consumption, body weight, or haematology. No leriglitazone-related macroscopic, neoplastic, or non-neoplastic findings were noted.

The most common neoplastic findings in these animals were pars distalis adenoma of the pituitary gland in both sexes and mammary adenocarcinoma and fibroadenoma in females. The most common non-neoplastic findings were compression of the brain, rodent progressive cardiomyopathy of the heart, increased alveolar macrophages of the lungs, age-related involution of the thymus, and extramedullary hemopoiesis in the spleen in both sexes; chronic progressive nephropathy of the kidneys of males; inflammation of the prostate of males; cystic degeneration and haemorrhage of the adrenals of females; angioectasis in females; intrasinusoidal erythrocytes of the mandibular lymph node of females; lobuloalveolar hyperplasia and dilatation of the mammary glands of females; absent corpora lutea and cyst in the ovary of females; and increased mucification of the vagina of females.

The study revealed no carcinogenicity. In addition, there were no increase in mortality, or leriglitazone-related clinical signs, effects on body weight, haematology, macroscopic or microscopic findings at necropsy. Heart weight increased in a dose-dependent way 1%, 8% and 19% (1, 2.5 and 8 mg/Kg/day) in males and 0% 2% and 14% (1.25, 4 and 12.5 mg/kg/day) in females and the increase was statistically significant at the maximum doses.

At the maximum tested doses, systemic exposures to unbound leriglitazone or leriglitazone in combination with metabolite M3 were identical or lower than target exposure in patients. In this respect, it is of note that, in the 26-week repeated dose toxicity study in rats, the subcutaneous malignant sarcoma found in one male given the high dose (50 mg/kg/day) was observed at higher systemic exposures to unbound fraction (approximately 4 times higher than target exposure in patients).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the long-term carcinogenicity study in mice, the tested dose levels were 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day, in males, and 5, 15, and 50 mg/kg/day, in females. As expected, systemic exposures reached in mice were relatively higher compared to those measured in the rat carcinogenicity study. At the maximum tested doses, systemic exposures to unbound leriglitazone in combination with metabolite M3 were up to 32.9-fold the maximum clinical exposure.

As for the rat study, the mouse carcinogenicity study revealed no carcinogenicity. Non-neoplastic microscopic findings were pre-sent in the heart and lungs of males and in the bone marrow, spleen, and adipose tissue of both sexes, and were consistent with known class effects of PPAR-γ agonists. A test article-related increase in mortality occurred in males administered 100 mg/kg/day, leading to early termination of this group in Week 95.

Additionally, considering the carcinogenicity observed in rats treated with pioglitazone (leriglitazone is a metabolite of pioglitazone), the Applicant has further addressed the low safety margins from the rat study. In this respect, the Applicant claimed that, while there is a low margin of exposure at the maximum tested doses in the rat carcinogenicity study, to date, no cases of fat cell tumours have been reported in humans for any PPAR gamma agonist and an increased incidence in bladder cancer in patients have been only associated to pioglitazone and not to the drug class. Moreover, leriglitazone does not have significant genotoxic potential, and there was no evidence of immune modulation or immunotoxicity observed in chronic studies with leriglitazone.

However, given the low safety margins in the rat study with leriglitazone, and the precedent information on pioglitazone, it is considered and specific information on carcinogenicity was included in section 5.3 of the SmPC.

## Reproductive toxicity

Reproductive toxicity studies included studies on fertility, embryo-foetal development and juvenile animal studies.

## Fertility and early embryonic development

Studies on fertility and early embryonic development comprised two GLP-compliant studies in rats, one with treatment of males only and the other of females only.

In both studies, leriglitazone was administered by oral gavage at dose levels of 12.5, 25 and 50 mg/kg/day, starting at 2 weeks pre-mating. These are the same dose levels as tested in the chronic toxicity study in rats. There were no effects on male fertility. In the study with treatment of females, at the high dose (50 mg/kg/day), there was a decrease in the number of live embryos, attributed to a slight reduction in corpora lutea and fewer implantation sites, as well as increases in the incidence of pre- and post-implantation loss. The NOAEL for males and females were set at 50 and 25 mg/kg/day, respectively.

None of the studies included toxicokinetic analysis. Based on extrapolation of toxicokinetic data obtained for the repeated dose toxicity studies in rats, it is estimated that, at the maximum tested dose, systemic exposures (AUC) to unbound leriglitazone and combined leriglitazone and M3 metabolite were 3.96 and 4.7-times, respectively, the target exposure in adult patients. At 25 mg/kg/day, the NOAEL for female fertility, systemic exposures were similar to that in patients.

Information regarding the results of the fertility studies in sections 4.6 and 5.3, as encountered in the proposed SmPC, is considered adequate.

## Embryo-foetal development

Studies on potential effects on embryo-foetal development were conducted in rats and rabbits, with the pivotal studies (GLP-compliant) preceded by the respective dose range finding studies. In the pivotal studies, leriglitazone was administered at 5, 12.5 or 30 mg/kg/day in rats, and at 5, 15 or 50 mg/kg/kg/day, in rabbits. Both studies included toxicokinetic analysis.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In both studies, the maternal NOAEL was set at the maximum tested dose. The development NOAEL was set at the mid tested dose, based on the observation of decreased foetal body weights.

Based on comparative AUC for unbound fraction, at the developmental NOAEL in rats and rabbits, systemic exposure to both leriglitazone and to combined leriglitazone and M3 metabolite were lower than the target systemic exposure in patients; at the maximum tested doses, only slightly higher.

The findings of the embryo-foetal development studies are of little relevance for the target patient population, since this consists of male patients only.

## Pre- and postnatal development

No pre- and postnatal development studies have been conducted. This is accepted. The patient population is limited to male patients and the package of studies conducted with leriglitazone also includes juvenile animal studies. Furthermore, the Applicant mentions that pre- and postnatal development studies are planned to be performed as post authorization measure (PAM).

## Juvenile animal studies

Studies in juvenile animals comprised two studies in rat, a dose range finding study and a GLP compliant 10-week study, with a 4-week recovery period. In the pivotal study, rats received leriglitazone by oral gavage at 12.5, 25 or 50 mg/kg/day, starting at postnatal day 22. In addition to standard repeated dose toxicity endpoints, the animals were also examined for long bone growth and bone mineral density, development and behavioural observations, reproductive performance, and sperm analysis.

Effects observed were generally consistent with those observed in adult animals, including, among others, increase in heart weight and changes in adipose tissue. Apart from reduced bone density of the vertebra, no effects other than those observed in adult animals were observed. In the absence of historical control data on bone density, it was not possible to determine whether the observed minimal to slight decreases in bone density were within the limits of the biological variability. Therefore, the relevance to the paediatric population cannot be totally excluded.

The NOAEL was set at 25 mg/kg/day, based on changes in adipose tissue and death of one male at 50 mg/kg/day. At the NOAEL, systemic exposure (AUC) to unbound leriglitazone or leriglitazone combined with M3 were only slightly higher than target exposure in adult patients; at the 50 mg/kg/day, approximately, 3 times higher.

Information on juvenile animal studies in section 5.3 of the SmPC is considered adequate.

## Local tolerance

No dedicated local tolerance studies of leriglitazone have been conducted. This is acceptable, since the oral route of administration is covered by the repeated dose toxicity studies.

## Other toxicity studies

No other toxicity studies have been conducted, except for a negative Ames test with a potential impurity (dimethylglyoxime), with the respective study report located in the quality sections of the dossier. This is acceptable. There are no impurities above the qualification threshold.

## Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment (ERA)

Leriglitazone is not classified as a persistent and bioaccumulative substance. A tailored risk assessment is not needed, since leriglitazone does not belong to the groups of endocrine active substances (EAS) or antibiotics. Therefore, no further screening for persistence, bioaccumulation, and toxicity (PBT) assessment is necessary.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Following EMA guidelines, the applicant's Fpen refinement relies on prevalence data for the orphan disease ALD within the European Union, which is 0.4 per 10,000 people. This information comes from the European Orphanet Report Series, 'Prevalence of Rare Diseases: Bibliographic Data - June 2013, Number 1,' corresponding to 3.5 cases per 100,000 individuals. For the current prevalence, the 2014 Orphanet Report Series provides detailed data, although the disease itself is not explicitly mentioned.

The PECSW is calculated with a refined Fpen. The Fpen refinement is performed according to the Q&amp;A document of EMA (EMEA, 2016b). Based on the refined Fpen of 0.00004 patient inh-1 and the DOSEai of 163.92 mg patient-1 d-1, the refined PECsurfacewater value is 0.00328 µg/l, which is below the action limit of 0.01 µg/L established by EMA.

Based on the values obtained for Refined PECsurfacewater and log Dow at three environmentally relevant pHs, which are below the action limits defined by EMA, no Phase II assessment is required. Since the partition coefficient for leriglitazone is below 4.5, there is no need for a definitive BPT assessment.

Therefore, it can be assumed that the approval of Nezglyal, an oral suspension at 13.7 mg/mL, is unlikely to pose a risk to the environment when used as prescribed in patients.

The applicant applied suitable precautionary and safety measures to inform patients and healthcare professionals, mitigate environmental risks, and promote environmental protection, as outlined in EMEA/CHMP/SWP/4447/00, 2024.

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

## Pharmacology

Leriglitazone is a selective CNS-penetrant PPARγ agonist with consistent neuroprotective, antiinflammatory, and remyelinating effects across in vitro and in vivo models relevant to cALD, supporting its proposed mechanism of action. Safety pharmacology and DDI assessments do not identify major concerns.

## Pharmacokinetics

The pharmacokinetic profile of leriglitazone is favourable, with high oral bioavailability, brain penetration, and good translational relevance across species, including paediatric considerations, and any potential transporter interactions appear of low clinical relevance.

## Toxicology

Toxicological studies with leriglitazone included studies on repeated dose toxicity (mouse, rat and dog during up to 13, 26 and 39 weeks, respectively), genotoxicity (Ames assay, mouse lymphoma assay, in vivo micronucleus and in vivo Comet assay), carcinogenicity (long-term studies in mice and rats) and reproductive toxicity (fertility and early embryonic development, embryo-foetal development and juvenile animal studies).

Altogether, results from the genotoxicity studies did not indicate a risk of genotoxicity. For the repeated dose toxicity studies, effects observed were considered expected effects for PPARγ agonists; no carcinogenicity was observed in the long-term studies in mice and rats; the fertility and early embryonic development studies showed a decrease in viable foetus upon treatment of females; the embryo-foetal development studies, a decrease in foetal body weight, in the absence of maternal toxicity; the juvenile animal studies showed no new toxicities but, based on the results from this study and lack of respective historical control, it was considered that the relevance for humans of the observed minimal to slight decreases in bone density was not excluded. In general, margins of exposure regarding NOAEL and also maximum tested doses in the different animal studies were low to null.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The available package of studies is considered adequate. The information on carcinogenicity in section 5.3 of the SmPC has been revised.

## ERA

A bioaccumulation potential is not indicated based on the log KOW &lt; 4.5. Therefore, a definitive PBT/vPvB assessment is not required.

PECsurfacewater for Leriglitazone hydrochloride remains below the action threshold of 0.01 µg/L. Accordingly, there is no necessity to conduct a Phase II risk assessment.

Therefore, it can be assumed that the approval of Nezglyal, an oral suspension at 13.7 mg/mL, is unlikely to pose a risk to the environment when used as prescribed in patients.

## 5.1.1. Good Clinical Practice (GCP) aspects

The Clinical trials were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the applicant.

A routine GCP inspection (Case ID: EMA/IN/0000293052) for the clinical study MT-2-02 has been conducted. No specific concerns are known to have been identified by the assessment at the time of adoption of the inspection request. General triggers have been used in the choice of this dossier and the sites involved in line with the guideline 'Points to consider for assessors, inspectors and EMA inspection coordinators on the identification of triggers for the selection of applications for 'routine' and/or 'for cause' inspections, their investigation and scope of such Inspections'. The reporting deadline for this routine GCP-inspection was set for 13/02/2026.
