## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.1. Methods

## Plasma

The assay instructions describe the quantitative determination of leriglitazone and M3 in human plasma samples in ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA). Sample processing is performed by protein precipitation using a 20.0 μL sample volume. Separation between potential metabolites and interfering endogenous compounds is achieved by HPLC.

During the course of the clinical studies, the existing method PRA˗NL˗SML˗1805 was adapted to another HPLC system, the Agilent UHPLC. A full validation was performed to comply with the latest guidelines.

Further Validation of specificity towards interference with concomitant medications has been assessed with method PRA˗NL˗SML˗2386. Validation procedures and acceptance criteria were based on the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) guideline M10 on bioanalytical method validation and study sample analysis (2022). A cocktail of the corticosteroid treatments included cortisone, dexamethasone, fludrocortisone, hydrocortisone, prednisone and methylprednisolone. A standard cocktail of over-the-counter medications included acetaminophen, caffeine, chlorpheniramine maleate, ibuprofen and naproxen.

## Urine

The assay instructions describe the quantitative determination of leriglitazone and M3 in human urine samples. Sample processing is performed by liquid-liquid extraction using a 50.0 µL sample volume. Separation between potential metabolites and interfering endogenous compounds is achieved by HPLC.

A triple quadrupole mass spectrometer (API 4000) equipped with a Turbo-ionspray source is used for detection in positive ion mode.

## CSF

The assay instructions describe the quantitative determination of leriglitazone and M3 in human CSF samples. Sample processing is performed by dilution using a sample volume of 20.0 µL. Separation between potential metabolites and interfering endogenous compounds is achieved by HPLC.

A triple quadrupole mass spectrometer (API 4000) equipped with a Turbo-ionspray source is used for detection in positive ion mode.

## Enantiomers of Leriglitazone

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The Assay Instructions describe the quantitative determination of and, each a mixture of two enantiomers of MIN˗102 in human EDTA plasma samples.

A triple quadrupole mass spectrometer (API 5000) equipped with a Turbo-ion spray source is used for detection in positive ion mode.

The method was adapted to allow quantification of two MIN˗102 enantiomers versus the two MIN˗102 enantiomers mixtures, instead of all four enantiomers.

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 5.2.2.1. Introduction

The Applicant has not conducted specific renal and hepatic impairment studies. However, precautions regarding usage of leriglitazone in these populations are included in the Summary of Product Characteristics (SmPC).

The Applicant is proposing to conduct two clinical DDI studies, post-approval. The first clinical DDI study, which include the effect of food, is to confirm the potential DDI through CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 inhibition and CYP3A/2C induction and has already been initiated and designed based on the feedback received during the previous MAA procedure, with results expected by mid-2026. The second clinical DDI study to confirm the potential DDI through BCRP substrates is planned post-approval. Until the clinical DDI results are available, precautions relating to concomitant administration of CYP2C8 and CYP3A inhibitors (e.g., gemfibrozil and itraconazole), CYP3A/2C inducers (e.g., carbamazepine) and BCRP substrates (e.g., rosuvastatin) have been included in the SmPC (Section 4.5).

## 5.2.2.2. Evaluation and qualification of models

## 5.2.2.2.1. Population pharmacokinetics

The initial PopPK study was undertaken using data from 130 individuals from studies MT-1-01, MT-201 and MT-2-03. The objective of the study was to describe the population PK properties of leriglitazone using non-linear mixed-effects modelling and to explore the influence of patient-specific factors (covariates, e.g., sex, age, weight, BMI, liver enzymes, creatinine clearance) on the exposure. Complete PK profiles were available from study MT-1-01 (healthy volunteers). In study MT-2-03 (FRDA patients), only pre-dose (trough) concentrations were taken at multiple visits, while in study MT-2-01 (ALD patients) also sparse post-dose samples were taken. Simulations of different dosing strategies in patients with AMN were carried out with this PopPK model in terms of their performance of reaching a given target exposure.

Subsequently, additional data from study MT-2-02 (children with cALD) and data from study MT-1-03 (mass-balance study in healthy volunteers) were used in the model update which included data of 156 individuals (additional 15 subjects from MT-2-02 and 11 from MT-1-03). Less than 1% of all samples were below the LLOQ. Plasma concentrations of the M3 metabolite were also intended to be described using the PopPK model.

The updated data set used valid plasma concentration measurements of leriglitazone and M3, respectively, from 156 individuals, for the analysis. The median aged was 34.5 years and ranged from 4 to 66 years. The median subject weight was 74.1 kg, ranging from 18.4 to 137.8 kg. Most subjects were white (81.4%, n = 127), race details were missing for 12.2% (n = 19). Eleven women (7.1%) from the study in FRDA (MT-2-03) were included in the analysis dataset.

The following key steps were taken in the analysis:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

1. Exploratory graphical analysis of concentration-time and covariate data.
2. Structural model development using only data from healthy volunteers (MT-1-01) and inclusion of key structural covariates (food, body weight).
3. Re-estimation of parameters on the full analysis dataset (MT-1-01, MT-2-01, MT-2-02, MT-2-03 and MT-1-03). Finalisation of base model.
4. Stepwise automatic covariate modelling using the base model.
5. Refinement of final model including significant covariates.
6. Qualification of final model (visual predictive check).
7. Simulations.

As the NEXUS trial in children with cALD (MT-2-02) was still ongoing at the time of the previous model update, data was not available from all of the study patients. The previous modelling work described above was therefore updated by re-estimating the parameters using the final data from this study. As external qualification of the model, simulations were performed and compared to actual observations from paediatric patients taking part in the compassionate use programme of leriglitazone.

The following key steps were taken in the analysis:

- Exploratory graphical analysis of concentration-time data with focus on the data from MT-2-02.

The model development was made as described. Significant covariates were identified in an automatic covariate search including a stepwise forward search (significance criterion P &lt; 0.05) and a subsequent backward deletion step with a stricter criterion (P &lt; 0.01).

After oral administration, leriglitazone PK was successfully described using a two-compartment model with first-order absorption and linear elimination.

For the joint parent-metabolite model, a straightforward approach was chosen. All parameters related to the parent kinetics were fixed to the final estimates of the updated parent-only model, followed by the assessment of the metabolite specific parameters, which included the possibility of pre-systemic (i.e., first-pass) formation of M3.

.. Given the high bioavailability of leriglitazone (&gt; 90%) and since IV administration is not foreseen in clinical practice, it was deemed justifiable to focus the modelling efforts on the oral data alone.

An exponential model was used to describe the inter-individual variability (IIV) of the model parameters, assuming log-normal distributions of the individual PK parameters. Correlations between random effect parameters were investigated by estimation of some selected off diagonal elements of the omega matrix. Inter-occasion variability in clearance was considered during model-refinement after the automated covariate search had been done. Data were modelled using a log-transform both sides approach whereby the residual error was incorporated as an additive error on the log scale. Separate residual error terms for each study were explored, as was the inclusion of IIV on the residual error (i.e., ETA-on-EPS).

Since the model was also aimed to be applied to children and studies MT-2-02 and MT-2-03 included patients as young as 2 years old, it was decided to account for the association between body weight and PK parameters according to allometric theory already in the base model. A fixed allometric exponent of 0.75 for clearance and inter-compartment flow (Q) was used while an exponent of 1.0 was used for the relationship between volume and body weight. The following covariates were tested in the model for their association with CL and relative bioavailability (F) using a forward inclusion - backward elimination approach: BMI, height, age, ethnicity, sex, laboratory variables: aspartate transaminase (AST), alanine transaminase (ALT), bilirubin, albumin, creatine clearance (Cockcroft-Gault).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The model developed was used to describe the data collected in the clinical studies with leriglitazone, and the final population PK parameters were obtained using the combined dataset.

The parameter estimates of the leriglitazone-only PopPK model including the clinically relevant covariates were presented for assessment.

Goodness-of-fit and residual plots of the final model demonstrated the virtual absence of systematic model misspecification in general.

VPCs demonstrated that the final model is capable to describe the data of both studies MT-2-01 and MT2-03. Additional data from studies MT-1-03 and MT-2-02 support that the model described the central tendency, as well as the variability of the concentration-time data.

The parameter estimates of the M3 model including the clinically relevant covariates were presented to the assessors.

Goodness-of-fit plots of the final metabolite model (run902) demonstrated that the data was generally well described by the model with no obvious indication of model misspecification.

VPCs for the M3 data for the five different studies (MT-1-01, MT-1-03, MT-2-01, MT-2-02 and MT-2-03) show generally a good description of the metabolite concentrations, with the exception of study MT-203 in FRDA. The model tends to under-predict the M3 levels observed in that particular study.

This model was subsequently re-run on the complete dataset, including the 8 additional patients from MT-2-02. Since this re-run indicated only a minor difference in the parameter estimates, NONMEM was executed in evaluation mode only to see if an update of parameters was necessary. The previously developed 2-compartment population PK model with linear elimination successfully fitted the additional data of 8 subjects from the NEXUS study that was not available previously.

## 5.2.2.2.2. Physiology based pharmacokinetic model

A series of PBPK modelling studies have been completed. These have been updated as new data became available during the development programme. An initial model was developed to enable estimation of starting doses for paediatric evaluation (NEXUS study). This was subsequently updated for prediction of CYP and transporter-mediated DDI liability. Further refinement was undertaken to define the different clearance routes using new data from clinical mass balance and ABA studies in healthy volunteers. Finally, the model was further refined and expanded the model for evaluation of DDI liability as a precipitant and an object of CYP3A4.

The PBPK model was further updated and refined using data from the clinical mass balance and ABA studies in healthy volunteers (MT-1-03) in accordance with the EMA guidance on drug interactions (EMA, 2012). This updated PBPK model was then used to:

- Assess the DDI liability of leriglitazone as an object of CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 inhibition, and P-gp inhibition in healthy subjects.
- Assess the DDI liability of leriglitazone as an inhibitor of the CYP2C9 substrates (S-warfarin, celecoxib and tolbutamide) in healthy subjects.
- Assess the effect of hepatic and renal impairment on leriglitazone exposure.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Assess the effect of CYP2C8 genotype leriglitazone exposure.

## 5.2.2.3. Absorption

Leriglitazone was absorbed relatively rapidly after oral administration, and the median Tmax ranged from about 0.63 to 8 hours. The Tmax for M3 ranged from 0.25 to 6 hours. The highest proposed dose is 180 mg of leriglitazone hydrochloride. Leriglitazone hydrochloride shows low solubility. This was confirmed in human clinical trials where absolute oral bioavailability was &gt; 90% (MT-1-03), indicating high absorption. Therefore, leriglitazone can be considered a biopharmaceutic classification system Class II compound (poor solubility - high permeability).

Following a single oral dose followed by a 15-minute IV infusion (Part B - ABA), leriglitazone was absorbed relatively rapidly, with a median Tmax ranging from 1.43 - 4.00 hours; the median Tmax for M3 also ranged from 1.43 to 4.00 hours. For 14 C-leriglitazone, the median Tmax was 0.23 hours (), while for 14 C-M3, the median Tmax ranged from 0.33 to 0.57. From the overall exposure the absolute oral bioavailability of leriglitazone was determined to be 91.1% (

The consumption of food before administration of 90 and 270 mg leriglitazone hydrochloride showed a delay in the uptake of leriglitazone, with slightly lower maximum plasma concentrations reached slightly later, although the AUC was not significantly affected. The PopPK analysis revealed that absorption rate constant was reduced by 83.5% when leriglitazone was taken with food, also leading to a lower Cmax and a longer Tmax. Despite this delay in uptake, the extent of exposure (as measured by AUC) was essentially not different from the fasting state, as food reduced the bioavailability by only 4.1%. Based on this analysis, the conclusion was that leriglitazone could be taken with or without food.

## 5.2.2.4. Bioequivalence

The formulation employed for Phase I clinical studies MT-1-01 and MT-1-03 Part A was a suspension for immediate use containing leriglitazone 5.46 mg/mL, polysorbate 80, carboxymethylcellulose sodium and purified water. The suspension was compounded as a single use oral suspension. The formulation was modified to allow for longer stability for dosing during subsequent studies and for commercial use. It is noteworthy that the removal of the polysorbate 80 from this formulation had no discernible impact in the suspension due to the intrinsic nature of the composition (viscosifying system; thixotropic suspension). The commercial formulation is the same as the formulation used in studies MT-1-03 Part B, MT-2-01 and MT-2-02 (which corresponds to 13.7 mg leriglitazone;15 mg/mL leriglitazone hydrochloride).

## 5.2.2.5. Distribution

As no concentration dependency in protein binding was found, the unbound fraction value of 3.6% for leriglitazone is obtained as average for the different concentrations.

As no concentration dependency in protein binding was found, the unbound fraction value of 1.8% for M3 is obtained as average for the different concentrations.

The blood/plasma partitioning (Kblood/plasma) values for leriglitazone and M3 were 0.725 and 0.786, respectively, when incubated for 60 minutes, indicating that they do not extensively bind into the cellular fraction of blood.

In the PopPK analysis, the estimated apparent central volume of distribution was 18.2 and the estimated apparent peripheral volume of distribution was 4.27 The inter-individual variability of peripheral volume of distribution was estimated to be 34.4%.

Leriglitazone was observed in CSF at the 4 hours post-dose sampling time point following 135 mg and

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

270 mg doses. Leriglitazone and M3 concentrations in CSF increased dose proportionally, by a factor of 1.8 and 1.7 respectively, and correlated with the plasma concentrations. The CSF/plasma ratio for leriglitazone was on average 0.022, which is in line with peripheral unbound fractions determined in plasma of 0.036. The CSF/plasma ratio was only 0.0004 for the main metabolite M3, confirming that the main compound responsible for CNS activity is the parent compound leriglitazone.

## 5.2.2.6. Metabolism

Good microsomal stability of leriglitazone was found in human liver microsomes, along with results pointing to CYP2C8 and CYP3A as the key oxidative enzymes involved in the metabolism of leriglitazone, and CYP2C8 the main enzyme involved in formation of M3. Stability was also seen in human hepatocytes. The fmCYP2C8 and fmCYP3A4 have been estimated by a PBPK model to be 0.63 and 0.30, respectively, and are in line with the experimentally determined values.

Very low or no disappearance of M3 was observed with recombinant enzymes. Leriglitazone was formed with all the enzymes studied, indicating that CYP P450 reductase is participating to the conversion of M3 to leriglitazone rather than any CYP enzyme, as the reductase is included in all recombinant CYP formulations. Less leriglitazone appeared to be formed with recombinant human CYP2C8 and rhCYP2D6, which is due to the conversion of leriglitazone back to M3 by these enzymes. The results showed conversion of M3 to leriglitazone is a non-CYP catalysed reaction, whereas conversion of leriglitazone back to M3 is CYP-mediated. Based on the human liver microsome and cytosol data, the metabolism of M3 is likely catalysed by aldo-keto reductase (AKR), while the recombinant enzyme data suggest that CYP P450 reductase and carbonyl reductase (CBR) may be involved in the formation of leriglitazone.

Leriglitazone is a substrate of UGT (UGT2B7 and UGT1A4) and SULT (SULT1A2, SULT1A1*2, SULT1A1*1 and SULT1C4) enzymes but this elimination pathways are not clinically relevant as they represent less than 25% of leriglitazone transformation.

Metabolite profiling in plasma, urine and faeces was conducted as part of the mass balance study. Cumulative recovery of total radioactivity in urine and faeces and by Day 14 suggest that excretion was complete (fetotal = 101.2%). In plasma, two compounds were identified: the parent compound (leriglitazone) and its main metabolite, M3 (an oxidised analogue of leriglitazone). In urine, 11 compounds were identified. The most abundant metabolite in urine was U11, a demethylated analogue oxidised to a carboxylic acid, accounting for 30% of the metabolites in urine. Seven other metabolites were identified as glucuronic acid conjugates and one sulphate conjugate. In faeces, 6 compounds were identified. The most abundant was a sulphated metabolite, accounting for 42.1% of the metabolites in faeces. Unchanged leriglitazone accounted for 38.7% of the metabolites in faeces, and M3 accounted for only 3.9% of the metabolites in faeces.

No notable oral first-pass effect was recorded for leriglitazone (after oral and IV dosing).

Leriglitazone has 2 chiral centres (C2 in the ethyl side chain and C5 in the glitazone ring) and is produced as a mixture of 4 stereoisomers. To investigate the in vivo epimerisation of the asymmetric carbon C2 of leriglitazone, 2 pairs of diastereomers were synthesised: E1 and E2. Each pair consisted of 2 compounds having the same configuration at C2 but different configuration at C5. Plasma samples from healthy volunteers dosed with oral leriglitazone at 30, 90 and 270 mg were analysed for E1 and E2 at 2, 4 and 24-hours post-dose in study MT-1-01. Plasma concentrations of both diastereomer pairs E1 and E2 were above the LLOQ at all relevant time points post-dose. Concentrations of E2 were considerably higher than those of E1. Ratios of E1:E2 ranged from 0.0 (&lt; 0.05) to 0.4 on individual time points for individual subjects. Geometric mean ratios ranged from 0.1 to 0.3 and did not show any trend with dose administered nor time point of the samples. Dose-response curves of PPARγ agonism of leriglitazone, E1 and E2 in radio-ligand binding assay show that both diastereomers, E1 and E2, exhibited equipotent in vitro activity as PPARγ agonist with a half maximal effective concentration (EC50) of 13 µM and 18 µM, respectively, which is similar to the racemate (10 µM).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.7. Elimination

Approximately 6% of leriglitazone administered orally was excreted in urine as parent during the first 48 hours (ranging from geometric mean recovery of 5.8% after 90 mg in fed condition to 6.9% after 30 mg in fasted condition). Most of the parent was recovered in the conjugated state. Urine concentrations of free leriglitazone were detectable from the first collection period onwards at all doses of leriglitazone; however, total excretion as free leriglitazone did not increase above 0.4% of the administered dose over the 48-hour collection period (range 0.1 - 0.4%). Renal clearance was low and approximately 2 mL/h for all doses and with no differences between fed and fasted state.

Following administration of 14 C-leriglitazone (single oral and oral plus IV), &gt; 60% of total radioactivity was excreted during the first 48 hours, with most of the total radioactivity (&gt; 91.5%) being excreted over 96 hours. Overall, after 312 hours post-dose, &gt; 100% of the administered oral dose of radioactivity was recovered in urine and faeces. The major route of excretion was via urine (≥ 77.0%), with the remainder (&gt; 21%) being excreted in faeces. Very small amounts of unchanged leriglitazone and M3 were excreted in urine (≤ 0.7% and ≤ 0.03%, respectively). Unchanged 14 C-leriglitazone and 14 C-M3 were excreted in very small amounts in urine (≤ 2.1% and &lt; 0.05% of the administered doses, respectively) and faeces (≤ 5.1% and ≤ 0.3% of the administered doses, respectively).

A first-order absorption and linear elimination two-compartment model was used for the PopPK analysis to characterise the PK of leriglitazone. The apparent clearance was estimated to be 0.739 L/h (, the predicted inter-compartment flow rate was 0.823

## 5.2.2.8. Dose proportionality and time dependency

Maximum concentrations of both leriglitazone and M3 increased with dose in a slightly less than doseproportional way. After reaching Cmax leriglitazone plasma concentrations decreased with a geometric mean t½ of 23.6, 24.2 and 22.7 hours, respectively, for 30 mg, 90 mg and 270 mg leriglitazone. M3 plasma concentrations showed a similar half-life (22.1, 23.2 and 21.6 hours). Overall exposure to leriglitazone and M3 increased in a basically dose-proportional fashion for both AUC0-t and AUC0-inf. Maximum concentrations of leriglitazone were approximately double those of M3, and AUC of the leriglitazone concentration-time profile were between two and three times the values for M3.

Under multiple dose administrations and after the final repeat dose on Day 8 maximum concentrations were approximately 1.7 times higher than on Day 1 (135 mg ratio of Cmax on the final day to Cmax on day 1 [RCmax] = 1.73; 270 mg RCmax = 1.72). Maximum concentrations of M3 increased with a similar ratio from Day 1 to Day 8 (135 mg RCmax = 1.69; 270 mg RCmax = 1.57). The accumulation of leriglitazone was approximately 1.7 as well (135 mg AUC0-t on the final day to AUC0-t on Day 1 [RAUC] = 1.70; 270 mg RAUC = 1.81) as was the case for M3 (135 mg RAUC = 1.73; 270 mg RAUC = 1.63). The observed accumulation ratios were consistent with the observed t1/2, which is similar to the dosing interval (24 hours). Steady state was reached after 4 to 5 days. Based on this, it can be assumed that the PK on Day 8 is measured in a steady state.

## 5.2.2.9. Pharmacokinetics in the target population

The PK of leriglitazone in cALD patients was evaluated in a subgroup of patients in study MT-2-01 and in patients included in study MT-2-02. The PK of leriglitazone was also studied in patients with Friedreich's ataxia in study MT-2-03.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Study MT-2-01 Part 1 was a Phase II/III, randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in adult male ALD patients with evidence of spinal cord involvement (AMN) and without brain lesions or with Gd negative brain lesions (i.e., cALD). A total of 116 patients were randomised 2:1 (77 to leriglitazone and 39 to placebo), and 96 patients completed the double-blind treatment phase. Thirty-three of the subjects had brain lesions at baseline (19 received leriglitazone and 14 received placebo). The objective of the study was to assess the effect of leriglitazone on the progression of myelopathy and cerebral lesions in adult ALD patients after 96 weeks of treatment. Subjects who completed the double-blind period of this study were eligible to participate in an OLE study (study MT-2-01 Part 2 OLE), 88 patients (92%) joined the OLE study, 23 of whom had brain lesions at baseline. The starting dose of leriglitazone was 10 mL oral suspension (150 mg leriglitazone hydrochloride). The dose was then individually adjusted in each patient to achieve a target exposure of 200 µg*h/mL, as translated from animal efficacy studies and study MT-1-01. After the last possible dose adjustment, based on plasma concentrations at Week 12, study medication dose was not changed, except if required for safety or tolerability reasons. All patients in Part 2 (OLE), including those randomised to placebo in Part 1, received a leriglitazone dose to achieve the predefined target plasma exposure of 200 µg*h/mL. Patients who were taking doses of ≤ 10 mL leriglitazone oral solution at the end of Part 1 of the study started Part 2 with the same dose. All other patients, including patients who received placebo in Part 1, reverted to a starting dose of 10 mL of leriglitazone oral suspension since patients who were on a stable leriglitazone dose during Part 1 may no longer have been at their optimum exposure level at Week 96. Dose adjustments to achieve the target plasma exposure were allowed until informed by PK parameters obtained at Week 108. In Part I, leriglitazone and M3 plasma levels were determined predose (preferably fasted) at Weeks 4, 12, 24, 48, 72 and 96. In addition plasma levels 2, 4 and 6-hours post-dose were determined at the Week 12 visit. The leriglitazone enantiomer ratio (E1 and E2) was determined in a subset of patients (n = 16) at Weeks 4, 12, 48 and 96. Leriglitazone and M3 CSF concentrations were measured at baseline and at Week 96 (n = 2 per treatment arm) and compared with corresponding plasma levels. In the OLE study, leriglitazone and M3 plasma levels were determined at Weeks 96, 100, 108, 144, 192 and 240. In addition, plasma levels 2, 4 and 6-hours post-dose were determined at the Week 108 visit. In part 1 of the study and after dose adjustment (Week 4) and dose confirmation (Week 12), all patients taking the recommended dose based on PK measurements reached the target exposure of 200µg*h/mL. The average recommended doses were 180 ± 45 mg and 177 ± 44 mg, based on PK measurements at Week 4 or Week 12, respectively. Additionally, all patients who required a dose reduction due to safety reasons maintained a plasma exposure above the minimum efficacious exposure of 100 µg*h/mL as translated from animal efficacy studies (see following Figure).

Figure 2: Leriglitazone AUC (µg*h/mL) with Dose as Recommended by PK Expert or Dose Reduced (Study MT-2-01, Part 1)

<!-- image -->

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the part 2, at Week 108, 42 of the 82 patients in the leriglitazone group (51.2%) received the overall dose as recommended and 40 of the patients (48.8%) received a reduced dose defined as a dose between 10% and 50% lower than the PK recommended. The mean plasma exposure of leriglitazone and M3 was maintained at a relatively constant level from Week 100 through Week 240, with an overall minimum AUC0-24 value of 157.9 µg*h/mL at Week 192, and an overall maximum AUC024 value of 177.8 µg*h/mL at Week 108 (see following Figure). For all visits combined, the mean metabolic ratio between leriglitazone and M3 was approximately 0.4, irrespective of dose adjustments.

Study MT-2-02 was a Phase II/III, open-label, multicentre study assessing the effects of leriglitazone treatment on disease progression prior to HSCT human stem cell transplant, in male paediatric patients with or without gadolinium (Gd)-enhancing cerebral lesions. The study also assessed the PK, safety, and tolerability of leriglitazone in this patient population. PK is assessed in terms of plasma concentrations of leriglitazone and M3 at concentration at 2 hours (C2h), and Cmin. Twenty-three subjects (mean age 7.8 years; age range: 4-12 years) were enrolled in the study and received at least one dose of leriglitazone. Of these, 13 (81.3%) patients were white, 1 (6.3%) patient was American Indian (Alaskan native), and 2 (12.5%) patients were of other races. The starting dose was established per body weight and age in each subject, as determined by the PBPK modelling, to achieve an exposure of approximately 170 µg*h/mL and then re-adjusted if needed to achieve this target exposure. Plasma concentrations at the Baseline Visit (V0) (pre-dose, 2, 6, 12, and 24 h post-dose) and V2 (Cmin) were reviewed by an experienced pharmacokineticist who predicted steady state exposure from these data and make dose recommendations to achieve the target plasma exposure. Dose reductions up to 20% were allowed. In this study, dose reductions due to safety concerns were not necessary in any patient. Subjects took one dose of leriglitazone at approximately the same time each morning, administered orally via a 5 mL syringe. The starting leriglitazone doses (based on the age ranges of the participants) were as follows: for patients aged 2-5 years, 2.6 mg/kg (0.17 mL/kg/day); for patients aged ≥6 - 11, 2.4 mg/kg (0.16 mL/kg/day); and for patients aged 12 years, 2.2 mg/kg (0.15 mL/kg/day). After initial dose adjustment, mean plasma exposure of leriglitazone remained within the target therapeutic dose range from baseline through Week 96 (see following Figure). In subjects underdoing HSCT, mean AUC at the visit prior to HSCT was 181.90 μg*h/mL, well within range of the target plasma exposure.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 4: Mean Leriglitazone AUC Over Time - Pharmacokinetic Analysis Set

<!-- image -->

AUC = area under the concentration-time curve. Dotted lines indicate target range exposure 170 µg*h/mL ± 20%.

Study MT-2-03 was a double-blind, placebo-controlled study on the effects of leriglitazone on biochemical, imaging, neurophysiological, and clinical markers in male and female patients aged ≥ 12 and ≤ 60 years with FRDA. Thirty-nine eligible patients were randomised in a 2:1 ratio to treatment for 48 weeks with leriglitazone (n = 26) or placebo (n = 13). Twenty-two patients were male, and 17 patients were female. Fourteen patients (35.9%) were between 12 and 17 years old (10 in the leriglitazone group) and 25 patients were between 19 and 55 years (16 in the leriglitazone group).

Subjects received individualised starting doses based on gender and age. Subjects 17 years of age or younger received a starting dose of 2.2 mg/kg (0.15 mL/kg), adult males received 150 mg (10 mL) and females received 103 mg (8.5 mL). Dosage was subsequently modified based on PK parameters obtained from blood samples at Week 4 to achieve a target leriglitazone exposure of 170 μg*h/mL, based on the PBPK modelling. The dose remained stable following PK adjustment throughout the study, other than decreasing dosage for safety, tolerability or other special circumstances in individual patients. Dose reductions up to 40% were allowed. There was a total of 26 evaluable PK patients, of whom 18 were dosed as recommended by PK expert for at least 50% of the days between Weeks 4 to 48, 7 had their dose reduced between 10-40% lower than the recommended dose for at least 50% of the time, and one was not assigned to any subgroup because this patient was discontinued before the dose recommended by the PK expert was applied. The geometric mean derived extrapolated AUC0-24 over Weeks 4 to 48 was approximately 175 μg*h/mL (CV 9.35%) for patients receiving the recommended dose, compared to approximately 150 μg*h/mL (CV 12.39%) for those receiving the reduced dose. The overall average extrapolated AUC0-24 was approximately 166 μg*h/mL (CV 13.13%), close to the target value of 170 μg*h/mL.

Finally, PK data from 44 children less than 18 years of age administered leriglitazone under a compassionate use programme are included in the most recent population PopPK analyses. Leriglitazone administration was in accordance with the dosage regimen described for study MT-2-02. One patient was 5 years of age, 24 were between 6 - 11 years of age and 9 were between 12 -16 years of age (mean age 9.1 years). Mean weight was 34.7 kg (range 16 - 75.5 kg). Blood samples were drawn for the purpose of concentration-based dose adjustment, similarly as for the patients in MT-2-02. Most patients had four post-dose samples (2, 6, 12 and 24 hours) drawn after the first dose. In some cases the first PK sample was, however, a single trough sample taken at steady state. A total of 146 valid concentration measurements were available.

In the popPK final model, there was no indication of differences in PK between patients and healthy volunteers.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.10. Special populations

In the PopPK analysis, the effects of the following covariates on PK were evaluated: BMI, height, age, ethnicity, sex, laboratory variables: AST, ALT, bilirubin, albumin, creatine clearance (Cockcroft-Gault).

The results indicated that sex, age, body weight and BMI were significant covariates on CL and F impacting AUC. However, the changes in the AUC were not clinically significant as there were between 0.8-1.25 GMR ratio.

Low renal excretion of leriglitazone was measured in the Phase I MT-1-01 study, where only approximately 6% of the leriglitazone administered dose was excreted in urine as parent and 0.3% as M3, mostly recovered in conjugated state. Confirmation of low renal excretion was obtained from the ABA/ hAME study, where although the main route of excretion for leriglitazone was via urine (≥ 77%), unchanged leriglitazone and M3 were excreted in urine in very small amounts (≤ 2.1% and &lt; 0.05% of the administered dose, respectively). The lack of inclusion of renally impaired patients in the studies undertaken means that the renal impairment effect on PK parameters could not be studied by modelling and simulations through a PopPK analysis. Simulations carried out in the PBPK study predicted no clinically significant impact of mild renal impairment on leriglitazone exposure. Moderate and severe renal impairment are predicted to have only a low impact on leriglitazone exposure.

Hepatic function is usually preserved in cALD patients, and they very rarely suffer from hepatic impairment. Simulations carried out in the PBPK study predicted no clinically significant impact of mild hepatic impairment on leriglitazone exposure. Moderate and severe hepatic impairment are predicted to have only a low impact on leriglitazone exposure.

Gender (sex) was used a covariate in the PopPK study, in the parent-only model for leriglitazone. Lower clearance (-18.8%) and higher AUC (+23%) values are predicted to occur in women, compared to men of similar weight and BMI. However, it should be noted that the proposed indication for leriglitazone is for men only.

Body weight was used as a covariate in the PopPK study, in the parent-only model for leriglitazone. Results showed that BMI was significantly correlated with (relative) bioavailability, where higher values are expected for patients with higher BMI values. Also, weight and BMI were important factors affecting the AUC and Cmax; subjects with lower weight and BMI have approximately 10% higher AUC and Cmax values, while persons with higher weights and BMIs have approximately 20% lower AUC.

Age was used a covariate in the PopPK study, in the parent-only model for leriglitazone. Results showed that age is significantly associated with clearance (younger patients showed a lower clearance and higher AUC and Cmax values). However, the clinical effects are not expected to be relevant.

## 5.2.2.11. Pharmacokinetic interaction studies

## 5.2.2.11.1. Special populations

No information provided.

## 5.2.2.11.2. PK drug-drug interactions

Leriglitazone and M3 are both reversible inhibitors of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 with IC50 values ranging from 73 to 102 μM for leriglitazone and from 66 to 109 μM for M3. The risk of clinical DDI through CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inhibition by leriglitazone was evaluated based on the static mechanistic model calculations and it was confirmed to be negligible. Leriglitazone and M3 are not inhibitors of CYP1A2,

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

2A6, 2B6, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4. Leriglitazone and M3 are inducers of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 at different concentrations tested (up to 240 μM). Based on a calibration curve of RIS versus the in vivo induction effect of a set of 9 control inducers/non-inducers of CYP3A4, leriglitazone has similar magnitude of an in vivo induction as the weak inducer pioglitazone, for which relevant clinical DDI have not been reported.

Leriglitazone and M3 are substrates of the BCRP efflux transporter and only leriglitazone is substrate of MDR1 (P-gp) efflux transporter. Leriglitazone is not a substrate of the OATP1A2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OATP2B1 uptake transporters and M3 could be substrate of OATP1B1 but not of OATP1B3, OATP1A2 and OATP2B1 transporters. Leriglitazone may be an inhibitor of the BCRP transporter; but in the absence of a concentration dependent response the IC50 could not be calculated. M3 is likely to be an inhibitor of the BCRP efflux transporters up to 100 μM tested, but the risk of drug interactions at the level of the intestine is considered to be negligible as M3 is expected to be formed mainly in the liver. Leriglitazone and M3 are not inhibitors of the MDR1 (P-gp) efflux transporter at up to maximum concentrations evaluated, i.e., 175 μM and 100 μM, respectively. Leriglitazone is considered an in vitro inhibitor of OCT1 and OCT2, OAT1, OAT3 and MATE1 but not MATE2-K SLC transporters The potential clinical risk of inhibition by leriglitazone is only predicted to exist with BCRP, OAT1B1, OATB13 and OCT1. M3 is considered an in vitro inhibitor of the OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, OAT3, MATE1 and MATE2-K SLC transporter. The potential clinical risk of inhibition by leriglitazone is considered low to all transporters.

The conduct of a dedicated DDI study to confirm the potential DDI through CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 inhibition and CYP3A/2C induction was planned as a post-approval commitment for the previous MAA procedure. This study has already been initiated and designed based on the feedback received during the previous MAA procedure, with results expected by mid-2026.

The potential for inhibition of BCRP at the level of the intestine cannot be completely ruled out by the in vitro data. A dedicated DDI study to confirm the potential DDI through BCRP substrates is planned post-approval.

Currently, the Applicant included cautionary language in the SmPC to inform practitioners on potential drug-drug interactions.

## 5.2.2.11.3. Dose proportionality/time independence/accumulation

No information provided.

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

Leriglitazone is an orally bioavailable, brain penetrant and selective peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma (PPAR) agonist for the treatment of cALD. Leriglitazone acts as a disease modifying therapy that can achieve adequate exposure within the central nervous system to modulate critical pathways involved in cALD progression by decreasing neuroinflammation, enhancing blood-brain barrier (BBB) integrity, intending to promote myelination and improving mitochondrial function.

## 5.2.3.2. Primary and secondary pharmacology

Data from PD endpoints of study MT-1-01 indicated biological activity of leriglitazone on biomarkers related to PPARγ activity and are aligned with results obtained in preclinical studies. In line with this, changes seen in the biomarkers analysed after multiple doses of leriglitazone were consistent with PPARγ agonism related pharmacology. Single doses of leriglitazone did not affect plasma concentrations of adiponectin and FABP4, but multiple doses showed effects on several biomarkers.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

After 8 days of leriglitazone dosing, plasma concentrations of adiponectin increased in a dosedependent manner, FABP4 plasma concentrations increased and CXCL10-IP10 decreased compared to placebo, but without a clear dose dependency.

Although this data supports the MoA target of leriglitazone, it does not have clinical correlation to validated PD endpoints on cALD.

Neurofilament light chain (NfL) is a marker of neuroaxonal damage and therefore a potential marker of injury to the brain and spinal cord. It can be measured in the CFS, blood and plasma. NfL is a major axonal cytoskeleton protein, especially expressed in long myelinated axons, which is released upon axonal damage. Elevations in CSF or blood NfL levels have been reported in multiple neurodegenerative diseases in adults. In addition, it has a major potential to monitor treatment and side-effects. However, little is known about NfL in paediatric neurological diseases.

In ADVANCE, plasma NfL levels increased significantly at 96 weeks compared to baseline in the placebo arm (mean change of baseline at Week 96 of 5.17± 17.04 pg/mL) but did not increase significantly in the active arm (mean change of baseline at Week 96 of 0.97 ± 3.89 pg/mL). At the end of the double-blind phase, plasma NfL concentration in the placebo arm was significantly higher compared to active arm (p=0.002), mostly driven by those patients developing cerebral lesion progression.

<!-- image -->

NfL level has been evaluated as potential biomarker for disease activity and potential biomarker for treatment response in various paediatric neurological diseases NfL increase occurs across a plethora of disorders, independent of the underlying pathology. The relationship between severity of the disorder and NfL levels is not clear.

For the use of NfL as surrogate PD endpoint that could be translated to clinical efficacy outcome, the following assumptions should be further supported 1) the treatment has a significant impact on the surrogate endpoint 2) treatment has a significant impact on the true clinical endpoint 3) the effect of treatment upon the true endpoint is captured by the surrogate. A simple correlation of NfL to a clinical endpoint can be agreed to exist, but further information and support is needed on the profile of NfL regarding: normal baseline levels between healthy population and patients; levels in different courses of disease and different disease progression; levels according to disease severity and age ranges; correlation of NfL levels and clinical endpoints and/or imaging outcomes and, most importantly, the profile of the response of NfL levels to treatments.

Regarding other PD biomarkers such as: MM9, IL-18, IL-1beta, IL-1ra or MIP-1 beta, although it is recognised the usefulness of these endpoints in the research of ALD and prospective treatments, they have still a very limited use in a regulatory context to establish clinically significant correlations to clinical efficacy outcomes.

Considering the use of NfL levels as a surrogate PD endpoint that can be translated to clinical efficacy outcomes, the applicant was asked to provide adequate evidence of the following: normal baseline levels between healthy population and patients; levels in different courses of disease and different disease progression; levels according to disease severity and age ranges; correlation of NfL levels and clinical endpoints and/or imaging outcomes and, most importantly, the profile of the response of NfL levels to treatments.

The applicant has provided relevant references, that can be partially supportive of the use of NfL levels as a surrogate PD endpoint. In particular, Weinhofer 2021, Wang 2022, Weinhofer 2023, Lund 2025, and Pierpont 2025 show that blood NfL is markedly higher in cerebral ALD than in controls, is associated with MRI lesion burden/severity, and decreases after successful disease-arresting treatment such as HSCT. The applicant has provided several references that cannot be considered supportive given that the evidence was generated in a different disease.

- a) Baseline levels in healthy individuals versus patients

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Weinhofer 2021 provides baseline blood NfL values across controls and ALD phenotypes, showing lower values in childhood/adolescent controls than in childhood/adolescent cALD, and also lower values in asymptomatic X-ALD than in cALD. Wang 2022 independently confirms higher plasma NfL in paediatric cALD versus paediatric controls and also shows a significant correlation with Loes score.

## b) Levels across disease courses / different phenotypes / progression states

Weinhofer 2021 study covers asymptomatic X-ALD, AMN, childhood/adolescent cALD, adult cALD, selfarrested cALD and post-HSCT cALD, and shows a separation between phenotypes, with the highest levels in childhood/adolescent cALD. It also reports that NfL can distinguish non-converting AMN from those later developing cALD.

Weinhofer 2023 compares different biological entities: childhood/adolescent cALD and adult cALD. The study reports that adult cALD can show lower blood NfL levels despite similar MRI lesion severity, indicating that the relationship between axonal injury and circulating NfL differs with age or disease biology.

Lund 2025 adds prospective longitudinal evidence that within individual boys with ALD, baseline plasma NfL can be established and that a rise of &gt;50% above baseline was associated with lesion development on MRI.

## c) Correlation with imaging and clinical outcomes

Weinhofer 2021 states that NfL reflected brain lesion severity in cALD. Wang 2022 reports a significant correlation between plasma NfL and MRI Loes score. Weinhofer 2023 also supports lesion discrimination and relationship to cerebral involvement, including correct classification of some patients with Loes score 1 and intact BBB.

## d) Response of NfL to treatment

Weinhofer 2021 states that NfL gradually normalized after HSCT. Wang 2022 provides more concrete paediatric post-treatment data: 9/11 patients had decreased plasma NfL at one year after HCT, with an overall mean decrease of 50%, while little change was seen in the first 60 days. Golse 2024 further suggests that in adults with early cALD treated with leriglitazone, plasma NfL stabilized in the clinically/radiologically stabilized patients and correlated with lesion load.

In conclusion, although the use of NfL as PD biomarker is supported, its use as a fully translatable PD/efficacy biomarker still presents itself with some disadvantages, namely, it still presents with age dependence interpretation with phenotype dependence (adult cALD behaves differently from childhood/adolescent cALD) and it has limited evidence for quantitative assessment of the magnitude of NfL change and a defined clinical benefit threshold, with this being a major disadvantage that has still not been addressed by the applicant.

The treatment-response evidence provided in the references is still mostly associated with successful disease arrest, and not a formal biomarker qualification as a surrogate for clinical efficacy. The literature supports that NfL is treatment-responsive and disease-relevant; it does not fully prove that a given magnitude of NfL change can substitute for demonstration of clinical benefit.

In summary, the references provided by the applicant are sufficient to support the concept that NfL is a biologically and clinically relevant PD biomarker in cerebral ALD, including baseline discrimination, phenotype/severity association, MRI correlation, and response to successful treatment. However, they are not sufficient, on their own, to fully validate NfL as a surrogate endpoint that can be directly translated into clinical efficacy claims without further disease and treatment-specific justification.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant has agreed with the conclusion that, based on the currently available evidence, neurofilament light chain (NfL) cannot be considered a fully validated surrogate endpoint for clinical efficacy in cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD).

Although the applicant considers that the available literature submitted supports NfL as a biologically and clinically relevant pharmacodynamic (PD) biomarker in cALD, the applicant recognises that the current evidence is not sufficient, on its own, to fully validate NfL as a surrogate endpoint that can be directly translated into clinical efficacy claims, due to the lack of a defined quantitative threshold associated with clinical benefit and the presence of age- and phenotype-dependent variability.

## 5.2.3.3. Pharmacodynamic interactions with other medicinal products or substances

The sponsor has not presented PD-specific interactions with other medicinal products. Although being a PPAR-gamma agonist, specific effects on glycemia have not been demonstrated, however on Section 4.3 the applicant has included some warnings and precautions for use with blood-glucose-lowering treatments. Furthermore, in this section, the applicant has also included the warning that diuretics are not recommended for management of oedema as they may decompensate electrolytes in patients receiving corticosteroid hormone replacement for ALD associated adrenal insufficiency.

## 5.2.3.4. Genetic differences in PD response

No pharmacodynamic effects are foreseen regarding genetic differences.

## 5.2.3.5. Immunological events

Not applicable.

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

Exposure-response relationships were evaluated using data from study MT-2-01.

Regarding the intended PK/PD/efficacy translation from the population of children 2 - 12 years of age to 13 - 17 years of age, some pharmacodynamic considerations should be performed.

The pivotal trial NEXUS assessed efficacy in children 2 - 12 years of age, while ADVANCE assessed efficacy in adults without and with Gd negative lesions.
