<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As the age range 13 - 17 years is not covered by the clinical program, the applicant proposed to extrapolate efficacy and safety from children and adults to adolescents aged 13-17 years in order to support a therapeutic indication covering patients over 2 years of age.

Extrapolation of efficacy was based on several considerations aimed to support similar disease course and medicine effect across age groups, including Comparable PK and PD data for cALD relevant markers (e.g., changes in NfL, MMP-9, adiponectin). However, as mentioned in the Primary Pharmacology section, several limitations exist in the use of these exploratory biomarkers.

Results from the PK/PD post-hoc analysis of study MT-2-01 have not revealed an exposure-response correlation. The sponsor has attributed the lack of correlation to the narrow range of exposures, the limited sample size, and the high variability of the endpoints.

Therefore, while the data provided could add support to the pharmacodynamic activity, they do not establish a robust quantitative exposure-efficacy relationship for clinically meaningful outcomes. Residual uncertainty remains regarding the optimal exposure required to maximise efficacy and its relationship to clinical benefit.

The applicant was asked to support the argument that the exploratory pharmacodynamic markers used to establish PD translation from 2-12y to 13-17y, namely that these biomarkers have similar responses according to stage of disease, timeline/kinetics, magnitude of its levels and most importantly that they can be used as a treatment biomarker within the same age group and exhibit the same correlation of response to a comparable age group.

The applicant argued that pharmacodynamic (PD) responses to leriglitazone are consistent across paediatric and adult populations based on three elements: (i) target engagement demonstrated by increases in adiponectin, (ii) modulation of exploratory disease-related biomarkers (NfL and MMP-9), and (iii) modelling of Loes score progression compared with natural history data. While these elements provide some evidence that the drug is biologically active across populations, the overall justification for extrapolating PD effects to adolescents aged 13-17 years remains incomplete.

First, the evidence on target engagement (adiponectin) confirms activation of the pharmacological target (PPARγ) across age groups. However, adiponectin is a mechanistic biomarker of receptor activation and does not demonstrate that downstream disease processes relevant to cerebral ALD (cALD) are modulated in a comparable manner across populations. Consequently, similar adiponectin responses cannot be interpreted as evidence of comparable pharmacodynamic efficacy.

Second, the applicant refers to modulation of exploratory disease-related biomarkers (NfL and MMP-9). Although stabilisation or reductions of these biomarkers are reported in treated patients across different studies, the comparisons are largely qualitative and indirect. The datasets differ substantially in terms of patient population, disease stage and study design. Therefore, the available evidence does not demonstrate that the magnitude or dynamics of biomarker modulation are quantitatively comparable across age groups.

Third, the applicant presents Loes score disease-progression modelling comparing treated patients with natural history data and stratified by age groups. While this analysis suggests that disease progression after lesion activation may follow broadly similar trajectories across ages, several limitations remain. The baseline Loes scores differ substantially between the age groups, suggesting that the compared populations are not clinically equivalent at baseline. Furthermore, the comparison with natural history datasets introduces potential biases related to differences in disease monitoring, inclusion criteria and diagnostic timing. Consequently, the modelling cannot be considered confirmatory evidence of comparable treatment response across age groups.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Importantly, the extrapolation to adolescents (13-17 years) relies primarily on interpolation between paediatric and adult populations, rather than direct demonstration in a sufficiently characterised adolescent cohort. The available analyses do not clearly demonstrate that pharmacodynamic relationships (e.g., exposure-biomarker relationships or biomarker-clinical outcome relationships) are quantitatively similar across the age spectrum.

Overall, the totality of evidence indicates that leriglitazone engages its pharmacological target and modulates exploratory biomarkers associated with disease activity. However, the available analyses do not adequately demonstrate that pharmacodynamic responses are quantitatively comparable between children, adolescents and adults, nor that biomarker changes consistently translate into comparable clinical or radiological outcomes across these populations. As such, the justification for extrapolation of pharmacodynamic effects to adolescents aged 13-17 years remains insufficiently substantiated.

In view of the limitations of the cross-study comparisons and the exploratory nature of the biomarkers presented (adiponectin, NfL and MMP-9), the applicant was requested to further substantiate the extrapolation of pharmacodynamic response to adolescents (13-17 years). In particular, the applicant was asked to provide an integrated quantitative analysis demonstrating comparable exposurebiomarker relationships and biomarker-radiological outcome relationships across children, adolescents and adults, considering potential differences in baseline disease characteristics and disease stage.

The Applicant has not provided a formal integrated quantitative analysis demonstrating comparable exposure-biomarker and biomarker-radiological outcome relationships across children, adolescents and adults, mainly due to the limited availability of adolescent data. Therefore, the requested crossage pharmacodynamic bridging has not been directly demonstrated.

The applicant supported the extrapolation to adolescents based on adult exposure-response analyses, highlighting that expected adolescent exposures are above the adult exposure range associated with pharmacodynamic activity, and limited compassionate-use observations showing clinical and radiological stability in adolescents. The applicant addresses the restriction of extrapolation to adolescents with Gd-negative lesions by assuming that this subgroup may have a disease trajectory closer to adult cALD than to rapidly progressive childhood cALD.

However, the supporting adult exposure-response analyses are derived from ADVANCE, a study with limited efficacy outcomes. This limits the strength of the adult dataset as a basis for extrapolating efficacy or pharmacodynamic response to adolescents. Moreover, the adolescent evidence remains sparse, uncontrolled and largely observational, and no adolescent-specific exposure-biomarker or biomarker-radiological outcome relationship has been demonstrated.

Overall, while the totality of evidence could be used to argue against a pharmacodynamic extrapolation to adolescents aged 13-17 years with Gd-negative lesions, this carries important residual uncertainty. Further adolescent-specific exposure, biomarker, radiological and clinical outcome data is necessary to support the applicant's assumptions.

## 5.2.5. Dose selection and therapeutic window

The dose used in the main clinical studies was calculated through the translation of non-clinical data taking into account the exposure obtained in rodents and effects seen in non-clinical models. Those results were then validated in Phase 1 clinical studies and adapted to adult and children with support of PopPK/PBPK models. This rational was in part supported by Scientific Advice from the EMA.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.6.1. Discussion

## Pharmacokinetics

The pharmacokinetics of leriglitazone and its active metabolite M3 were investigated in five clinical studies: two trials in healthy adult males; one trial in male patients with adrenomyeloneuropathy (AMN); one trial including male and female patients with Friedreich's ataxia; one trial in boys aged 2-12 years with cerebral ALD (cALD); and a subsequent compassionate use program enrolling boys with cALD.

The results from these clinical studies, including the determination of leriglitazone pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) parameters, alongside extensive physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) and population pharmacokinetic (PopPK) modelling, have been used to support the proposed clinical use of Nezglyal Oral Suspension.

Additionally, in vitro studies were conducted to evaluate plasma protein binding, metabolic pathways, enzyme identification, transporter substrate specificity, and drug-drug interactions (DDIs) involving leriglitazone and M3 as perpetrators via inhibition and induction mechanisms. M3 exhibits pharmacological activity comparable to leriglitazone and may contribute to the overall efficacy and safety profile of Nezglyal.

Overall, the clinical pharmacology program was considered extensive and well-structured, delivering a comprehensive pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic characterization of leriglitazone that meets regulatory expectations and provided data to evaluate its proposed clinical use.

## Methods

Bioanalytical methods for the quantification of leriglitazone and M3 in human plasma, urine and CSF were developed by the applicant. These performed the separation of the analytes and interfering compounds by HPLC In general, the methods showed appropriate performance. A method for the quantification of the several enantiomers of Leriglitazone was also developed.As such, although the method is scientifically qualified, a full validation was not feasible.

An additional fit for purpose validated LC-RAD and LC-HRMS method for the metabolite profiling and identification of 14 C-MIN-102 and 14 C-M3 in human plasma, urine and faeces homogenate samples was used. The pre-study validation experiments, including Precision of peak area and retention time by LC-MS(/MS), Precision of peak area and retention time by LC-RAD, Extraction recovery of matrix samples, Column recovery and Carryover met the previously made criteria, and the analytical approach and methods can be considered suitable for the intended use.

## Models

The popPK model for Leriglitazone and M3 was developed in several phases. Initially, data from 130 individuals from studies MT-1-01, MT-2-01 and MT-2-03 was used, including both rich and sparce data in healthy and FRDA/ ALD patients. Next the model was updated with data from study MT-2-02 (children with cALD) and from study MT-1-03 (mass-balance study in healthy volunteers), totalling 156 subjects. Based on these subjects, the following steps were performed for the model development: 1) Structural model development using only data from healthy volunteers (MT-1-01) and inclusion of key structural covariates (food, body weight); 2) Re-estimation of parameters on the full analysis dataset (MT-1-01, MT-2-01, MT-202, MT 2 03 and MT 1-03). Finalisation of base model; 3) Stepwise automatic covariate modelling using the base model; 4) Refinement of final model including significant covariates; Qualification of final model. After this step, an addition update was made by taking in consideration an additional 8 children with cALD (MT-202), totalling 164 individuals (ages ranging from 3 to 66 years old, weights from 17.2 to 136.8 kg). In this phase a re-run of the previous leriglitazone and M3 models on the full dataset with and without re-estimation of parameters followed by the assessment of the predictive performance by comparing simulations of leriglitazone to data from compassionate use, that were not used for model building or model update. After oral administration, leriglitazone PK was successfully described using a two-compartment model with first-order absorption and linear elimination. An exponential model was used to describe the inter-individual variability (IIV) of the model parameters, assuming log-normal distributions of the individual PK parameters. Correlations between random effect parameters were investigated by estimation of some selected off diagonal elements of the omega matrix. Inter-occasion variability in clearance was considered during modelrefinement after the automated covariate search had been done. Since the model was also aimed to be applied to children and studies MT-2-02 and MT-2-03 included patients as young as 2 years old, it was decided to account for the association between body weight and PK parameters according to allometric theory already in the base model. A fixed allometric exponent of 0.75 for clearance and inter-compartment flow (Q) was used while an exponent of 1.0 was used for the relationship between volume and body weight. The parameter estimates of the leriglitazone-only PopPK model including the clinically relevant covariates were presented for assessment. The model parameters were all estimated with good accuracy and were confirmed to be statistically significant by the Sampling Importance Resampling with 1000 resamples. pcVPC were also showing that the model is capturing well the central tendency as well as the overall variability. The percentiles of the observed concentrations fall well within the 95% confidence interval of the simulations, except for the 2-hour time point where levels were slightly underestimated. This may be due to uncontrolled food intake during the compassionate use program leading to a miss-prediction of the absorption phase. For the joint parent-metabolite model, a straightforward approach was chosen. All parameters related to the parent kinetics were fixed to the final estimates of the updated parent-only model, followed by the assessment of the metabolite specific parameters, which included the possibility of pre-systemic (i.e., firstpass) formation of M3. Again, the parameters were estimated with good accuracy and low shrinkage. The data and most of the pcVPC, again, show the model ability to predict the central tendency and variability of the data, except in the FRDA patients where the central tendency was under-predicted. This is of no major concern in the present application. Overall, both models conveniently describe the observed clinical data and can be considered fit-for-purpose.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A series of PBPK models were built during the different phases of the clinical development and updated as new data was made available. The last version was based on a middle-out approach with in vitro data on solubility, permeability, in vitro recombinant clearance data and in vivo profiles for optimization of the bioavailability and CL related parts of the model. The refined model was then verified using independent clinical data profiles. This model, developed in SimCYP version 21, was then used for evaluating DDIs (with leriglitazone as victim of CYP2C8, CYP3A4/P-GP and P-GP/BCRP inhibition, as perpetrator of CYP2C9 inhibition) and intrinsic factors (hepatic and renal impairment and CYP2C8 genotyping). In all cases, insufficient qualification was provided and the model cannot be used for median/high risk decisions, and the model is only able to be prospectively used to predict the effects prior to conducting dedicated clinical studies.

## Absorption

Based on the in vitro data, Leriglitazone solubility goes from slightly soluble at pH 2 to practically insoluble at pH 7 resulting that it can be considered a low solubility compound. In Caco-2 cells, its permeability is high and suggest complete absorption. As such, leriglitazone may be considered a BCS class II drug. Based on the in vivo studies, Leriglitazone was showed a fast absorption after oral administration with the median Tmax ranging from about 0.63 to 8 hours. The ka estimated in the popPK model, with a value of 5.33 h -1 confirms this. Its high variability is also compatible with the expected behaviour for a BCS class II drug.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The absolute oral bioavailability of leriglitazone was investigated in the mass balance study after the administration of a single oral dose of 180 mg leriglitazone hydrochloride as an oral suspension, followed by a 15-minute intravenous (IV) infusion of approximately 100 μg 14 C-leriglitazone hydrochloride administered 1.5 hours after the oral dose administration. Again, the oral formulation showed a fast absorption, with a median Tmax ranging from 1.43 - 4.00 hours. From the overall exposure the absolute oral bioavailability of leriglitazone was determined to be &gt;90%, confirming the high permeability of the drug.

During the clinical part, two formulations were used. The initial one was intentionally developed as a simple formulation for immediate use in the Phase I clinical study (MT-1-01), which was conducted in one site Europe. This formulation for Phase I was a 5.46 mg/mL oral suspension that consisted of leriglitazone, polysorbate 80, carboxymethylcellulose sodium, and water for injection. The initial clinical formulation was modified based on the results obtained with the formulation used in clinical study MT-1-01 to allow for longer stability for dosing during future studies and for commercial use. This latter formulation is a 15 mg/mL oral suspension that consisted of leriglitazone, sorbitol, Microcrystalline cellulose, Carmellose sodium, Saccharin sodium, Sodium benzoate, Sodium citrate, Citric acid monohydrate, Strawberry flavour, Citric acid monohydrate and Purified water. No dedicated BE studies were performed and the popPK model concluded that the Phase I formulation is absorbed faster but with the same extent as the commercial (and Phase 2/3) formulation.

The food effect was studied in the phase I formulation under single dose administration. Under these conditions, food delayed the rate of absorption but with a neglective effect in the leriglitazone bioavailability. This was also observed in the popPK analysis were food effected the ka by reducing it round 80% and the F, also reducing it but only by 4%. Regarding the commercial formulation, the food effect in it was not directly evaluated although the exposure under food (but not controlled) conditions in patients seems similar to the one observed in the phase I formulation administered with food in healthy subjects. To clarify this, the applicant is currently assessing the effect of food on administration of the commercial formulation of leriglitazone oral suspension in a DDI study, with results expected by Q3-2026. The applicant confirmed that the results from the food effect study will be submitted once the study is finalised and the report is available. This issue is considered resolved at this stage but should be followed in a post-approval commitment.

## Distribution

The unbound plasma percentage of leriglitazone ranged from 3 to 4.3% and was concentration independent. The unbound plasma percentage of M3 ranged from 1.8 to 1.9% and was also concentration independent. The in vitro blood/plasma partitioning (Kblood/plasma) values for leriglitazone and M3 were 0.725 and 0.786, respectively, indicating that they do not extensively bind into the erythrocytes. This was confirmed in vivo were the whole blood to plasma ratio for total radioactivity was consistent over time with geometric mean ratios ranging from 0.543 to 0.62.

The estimated apparent volume of distribution in steady state in the PopPK analysis was of 22.5 L, at around 0.32 L/kg. This does not show an extensive distribution in the body. The distribution of leriglitazone to the cerebrospinal fluid was also investigated. Leriglitazone and M3 concentrations in CSF increased dose proportionally and correlated with the plasma concentrations. The CSF-to-plasma ratio is approximately 0.022 for leriglitazone and 0.004 for M3.

## Elimination

The elimination half-life of leriglitazone is around 24h in healthy subjects and on the same order between the studied doses and after steady state. In the mass balance study, similar elimination half-lives were observed between the total radioactivity in plasma and leriglitazone itself. Based on the popPK CL (0.739 L/h), V1 (18.2 L), Q (0.823 L/h) and V2 (4.27 L), the average elimination half-life for a 70 kg subject is confirmed to be also around 24h. In the single dose study in healthy subjects, approximately 6% of leriglitazone administered was excreted in urine as parent during the first 48 hours, mostly in the conjugated state. Total excretion as free leriglitazone did not increase above 0.4% of the administered dose over the 48-hour collection period. Renal clearance was low and approximately 2 mL/h for all doses and with no differences between fed and fasted state. This value is compatible with a simple filtration based renal elimination. Following single oral administration of 180 mg 14 C-leriglitazone hydrochloride containing 3.7 MBq of radioactivity, most of the plasma radioactivity was from leriglitazone (53%) and the metabolite M3 (22%). The AUC of the M3 was 40% of the leriglitazone one, making this a major metabolite and the only one identified in the plasma. More than 60% of total radioactivity had been excreted by 48 hours post-dose and more than 90% by 96 hours. By 312 hours post-dose, recovery of the radiolabelled oral dose was complete with an arithmetic mean of 101.2%. The major route of excretion of total radioactivity was via urine (mean 77.0%). The remainder of total radioactivity was excreted in faeces (mean 24.2%). Very small amounts of the administered dose were excreted in urine as unchanged leriglitazone and unchanged M3 (0.4% and &lt; 0.02%, respectively), and in faeces as unchanged 14C-leriglitazone (5.1%) and as 14C-M3 (0.3%). Leriglitazone was poorly metabolized in both human liver microsomes and human hepatocytes, with 91% still remaining after 60-minutes microsomal incubation with NADPH and without inhibitors and 76% still remaining after the 120-minute incubation period in hepatocytes. Metabolite M3 formed via alcohol dehydrogenation was the predominant/ main metabolite with human liver microsomes, although other minor metabolites were also observed. Due to the high stability, the effect of CYP inhibitors for the total metabolism (fraction metabolised) was not evaluated, but by the inhibition of the formation of the metabolites, it was concluded that CYP2C8 and CYP3A are the key oxidative enzymes involved in the metabolism of leriglitazone, with CYP2C8 being mainly in charge of the formation of main metabolite, M3. As observed before, M3 was the only metabolite identified in plasma. Regarding M3, when incubated in hepatocytes was less stable than leriglitazone, with 40% remaining at 120 minutes. Its disappearance in both cytosol and microsomes correlated well with the formation of leriglitazone and was NADPH-dependent. The observed metabolism of M3 to leriglitazone in liver cytosol is likely catalysed by carbonyl reductase (CBR) or aldo-keto reductase. In liver microsomes, the reaction may be catalysed by microsomal CBR or CYP P450 reductase, but CYP enzymes may also contribute to the metabolism of M3. In urine (pooled urine sample of 5 subjects), 11 compounds were identified. The most abundant metabolite in urine was a demethylated analogue oxidised to a carboxylic acid (U11), accounting for 30% of the metabolites in urine. In faeces (pooled homogenate of 5 subjects) six compounds were identified. The most abundant signal could be assigned to a sulphated metabolite (representing 42.1% of the metabolites in faeces) followed by unchanged leriglitazone (38.7%). Leriglitazone is a mixture of four stereoisomers corresponding to two pairs of diastereomers, E1 and E2. The two isomers present in E1 have the same configuration at C2 and a different configuration at C5 and, in E2, the same configuration at C5 and a different configuration at C2. The ratio between E1 and E2 concentrations in humans is dominated by E2. Geometric mean E1/E2 ratios ranged from 0.1 to 0.3 and did not show any trend with dose administered, fasted or fed status, or time point of the samples. No consequences are expected for efficacy as both E1 and E2 display similar affinity for the PPAR gamma receptor. The leriglitazone enantiomer ratio (E1 and E2) was also determined in a subset of patients at Weeks 4, 12, 48 and 96. Leriglitazone diastereomers ratios were consistently maintained from Week 4 until Week 96 and also at all timepoints at Week 12. From the SAD study, it was observed that the PK of the M3 metabolite followed very closely the PK of leriglitazone, with a similar Tmax and a similar elimination t1/2. This was also concluded in the popPK model, were pre-systemic metabolism with fast absorption of M3 is considered together with a systemic formation. The M3 model parameters also indicate a flip-flop phenomenon were the elimination of M3 is, in fact, governed by its formation from leriglitazone itself.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The consequences of a genetic polymorphism in CYP2C8 were assessed by means of the PBPK model, by testing a decreased or an increased in CYP2C8 clearance to simulate the substrate-dependency of CYP2C8*2 and CYP2C8*3 polymorphisms. The model showed that CYP2C8 genotype could have a non-negligible impact on leriglitazone exposure, and the applicant will explore the effect of polymorphisms of CYP2C8 in the planned DDI study in healthy volunteers. The applicant confirmed that the DDI through CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 inhibition and CYP3A/2C induction study is not yet completed and will only be provided after Q3 2026. The potential DDI through BCRP substrate rosuvastatin is planned to be conducted as a post approval commitment. The applicant confirmed that the results from the two DDI studies will be submitted once the study is finalised and the report available. These issues are considered resolved at this stage but should be followed in post-approval commitments.

## Dose proportionality and time dependency

Dose linearity was assessed after single increasing doses of leriglitazone by means of a power model on the log-transformed Cmax and AUC for both leriglitazone and its metabolite M3. Results showed a dose proportionality situation for the AUC on both analytes. However, for Cmax, a slightly less than dose proportionality was observed. Being leriglitazone a BCS class II drug, this may indicate that solubility issues may slightly delay the rate of absorption of the drug.

Regarding accumulation and time dependency, after multiple doses, steady state was observed at days 4-5 with an accumulation of 1.7 on both Cmax and AUC for both leriglitazone and M3 was observed. This is consistent with the observed elimination t1/2 of around 22h. In this line of reasoning, no non-linearity or time dependency was considered in the PopPK model.

According to the PopPK model, the inter-individual variability in F, ka, CL and V2 was, respectively, 13%, 65%, 15% and 40%, showing that most of the drug's variability is due to its oral absorption process. The inter-occasion variability in CL was around 14%.

## Pharmacokinetics in the target population

The pharmacokinetics of leriglitazone have been assessed across several clinical trials and in different patient populations. Adult male patients, mainly with ALD and a small subgroup with cALD (study MT-2-01), were studied in a Phase II/III randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial. A total of 116 patients were enrolled, of whom 77 received leriglitazone and 39 placebo. Dosing started at 150 mg and was subsequently individualized based on plasma levels to achieve a predefined target exposure of 200 µg*h/mL. After dose adjustments at Week 4 and confirmation at Week 12, patients maintained stable plasma concentrations throughout the study. The average recommended doses were 180 ± 45 mg and 177 ± 44 mg, based on PK measurements at Week 4 or Week 12, respectively. The long-term extension (OLE) confirmed that mean exposures remained within the therapeutic range (157.9-177.8 µg*h/mL) up to Week 240, even in patients requiring dose reductions for safety reasons, who nonetheless stayed above the minimum efficacious exposure of 100 µg*h/mL. It should be mentioned that in the SmPC, the dosing for adults (&gt;18 years) follows the results from this clinical trial and recommends that the starting dose should 150 mg once daily, and that after 1 month from initiation of treatment the dose should be increased to 180 mg.

Paediatric cALD patients (study MT-2-02), were assessed in a Phase II/III open-label multicentre study that evaluated leriglitazone before HSCT. Twenty-three boys (mean age 7.8 years, range: 4-12 years) were treated with doses adjusted for body weight and age using PBPK modelling to target an exposure of 170 µg*h/mL. Plasma concentrations were monitored at multiple time points, and doses were modified as necessary. Overall, mean exposures remained within the therapeutic range up to 96 weeks, including in patients undergoing HSCT, and no dose reductions were required for safety reasons.

The pharmacokinetics of leriglitazone were also studied in patients with FRDA in study MT-2-03, a doubleblind, placebo-controlled trial enrolling 39 male and female patients aged 12 to 60 years. Dosing was individualized according to age and sex to achieve the same target exposure of approximately 170 µg*h/mL.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

After adjustment at Week 4, patients maintained stable levels for 48 weeks, with an average AUC close to target (166 µg*h/mL). Patients who required reduced doses for safety reasons continued to show exposures within or near the predefined therapeutic window.

In addition to formal clinical trials, pharmacokinetic data from 44 children under 18 years of age treated under a compassionate use program were incorporated into the PopPK analyses. These patients received leriglitazone following the paediatric dosing regimen from MT-2-02, and blood samples were taken for concentration-based dose adjustments.

Overall, the popPK model results confirmed consistent pharmacokinetics with no relevant differences compared to healthy volunteers.

The therapeutic window was defined based on preclinical studies where an estimated AUC of &gt; 100 µg*h/mL was considered needed for efficacy and the target plasma exposure was selected at 200 μg*h/mL in adults and 170 μg*h/mL in children to account for the highest possible efficacious exposure that still fell within the NOAEL determined in the 13-week toxicity study in rats and 10-week toxicity study in juvenile rats, respectively.

## Special populations

Based on the results of the mass balance study, &gt;77 % of the leriglitazone dose was excreted in urine, with only a limited fraction as unchanged drug or M3 metabolite. This was also confirmed in the phase I SD study. As such, the impact of impaired renal function on the leriglitazone PK is not expected to be significant. The SmPC indicates that leriglitazone as not recommended in patients with moderate to severe renal impairment. No dedicated renal impairment studies were performed for leriglitazone. No renal impaired subjects were included in the clinical trials as renal impairment is not a common comorbidity in ALD patients with cerebral ALD. Leriglitazone elimination (and its main metabolite M3) is mainly by metabolism and only around 2% of the dose is excreted as parent drug. So, renal function is not expected to significantly influence the clearance of both leriglitazone and it M3 metabolite. Due to the rarity of the existence of real impairment in the patient population, the restriction of the use of the drug for the moderate and severe renal impaired subjects seems acceptable.

No dedicated studies in impaired hepatic function subjects were performed. However, results from the mass balance study indicate that hepatic function plays a prominent role in the pharmacokinetics of leriglitazone, where leriglitazone is extensively metabolised and partially excreted via bile acids. In the clinical studies, patients with mild hepatic impairment were able to be included but none presented such condition. As such, this seems rare in the population (as the renal impairment). However, hepatic metabolism is the major route of elimination of leriglitazone and, although a PBPK model did not predict major changes in CL due to hepatic impairment, this model is not considered acceptable for high-impact decisions. As such, the applicant decided to not recommend the use of leriglitazone in patients with any level of hepatic impairment. This is acceptable.

Although a lower clearance was predicted for women in the popPK model, the proposed indication is for men only. Thus, gender differences are of little relevance for the current application.

The effect of race was tested in the popPK model. Although most of the subjects were white (79.9%), ethnicity was not considered a significant co-variable describing the variability in the PK of leriglitazone.

In the PopPK model, body weight was used as a covariate for leriglitazone. The analysis showed that body mass index (BMI) was significantly directly correlated with relative bioavailability. Also, patients with higher BMI tended to have lower exposure, while those with lower BMI had higher exposure. Specifically, subjects with lower weight and BMI showed about 10% higher AUC and Cmax values, whereas those with higher weight and BMI had around 20% lower values.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Simulations with extreme body sizes, based on the 5th and 95th percentiles from the NHANES dataset, confirmed these effects. Smaller patients (60 kg, BMI 20) were predicted to reach higher plasma concentrations, while larger patients (129 kg, BMI 41) had lower exposures and trough levels slightly below the target concentration of 5.716 μg/mL (AUC 200 μg*h/mL). Steady state was projected to be achieved by the 3rd or 4th dose in all groups.

When simulating the results of the proposed flat dose for adults, the exposure was shown to remain above the minimum efficacious exposure determined preclinically (100 μg·h/mL) for almost all patients (99.8%). The distribution of AUC and Cmax values at steady state expected for male AMN patients taking 180 mg are presented below. For reference, in the multiple ascending dose study MT-1-01 the geometric mean Cmax at day 8 after 270 mg q.d. was 17.2 μg/mL, range: 14.1-23.7).

<!-- image -->

Lines and numbers repreaent the 5th, 50th and 95th percentiles with their reapective 90%Cls

So, although weight and BMI largely influence the leriglitazone PK, the simulated exposures of the proposed adult fixed dose seem to be within the values observed in the clinical trials.

In paediatric patients, using time-varying measures of weight and BMI gave a better fit than baseline values, reflecting natural growth during long-term treatment. This indicated that clearance increases as children grow, making it necessary to regularly adjust dosing in clinical practice, typically on an annual basis, to maintain optimal exposure.

Based on this, the SmPC proposes a different dosing scheme from the one used in the clinical trials. The new posology adjusts the paediatric dose based on bodyweight, height and age. The new posology in paediatric and adolescent patients is based on dosing simulations that targeted an AUC of 170 μg*h/mL ±20%. These were the same targeted exposures optimized in the clinical trials for children. The results are summarised in the table below, in which 1) dose concentration-based original is the therapeutic drug monitoring algorithm based on Ctrough that was used in study MT-2-02, 2) the dose concentration-based optimised represents a similar therapeutic drug monitoring based on Ctrough but updated with the new population pharmacokinetic analyses, 3) Dose cov-formula is based on the AUC calculation in the updated population pharmacokinetic analyses (D = CL*AUC, in which covariates on CL and AUC were used to estimate AUC), 4) Dose Cov-Table 0.1 mL - 0.5 mL are look up tables based on the dose cov-formula, 5) Flat dose per age group was based on the population pharmacokinetic model and 6) dose per kg original and 7) optimised are dosages per kg based on the updated and original population pharmacokinetic models.

The concentration-based scenario was considered as the best-case scenario. The second-best scenario was dosing based on a similar covariate model as implemented in the model on Cl and F. However, this was assumed to be difficult to implement in clinical practice. Therefore, dosing tables for different age categories (2-5, 6-11 and 12-17 years) were derived. These were found to perform better than bodyweight-based dosing. These dosing tables are proposed for paediatric dosing in the SmPC. It should however be noted that the youngest patient included in the population pharmacokinetic analysis was 4 years of age. Allometric scaling was the main factor explaining differences between paediatric patients and adult patients. The Applicant is applying for a paediatric indication down to 2 years of age. The plasma exposure can be expected to be reasonably well extrapolated with bodyweight up to the age of 2. Nonetheless, there are no data to confirm this extrapolation exercise in paediatrics &lt;4 years of age. The applicant has additionally justified and refined the assumptions underlying the acceptability of allometric scaling. The popPK model incorporates age and body mass index (BMI) as covariates, capturing maturation- and size-related variability. To characterise the impact of uncertainty in these covariate effects, the applicant conducted a sensitivity analysis in which the AGE and BMI coefficients were increased or decreased by a factor of two. It was found that the sensitivity analysis did not indicate an increase outside the studied range in adults; the AUC would remain within the adult target range of 200 µg·h/mL ± 20%. Based on this modelling approach, it was agreed that safety concerns related to potential overexposure are unlikely. Additionally, the applicant has provided a reassuring assessment of maturation considerations through PBPK modelling. In general, the results did not identify a potential risk, given the limited impact of CYP3A4 and CYP2C8 ontogeny in the age group of 2-6 years. These findings support the conclusion that overexposure in children aged 2-4 years is unlikely. It is supported that the findings from the popPK analysis, together with literature data, demonstrate enough justification for the assumption that the therapeutic range in the paediatric population can be expected to align with the relevant exposure metrics linked to efficacy and safety previously established in adults. The available evidence indicates that the exposure-response relationship defined in adults is applicable when extrapolating to paediatric patients.

## DDI

## In vitro

The in vitro drug-drug interaction potential of leriglitazone and its main metabolite M3 was extensively assessed across CYP enzyme inhibition and induction studies, as well as transporter interaction studies. Cut-off evaluations indicated that leriglitazone concentrations after a 180 mg dose fall above the basic thresholds for some enzymes, warranting further analysis. Initial experiments with human liver microsomes demonstrated that leriglitazone showed little or no reversible inhibition of major CYP enzymes, with IC50

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

values exceeding 100 µM. The most notable inhibitory effects were observed on CYP2C8 and CYP2C9, but even here the IC50 values remained above the cut-off values. No time-dependent inhibition (TDI) was detected for leriglitazone at any concentration. A subsequent study investigated direct inhibition and TDI towards major drug-metabolising CYP enzymes with both leriglitazone and M3 confirmed similar inhibition profiles, with mild reversible inhibition towards CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 at high concentrations (but slightly below the cut-off value), and no meaningful TDI. M3 showed slight IC50 shifts for CYP2B6 and CYP3A4, but these were small and within expected assay variability. Static mechanistic modelling in accordance with ICH M12 guidelines confirmed that the small in vitro signals for CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inhibition by leriglitazone are not clinically relevant, with predicted AUC ratios for sensitive substrates (repaglinide and warfarin) remaining close to unity (1.04 and 1.06, respectively).

Enzyme induction was also examined in cryopreserved human hepatocytes, showing minor induction at supratherapeutic concentrations for CYP2B6 and CYP3A4. Induction of CYP1A2 was seen in one donor but was inconsistent and likely overestimated, making a clinically relevant effect unlikely. Overall, it can be concluded that both leriglitazone and M3 have a low potential to cause CYP-mediated drug interactions as perpetrators.

Transporter studies revealed that leriglitazone has limited inhibitory activity on common uptake and efflux transporters, except for some signal for BCRP, OAT1B1, OATB13 and OCT1 inhibition. Bidirectional permeability assays demonstrated active efflux of leriglitazone by BCRP and MDR1, but no active uptake through OATP transporters. M3, on the other hand, showed evidence of efflux via BCRP and potential uptake by OATP1B1, as indicated by increased accumulation and inhibition by rifampicin. No relevant interaction with other uptake transporters was detected.

## In silico

A series of physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modelling studies were performed and updated as new data became available during the development of leriglitazone. These analyses were used to predict drug-drug interaction (DDI) risks in line with the ICH M12 guideline, covering inhibition, induction, and transporter effects for both leriglitazone and its metabolite M3. The models estimated that CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 are responsible for the majority of leriglitazone elimination (64% and 30%, respectively), suggesting potential interactions with inhibitors or inducers of these enzymes. Simulations predicted low to moderate exposure changes with strong CYP2C8 inhibitors such as gemfibrozil and CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitors such as verapamil or itraconazole. The PBPK models further confirmed no clinically relevant effect on CYP3A4 substrates, such as midazolam, via enzyme induction. These conclusions, due to their highregulatory impact, require confirmation by in vivo DDI studies.

Transporter effects were also explored, with incorporation of intestinal efflux mediated by BCRP and P-gp, which contributed minimally to absorption (Fa = 0.92). As a result, the potential for interactions through inhibition of these transporters, for example with cyclosporine, was considered low. Finally, simulations predicted no relevant effect on OATP1B1 or OATP1B3 substrates, with rosuvastatin exposure increasing only marginally (AUC ratio 1.13; Cmax ratio 1.14). However, based on the outcome of the in vitro results, the applicant was planning a DDI study investigating leriglitazone and M3 as perpetrator on the PK of substrates of BCRP, OATP1B1 and OATP1B3.

## In vivo

No dedicated DDI in vivo studies were presented by the applicant. However, the conduct of a dedicated DDI study to confirm the potential DDI through CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 inhibition and CYP3A/2C induction was planned as a post-approval commitment for the previous MAA procedure. Also, a dedicated DDI study to confirm the potential DDI through BCRP substrates (rosuvastatin, also a probe for OATP1B1 and OATP1B3) is planned post-approval.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Currently, the Applicant has included the following language in the SmPC to inform practitioners on potential drug-drug interactions:

Leriglitazone is predominantly metabolized by cytochrome P450 (CYP)2C8 and CYP3A4. Leriglitazone may be an in vitro inhibitor of BCRP transporters.

Therefore, leriglitazone should be used with caution during concomitant administration of CYP2C8 and CYP3A inhibitors (e.g., gemfibrozil and itraconazole), CYP3A/2C inducers (e.g., carbamazepine) or BCRP substrates (e.g., rosuvastatin).

This is acceptable.

## Pharmacodynamics/ Mechanism of action

Leriglitazone is an orally bioavailable and selective peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma (PPARγ) agonist. Its mechanism of action-encompassing modulation of anti-inflammatory pathways, improvement of mitochondrial function, and reduction of oxidative stress within the central nervous system (CNS)-is considered potentially relevant for the treatment of cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD). Activation of PPARγ has been associated with neuroprotective effects, including the downregulation of proinflammatory cytokine release through inhibition of the nuclear factor kappa B (NF-κB) signaling pathway, stabilization of the blood-brain barrier (BBB), preservation of myelin integrity, and attenuation of neuronal loss.

For the use of NfL as surrogate PD endpoint that could be translated to clinical efficacy outcome, the following assumptions should be further supported 1) the treatment has a significant impact on the surrogate endpoint 2) treatment has a significant impact on the true clinical endpoint 3) the effect of treatment upon the true endpoint is captured by the surrogate. A simple correlation of NfL to a clinical endpoint can be agreed to exist, but further information and support is needed on the profile of NfL regarding: normal baseline levels between healthy population and patients; levels in different courses of disease and different disease progression; levels according to disease severity and age ranges; correlation of NfL levels and clinical endpoints and/or imaging outcomes and, most importantly, the profile of the response of NfL levels to treatments.

Regarding other PD biomarkers such as: MM9, IL-18, IL-1beta, IL-1ra or MIP-1 beta, adiponectin, FABP4, CRP, MCP-1, CXCL-10 and IP10, although it is recognised the usefulness of these endpoints in the research of ALD and prospective treatments, they have still a very limited use in a regulatory context to establish clinically significant correlations to clinical efficacy outcomes.

Considering the use of NfL levels as a surrogate PD endpoint that can be translated to clinical efficacy outcomes, the applicant was requested to provide adequate evidence of the following: normal baseline levels between healthy population and patients; levels in different courses of disease and different disease progression; levels according to disease severity and age ranges; correlation of NfL levels and clinical endpoints and/or imaging outcomes and, most importantly, the profile of the response of NfL levels to treatments. The Applicant acknowledged that NfL cannot currently be considered a fully validated surrogate endpoint for clinical efficacy in cALD and clarified that NfL is only used as supportive pharmacodynamic evidence, not as a primary basis for efficacy conclusions. The Applicant further clarified that conclusions regarding exposure-response and extrapolation to adolescents are intended to rely primarily on radiological and clinical endpoints, with NfL used only to complement and support those analyses. While this could be considered appropriate, the limitations for the use of NfL cannot be undervalued, including its age- and phenotype-dependent variability and lack of a validated quantitative threshold predictive of clinical benefit. Therefore, this issue was not further pursued as a standalone concern. Any remaining limitations regarding the use of NfL as supportive evidence were addressed in the assessment of the exposure-response analysis and the extrapolation of pharmacodynamic response to adolescents (see below).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant was asked to support the argument that the exploratory pharmacodynamic markers used to establish PD translation from 2-12y to 13-17y, namely that these biomarkers have similar responses according to stage of disease, timeline/kinetics, magnitude of its levels and most importantly that they can be used as a treatment biomarker within the same age group and exhibit the same correlation of response to a comparable age group.

The applicant has provided relevant references, that can be partially supportive of the use of NfL levels as a surrogate PD endpoint. In particular, Weinhofer 2021, Wang 2022, Weinhofer 2023, Lund 2025, and Pierpont 2025 show that blood NfL is markedly higher in cerebral ALD than in controls, is associated with MRI lesion burden/severity, and decreases after successful disease-arresting treatment such as HSCT. The applicant has provided several references that cannot be considered supportive given that the evidence was generated in a different disease.

## a) Baseline levels in healthy individuals versus patients

Weinhofer 2021 provides baseline blood NfL values across controls and ALD phenotypes, showing lower values in childhood/adolescent controls than in childhood/adolescent cALD, and lower values in asymptomatic X-ALD than in cALD. Wang 2022 independently confirms higher plasma NfL in paediatric cALD versus paediatric controls and shows a significant correlation with Loes score.

## b) Levels across disease courses / different phenotypes / progression states

Weinhofer 2021 study covers asymptomatic X-ALD, AMN, childhood/adolescent cALD, adult cALD, selfarrested cALD and post-HSCT cALD, and shows a separation between phenotypes, with the highest levels in childhood/adolescent cALD. It also reports that NfL can distinguish non-converting AMN from those later developing cALD.

Weinhofer 2023 compares different biological entities: childhood/adolescent cALD and adult cALD. The study reports that adult cALD can show lower blood NfL levels despite similar MRI lesion severity, indicating that the relationship between axonal injury and circulating NfL differs with age or disease biology.

Lund 2025 adds prospective longitudinal evidence that within individual boys with ALD, baseline plasma NfL can be established and that a rise of &gt;50% above baseline was associated with lesion development on MRI.

## c) Correlation with imaging and clinical outcomes

Weinhofer 2021 states that NfL reflected brain lesion severity in cALD. Wang 2022 reports a significant correlation between plasma NfL and MRI Loes score. Weinhofer 2023 also supports lesion discrimination and relationship to cerebral involvement, including correct classification of some patients with Loes score 1 and intact BBB.

## d) Response of NfL to treatment

Weinhofer 2021 states that NfL gradually normalized after HSCT. Wang 2022 provides more concrete paediatric post-treatment data: 9/11 patients had decreased plasma NfL at one year after HCT, with an overall mean decrease of 50%, while little change was seen in the first 60 days. Golse 2024 further suggests that in adults with early cALD treated with leriglitazone, plasma NfL stabilized in the clinically/radiologically stabilized patients and correlated with lesion load.

In summary, although the use of NfL as PD biomarker is supported, its use as a fully translatable PD/efficacy biomarker still presents itself with some disadvantages, namely, it still presents with age dependence interpretation with phenotype dependence (adult cALD behaves differently from childhood/adolescent cALD) and it has limited evidence for quantitative assessment of the magnitude of NfL change and a defined clinical benefit threshold, with this being a major disadvantage that has still not been addressed by the applicant.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The treatment-response evidence provided in the references is still mostly associated with successful disease arrest, and not a formal biomarker qualification as a surrogate for clinical efficacy. The literature supports that NfL is treatment-responsive and disease-relevant; it does not fully prove that a given magnitude of NfL change can substitute for demonstration of clinical benefit.

As a conclusion, the references provided by the applicant are sufficient to support the concept that NfL is a biologically and clinically relevant PD biomarker in cerebral ALD, including baseline discrimination, phenotype/severity association, MRI correlation, and response to successful treatment. However, they are not sufficient, on their own, to fully validate NfL as a surrogate endpoint that can be directly translated into clinical efficacy claims without further disease and treatment-specific justification.

Given that reliance on NfL as a surrogate marker will not have a relevant impact on the efficacy and B/R assessment, although the issue was not fully responded, it was not further pursued.

## Secondary Pharmacodynamics

While the non-clinical data has not risen concerns regarding the effect of leriglitazone on the QTc interval and no patients were reported to show QTc intervals higher than 500 msec, 5.2% in the developed a QTc interval &gt; 450 msec and ≤ 480 msec, the change from baseline &gt; 30msec was consistently higher in treated arm and extrasystoles/palpitations were reported in the treated patients of MT-2-01 study. The applicant has presented a correlation analysis to assess the relationship between QTc prolongation and the occurrence of cardiac adverse events, including extrasystoles and palpitations, at the individual patient level. The analyses presented do not demonstrate a temporal or patient-level correlation between QTc prolongation and the occurrence of cardiac AEs.

## Pharmacodynamic interactions

Pharmacodynamic interactions have not been discussed by the applicant. At the same time, PI includes specific warning and precautions, e.g., for the concomitant use with blood glucose lowering drugs. The information in the PI is considered acceptable.

## Exposure-response

Results from the PK/PD post-hoc analysis of study MT-2-01 have not revealed an exposure-response correlation. The sponsor has attributed the lack of correlation to the narrow range of exposures, the limited sample size, and the high variability of the endpoints. However, the exposures ranged from around 2500 ng/mL to 7500 ng/mL (excluding the almost 10.000 ng/mL outlier), the sample size is the same for other analysis and endpoints used are validated.

The absence of a clear exposure-response relationship represents a serious deficiency, as such analyses are fundamental for confirming the pharmacological plausibility of the proposed mechanism of action, supporting dose selection, and justifying the recommended posology. A robust E-R relationship is particularly important in this context, given the limited understanding of leriglitazone pharmacokinetics in the target population and the uncertainty regarding the degree of central nervous system (CNS) exposure required to achieve a therapeutic effect.

The applicant was asked to further elaborate on the absence of E-R correlation.

The original E-R analysis in MT-2-01 did not demonstrate a clear relationship between exposure and the AMN-related clinical endpoints evaluated. The applicant argued that these endpoints were not appropriate to capture the cerebral disease component and therefore performed additional analyses focusing on cALDrelated biomarkers.

For adiponectin and MMP-9, the applicant reports concentration-dependent changes based on naïve-pooled Emax modelling. However, these biomarkers have limitations in the context of dose justification and adequate E-R relationship, when taken together with the limitations of use of these biomarkers as correlatable to efficacy endpoints. Adiponectin reflects PPARγ target engagement rather than disease modification and therefore does not directly demonstrate therapeutic efficacy. MMP-9 is an exploratory biomarker associated with blood-brain barrier disruption, but its quantitative relationship with clinical or radiological outcomes in cALD remains insufficiently established. Furthermore, the modelling was based on naïve pooled analyses with substantial variability and limited data at lower exposure ranges, resulting in imprecise EC50 estimates.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For NfL and Loes score, the applicant acknowledged that quantitative E-R modelling was not feasible due to the low number of cALD progression events. Instead, a qualitative comparison based on exposure is presented. While the plots suggest differences in NfL and Loes score when compared to patients with higher leriglitazone concentrations, these analyses remain descriptive and cannot establish a robust concentrationresponse relationship. The stratification by median exposure further limits interpretability, as it does not allow identification of exposure thresholds associated with maximal efficacy.

Overall, the additional analyses provide some evidence of pharmacological activity and target engagement but remain insufficient to demonstrate a clear exposure-response relationship for clinically meaningful endpoints.

In conclusion, the applicant has provided additional exploratory exposure-response analyses using cALDrelated biomarkers. However, the analyses rely primarily on naïve-pooled modelling for exploratory biomarkers and qualitative comparisons for NfL and Loes score and therefore do not establish a robust quantitative relationship between exposure and clinically relevant disease outcomes.

The applicant was further requested to elaborate a more robust integrated evaluation of the exposureresponse relationship for leriglitazone, including the relationship between systemic exposure and radiological disease progression (Loes score) and/or NfL dynamics, and to clarify whether the available data support pharmacodynamic dose-dependent activity.

ADVANCE had limited efficacy outcomes, and the exposure-response analyses are therefore derived from a study without confirmatory evidence of efficacy on its primary outcome. This substantially limits the weight that can be given to post hoc or exploratory exposure-response findings for dose justification.

Therefore, while the data could add support to the pharmacodynamic activity, they do not establish a robust quantitative exposure-efficacy relationship for clinically meaningful outcomes. Residual uncertainty remains regarding the optimal exposure required to maximise efficacy and its relationship to clinical benefit.

Regarding the intended PK/PD/efficacy translation from the population of children 2 - 12 years of age to 13 - 17 years of age, some pharmacodynamic considerations should be performed.

The pivotal trial NEXUS assessed efficacy in children 2 - 12 years of age, while ADVANCE assessed efficacy in adults without and with Gd negative lesions.

As the age range 13 - 17 years is not covered by the clinical program, the applicant proposed to extrapolate efficacy and safety from children and adults to adolescents aged 13-17 years to support a therapeutic indication covering patients over 2 years of age.

Extrapolation of efficacy was based on several considerations aimed at supporting similar disease course and medicine effect across age groups, including Comparable PK and PD data for cALD relevant markers (e.g., changes in NfL, MMP-9, adiponectin). However, as mentioned in the Primary Pharmacology section, several limitations exist in the use of these exploratory biomarkers.

The applicant was asked to support the argument that the exploratory pharmacodynamic markers used to establish PD translation from 2-12y to 13-17y, namely that these biomarkers have similar responses according to stage of disease, timeline/kinetics, magnitude of its levels and most importantly that they can be used as a treatment biomarker within the same age group and exhibit the same correlation of response to a comparable age group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant argued that pharmacodynamic (PD) responses to leriglitazone are consistent across paediatric and adult populations based on three elements: (i) target engagement demonstrated by increases in adiponectin, (ii) modulation of exploratory disease-related biomarkers (NfL and MMP-9), and (iii) modelling of Loes score progression compared with natural history data. While these elements provide some evidence that the drug is biologically active across populations, the overall justification for extrapolating PD effects to adolescents aged 13-17 years remains incomplete.

First, the evidence on target engagement (adiponectin) confirms activation of the pharmacological target (PPARγ) across age groups. However, adiponectin is a mechanistic biomarker of receptor activation and does not demonstrate that downstream disease processes relevant to cerebral ALD (cALD) are modulated in a comparable manner across populations. Consequently, similar adiponectin responses cannot be interpreted as evidence of comparable pharmacodynamic efficacy.

Second, the applicant refers to modulation of exploratory disease-related biomarkers (NfL and MMP-9). Although stabilisation or reductions of these biomarkers are reported in treated patients across different studies, the comparisons are largely qualitative and indirect. The datasets differ substantially in terms of patient population, disease stage, and study design. Therefore, the available evidence does not demonstrate that the magnitude or dynamics of biomarker modulation are quantitatively comparable across age groups.

Third, the applicant presents Loes score disease-progression modelling comparing treated patients with natural history data and stratified by age groups. While this analysis suggests that disease progression after lesion activation may follow broadly similar trajectories across ages, several limitations remain. The baseline Loes scores differ substantially between the age groups, suggesting that the compared populations are not clinically equivalent at baseline. Furthermore, the comparison with natural history datasets introduces potential biases related to differences in disease monitoring, inclusion criteria, and diagnostic timing. Consequently, the modelling cannot be considered confirmatory evidence of comparable treatment response across age groups.

Importantly, the extrapolation to adolescents (13-17 years) relies primarily on interpolation between paediatric and adult populations, rather than direct demonstration in a sufficiently characterised adolescent cohort. The available analyses do not clearly demonstrate that pharmacodynamic relationships (e.g., exposure-biomarker relationships or biomarker-clinical outcome relationships) are quantitatively similar across the age spectrum.

Overall, the totality of evidence indicates that leriglitazone engages its pharmacological target and modulates exploratory biomarkers associated with disease activity. However, the available analyses do not adequately demonstrate that pharmacodynamic responses are quantitatively comparable between children, adolescents and adults, nor that biomarker changes consistently translate into comparable clinical or radiological outcomes across these populations. As such, the justification for extrapolation of pharmacodynamic effects to adolescents aged 13-17 years remains insufficiently substantiated.

In view of the limitations of the cross-study comparisons and the exploratory nature of the biomarkers presented (adiponectin, NfL and MMP-9), the applicant was further requested to substantiate the extrapolation of pharmacodynamic response to adolescents (13-17 years). In particular, the applicant was asked to provide an integrated quantitative analysis demonstrating comparable exposure-biomarker relationships and biomarker-radiological outcome relationships across children, adolescents and adults, considering potential differences in baseline disease characteristics and disease stage.

The Applicant has not provided a formal integrated quantitative analysis demonstrating comparable exposure-biomarker and biomarker-radiological outcome relationships across children, adolescents and adults, mainly due to the limited availability of adolescent data. Therefore, the requested cross-age pharmacodynamic bridging has not been directly demonstrated.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant supported the extrapolation to adolescents based on adult exposure-response analyses, highlighting that expected adolescent exposures are above the adult exposure range associated with pharmacodynamic activity, and limited compassionate-use observations showing clinical and radiological stability in adolescents. The applicant addresses the restriction of extrapolation to adolescents with Gdnegative lesions by assuming that this subgroup may have a disease trajectory closer to adult cALD than to rapidly progressive childhood cALD.

However, the supporting adult exposure-response analyses are derived from ADVANCE, a study with limited efficacy outcomes. This limits the strength of the adult dataset as a basis for extrapolating efficacy or pharmacodynamic response to adolescents. Moreover, the adolescent evidence remains sparse, uncontrolled and largely observational, and no adolescent-specific exposure-biomarker or biomarker-radiological outcome relationship has been demonstrated.

Overall, while the totality of evidence could be used to argue against a pharmacodynamic extrapolation to adolescents aged 13-17 years with Gd-negative lesions, this carries important residual uncertainty. The Applicant has decided not to seek approval in adults and adolescents during assessment, proposing an indication restricted to children. Consequently, justification for extrapolation of pharmacodynamic response to adolescents aged 13-17 years was not further pursued.

## Dose selection

Furthermore, the lack of correlation raises concerns about the dose-effect relationship and the appropriateness of the selected dose range for maintaining efficacy while minimizing potential safety risks. It remains unclear whether the absence of an observed relationship reflects (i) a true lack of pharmacodynamic sensitivity within the evaluated exposure range, (ii) suboptimal study design (e.g., narrow dose range, insufficient exposure variability - although it appears to be quite wide), or (iii) limitations of the chosen efficacy endpoints to detect pharmacological activity.

The Applicant further justified the proposed paediatric exposure target based on a predefined targetexposure strategy in NEXUS and on non-clinical, translational, and clinical data. While these considerations provide additional support for dose selection and are consistent with pharmacological activity at the proposed exposure range, uncertainty remains regarding the relationship between exposure and clinically meaningful efficacy outcomes, which limits the strength of the conclusions.

## 5.2.6.2. Conclusions

## Pharmacokinetics

The PK of leriglitazone and it pharmacologically active metabolite M3 were investigated in five clinical studies and a compassionate use program; two studies in healthy male volunteers, one study in male patients with AMN, one study in male and female patients with Friedreich's Ataxia and one study in boys aged ≥2 and ≤12 years with cALD, as well as the compassionate use program following the end of the study with boys with cALD. There are uncertainties regarding the effect of food on the commercial formulation, dosing for special populations and drug-drug interactions. This are yet to be resolved as the studies are still under development or will be performed as a PAM.

## Pharmacodynamics

Issues regarding the limitations in the exposure-response evidence and the pharmacodynamic extrapolation to adolescents aged 13-17 years were not fully justified, but it was decided not to further pursue these aspects in the assessment procedure. Regarding exposure-response, uncertainty remains regarding the relationship between exposure and clinically meaningful efficacy outcomes, which limits the strength of the conclusions. However, since efficacy is demonstrated in Gd negative population, this uncertainty does not impact B/R of the product.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

## 5.3.1. Dose response studies

The results of the Applicant's sponsored pharmacology studies demonstrated a potent, dose-related effect of leriglitazone in a wide range of in vitro and in vivo models relevant to cALD at doses and/or exposures equivalent to those achieved in patients.

Classical dose finding studies were not feasible due to the low number of patients available.

The final dose selection was derived from Phase I data with the goal to achieve the highest target exposure (AUC) that would reduce the incidence and progression of the disease while maintaining an acceptable safety profile. Therefore, the selection of the target exposure and the corresponding dose was based on non-clinical in vivo disease models, the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL), and on Phase I PK data.

The target plasma exposure was then selected at 200 μg*h/mL in adults and at 170 μg*h/mL in children, respectively, to achieve the highest possible efficacious exposure while remaining within the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) determined in rat toxicity studies.

The dose to achieve the target exposure in patients was selected based on the PK findings from the Phase I study MT-1-01, which showed that as predicted in preclinical studies, in humans, similar plasma concentrations of leriglitazone provide similar PPARγ engagement and lead to similar CSF concentrations thus confirming very good PK/PD translation between preclinical and clinical data, in line with the conclusions of the SA on the dose finding approach (EMA/CHMP/SAWP/645714/2016). Moreover, at these exposure levels, leriglitazone showed a decrease on proinflammatory cytokines and chemokines in CSF.

## 5.3.2. Main study

## 5.3.2.1. NEXUS (MT-2-02)

## 5.3.2.1.1. Study title

NEXUS: Treatment of Children with cALD (Gd negative or Gd positive lesions at baseline)

## 5.3.2.1.2. Study design

NEXUS was an open-label, Phase II/III, multicentre study in male paediatric patients ≥2 and ≤12 years of age, with a diagnosis of the cALD phenotype of X-linked ALD, to assess the effects of leriglitazone treatment on disease progression prior to HSCT.

Patients were eligible for the study at the first occurrence of cerebral MRI abnormalities and were assigned into one of two groups: patients with Gd negative cerebral lesions (Population 1; Gd-) and patients presenting Gd positive lesions (Population 2; Gd+) at baseline.

The study was designed to assess disease progression at two critical timepoints: 24 weeks after inclusion of the study (interim analysis to assess study continuation) and 96 weeks (primary/confirmatory analysis).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The first assessment was conducted when up to 13 evaluable subjects reached 24 weeks of treatment with the study continuing if a minimum of 4 of 13 evaluable patients met the endpoint of 'arrested disease' or if the overall assessment from central reading suggests lesion growth deceleration ('continuation criteria'). Lesion deceleration was considered when lesion growth determined by central readers assessment was below what would be expected based on the literature and/or when lesion growth rate of the latest measure was lower than previous ones. Growth rate was defined as the increase in T2/FLAIR lesion volume since previous MRI divided by the number of months elapsed since that previous MRI.

Each subject underwent screening for a maximum of 15 days, followed by a treatment period. If the screening procedures were performed within 7 days prior to Baseline (Visit 0), all results obtained from the screening evaluations served as the baseline values, and baseline procedures performed on Day 0 prior to dosing consisted of the clinical efficacy assessment (NFS-MFD scale), blood sampling for biomarkers and leriglitazone plasma levels, and optional CSF sampling for biomarkers.

Subjects were evaluated at the Screening Visit, at the Baseline Visit when the first dose of study drug was administered, and at regular biweekly intervals thereafter until Week 12 (Visit 6). After Visit 6, evaluations occurred at Week 18 (Visit 7), Week 24 (Visit 8), Week 36 (Visit 9), Week 48 (Visit 10), Week 72 (Visit 11), Week 96 (Visit 12), and at further 24-week intervals thereafter until end-of-treatment criteria were met. The subjects were to adhere to the visit schedule until the initiation of HSCT procedures. Prior to the initiation of HSCT procedures, the subject was scheduled for another visit irrespective of the time since the Baseline Visit: the 'Visit Prior to HSCT'. The last dose of the study drug was administered one day prior to this visit. This visit was the final visit in the study for subjects undergoing HSCT.

## Study Design of NEXUS (Study MT-2-02)

<!-- image -->

GIS: gadolinium intensity score; HSCT: haematopoietic stem cell transplantation; MRI: magnetic resonance imaging; V: visit.

## Treatment

All patients were administered daily leriglitazone treatment, orally, at a starting dose dependant on body weight as determined by PBPK modelling to achieve a target plasma exposure of approximately 170 µg·h/mL (±20%).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Blood samples were obtained from patients at each study visit to monitor leriglitazone plasma concentrations to determine potential dose adjustments to achieve the target plasma exposure range.

Patients received leriglitazone in addition to the option of SoC HSCT, which was possible at any timepoint during the study if subjects met the HSCT criteria [i) GIS of 1, 2, or 3, or ii) Significant T2/FLAIR lesion growth as assessed by central reading, present at two consecutive MRIs post baseline at least 12 weeks apart from each other].

## Patient population

Patients aged 2-12 years were eligible for the study at the first occurrence of cerebral MRI abnormalities.

Patients were eligible to receive study treatment if they had a diagnosis of X-ALD based on genetic testing, or, in absence of genetic testing, elevation of VLCFA and confirmed by family history of X-ALD with clinical symptoms and elevation of VLCFA or by genetic testing of a family member; white matter involvement in MRI [without Gd positive brain lesions at baseline (Population 1) or with Gd positive brain lesions as baseline (Population 2)], MFD score of 0, baseline Loes score &gt;0 and &lt;10, and baseline GSI &lt;3.

The study was conducted in Europe.

## 5.3.2.1.3. Objectives and estimands

## Primary objective

The primary objective of the study was to evaluate whether leriglitazone can arrest progression of cALD at Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT as determined by serial clinical and MRI investigations in paediatric subjects.

'Arrested disease' was defined using the following parameters:

1. Change in NFS from baseline ≤1 point (Week 24) or ≤5 points (Week 96)
2. Free of MFD
3. Lack of lesion progression on MRI, fulfilling the following:
4. o No conversion to Gd positive lesions (Population 1) defined as GIS score of 0.
5. o Disappearance of persistent Gd positive lesions (Population 2) defined as change in GIS score from 1, 2, or 3 to 0. Persistent Gd positive lesions were defined as those present in ≥ 2 consecutive MRIs spanning a minimum of 6 months.
6. o No significant growth of T2/FLAIR lesions compared to the previous MRI (both populations) as assessed by central reading.

## Estimands for the primary objective
