## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on primary estimands

For the purposes of analysis, the following analysis sets were defined.

- Safety Analysis Set (SAS): All subjects who took at least 1 dose (partial or complete) of leriglitazone.
- Modified Intent-to-Treat (mITT) Analysis Set: All subjects who took at least 1 dose (partial or complete) of leriglitazone and had at least 1 post-baseline MRI assessment with Gd. The mITT was further broken down into subjects with Gd negative lesions at baseline (Population 1) subjects with Gd positive lesions at baseline (Population 2).
- Evaluable Set (ES): Subjects were considered 'evaluable' if they provided enough data to assess 'arrested disease', and to assess 'arrested disease', ≥ 2 consecutive MRIs spanning a minimum of 12 weeks on post-baseline are needed'.
- Pharmacokinetic Analysis Set (PKS): All subjects who took at least 1 dose (partial or complete) of leriglitazone and had at least 1 plasma sample with leriglitazone concentration. Protocol deviations that may have affected the analysis (e.g., incomplete dose or sample out of the window) were evaluated on a case-by-case basis, and profiles or individual time points could be excluded from summaries and analysis.
- Pharmacodynamics Analysis Set (PDS): All subjects who took at least 1 dose (partial or complete) of leriglitazone and had at least 1 post-baseline measurable biomarker concentration available in plasma or CSF. Protocol deviations that may have affected the analysis (for example, incomplete dose or sample out of the window) were evaluated on a case-by-case basis, and profiles or individual time points could be excluded from summaries and analysis. The PDS was further broken down into Population 1, which included subjects without Gd-enhancing cerebral lesions at baseline and Population 2, which included subjects presenting Gd-enhancing lesions at baseline.

All efficacy analyses related to 'arrested disease' were performed on the ES and all other efficacy analyses were performed using the mITT analysis set.

The study was considered successful if a minimum of 5 evaluable subjects met all 'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 or the visit prior to HSCT, except for MFD where &gt;70% of all evaluable subjects at Week 96 had to be free of MFD. Thus, primary efficacy was evaluated using co-primary endpoints:

- Co-Primary Endpoint 1: Arrested disease at Week 96 or Visit Prior to HSCT not including being free of MFD.
- Co-Primary Endpoint 2: Arrested disease at Week 96 or Visit Prior to HSCT including being free of MFD.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- These endpoints were tested in a hierarchical manner, where Co-Primary Endpoint 1 was first assessed using all evaluable subjects, then Co-Primary Endpoint 2 was assessed using the subset of evaluable subjects that met Co-Primary Endpoint 1.

At Week 24, the number of subjects meeting 'arrested disease' and the continuation criteria were assessed.

No decisions in terms of efficacy were made at this timepoint based on evaluation of 'arrested disease'. 'Arrested disease' at Week 24 was descriptively summarised, including 95% CI and graphical representations, by Population 1, Population 2, and Overall.

The study continued if the one-sided 95% CI for the proportion of evaluable subjects meeting continuation criteria at Week 24, i.e., meeting 'arrested disease' or lesion growth deceleration at Week 24, as per overall assessment from central reading was above 10%.

To evaluate the continuation condition, the following endpoints were presented:

- Number of subjects meeting 'arrested disease' at Week 24;
- Number of subjects meeting lesion growth deceleration at Week 24;
- Number of subjects meeting 'continuation criteria' at Week 24.

## Secondary objectives

Secondary objectives included assessment of changes in neurological function (NFS-MFD scale), assessment of lesion progression (Loes score and GIS), overall survival and number of patients meeting per protocol HSCT criteria.

The study also assessed the PK, safety, tolerability and palatability of leriglitazone.

## Estimands for the secondary objectives

The secondary efficacy endpoints include:

- Sustained change from baseline in the score composed of NFS items (1 - hearing/auditory processing, 2 - aphasia/apraxia, 4 - vision impairment, 10 - spastic gait and 13 - incontinence),
- Sustained change from baseline in total NFS score:
- 'Sustained change' was defined as the same total score for NFS items 1, 2, 4, 10, and 13 and no change &gt;1 in NFS total score observed in two consecutive Visits 6 to 8 and Visits 11 to 12, respectively. If 'sustained change' definition was not met, the average of the two scores of Visits 6 and 8, and Visits 11 and 12, respectively. If NFS total score differed by 1 point between Visits 6 to 8 or Visits 11 to 12, the higher of the two scores was used.
- Change from baseline for observed scores of NFS items 1, 2, 4, 10, and 13, and for NFS total score were also descriptively summarised for each visit without applying the 'sustained change' rules.
- Change from baseline in Loes MRI severity score
- Change from baseline in GIS
- Overall survival of patients who had not undergone HSCT

Number of patients meeting HSCT criteria.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on the secondary estimands

Each of the secondary endpoints was descriptively summarised, as well as CI, and graphical representations (mean values or proportions over time, or Kaplan-Meier [K-M] curves in the case of time to event endpoints). All secondary endpoints were descriptively summarised by Population 1, Population 2, and Overall.

Time to first Gd enhancement (change from GIS = 0 to GIS = 1, 2, or 3; Population 1 only) and time to onset of HSCT was assessed using the K-M method. Time to persistent Gd enhancement (change from GIS = 0 to GIS = 1, 2, or 3 in 2 consecutive MRIs spanning a minimum of 6 months.

in total, Population 1 only), overall survival of patients who have not undergone HSCT and time to onset of HSCT was assessed using the K-M method.

## Exploratory objectives

Exploratory objectives included assessment of lesion volume, white matter fibre structure, other cerebral assessments and plasma and CSF biomarkers.

## Estimands for the Exploratory objectives

Exploratory endpoints included:

- Change from baseline in T2/FLAIR lesion volume
- Lesion volume fold change slope, estimated linearly for each individual under treatment, over 98 weeks of follow-up
- MFD-free survival compared to natural history
- Change from baseline in white matter fibre structure as determined by FA and/or apparent diffusion coefficient (ADC) on DTI
- Change from baseline in cerebral blood flow (CBF) and cerebral blood volume (CBV), capillary transit time heterogeneity (CTH), and constant of permeability (K2 and Kapp) as determined by DSC MRI Changes from baseline in the levels of each biomarker in plasma and CSF (optional).
- Additional analyses were conducted based on updated methodology for assessment of early lesions in paediatric cALD with Gd negative and Gd positive lesions using MRI volumetric evaluation, which is a more objective measure for disease progression in contrast to the semi-quantitative MRI assessment of cerebral disease evaluation using the Loes score.

The objectives of these additional analyses were to compare NEXUS patients to natural history data on MRI volumetric data regarding lesion growth from baseline over 96 weeks and MFD-free survival data.

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on the tertiary estimands

Additional calculations were derived for normalised lesion volume, fold change from baseline, fold change from baseline per month, growth rate [velocity (cc/month)], and acceleration (cc/month 2 ). Each of these was descriptively summarised by population (1 and 2) and overall, as well as the 95% CIs presented. Where applicable, they were compared with natural history data from literature. In addition, the mean (95% CI) change from baseline over time was presented using line/dot (whiskers) plot for each measure.

Growth rate (velocity) was defined as the increase in T2/FLAIR lesion volume since previous MRI divided by the number of months elapsed since that previous MRI. Assessment of growth rate was based on the data obtained at the specified visits that were at least 12 weeks (±15 days) apart from each other: Baseline Visit, Visit 6 (Week 12), and Visit 8 (Week 24), Visit 9 (Week 36), Visit 10 (Week 48), Visit 11 (Week 72), Visit 12 (Week 96), and further visits, as applicable. If after Visit 10, an MRI was performed at an unscheduled visit or visit prior to HSCT, the growth rate would only be evaluated at this intermediate timepoint if at least 12 weeks (±15 days) had elapsed from the previous visit.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Acceleration was defined as the change in growth rate (velocity) divided by the number of months elapsed. Assessment of acceleration was performed if at least 2 valid assessments of velocity had been obtained.

## 5.3.2.1.4. Results

## Participant flow and numbers analysed

Twenty-seven subjects were screened, with 4 screen failures.

A total of 23 subjects were enrolled in the study, and all 23 subjects received at least one dose (partial or complete) of leriglitazone. These subjects were included in the SAS.

Twenty-three subjects had at least one post-baseline MRI assessment with Gd and were included in the mITT analysis set.

Of these 23 subjects: 9 had Gd negative brain lesions (Population 1) and 14 had Gd positive lesions (Population 2) at baseline.

Three patients were excluded from the evaluable set because they exited the study prior to Week 24 to undergo HSCT procedures and thus could not provide the required data to assess arrested disease (≥2 consecutive MRIs spanning a minimum of 12 weeks post-baseline).

This resulted in the evaluable set (ES) comprising 20 patients (9 from Population 1 and 11 from Population 2). The primary efficacy results are presented for this set.

During the study, fourteen (14) subjects proceeded to undergo HSCT or gene therapy after receiving at least one dose of leriglitazone.

One subject () continued in the study past 96 weeks of treatment but did not attend the Week 96 visit, this is why 9 patients were considered as ongoing at Week 96, but efficacy data is only available for 8 subjects at Week 96. On the other hand, for this subject the Visit Prior to HSCT was completed by phone, therefore efficacy assessments were not performed at that visit either. Solely for assessment of the primary endpoint and of HSCT criteria, data from the last available efficacy assessment (performed at Week 72) was considered for Subject. At that timepoint, the subject was not meeting the condition of 'arrested disease'.

## Outcomes and estimation

At Week 96 or Visit Prior to HSCT, of 20 evaluable subjects, 7 (35%) subjects met the 'arrested disease' criteria not including being free of MFD (95% CI: 15.4, 100, p=0.0024). Of these 7 subjects, all subjects (100%) met the 'arrested disease' criteria, including being free of MFD (95% CI: 59.0, 100, p=&lt;0.0001). Thus, the primary endpoint of the study was met, with 35% of subjects meeting

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT, representing a statistically significant improvement over the 10% rate of disease arrest expected in natural history patients. All subjects remained clinically stable for the entire study follow-up.

Of the 20 subjects included in the ES, the moment of 'arrested disease' criteria were at the Week 96 study visit for 8 patients and at the visit prior to HSCT for the remaining 12. A majority of subjects (6/9 [66.7%] subjects) with Gd negative cerebral lesions at baseline (Population 1) met the criteria for 'arrested disease' at Week 96, while 1/11 (9.1%) subject with Gd positive cerebral lesions at baseline (Population 2) met 'arrested disease' criteria at the visit prior to HSCT. All subjects in the ES remained clinically stable at Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT; 20 (100%) subjects had a change of ≤ 1 from baseline in NFS and were free of MFD. Therefore, whether a subject met the 'arrested disease' criteria was driven by whether lesion progression was observed on MRI assessment, determined by central readers who assessed Loes score and GIS, performed segmentation for volumetrics analyses, and provided an expert assessment of lesion volumetrics compared with the natural history data.

- Overall, 14 (70.0%) of 20 evaluable subjects had no significant T2/FLAIR lesion growth at Week 96 or the visit prior to HSCT, including 7 (77.8%) of 9 subjects in Population 1 and 7 (63.6%) of 11 subjects in Population 2.
- Overall, 7 (35.0%) of 20 evaluable subjects had no Gd enhancement at Week 96 or the visit prior to HSCT; a majority of evaluable subjects in Population 1 reported no conversion to Gd positive lesions (6 [66.7%] of 9 subjects), while 1 (9.1%) of 11 subjects in Population 2 showed disappearance of persistent Gd positive lesions.

Of the 12 subjects in the ES that were assessed for 'arrested disease' criteria at the visit prior to HSCT, 3 had Gd negative cerebral lesions at baseline (Population 1), and 9 subjects were Gd positive at baseline (Population 2). All 12 (100%) subjects were free of MFD and had a change from baseline in NFS score ≤ 1 at the visit prior to HSCT. However, a majority (11 [91.7%]) of these subjects demonstrated lesion progression on MRI assessment and were proposed for HSCT. The 3 patients with Gd negative cerebral lesions at baseline progressed to Gd positive lesions with 2 of them also having significant growth in T2/FLAIR lesions. Of the 9 patients Gd positive at baseline, 8 remained Gd positive and 7 (77.8%) demonstrated no significant T2/FLAIR lesions growth at the visit prior to HSCT.

In summary, clinically meaningful results were observed across all criteria informing 'arrested disease' at Week 96 or visit prior to HSCT, including NFS, MFD, lesion progression, Gd enhancement, and lesion growth.

- 20 out of 20 patients (100%) of the evaluable patient population remained clinically stable with a change of ≤ 1 from baseline in NFS and were free of MFD;
- 14 of the 20 patients (70%) of the evaluable patient population had no significant lesion growth; and
- 7 of 20 patients (35%) of the evaluable patient population showed no significant lesion growth and no Gd enhancement, including one patient who was initially Gd positive and no longer showed Gd enhancement.

## Source: CSR MT-2-02 In-text Table 14.2.1.2.1

Overall, in 8 subjects assessed at Week 96, 6 (75.0%) subjects showed no signs of accelerated lesion growth compared to untreated natural history and were assessed as having no significant lesion growth. All but 1 of these 6 subjects (Subject 103001) demonstrated stable lesions with no further growth; Subject 103001 demonstrated non-significant lesion growth below what would be expected in natural history. The 2 subjects showing accelerating growth at or above that expected in natural history at Week 96 had Gd positive lesions at baseline (Population 2).

For subjects with a Visit Prior to HSCT (14 subjects), most (11 [78.6%]) of these subjects were from Population 2. Overall, 9 (64.3%) of the 14 subjects showed no significant lesion growth at the visit prior to HSCT; 5 of these subjects had lesion growth below what would be expected in untreated natural history and the remaining 4 subjects had no lesion growth (fold change &lt;1.2 since their previous MRI). In the 5 (35.7%) of 14 subjects demonstrating significant lesion growth (progression according to or above untreated natural history), 2 were in Population 1 and 3 were in Population 2.

Subjects meeting 'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 continued to remain clinically and radiologically stable at all study visits beyond Week 96. Of these 6 subjects, 5 were evaluated at Week 120. All 5 subjects continued to show no significant growth, with 4 subjects having stable lesions with no growth and 1 subject demonstrating lesion growth below what would be expected in natural history. Two of these subjects were assessed beyond Week 120, and both continued to show stable lesions with no significant growth at all visits at the time of data cutoff.

## Sustained Change from Baseline in Neurological Function Score (Total NFS Subscore - Items 1, 2, 4, 10 and 13; Total NFS score)

Clinical function was assessed by the NFS scale. This scale rates 15 domains of neurological status that are most affected in cALD; 6 of these domains rate the most serious symptoms, summarised as MFDs. The NFS items that were categorised as MFDs were cortical blindness, loss of communication, no voluntary movement, total incontinence, tube feeding, and wheelchair dependence.

At Week 24 for the Overall Population, mean (SD) sustained change from baseline was 0.1 (0.31) for total NFS score and 0.0 (0.11) for the total NFS subscore in 20 evaluated subjects. Mean sustained

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

change from baseline was similar between Population 1 and Population 2 for both total NFS score (0.1 in both populations) and total NFS subscore (0.1 in Population 1 vs 0.0 in Population 2).

At Week 96, mean (SD) sustained change from baseline in the Overall Population was 0.1 (0.35) for the total NFS score and 0.0 (0.00) for the total NFS subscore in 8 evaluated subjects. While a greater mean sustained change from baseline in total NFS score was observed in Population 2 (0.5) compared to Population 1 (0.0), it should be noted that NFS scores were available for only 2 subjects in Population 2 at Week 96. There were no differences between mean sustained change from baseline in total NFS subscore between populations at Week 96.

No subjects showed a change &gt;1 in total NFS score or total NFS subscore.

All subjects remained clinically stable throughout the study, remained MFD-free, and all subjects had changes from baseline of total NFS ≤ 1.

## Change from Baseline in Loes MRI Severity Score

At baseline, mean (SD) Loes score was 1.98 (1.03) in the Overall Population. Mean (SD) scores were similar in Population 1 and Population 2 (2.00 [0.83] vs 1.96 [1.17], respectively) and individual scores ranged from 1.0 to 3.0 in Population 1 and 0.5 to 4.5 in Population 2.

Lower mean (SD) increases from baseline were observed in Population 1 compared to Population 2 at Week 12 (0.11 [0.22] vs 0.46 [0.99]), Week 24 (0.28 [0.36] vs 0.86 [1.25]), and Week 36 (0.44 [0.53] vs 0.78 [0.97]), while similar mean (SD) increases from baseline were observed in Population 1 and Population 2 at Week 48 (0.56 [0.78] vs 0.58 [0.67]).

At Week 96, mean (SD) Loes score in the Overall Population was 2.69 [1.51], reflecting a mean (SD) change from baseline of 0.63 [1.09]. In the 6 subjects in Population 1 evaluable at Week 96, the mean (SD) score was 2.17 (0.82) with a mean (SD) increase from baseline of 0.08 (0.20), compared to mean (SD) 4.25 (2.48) in 2 evaluable subjects in Population 2 (mean [SD] increase of 2.25 [1.06] from baseline).

In subjects undergoing HSCT, mean (SD) Loes scores were similar across Population 1 (3.83 [0.29] in 3 subjects) and Population 2 (3.50 [2.54] in 11 subjects) at the Visit Prior to HSCT.

Stabilisation of Loes score was observed in most subjects through Week 48, with a plateau in mean change from baseline observed in both populations. Separation between populations was observed after Week 48 as the number of subjects in Population 2 evaluated at later study visits began to decrease, but the plateau in mean change from baseline was maintained in Population 1 through Week 192.

By-subject Loes scores are presented for each population in the figure below.

## Change from Baseline in Gadolinium Intensity Score (GIS)

In the Overall Population, mean (SD) GIS at baseline was 0.82 (0.853). By definition, all subjects with Gd negative lesions at baseline in Population 1 had a GIS of 0 at baseline, while subjects with Gd positive lesions at baseline in Population 2 had a mean (SD) of 1.29 (0.726); GIS in Population 2 ranged from 1 to 3 at baseline.

Three subjects in Population 1 reported initial conversion to Gd positive lesions at Week 24 and proceeded to undergo HSCT; 6 of the 9 subjects in Population 1 remained at a GIS of 0 at Week 24, which was in contrast to prior studies demonstrating that half of Gd negative natural history patients become Gd positive within 6 months of diagnosis. One subject in Population 1 showed transient Gd enhancement. Subject 103004 first converted to a Gd positive lesion at Week 36. This subject continued to show Gd positive lesions at Week 48 and an unscheduled visit on Study Day 393, before converting back to Gd negative lesions at Week 72. Subject 103004 continued to be Gd- through Week 144 (last visit prior to data cut-off).

In Population 2, mean GIS scores were static at Weeks 4 and 12 and mean decreases from baseline were observed at Week 24 (-0.27) and Week 36 (-0.11). The only mean increase from baseline in Population 2 was observed at Week 48 (0.20). Two subjects in Population 2 did not show Gd positive lesions at Week 24: Subject 203001 converted from a GIS of 1 at baseline to a GIS of 0 at Week 12 through Week 48 before proceeding to HSCT, and Subject 103010 converted from a GIS of 1 at baseline to a GIS of 0 at Week 12 through Week 24, then back to a GIS of 1 at Week 36 before proceeding onto HSCT. In the 11 subjects in Population 2 evaluated at the visit prior to HSCT, mean (SD) GIS was 1.18 (0.751) with scores ranging from 0 to 3, reflecting a mean (SD) decrease from baseline of -0.18 (0.405). No subjects in Population 2 reported a sustained worsening of GIS. In the 2 subjects that reported GIS &gt;1 at baseline, Subject 602001 (GIS 3 at baseline) decreased to GIS 2 by Week 24 before proceeding into HSCT, while Subject 602004 remained static at GIS 3 from baseline until HSCT following Week 36. These results are in contrast to HSCT, where resolution of Gd enhancement is seen typically after 1 month. The fact that leriglitazone and HSCT based treatments have a different impact on Gd enhancement while impact on both lesion growth and clinical progression is similar, is explained by the differences in the mode of action. While both modalities have an impact on pathways critical for lesion growth and hence clinical progression (e.g., inflammation), HSCT also results in a replacement of the endothelial cells that form the BBB which is reflected by a reconstitution of the BBB and rapid conversion to Gd negative. These results also reflect that while Gd enhancement is a critical predictor of lesion growth in untreated patients, in patients treated with leriglitazone persistence of Gd enhancement does not necessarily predict further lesion growth.

A Kaplan-Meier (K-M) estimate for time to first persistent Gd enhancement in Population 1 was provided. The K-M estimate for the proportion of subjects in Population 1 with persistent Gd enhancement by Week 24 was 33.3% (95% CI: 12.2, 71.8); which remained unchanged by Week 96. In the natural history patients, the median K-M estimate of time from diagnosis to lesional enhancement was 6.0 months (95% CI: 3.6, 17.8), and by 18 months, 81.5% (95% CI: 53.0, 92.7) of these patients were estimated to have Gd positive cerebral lesions.

## Overall Survival of Subjects Who Have Not Undergone HSCT

No subjects died while on treatment with leriglitazone, including those with follow-up beyond 96 weeks. A K-M plot of overall survival in mITT compared to natural history (figure below) shows separation of survival curves as early as 6 weeks following treatment and continued to increase through Week 96, where survival probability for untreated natural history patients was approximately 80%. Of note, NH data used for this comparison was not perfectly comparable with the MT-2-02 study population as patients had a significant higher disease burden at baseline.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 8: Kaplan-Meier Estimates for Overall Survival vs Natural History Untreated Cohort - Modified Intent-to-Treat

<!-- image -->

## Number of Subjects Meeting HSCT Criteria

During the study, transplant decision was taken for 15 patients who underwent the corresponding visit prior to HSCT (3 in Population 1, 12 in Population 2). Out of the 15 patients, 12 patients had the visit prior to HSCT at ≥ 24 weeks (3 Population 1, 9 Population 2), while for 3 this was prior week 24 (all Population 2).

At Week 24, 9 (45.0%) of 20 evaluable subjects met HSCT criteria. All 9 of these subjects were from Population 2.

In the 12 evaluable subjects that completed the Visit Prior to HSCT, 11 (91.7%) met the HSCT criteria at the visit prior to HSCT, 3 from Population 1 and 8 from Population 2. Nine (75.0%) subjects met the criteria due to persistent gadolinium enhancement but did not show significant lesion growth at two consecutive visits. Only 2 (16.7%) subjects met both components of the HSCT criteria, 1 in each population. 1 patient from Population 2 was transplanted without meeting HSCT criteria, which means this patient remained clinically stable and, lesions were stable and Gd negative.

All patients who underwent HSCT remained clinically stable at the Visit Prior to HSCT.

## Exploratory analyses

## Lesion Volumetrics

At baseline, mean (SD) lesion volume (cc) was lower in Population 1 (2.469 [2.6143] cc) than in Population 2 (4.203 [7.2477] cc). Mean increases from baseline were observed in both populations at all visits through Week 48, about 5 times greater in Population 2 (5.295 cc) than in Population 1 (0.937 cc). At Week 72 onwards, in the decreasing number of subjects that remained on treatment, Population 2 continued to show substantial increases from baseline in mean lesion volume, while mean decreases from baseline were observed at Weeks 72 to 192 in Population 1.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 9: By-Subject Lesion Volume Change from Baseline - Modified Intent-to-Treat

<!-- image -->

Source: CSR MT-2-02 In-text Figure 18

Monthly lesion volume fold change from baseline remained below 1.2 at all study visits for Population 1. Mean monthly fold change was &gt;1 at Weeks 12 to 48 but then dropped below 1 at Week 72 (0.9972) and remained less than 1 at all study visits through Week 192 except for Week 120 (1.0006). For Population 2, the mean monthly fold change was &gt;1 at all study visits but was &gt;1.2 only at Week 12. At the visit prior to HSCT, mean (SD) monthly fold changes were similar in Populations 1 and 2 (1.1870 [0.08073] vs 1.1643 [0.12039]). Most subjects showed reduction in monthly fold changes over time, reflecting lesion deceleration. A plateau profile was observed for both Population 1 and Population 2; the fold change from baseline was below that expected in the natural history from Mallack and colleagues.

Accelerating lesion growth was observed in Population 1 (mean [SD]: 0.0038 [0.14418] cc/month 2 ) at Week 24, while Population 2 demonstrated initial decelerating lesion growth at Weeks 12, 24, and 36. Overall and in both Population 1 and Population 2, most subjects on treatment with leriglitazone demonstrated lesion growth acceleration below that expected in untreated natural history patients.

DTI measurements were used to assess white matter integrity. Treatment with leriglitazone is neuroprotective and preserves lesional structural integrity. The cALD lesions in treated subjects do not progress to liquefactive necrosis or non-viable tissue, and the cALD involved in brain tissue preserves its metabolic demand and remains viable. Treatment with leriglitazone improved microcirculatory blood flow, as evidenced by a reduction in capillary flow heterogeneity (measured as relative transit time heterogeneity and a decrease in lesional BBB permeability during the early treatment phase, two markers previously correlated with the inflammatory activity of cALD lesions. In addition, distant, normal appearing white matter, which is not directly affected by cALD lesions, also exhibits improved microcirculatory blood flow suggesting that leriglitazone may exert widespread neurovascular benefits across the entire brain.

The estimated probability of remaining MFD-free for subjects on leriglitazone was 100% at all timepoints, compared to approximately 80%, 70%, and 55% chances of remaining MFD-free at Weeks 24, 48, and 96 for untreated natural history patients as it is illustrated below in the K-M plot of time to MFD for the mITT compared to untreated natural history.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 10: Kaplan-Meier Estimate for Time to MFD vs Untreated Natural History

<!-- image -->

MFD: major functional disabilities.

Source: CSR MT-2-02 In-text Figure 26

When looking at Population 2 compared with Gd positive natural history patients, the difference in likelihood of remaining MFD was even greater, especially at Week 96 (100% in Population 2 compared with ~75%, ~70%, and ~30% at Weeks 24, 48, and 96, respectively).

<!-- image -->

MFD: major functional disabilities.

Source: CSR MT-2-02 In-text Figure 27

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A comparison of volumetric MRI data from the mITT to a cohort of untreated natural history patients from published data was made.

Mean (SD) age in the mITT group (7.70 [2.93] years) was higher than in the natural history group (5.83 [1.86] years). Mean (SD) lesion volumes were similar at baseline between mITT and natural history (3.52 [5.85] cc vs 3.47 [7.05] cc, respectively) but mean (SD) Loes score at baseline was slightly higher in the mITT (1.98[1.03]) than in natural history (1.60 [1.41]) and a greater proportion of subjects in the mITT had Gd positive lesions at baseline compared to natural history (60.9% vs 45.8%).

At the time of the pre-planned Week 24 interim analysis, 15 subjects had at least one post-baseline MRI assessment with Gd and were included in the mITT analysis set, while at time 11 patients were considered evaluable.

Of the 20 subjects evaluable at W96, 17 (85.0%) (95% CI: 62.1, 96.8) had met the continuation criteria at W24. Seven subjects (35%, 95% CI: 15.4, 59.2) met the arrested disease criteria at Week 24, and 17 (85.0%) showed lesion deceleration.

The primary endpoint was met in each of the sensitivity analyses performed, reflecting a statistically significant improvement over the 10% rate of disease arrest expected in natural history patients.

## 5.3.3. Clinical studies in special populations

Not applicable.

## 5.3.4. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for efficacy

Not applicable.

## 5.3.5. Supportive studies

ADVANCE was a placebo-controlled study (randomisation 2:1) designed to assess the efficacy and safety of leriglitazone in adult male ALD patients with evidence of spinal cord involvement (AMN; adrenomyeloneuropathy) and with or without brain lesions (i.e., cALD) but without evidence of progressive cALD at baseline, i.e., patients with Gd positive lesions were excluded for participation.

The study consisted of a 96-week placebo-controlled double-blind phase followed by a 3-year OLE with all patients under leriglitazone treatment. A total of 116 patients were randomised 2:1 (77 to leriglitazone and 39 to placebo). At baseline, 27% of the population enrolled had Gd negative brain lesions, thus had cALD.

Figure 12: Design of Study MT-2-01

<!-- image -->

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The study assessed the efficacy and safety of leriglitazone treatment, including screening and baseline visits, and after daily leriglitazone administration for 96 weeks.

Patients who completed the 96-week double-blind treatment phase and had the option to enter the OLE study (Part 2), at the end of the double-blind period in which placebo patients crossed over to leriglitazone treatment. Ninety-six (96) patients completed the double-blind treatment phase (Part 1), and 88 patients (92%) continued in an OLE (MT-2-01 OLE Part 2).

The starting dose was 150 mg leriglitazone hydrochloride administered once daily, corresponding to a volume of 10 mL of leriglitazone or placebo oral suspension. Dosing was adjusted on an individual patient basis, based on PK data obtained up to 12 weeks after first dose, to achieve leriglitazone target plasma exposure of 200 µg·h/mL.

In order to participate, patients had to be male, ≥18 to ≤65 years of age (or ≥18 to ≤60 years in France) with diagnosis of ALD based on genetic testing and clinical evidence of spinal cord involvement (AMN), and with or without cerebral abnormalities (except Gd positive lesions). Inclusion criteria #7 (either a normal brain MRI or a type-1 through type-5 pattern MRI abnormality in which the abnormality did not show presence of inflammation) was intended to allow participation in the study to any patient with the MRI abnormalities characteristic of this population but excluding those with gadolinium enhancing (Gd positive) lesions.

The primary endpoint, the change from baseline to Week 96 in total walking distance in the six-minute walk test (6MWT). While this primary endpoint is sensitive in a population with myelopathy, it has no clinical relevance for assessing cerebral lesion development and progression in patients with cALD. The results for the primary and secondary endpoints related to myelopathy are not discussed in this dossier as they are not considered important for the cALD discussion.

Pre-specified cALD specific secondary outcome measures to assess cerebral lesion progression were the incidence of cerebral lesion progression and Loes severity score, which were reported by central MRI readers:

Incidence of cerebral lesion progression based on cerebral MRI was defined as:

- i. Incidence of Gd positive lesions
- ii. Growth of existing Gd negative lesions since Screening or Baseline
- iii. Occurrence of new Gd negative lesions after Screening or Baseline.

Clinical endpoints relevant to the cALD population, which were evaluated as secondary endpoints at Week 96, were the EDSS, Clinical Global Impression-Improvement (CGI-I), CGI-Severity (CGI-S), Patient Global Impression-Improvement (PGI-I), and European Quality of Life 5 Dimensions (EQ-5D5L). In addition, progressive cALD was reported as a serious adverse event (SAE), if clinically diagnosed by the investigators during the study as per standard of care.

The exploratory objectives of the study were to evaluate the effects of leriglitazone on various biochemical markers in plasma such as NfL and MMP-9.

FAS was used for all efficacy endpoints which was regarded as the primary analysis. The statistical analysis was repeated based on the PPS population.

A total of 136 patients were screened, and 116 patients were randomised (2:1). Twenty (20) patients were screening failures, none of whom were rescreened. Of the 116 patients who were enrolled (FAS), 77 were randomised to receive leriglitazone and 39 patients were randomised to receive placebo. Of the 116 randomised patients, 96 patients (82.8%) completed the double-blind treatment period and 20 patients (17.2%) prematurely discontinued from treatment. The most frequent reasons for premature treatment discontinuation were adverse events reported for 2 patients (5.1%) in the placebo group compared with 8 patients (10.4%) in the leriglitazone group, and withdrawal of consent reported for 3 patients (7.7%) in the placebo group and 6 patients (7.8%) in the leriglitazone group. The per protocol set (PPS) included all patients in the FAS who did not prematurely discontinue and had no other major protocol deviations (n=92).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

SAFETY SET (SS) includes all patients who were administered at least one dose (partial or complete) of study medication (leriglitazone or placebo). Patients were analysed according to treatment received. The SS was used for the presentation of the baseline characteristics and the analysis of safety endpoints.

FULL ANALYSIS SET (FAS) includes all patients in the safety set, i.e., all randomised patients with at least one intake of study medication were included in the FAS. Patients in this set were used for all efficacy analyses. Patients were analysed as randomised (intention-to-treat). Note: The replacement of a missing post-baseline assessment is described in SAP MT-2-01 Section 6.3.

PER PROTOCOL SET (PPS) includes all patients in the FAS without a major protocol deviation as determined during the blinded review meeting (BRM).

Demographic and baseline characteristics were similar in the two treatment groups including height, weight, and body mass index (BMI). The age ranged from 19 years to 66 years overall.

Ot the 116 randomized, 31 had Gd negative lesions at baseline (18 in the leriglitazone group, 13 in the placebo group). In addition, although patients with Gd positive lesions were meant to be ineligible, 2 patients were erroneously enrolled (1 in each treatment group). A slightly higher baseline Loes observed in the placebo group compared to the leriglitazone group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Incidence of cerebral lesion progression, which was defined as appearance of Gd positive lesions and/or appearance or growth of Gd negative lesions together with Loes scoring were rated by two central readers blinded to treatment.

A total of 11 patients showed cerebral lesion progression during the double-blind phase of the study. In the placebo arm, this affected 8 out of the 39 patients (20.5%) while in the active arm, it only affected 3 out of 77 patients (3.9%) (proportion difference of -0.17, Newcombe-Wilson CI of proportion difference -0.32 to -0.05; p=0.007, two-sided Fisher's exact test). Moreover, only patients in the placebo arm developed post-baseline Gd positive lesions (4 patients; 10.2%)

The patients in the placebo group showed a higher mean (SE) increase in Loes score (0.696 [0.202]) when compared with patients treated with leriglitazone (0.114 [0.151]) and the difference was nominally significant (LS means difference [SE] of -0.582 [0.254]; 95% CI 1.088, -0.077; p=0.024, analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) with baseline Loes as covariate). Such difference is mostly driven by the patients developing progressive cALD in the placebo arm. These results are closely aligned with those from incidence of cerebral lesion progression.

Similarly, NfL levels only showed increased in the placebo arm with an overall increase significantly larger than in the active arm (p &lt;0.05).

Although patients with Gd positive lesions at baseline were not eligible for study participation per exclusion criteria, two patients presenting with Gd positive lesions at baseline were erroneously enrolled - one randomised in the placebo group and one randomised in the active group. The patient randomised to leriglitazone remained stable up to Week 90 when he withdraws consent, with an only a minimal increase of Loes score (from of 10.5 to 11) at Week 90. The patient receiving placebo had disease progression both in terms of Loes score (increase from 9.5 to 15 in 96 weeks) and NfL elevation until the patient dropped out at Week 96 due to withdrawal of consent.

Neurological disability was measured by EDSS, a composite from scoring on 8 domains: visual, brainstem, pyramidal, cerebellar, sensory, bowel and bladder, cerebral functions and ambulation. Leriglitazone showed favourable trends when compared to placebo (LS mean difference -0.34; 95% CI -0.72, 0.05; p=0.085, ANCOVA)

Clinician-based Assessment of Global Improvement (CGI-I) results showed a trend in different distributions across the seven categories (p=0.093, Wilcoxon rank sum test) which favoured leriglitazone, with only patients in the active arm showing improvement at Week 96 (proportion difference 6.5%; 95% CI -0.13, 0.25; p=0.166, Fisher's exact test).

The results of the Clinical Global Impression - Severity Scale (CGI-S) showed a trend in different distributions across the seven categories (p=0.036, Wilcoxon rank sum test). However, differences already existed at baseline and a similar percentage of patients in each treatment group showed worsening from baseline (leriglitazone 20/77, 26.0%, placebo 10/39, 25.6%; proportion difference 0.3%; 95% CI -0.19, 0.19; p=1.0, Fisher's exact test).

A numerical trend favouring leriglitazone was also observed in the EQ-5D-5L Health Scale Index Value consistent with the observations on the other endpoints previously described (LS mean difference 0.043; 95% CI -0.009, 0.095; p=0.106).

Progressive cALD was reported as an SAE, if clinically diagnosed by the investigator during the study. The 6 reported cases with progressive cALD were all in the placebo arm (6 out of 39; 15.4%). Even though this was not a pre-defined efficacy outcome, it represents a meaningful difference among treatment arms (p=0.001; two-sided Fisher's exact test). When these results are compared with those from 'cerebral lesion progression' reported by central readers it needs to be taken into account that central readers reported all patients showing radiological changes even if such changes were small.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

This differs from the standard of care criteria used by treating physicians to diagnose progressive cALD, which requires clinically meaningful changes in the MRI, and explains why 11 patients showed radiological changes while 6 patients were diagnosed with progressive cALD.

Out of these 6 patients that developed clinically progressive cALD during the double-blind phase, at baseline, 3 patients did not have brain lesions at baseline (5010-07, 8010-07, 8020-06) while the other three had Gd negative lesions at baseline (1020-06, 3010-03, 8030-01).

Patients in the placebo arm showed a slightly higher average Loes score compared to the leriglitazone group (2.408 vs 1.375, respectively). However, this difference is partially driven by a single patient in the placebo arm with a very high baseline Loes (importantly, this patient did not develop progressive cALD), with the rest of placebo patients having a distribution of baseline Loes comparable with the active arm (see Figure 35). In fact, even if the placebo arm has a higher percentage of patients with baseline Loes &gt; 0, due to the 2:1 randomisation in terms of raw numbers, the active arm has a larger number of patients with Loes &gt; 0 than placebo (38 vs. 21, respectively).

In terms of severity in Loes score, in the placebo arm three of the patients developing progressive cALD are among those with higher Loes score. Patients with similar Loes score were also enrolled in the active arm and none of them developed progressive cALD.

Three patients without lesions at baseline developed progressive cALD in the placebo arm compared to none in the active arm.

Analogous analyses with other baseline characteristics (e.g., age, EDSS or years since onset of myelopathy symptoms) also shows that baseline characteristics are not influencing results on the incidence of progressive cALD. Consequently, the fact that, in contrast to the placebo arm, there were no cases of progressive cALD in the active arm is unlikely to be due to differences in baseline characteristics among arms.

Plasma NfL levels increased significantly at 96 weeks compared to baseline in the placebo arm (mean change of baseline at Week 96 of 5.17± 17.04 pg/mL) but did not increase significantly in the active arm (mean change of baseline at Week 96 of 0.97 ± 3.89 pg/mL). At the end of the double-blind phase, plasma NfL concentration in the placebo arm was significantly higher compared to active arm (p=0.002), mostly driven by those patients developing cerebral lesion progression.

Plasma MMP-9 levels significantly decreased in the leriglitazone arm compared to baseline (from 179.62 ng/mL at baseline to 117.25 ng/mL at Week 96), resulting in significant lower concentrations in the active group compared to the placebo at 96 weeks (220.5 ng/mL placebo and 117.25 ng/mL leriglitazone at 96 weeks; p&lt; 0.001). Increased plasma MMP-9 was found in both placebo patients with and without cerebral lesion progression.

Similar to results observed with NfL, some neuroinflammatory biomarkers in plasma showed elevations in the placebo arm (IL-18, MIP-1 beta), which was mostly driven by those patients showing cerebral lesion progression. At Week 96, levels were significantly lower in the leriglitazone group compared to placebo (IL-18, MIP-1 beta, IL-1Ra).

Pre-planned investigations with advanced brain and spinal cord MRI techniques such as magnetisation transfer, diffusion-weighted or diffusion tensor based imaging, and magnetic resonance spectroscopy (MRS) were conducted to evaluate the potential effect of leriglitazone. These investigations were conducted in two separate sub-studies in France and the Netherlands including 21 patients (8 placebo; 13 active) and 10 patients (4 placebo; 6 active), respectively. The sub-studies failed to detect significant differences but suggested a potential effect of leriglitazone in preventing neuronal damage, stimulating myelin production and stabilising microstructural alterations in adult ALD patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, the cALD-related outcomes in ADVANCE confirm the clinically meaningful treatment effect of leriglitazone in the target population. In ADVANCE, across all cALD subgroup-related outcomes, leriglitazone has been shown to reduce the incidence and progression of lesions in adult ALD patients either without visible lesions or with Gd negative lesions.

Clinical diagnosis progressive cALD and

<!-- image -->

cALD: cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy; CGI-I: Clinical Global Impressions - Improvement; CGI-S: Clinical Global Impressions - Severity; CI: confidence interval; CSR: clinical study report; EDSS: Expanded Disability Status Scale; EQ-5D-5L: European Quality of Life 5 Dimensions; FAS: full analysis set; NfL: neurofilament light chain; PD: pharmacodynamic; PGI-I: Patient Global Impressions - Improvement.

Graphical representation of the treatment effect is presented through the LS means difference/difference in %, and respective 95% CIs for the FAS populations, for the pre-specified clinical secondary and exploratory endpoints in ADVANCE related to cALD. For cerebral lesion progression and incidence of progressive cALD, Clopper - Pearson CI are used, and for Global impressions (CGI-I; PGI-I; CGI-S), Newcombe-Wilson CI have been implemented. For NfL the 95% CI is shown to be in line with the rest of the results, LS Mean Difference between arms -6.62, 95% CI (- 2.38; -10.86). It is to be noted that in the CSR MT-2-01 the analysis of this PD endpoint was performed using the 90% CI.

In order to further explore the effect of treatment on patients with brain lesions compared to patients with no lesions, an additional subgroup analysis was conducted on patients from ADVANCE namely by stratifying patients by lesion status at baseline. Descriptive results are presented for the efficacy endpoints.

In both subgroups, placebo and active arms were comparable in cALD-related parameters at baseline (i.e., Loes and NfL). Leriglitazone reduced the number of patients showing cerebral lesion progression (MRI central readers) in both subgroups. In the subgroup with brain lesions, the incidence of cerebral lesion progression assessed by the central reader was 15.8% leriglitazone vs 35.7% (placebo). In the subgroup with no visible lesions at baseline (non-cALD), the incidence of cerebral lesion progression assessed by the central reader was 0% leriglitazone vs 12.0% (placebo). Clinical diagnosis of progressive cALD was only made in patients receiving placebo. Under placebo, 12% patients who had no visible lesions at baseline (non-cALD) and 21% who had brain lesions at baseline (cALD) were diagnosed with progressive cALD. In contrast, no patients receiving leriglitazone, regardless of baseline lesion status, received such a diagnosis. The presence of brain lesions at baseline almost doubled the risk of developing progressive cALD. In both subgroups, the Loes score increase was larger in the placebo arm driven by patients developing progressive cALD. Moreover, Loes increase tended to be larger in the subgroup of patients with brain lesions at baseline, consistent with the increased risk of developing progressive cALD once lesions are present. The mean (SD) change from baseline to Week 96 in Loes score was 0.19 (0.544) in leriglitazone vs 0.92 (1.835) in placebo. In the subgroup with no visible lesions at baseline, the mean (SD) change from baseline to Week 96 in Loes score was 0.057 (0.2742) in leriglitazone and 0.619 (1.9679) in placebo. The estimated difference between the two treatments in the subgroup with brain lesions was -0.614 with 95% CI (-1.618,0.390) and in the subgroup with no visible lesions it was -0.578 and 95% CI (1.179,0.024). For NfL, placebo patients showed an increase of levels at Week 96 in both subgroups whereas for leriglitazone treated patients NfL was stable. In the subgroup with brain lesions, the mean (SD) change from baseline was 0.99 (3.65) in leriglitazone vs 7.57 (27.35) in placebo. In the subgroup with no visible lesions, the mean (SD) change from baseline was 0.97 (4.06) in leriglitazone arm and 3.86 (7.71) in placebo arm. With respect to clinical outcomes (Global impressions, EDSS, and QoL), results in both subgroups are in line with those from FAS. The percentage of patients reporting an improvement at Week 96 in PGI-I was higher in the leriglitazone arm than in the placebo arm with improvement being reported only for patients on active treatment (placebo: 0%, leriglitazone: 15.8%). A similar percentage of patients in the placebo arm and in the leriglitazone arm reported worsening (placebo: 42.9%, leriglitazone: 36.8%). Nonetheless, only placebo patients showed worsening at the higher categories which corresponded to patients with progressive cALD (10206, 803001, 601004) (Figure 39). Similarly, in CGI-I the percentage of patients reporting an improvement at Week 96 was also higher in the leriglitazone arm than in the placebo arm with improvement only reported for patients on active (placebo: 0%, leriglitazone: 15.8%). A higher percentage of patients in the placebo arm than in the leriglitazone arm reported worsening (placebo: 35.7%, leriglitazone: 10.5%), and only patients in the placebo arm showed worsening at the higher categories, again corresponding to patients with progressive cALD (102006, 601004). In CGI-S, no clear differences were observed among arms and, as in the FAS, distribution at Week 96 was similar to baseline. Finally, patients in placebo showed numerically a higher increase (worsening) in EDSS at Week 96 than those on active, while in quality of life (EQ-ED-5L) at Week 96 the change from baseline was minimal for the leriglitazone arm whereas a decrease (worsening) was observed in the placebo arm (Figure 40).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, results from subgroup analysis are in line with those in the FAS showing that in both subgroups all cALD-related outcomes favour leriglitazone. Differences between leriglitazone and placebo became more pronounced in the subgroup of patients with brain lesions at baseline (i.e., those that would be included in the proposed label) which is consistent with the increased risk that these patients have of developing progressive cALD.

## 3.7.2 - ADVANCE (MT-2-01), Part 2

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

ADVANCE OLE (MT-2-01 Part 2) was an open-label treatment extension study which started immediately after the day of last treatment in Part 1 at V6, which, after 96 weeks of double-blind treatment in Part 1, was simultaneously the Baseline Visit of Part 2 of the study. In principle, all patients completing Part 1 (the 96-week double-blind treatment phase) were eligible for Part 2.

Patients were evaluated with 144 weeks of daily administration of study medication and periodic onsite visits and follow-up phone calls. After 144 weeks regular visits and safety visits or follow-up phone calls were each to take place at 6-month intervals for an unlimited time until either: (1) the patient withdrew consent; (2) significant safety or tolerability issues appeared and/or the patient's general health status, in the clinical judgement of the investigator, declined to an extent that the risks of treatment with leriglitazone outweighed the potential benefits; (3) the patient underwent HSCT; (4) leriglitazone received marketing authoridation for the treatment of AMN; or (5) Minoryx Therapeutics decided to terminate this study, whichever of these events occurred first.

All patients in Part 2 (including those randomised to placebo in Part 1) received a leriglitazone dose to achieve the pre-defined target plasma exposure of 200µg*h/mL. After the potential adjustment informed by PK results obtained at 100 weeks (V7) and 108 weeks (V8), the study medication dosage could not otherwise be changed except if required for safety/tolerability reasons.

The primary objective of Part 2 of the study was to assess the safety and tolerability of leriglitazone upon long-term treatment.

The secondary objectives of Part 2 of the study were to evaluate the long-term effects of leriglitazone on cerebral lesion progression and on myeloneuropathy progression.

The cALD-relevant efficacy endpoints of the OLE were:

## Cerebral Lesion Progression

- Incidence of clinically progressive cALD as judged by the investigator and reported as SAE
- Cerebral lesion progression (assessed by MRI central readers)
- Loes severity score (assessed by MRI central readers)

Global clinical assessment and quality of life assessments

- EDSS step (global score of the Expanded Disability Status Scale)
- European Quality of Life 5 Dimensions (EQ-5D-5L)

The exploratory analysis of biomarkers in plasma included NfL and MMP-9.

Overall, 33 patients randomised to placebo in the double-blind phase of ADVANCE joined the OLE (PL group). As for the patients initially randomised to leriglitazone, 55 patients joined the OLE continuing treatment with leriglitazone (LL group). One patient in each group did not join the OLE within 2 months from finalising Part 1, thus the FAS of Part 2 has 32 patients in PL group and 54 patients in LL group.
