BMI: body mass index; EDSS: Expanded Disability Status Scale; LL: patients in OLE who were taking leriglitazone in Part 1; n: number of patients in category; N: total number of patients; PL: patients in OLE who were taking placebo in Part 1; SD: standard deviation.

Source: Data extracted from CSR MT-2-01 Part 2 In-text Table 8 (Table 14.1.3.1.1) for Demographics; Table 14.2.5.2 for EDSS step; Table 14.2.1.2 for Loes score (placebo n=31, leriglitazone n=53).

Patients in the LL group showed an annualised incidence of cerebral lesion progression of 1.7 patients per year (3.0%) while for patients in the P group in Part 1 this was of 4.0 patients per year (10.3%).

The incidence per year of cerebral lesion progression was broadly similar (around 2 to 3%) in patients following treatment in leriglitazone in Part 1 (L), and those treated with leriglitazone in Part 2 (LL and PL).

During the OLE for the LL group there was no significant worsening in the annualised LS mean (SE) change of Loes score from baseline. In contrast, for the PL group, significant changes were observed in both the double-blind phase and the OLE, although the increase in the OLE was less than in the double-blind phase. Worsening of Loes score appeared to stabilise during OLE of the study, with smaller annualised mean increases from baseline. As for the patients developing progressive cALD during the double-blind phase, upon crossing over from placebo to active, Loes score progression in patients with cALD stabilises compared with the progression observed under placebo.

In EDSS step during the OLE, there was a parallel progression in both arms consistent with leriglitazone having a disease-modifying effect.

Adding to 6 cases reported in part 1, after 5 years of follow-up including the double-blind phase and the 3 years of OLE, only 2 additional cases of progressive cALD have been reported - one in PL and one in LL, none with Gd enhancement.

Mean change from baseline of NfL was stable, whether coming from leriglitazone or from placebo. Consistently with the diagnosis of progressive cALD and results on Loes score, the 2 patients with cALD progression showed an increase in NfL.

After crossing over to active, plasma MMP-9 elevation previously observed on placebo patients showed a decrease towards normalisation after 1 year of treatment with leriglitazone, reaching similar levels to those in patients continuing leriglitazone treatment.

## Compassionate Use

Leriglitazone has been available to adult and paediatric patients (2-17 years) with cALD within the framework of CU, which provides supportive evidence to the efficacy demonstrated in NEXUS and ADVANCE.

The global CU cohort encompassing 94 male cALD patients (40 adults, 54 boys) includes a large and diverse set of patients covering all ages groups, Gd negative and Gd positive lesions and patients with early disease (low lesion load /mild symptoms) and advanced disease (high lesion load and/or advanced symptoms) from centres in France, Spain, Germany, Italy, United Kingdom, Netherlands, Argentina, and Israel.

MRIs were performed at baseline and at varying intervals depending on site. Clinical outcomes were also collected including NFS/MFD for boys and AACS (total and cerebral domain) and EDSS for adults.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

French and German cohorts from the global CU cohort followed pre-specified treatment protocols within CU programmes which were approved by the respective national agencies (ANSM and BfArM). As of a data cutoff of 15 October 2024, data from 71 patients (35 adults, 36 boys), 47 of whom were treated in France (24 adults, 23 boys) and 24 in Germany (11 adults, 13 boys) was made available. In addition, aggregated data from 13 adult male patients with post-baseline data from the French cohort were analysed. For these cohorts the following definitions were used in the analyses:

- Disease stabilisation was defined as a composite of lesion stabilisation and clinical stabilisation.
- Lesion stabilisation is considered if a patient does not worsen in Gd status from baseline and have evidence of stable (no increase) in Loes score over at least 5 months from last follow-up visit.
- For adults, clinical stabilisation was defined as an AACS-C score that is no worse than the baseline AACS-C score, or an AACS-C score of 0 if baseline AACS-C score is missing. In the case of children, clinical stabilisation is defined as no increase in number of MFDs from baseline, and a total NFS score increase from baseline ≤ 1 within 2 years or ≤ 5 if greater than 2 years from baseline.
- Time to persistent Gd enhancement was defined as two consecutive Gd positive MRIs in a row, at least 8 weeks apart, or one Gd positive MRI, if that one measurement is the last observed for the patient. Only Gd negative patients at baseline were included in this analysis.
- Gadolinium resolution is defined as two Gd negative MRI results in a row, at least 8 weeks apart, amongst those who were Gd positive at baseline, or one Gd resolution MRI, if that one measurement is the last observed for the patient.

Of the 35 participants in the adult FAS, 25 were classified as having Early-Stage disease at baseline (Loes score &gt;0 and ≤12 and Cerebral Adult ALD Clinical Score (AACS-C) score ≤6) and 10 as having Advance-Stage disease (Loes score&gt;12 or AACS-C score&gt;6).

Radiologically, there was evidence of a slower Loes score increase over time in the Early-Stage group compared to the Advanced-Stage group. The adult CU population appeared to have a slower increase in Loes score compared to natural history (0.83 per year vs. 3.29 per year in natural history). This was more marked when comparing the Early-Stage Gd positive subgroup in the CU study to Early-Stage Gd positive natural history (0.16 per year vs. 3.33 per year in natural history).

- Of those with Gd- lesions at baseline (N=6), 33.3% experienced persistent gadolinium enhancement, whilst of those with Gd positive lesions at baseline (N=28), 25% experienced gadolinium resolution.
- Of those with enough data to determine a classification, at the final measurable assessment 61.9% of patients had stable lesions, as determined by Loes score and Gd status, 68.8% in the Early-Stage group and 40% in the Advanced-Stage group.

With regards to extent of disability, there was evidence that both total AACS and AACS-C score increased at a slower rate in the Early-Stage group compared to the Advanced-Stage group. Of those with enough data to determine a classification, at the final assessment 76% of patients had stable AACS-C, as determined by no increase from baseline, 85% in the Early-Stage group and 40% in the Advanced-Stage group.

In terms of disability-free survival without discontinuing treatment due to disease progression or unsatisfactory therapeutic effect, with varying severity of disability considered (AACS-C&lt;9 or AACSC&lt;12), longer event-free times were observed in the Early-Stage group compared to the AdvancedStage group. In addition, when restricting to the most comparable population to natural history, the overall Gd positive group comparison showed a median AACS-C 12 free survival time of 115.4 weeks in the CU study, compared to a median of 82.6 weeks in the natural history. When further restricting to early disease Gd positive patients, probability of an event in the CU group at 27 months was 0.9; this is the median Kaplan-Meier estimate in the comparable population from the natural history, i.e., the estimated probability of an event at the same time is 0.5.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

With the other endpoints indicating more favourable outcomes in the Early-Stage disease group, it is useful to estimate the probability of being event-free with regards to transitioning from Early-Stage disease to Advanced-Stage disease. Five (20%) patients experienced an event, with the median time to transition to Advanced-Stage disease estimated to be 88.6 weeks.

With regards to survival (assessed as time to death or treatment discontinuation due to disease progression or unsatisfactory effect), there was evidence of longer event-free times in the Early-Stage group than the Advanced-Stage group. In addition, when restricting to the most comparable population to natural history, the overall Gd positive group comparison showed a similar event-free probabilities over the comparable follow-up times. When restricting to Gd positive early disease, only 1 event (death) was observed in the CU study, translating to a probability of 0.923 of being event-free at the maximum follow-up time (148 weeks); this is compared to an early disease Gd positive natural history population, which has a probability of being event-free of approximately 0.58 at the same time point.

Of the 36 participants in the Paediatric FAS (pFAS), 17 were classified as having Early-Stage disease at baseline (Loes score &gt;0 and ≤10, MFD-free) and 19 as having Advanced-Stage disease (Loes score&gt;10 or &gt;0 MFDs). Again, patients had a higher disease burden at treatment initiation and were older compared to the NEXUS population. The overall mean (SD) age was 10.0 (2.90) and overall mean (SD) duration of cALD was 1.6 (3.45) years. With regards to disease characteristics, the overall mean (SD) Loes score at baseline was 9.15 (6.15), with lower mean Loes score in the Early-Stage group compared to the Advanced-Stage group by design (mean [SD] Early-Stage 3.79 [3.87], Advanced-Stage 13.95 [2.99]). On the disability scales, the Early-Stage group also had lower baseline disability, as determined by total NFS lower mean (mean [SD] Early-Stage 0.6 [1.09], Advanced-Stage 3.1 [1.85]) and number of MFDs (mean [SD] Early-Stage 0 [0.0], Advanced-Stage 0.3 [0.59]). In total, 28 (77.8%) were Gadolinium positive at baseline.

Once the NEXUS recruitment was completed on May 2023, eligibility criteria for CU were extended to patients meeting the inclusion criteria of NEXUS, which means that at the time of the cutoff, patients with these disease characteristics (typically Early-Stage) only had relatively short follow-up. The median follow-up length for NEXUS-eligible patients was 24.9 weeks, compared to 42 weeks for those not eligible for NEXUS. This has analysis implications, as there are distinct characteristics through time, with patients with Early-Stage disease eligible for NEXUS only contributing a small amount of follow-up to analyses. For continuous variables examining slope, the NEXUS-eligible patients will have less impact on the overall slope estimate than if their follow-up length was the same as those with advanced disease; similarly in survival analyses, these patients would likely represent a set of patients who would live longer; they will be censored at the last follow-up visit and so the population remaining would comprise a population with advanced cALD. These changes in eligibility criteria will lead to informative censoring in the survival and the MFD-free survival analyses, as the censoring is not random, but correlated with disease severity. For this reason, as well as relatively small sample size and number of events, particularly within subgroups, all Kaplan-Meier estimates in overall survival and MFD-free survival may be unreliable.

## Efficacy Results

Radiologically, in the paediatric population, Loes scores were estimated to increase at a similar rate whether the patient had Early-Stage disease of Advanced-Stage disease at baseline. Nonetheless, a higher proportion of patients in Early-Stage showed lesion stabilisation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Of those with enough data to determine a classification, at the final measurable assessment 40.9% had stable lesions, as determined by Loes score and Gd status, 50% in the Early-Stage group and 33% in the Advanced-Stage group.
- All patients that were Gd negative at treatment initiation (n= 4) remained Gd negative throughout follow-up.
- One patient out of 25 with Gd positive lesions achieved gadolinium resolution throughout followup. This patient was in the Early-Stage group.

With regards to extent of disability, there is evidence that both total NFS score and number of MFDs increase at a slower rate in the Early-Stage group compared to the Advanced-Stage group.

In terms of MFD-free survival without discontinuing treatment due to disease progression or unsatisfactory therapeutic effect, there was a higher event rate in the Advanced-Stage group but not reflected in the Kaplan-Meier estimates. When restricting to a NEXUS-like population, 2 events were experienced over the first 96 weeks (both increases in MFD), compared to none in NEXUS with a similar sample size. When restricting to the cohort characteristics as reported by Raymond et al. (2019), the median event-free time estimates were not comparable to the Raymond median estimates, due to the important caveats, namely i) the informative censoring in the CU, where patients that were healthier at baseline had shorter follow-up; ii) the natural history study was examining time from cALD diagnosis, whereas here time from leriglitazone treatment initiations were examined; iii) in the CU study, overall survival was approximated using death or discontinuation due to disease progression or unsatisfactory therapeutic effect, which was not done in the natural history analysis; and iv) the sample size, number of events and duration of follow-up in the CU study made Kaplan-Meier estimates very sensitive to each event.

With a trend towards more favourable outcomes in the Early-Stage group in other endpoints, it is useful to estimate the probability of being event-free with regards to transitioning from Early-Stage disease to Advanced-Stage disease. Four out of 13 (30.8%) transitioned from Early-Stage to Advanced-Stage disease over follow-up, with 0/4 transitioning from the Gd negative group at baseline.

With regards to survival without discontinuing due to disease progression or unsatisfactory therapeutic effect, analyses are sensitive to low counts and short follow-up, with a lower proportion of events in the Early-Stage group. Of 29 patients included in the analysis, 8 (27.6%) experienced an event, but only one of those was confirmed death, the rest were treatment discontinuations, implying the mortality rate is likely lower than reported here. When restricting to a NEXUS-like population, no events were experienced over the first 96 weeks, and just one over all follow-up (which was a treatment discontinuation due to disease progression, as opposed to a registered death), which mirrors the result in NEXUS. When restricting to the Raymond-like cohort characteristics, the median eventfree time estimates not compared to the Raymond median estimates for the same reasons as stated above.

## Summary and Conclusions for Adults and Children from the French and German Cohorts

In adult patients treated with leriglitazone under CU, the disease progression was slower in the EarlyStage group as compared to the Advanced-Stage group. Natural history data have shown across multiple variables that as a whole population, adults with Early-Stage of Advanced-Stage cALD have similar outcomes in terms of progression rate (see Adult cALD Natural History Report, Kohler 2023). The data from this analysis is beginning to differentiate the value of treatment benefits when patients are treated in the early stages of the disease compared to the advanced patient cohort where cerebral lesion damage and clinical decline may not be as amenable to similar outcomes. Where comparisons to Early-disease cohorts were available (Loes score over time, overall survival), the outcomes were estimated to be less severe in the CU protocol compared to natural history, indicating that treating adults early may slow the natural disease course.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As in the adult population, the disease progression in boys was slower in the Early-Stage group as compared to the Advanced-Stage group. The CU paediatric cohort started treatment with more Advanced-Stage patients due to ongoing recruitment of NEXUS. Those who met the NEXUS inclusion/exclusion criteria have shorter duration of follow-up as they could only be recruited to the CU protocol after NEXUS had finished recruiting. It is worth noting that median duration follow-up amongst those in the Early-Stage NEXUS group was 24.9 weeks, compared to a median of 42 weeks amongst those not in the Early-Stage NEXUS group, which will have an influence on the overall slope in models examining slope over time, and on the likelihood of being classed as 'unclassified' in analyses which require a minimum follow-up time, e.g., 5 months follow-up to classify lesion stability. It will also mean that in the survival analyses, those patients who will be, on average, healthier, are censored earlier, leading to probable overestimation of mortality rate in times once these patients have exited follow-up.

Data from a subgroup of 13 adult male patients with post-baseline data from the French cohort were analysed. These adult cALD patients (19-67 years of age), either not eligible to HSCT (n= 8) or awaiting HSCT (n= 5), were treated with leriglitazone and monitored every three months with standardised neurological scores, plasma biomarkers and brain MRI comprising lesion volumetrics and DTI. All patients were diagnosed with progressive cALD at baseline, 10 patients presented with Gd positive lesions and 3 with Gd negative lesions showing clinically meaningful growth prior treatment with leriglitazone.

- 10/13 remained clinically and radiologically stable
- o Among the 10 stable patients, 4 exhibited better proprioception and cognitive tests
- o The 2 patients showing continued decline presented prominent cognitive dysfunction at baseline
- o 1 patient died of COVID-19 after 3 months
- Patients with Gd enhancement of internal capsule lesions (poor prognostics for HSCT), showed Gd resolution
- The 5 patients that initiated treatment while waiting for HSCT were taken out of the waiting list on the basis of the positive results observed with leriglitazone

Overall, this cohort study indicated that leriglitazone can halt neuroinflammation and disease progression in adult patients with cALD at early disease stages, as evidenced by clinical, radiological, and biological stability for over one year in most patients.

For the global CU cohort (which also includes the patients from the 2 cohorts), follow-up MRI of ≥3 months is available for 67 patients (30 adults, 37 boys), which is the basis for the assessment of lesion stabilisation. At data cut-off, 14 patients were still on treatment but have not yet had post-baseline assessments and thus have not yet generated post-baseline data. Only 13 patients have discontinued prior to post-baseline assessments; the reason for discontinuation in these patients was primarily due to very advanced disease and associated difficulties attending clinic visits.

Overall, lesion stabilisation was reported for the majority of patients with post-baseline data in the global CU cohort, which aligns with the data from the NEXUS and ADVANCE clinical studies. The results further indicated that the most meaningful benefits were observed when treatment was initiated early in disease, which is similar to the HSCT experience.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As the 94 patients treated under CU have various disease severities, the patients have been categorised according to their baseline lesion load status (i.e., Loes) and presence of clinical symptoms for comparative purpose

Lesion stabilisation has been reported by the investigators for most patients with post-baseline data. Moreover, radiological stabilisation was generally accompanied by clinical stability in patients with low lesion load. In patients with high lesion load or advanced clinically, clinical progression was still observed despite lesion stabilisation, which is consistent with data from HSCT showing that clinical deterioration is not prevented when the procedure is initiated in patients with extensive lesions or advanced clinical deterioration. Even for those where lesions are stabilised, clinical progression is still expected as patients already had extensive lesions prior initiating treatment, and such lesions will in any case translate into clinical consequences as in cALD brain lesions precede clinical deterioration.

## Extrapolation of Efficacy to Patients aged 13-17 years

The pivotal trial NEXUS concluded the efficacy in children 2 - 12 years of age, while ADVANCE demonstrated efficacy in adults with Gd negative lesions. Efficacy in children, adolescents, and adults is supported by the CU programme.

As discussed once lesions become Gd positive, the course of cALD is the same in children, adolescents and adults. All adult and paediatric patients diagnosed with cALD (Gd negative lesions) are at risk for progressive cALD, and 90% of boys will eventually progress due to inflammatory demyelination with BBB breakdown.

A disease progression model was generated to support the extrapolation of available data from children aged 2-12 years and adults to adolescent cALD patients aged 13-17 years (24MIN0282). Data on the natural history of the Loes score were used to develop the model which was compared to changes in Loes score in leriglitazone treated patients from NEXUS and the compassionate use programme (CUP). The modelling showed that disease progression is similar between paediatric, adolescent and adult progressive patients when patients with the same baseline Loes are compared. The combined NEXUS and CUP data included 10 patients that were 13-17 of age at baseline (CUP) and another 8 patients that moved into this age range during follow-up. The results suggest that adolescents show a treatment effect similar to adults and boys. Extrapolation of efficacy to this age range is also supported by the totality of the evidence provided and based on the similar course of disease and comparable PK and PD data for important markers (e.g., changes in the PD markers NfL and MMP-9, and levels of adiponectin, a PPARγ engagement marker).

A longitudinal model was developed describing disease progression of cALD using Loes scores as a clinically relevant radiological endpoint of the disease. The goal of the analysis was to assess the natural history of disease progression in patients with progressive cALD and compare disease progression with data from patients treated with leriglitazone.

## 5.3.6. Analysis performed across trials (pooled analyses and metaanalysis)

The descriptive comparison of results across studies was performed for the studies in the target indication cALD (NEXUS and ADVANCE). Given the differences in study populations and study designs, efficacy data from the pivotal NEXUS study in paediatric cALD patients and the ADVANCE study in adult cALD patients have not been pooled.

## Study Designs

The pivotal NEXUS study was specifically designed to evaluate the effect of leriglitazone on cALD in children aged 2-12 years on lesion growth stabilisation and disease arrest prior to HSCT and included patients with either Gd negative lesions or Gd positive lesions. NEXUS was of an open-label design using established natural history of a 10% disease arrest rate for childhood cALD. As the course of disease is highly predictable in children, natural history cohorts from published data severed as external historical controls.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The primary, secondary and exploratory efficacy endpoints were selected for their sensitivity to determine progression in cALD:

- Primary: Arrested disease at W96 or visit prior to HSCT.
- Secondary: Neurological function (NFS/MFD), Loes score, GIS, survival of subjects not undergoing HSCT, number of subjects meeting HSCT criteria.
- Exploratory: Lesion volume, DTI, perfusion MRI, plasma and CSF biomarkers.

ADVANCE was a placebo-controlled study in adult ALD patients to primarily assess the effect of leriglitazone on the progression of AMN. To be eligible, patients had to have evidence spinal cord involvement and could also have Gd negative lesions at baseline. Patients with Gd positive lesions were explicitly excluded from participation.

The primary endpoint of this study was the 6MWT which relates with AMN progression. While this endpoint is sensitive in a population with myelopathy, it has no clinical relevance for assessing cerebral lesion development and progression in patients with cALD. Nonetheless, efficacy endpoints for this study covered the entirety of disease components in ALD patients, including cerebral lesion progression.

Two pre-specified MRI-based secondary endpoints specific of cALD were cerebral lesion progression and Loes score. In addition, NfL was analysed as an exploratory marker of axonal damage which is also reflecting progression of cALD.

## Study Populations

The pivotal NEXUS study included paediatric ALD patients with cALD, with either Gd negative or Gd positive lesions at baseline. In contrast, ADVANCE enrolled adult patients with ALD, including patients with Gd negative lesions (i.e., cALD) and patients without visible brain lesions. Patients with Gd positive lesions were excluded from participation. Hence, only a subgroup of ADVANCE patients had cALD. The comparative discussions will therefore only focus on ADVANCE patients with cALD, i.e., Gd negative lesions at baseline.

In NEXUS, patients ranged between 3-12 years, with an average age of 7.7 years. For the cALD subgroup in ADVANCE, patients ranged 24-60 years, with an average age of 38.3 years.

Only the baseline Loes score is available for both studies. Loes score was higher in the adults with Gd negative lesions in ADVANCE compared to children with Gd negative lesions in NEXUS (5.23 vs 1.78). This is expected as higher Loes scores are generally observed in older subjects, which may reflect how cALD is diagnosed and followed up in the clinical setting. Children may be diagnosed earlier and followed more closely, while in adults the first MRI assessment may generally be initiated when symptoms of the cerebral form of the disease occurs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>
