## Results

## NEXUS

In NEXUS, 7 of 20 (35%) evaluable subjects met the 'arrested disease' criteria (95% CI: 15.4, 100, p=0.0024) Of these 7 subjects, all subjects (100%) met the 'arrested disease' criteria, including being free of MFD (95% CI: 59.0, 100, p=&lt;0.0001). NEXUS therefore met its primary endpoint. The 35% of subjects meeting 'arrested disease' criteria is greater than what would be expected based on natural history, which indicates that 10% of patients had disease arrest

Clinically meaningful results were observed across all criteria informing 'arrested disease', including NFS, MFD, lesion progression, Gd enhancement, and lesion growth. No increases in clinical outcomes (NFS and MFD) were seen from baseline to Week 96, and radiological outcomes (lesion progression, Gd enhancement, lesion growth) showed stabilisation with changes below expected natural history.

Volumetric analyses indicate a stabilisation of lesion growth demonstrating that patients are following a trajectory well below natural history data, where the rate of increase in lesion volume of untreated subjects with cALD is expected to be accelerating at Week 24. Accordingly, the lesion volume growth rate in the natural history group was consistently estimated to be greater than in NEXUS patients, for whom a clear and notable plateau-like profile in lesion volume growth was shown.

Loes scores reported in patients undergoing HSCT or gene therapy are similar to those in NEXUS and thus provide a robust comparison of progression and stabilisation following leriglitazone. In NEXUS, Loes score, as observed in the lesion volume measures, show lesion growth stabilisation with a plateau profile for children with either Gd negative lesions or Gd positive lesions, reflecting little progression in

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

these patients. In children with Gd positive lesions, this is similar to the effect observed after gene therapy (eli-cel [Skysona ® ]) treatment and HSCT.

Patients with Gd negative lesions treated with leriglitazone show reduced conversion to Gd positive lesions when compared to natural history, with a clear delay in the time to Gd enhancement and most children (70%) remaining Gd negative after 2 years of treatment with leriglitazone.

Additional evidence for the efficacy of leriglitazone were provided by results from biomarkers, which supported the neuroprotective and anti-neuroinflammatory effects of treatment. Decreases in both NfL and MMP-9 were seen in patients following treatment with leriglitazone. The DTI and perfusion MRI data indicate anti-inflammatory and neuroprotective effect in the lesional area. Data also suggest a beneficial effect on normal appearing white matter distant from cALD lesions, as treatment improves microvascular function, an effect previously observed only following stem cell transplant.

Overall, the results from NEXUS confirmed that leriglitazone reduces disease progression reflected by the number of patients showing clinical and radiological disease arrest. Following treatment with leriglitazone, the progression of brain lesions in boys with cALD was significantly reduced, as shown by radiological outcomes (lesion volume, Loes score, and Gd enhancement). Moreover, this reduction in lesion progression directly translates to clinical stability, as reflected by the clinical outcomes (NFS and MFD), which show that patients were fully stable while on treatment. Overall, these results diverge from the natural history in paediatric cALD patients.

## ADVANCE

In adult ALD patients, data derived from this placebo-controlled study showed that leriglitazone significantly reduces cerebral lesion progression as determined in pre-defined efficacy outcomes scored by blinded central MRI reader. Such results are aligned with those from clinical diagnosis of progressive cALD, as only patients in the placebo group were clinically diagnosed with progressive cALD by treating physicians which suggests that leriglitazone reduced the risk of developing progressive cALD.

While positive results are observed in both patients with no visible brain lesions and patients with brain lesions (cALD) at baseline, results are particularly meaningful for patients with brain lesions as they have an almost doubled the risk of developing progressive cALD compared with patients with no visible lesions. Similarly, Loes score and NfL in the placebo arm showed greater increases compared to the leriglitazone arm in both subgroups (with no visible lesions and with brain lesion / cALD). The increase was larger in the placebo arm driven by patients developing progressive cALD. Moreover, Loes increase tended to be larger in the group of patients with brain lesions, consistent with the increased risk of developing progressive cALD once lesions are present.

In conclusion, leriglitazone significantly reduced cerebral lesion progression and incidence of life threatening progressive cALD. Additional analysis of the ADVANCE data stratified by presence of baseline lesions furthermore showed that all patients are at risk of developing progressive cALD and risk doubles in those patients with brain lesions at baseline. It should be noted that progressive cALD did not develop in any of the patients treated with leriglitazone compared to placebo, regardless of baseline lesions.

## Conclusion and Discussion

The pivotal NEXUS study in children and ADVANCE study in adults included different populations with respect to age and disease status at baseline. NEXUS enrolled children with Gd negative and Gd positive lesions at baseline, while in ADVANCE included adults ALD patients with AMN, with and without brain lesions but excluding those with Gd positive lesions.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The studies also differed with respect to primary objective. NEXUS was designed to specifically assess the effect of leriglitazone on disease progression in cALD. ADVANCE was primarily designed to evaluate the efficacy of leriglitazone on progression on AMN while it also included other outcomes to provide a holistic view of ALD progression including pre-defined outcomes related with progression of cALD.

Despite these differences, the results observed in both NEXUS and ADVANCE support the use of leriglitazone in ALD patients with cALD. More specifically, treatment with leriglitazone resulted in significant reductions in lesion progression in paediatric patients with non-gadolinium enhacing lesions, which provides a clinically meaningful benefit to these patients.

## 5.3.7. Observational data and Data from registries

NA.

## 5.3.8. Patient experience data (PED)

As part of an early dialogue with different stakeholders, input from two patient organizations were received by the CHMP.

Patient organizations mainly focus on the burden of the disease for patients and families. They stated that currently available treatments are scarce, not always with good results and/or with significant burden. They also report that treatments are not always available to all patients and /or become available at late stages of the disease when final functional status is already severely impaired even if treatment is efficacious.

They stress the need for new treatments with early access and that could halt the disease earlier, reduce hospitalization time, prevent neurological decline, delay or avoid HSCT and improve motor and cognitive function.

## 5.3.9. Healthcare professional engagement

As part of an early dialogue with different stakeholders, input from one Health Care Professional association was received by the CHMP.

Healthcare providers also focus on the poor response to currently available treatments warranting further developments.

## 5.3.10. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical efficacy

## 5.3.10.1. Discussion

cALD is a severe condition with a progressive clinical deterioration. The Natural history is generally accepted to be different in children compared to adults, warranting a paediatric dedicated development.

The only treatment available is HSCT which is known to have a significant burden and constraints related to preparation process and availability. There is a clear need for new treatment options.

## Design and conduct of clinical studies

The efficacy claims are based on a single pivotal Nexus study (MT-2-02) that was a 24-month, open-label, single-arm, phase 2/3, multicentre trial evaluating the effect of leriglitazone on the progression of cerebral lesions in 23 male patients with cALD aged 2-12 years. Subjects were categorised by baseline MRI status into those with non-enhancing (Gd-) (Population 1) or gadolinium-enhancing (Gd+) (Population 2) cerebral lesions. No scientific advice was sought for this study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The decision not to include a placebo arm is understandable considering the specificities of the condition, namely its rarity and severity. The fact that leriglitazone was given on top of the current SOC (patients had access to HSCT if and when indicated) assured that all patients were not deprived of the best treatment option currently available. This has been also agreed with PDCO (considering the approved PIP study). Nevertheless, uncontrolled study design is considered a limitation, because of heterogeneity of disease progression. To address this uncertainty, the applicant presented comparison with a natural history cohort.

The sample size was determined to estimate the proportion of subjects achieving 'arrested disease', aiming to distinguish a 40% response rate with treatment from a 10% spontaneous rate expected in untreated patients. With 80% power and a one-sided significance level of 0.05, a minimum of 13 subjects was required, with at least 4 achieving arrested disease to meet the primary efficacy objective. Additional enrolment was permitted following agreement between the Investigator and Sponsor, and ultimately 23 subjects were enrolled, a decision that was, according to the applicant, driven based on operational considerations (once the threshold of 13 evaluable patients was reached, additional patients were enrolled due to ongoing site-level recruitment activities that had already been initiated prior to communication of study closure). Subjects who discontinued treatment due to disease progression (meeting HSCT criteria or failing to achieve short-term arrested disease) were not replaced. The applicant provided a sensitivity analysis restricted to the first 13 evaluable patients, showing that the primary endpoint was met in this subset similar to the overall population.

There were 4 screen failures in the NEXUS study due to patients not fulfilling the inclusion criteria of having LS Score of &gt; 0 and ≤10.

The applicant presented and discussed the Protocol deviations. The 3 major deviations were related to earlier than expected echocardiography, switch of IP kits in one patient with his brother's (also enrolled in the trial) and bottle of IP being dispensed to a patient on this drug but not on the CT due to shortage. These deviations do not impact study results. When assessing the deviations classified as minor, nineteen (19) of these deviations were related to the fact that patients / families did not return the last kits dispensed because they were unable to return to the site after Visit Prior to HSCT. Only one of those patients had treatment compliance under 90%, but still with exposures within the target exposure range from baseline through Week 48 and slightly below after that but remained above the range of minimum efficacy. Assessment of the PK data of those cases with possible over-exposure based on compliance data support that this was not the case.

A 10% rate of disease arrest expected in natural history patients was proposed. The validity of the choice was questioned as other publications suggest higher rates, ranging from 10 to 21% (Liberato et al,2019) which could have an impact on the chosen estimand. The applicant provided insights on the process that lead to obtaining this average NH arrest rate both for Gd positive and Gd negative cohorts. This was set before the start of the study, resulting from the data available from 6 different studies/series including the Liberato et al, 2019. Although this study suggested a self-arrest rate of 21% when all patients are considered, a patient-level data assessment restricted to patients aged 212 years (inclusive), delivered a self-arrest rate of 8.7% which was computed in the stipulated final rate calculation. The applicant further defended that the proposed disease arrest rate for the NH cohort is adequate irrespective of the patients' Gd status and also for patients with Loes Score up to 4.5 at baseline.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Following the newly presented rationale (Liberato et al, 2019) and even considering that this might be acceptable for the Gd negative patients, it is not agreed that the same is true for Gd positive patients for whom a calculated self-arrested rate of 3-5% (depending on the studies included) was presented.

Large part of study participants received corticosteroids (hydrocortisone, fludrocortisone). Corticosteroids administered in the studies were physiological replacement doses for adrenal insufficiency and not anti-inflammatory treatments. These doses cannot modify cALD neuroinflammation or masking MRI activity. Their use was stable, balanced between arms, and unrelated to imaging timepoints. The applicant considers that corticosteroid use does not represent a confounding factor for the interpretation of MRI findings or clinical outcomes in the clinical study.

## Efficacy data and additional analysis

A total of 23 subjects were enrolled in the NEXUS study, and all 23 subjects received at least one dose (partial or complete) of leriglitazone (SAS) and had at least one post-baseline MRI assessment with Gd (mITT). Twenty of these subjects were considered evaluable (ES) for 'arrested disease', as they provided enough data to assess 'arrested disease' having ≥ 2 MRI assessments post baseline at least 12 weeks apart; 3 subjects underwent HSCT prior to Week 24 and were not included in the ES.

Patients were included in the NEXUS study as soon as they were identified and diagnosed with cALD. The date of the MRI for the diagnosis, including the date of the first Gd-positive lesion, was not collected in the eCRF precluding clear determination of the time of cALD diagnosis for all patients, and, for Pop 2, the time since the first Gd positive lesion detected in the MRI.

The enrolled patients in the NEXUS study had maximum Loes score at baseline of 4.5 (median 2.0) and maximum NFS of 1, which reflect an early stage / less severe disease, with no results having been generated for the more clinically severely impacted patients. This prevents extrapolation to patients with more advanced disease.

The applicant elaborated on the pattern of brain lesion distribution of the NEXUS population which is considered similar to the NH publication assessing the pattern distribution in cALD patients (Loes et al., 2003).

At Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT, of the 20 evaluable subjects, 7 (35%) subjects met the CoPrimary Endpoint 1 ('arrested disease' criteria not including being free of MFD; 95% CI: 15.4, 100, p=0.0024). Of these 7 subjects, all subjects (100%) met the Co-Primary Endpoint 2 ('arrested disease' criteria, including being free of MFD; 95% CI: 59.0, 100, p=&lt;0.0001). Thus, subject to the acceptance of the used NH data for comparison the primary endpoint of the study was met, with 35% of subjects meeting 'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT, representing a statistically significant improvement over the 10% rate of patients with disease arrest expected in natural history patients.

As all subjects in the ES remained clinically stable at Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT, with a change of ≤ 1 from baseline in NFS and being free of MFD, 'arrested disease' was driven by MRI assessment of the cerebral lesion evolution. Fourteen (70.0%) of 20 evaluable subjects had no significant T2/FLAIR lesion growth at Week 96 or the visit prior to HSCT, including 7 (77.8%) of 9 subjects in Population 1 and 7 (63.6%) of 11 subjects in Population 2. The crucial factor for determining lack of lesion progression and thus meeting the criteria for 'arrested disease', was whether a subject demonstrated no conversion to Gd positive cerebral lesions or disappearance of persistent Gd positive cerebral lesions. Of the 20 evaluable subjects at Week 96 or the visit prior to HSCT, 7 (35%) showed a lack of lesion progression with no Gd enhancement and no significant lesions growth (6 in Population 1 and 1 in Population 2), while 14 (70%) had no significant T2/FLAIR lesion growth at Week 96 or the visit prior to HSCT.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Of the 12 subjects in the ES that were assessed for 'arrested disease' criteria at the visit prior to HSCT all were free of MFD and had a change from baseline in NFS score ≤ 1 at the visit prior to HSCT. However, 11/12 (91.7%) of these subjects demonstrated lesion progression on MRI assessment and were proposed for HSCT. The 3 patients with Gd negative cerebral lesions at baseline progressed to Gd positive lesions with 2 of them also having significant growth in T2/FLAIR lesions. Of the 9 patients Gd positive at baseline, 8 remained Gd positive and 7 (77.8%) demonstrated no significant T2/FLAIR lesions growth at the visit prior to HSCT.

Of the 8 subjects assessed at Week 96, 6 (75.0%) showed no signs of accelerated lesion growth compared to untreated natural history (5 with no growth and 1 with a non-significant lesion growth below what would be expected in natural history).

Nine (64.3%) of the 14 subjects with a Visit Prior to HSCT showed no significant lesion growth (5 of these subjects had lesion growth below what would be expected in untreated natural history, and the remaining 4 subjects had no lesion growth). Five (35.7%) of the 14 subjects demonstrating significant lesion growth (progression according to or above untreated natural history), 2 were in Population 1 and 3 were in Population 2.

In the 11 subjects in Population 2 evaluated at the visit prior to HSCT, mean (SD) GIS was 1.18 (0.751) with scores ranging from 0 to 3, reflecting a mean (SD) decrease from baseline of -0.18 (0.405). No subjects in Population 2 reported a sustained worsening of GIS.

Multiple sensitivity analyses of the primary endpoint were performed to test the robustness of the primary analysis results. The primary endpoint was met in each of the sensitivity analyses performed, reflecting a statistically significant improvement over the 10% rate of patients with disease arrest expected in natural history patients.

Of the 20 subjects evaluable, 17 (85.0%) (95% CI: 62.1, 96.8) met the continuation criteria at Week 24 (either the protocol defined 'arrested disease' criteria or lesion growth deceleration). Seven subjects (35%, 95%CI: 15.4, 59.2) met the arrested disease criteria at Week 24, and 17 (85.0%) showed lesion deceleration. Of these subjects, 16 (80.0%) were assessed as not having accelerated lesion growth as compared to expected acceleration of untreated natural history subjects, a majority of which showed stable lesions with no further growth (10 subjects). Six of the 9 subjects in Population 1 remained at a GIS of 0 at Week 24, which contrasted with prior studies demonstrating that half of Gd negative NH patients become Gd positive within 6 months of diagnosis. The K-M estimate for the proportion of subjects in Population 1 with persistent Gd enhancement by Week 24 was 33.3% (95% CI: 12.2, 71.8); which remained unchanged by Week 96. In the NH patients, the median K-M estimate of time from diagnosis to lesional enhancement was 6.0 months (95% CI: 3.6, 17.8), and by 18 months, 81.5% (95% CI: 53.0, 92.7) of these patients were estimated to have Gd positive cerebral lesions.

At Week 24, 9 (45.0%) of 20 evaluable subjects met HSCT criteria. All 9 of these subjects were from Population 2. Transplant decision was taken for 15 patients who underwent the corresponding visit prior to HSCT (3 in Population 1, 12 in Population 2), 3 of them before W24. In the 12 evaluable subjects that completed the Visit Prior to HSCT, 9 (75.0%) subjects met the criteria due to persistent gadolinium enhancement but did not show significant lesion growth at two consecutive visits. Only 2 (16.7%) subjects went through HSCT after meeting both components of the HSCT criteria, 1 in each population.

A plateau profile was observed in fold change from baseline in the lesion volume, and in the lesion volume growth, for both populations in the study. Fold change from baseline in treated subjects was below that expected in the natural history from Mallack and colleagues. Leriglitazone treated subjects showed reduced lesion growth when compared with matching patients from natural history, the rate of percentage change in the leriglitazone group was estimated to be approximately half the rate of percentage change in the natural history group. The increase per month in the natural history was 23%, while in leriglitazone treated subjects it was 10% per month (p=0.003). Likewise, a plateau in Loes score over time was observed in most subjects treated with leriglitazone. In boys with cALD, Loes score is expected to increase over time; in Gd positive patients treated by HSCT or gene therapy, a plateau profile similar to that observed in subjects treated with leriglitazone was demonstrated. Subjects in Population 1 treated with leriglitazone show reduced evolution to Gd positive lesions; the K-M estimate for time to persistent Gd enhancement in Population 1 was 33.3% (95% CI: 12.2, 71.8) at both Week 24 and Week 96. NEXUS study results showed that non-arrested disease was mainly driven by Gd enhancement, which was also the main reason to decide on the need for HSCT for most of the enrolled patients that had a HSCT. So, based on these results Gd enhancement was also used as a leriglitazone treatment success criterion with impact on further treatment decisions. One subject in Population 1 (1030-04) showed transient Gd enhancement, firstly converting to a Gd positive lesion at Week 36 but converting back to Gd negative lesions at Week 72 [still negative Week 144 (last visit prior to data cut-off)]. The applicant elaborates on this Gd enhancement fluctuation. No matching cases in NH have been reported with the same profile, with those having showed a transient Gd enhancement eventually evolving to Gd positive lesions with time. No plausible reason was presented for this profile. Although, patient 1030-04 kept stable lesions across the entire follow-up (36 months) both in terms of Loes Score (maintained at 3) and Lesion Volume, this evolution to Gd positive enhancement will probably be the scenario with more follow-up time. So, there is no clear reason for this Gd enhancement fluctuation, and the case singularity and lack of similar cases described impairs true interpretation of its meaning. One patient (2030-01) from Pop2 converted to Gd- lesions already at W12 of treatment with leriglitazone. The applicant considers that this change is not related to methodological issues and insures site consistency regarding MRI acquisition protocols and readers' assessments. Similar cases were seen in the CU program. The applicant further elaborated on the time of leriglitazone impact on Gd enhancement (compared to HSCT). In HCST treated patients there is a rapid response with reconstitution of the BBB within the first month leading to conversion to Gd negative lesions. The impact on lesion growth on the other side is only seen about 6-9 months after the procedure. In leriglitazone treated Gd positive patients, although an impact in lesion growth is also seen, there was a lack of impact on Gd enhancement, with only one patient losing this enhancement, as per results of the NEXUS. Considering the information from CU program adult patients, the age group where higher proportion of patients have shown Gdresolution, the median time required to show changes was 63 weeks, suggesting that longer follow-up may be required in order to show Gd-resolution in patients treated with leriglitazone vs. those treated with HSCT based treatments. The applicant justifies the different response in terms of Gd enhancement with the different mechanism of action of the treatments and reinforces the need for more time to see an impact of leriglitazone in Gd enhancement. The observed improvement in the primary endpoint was limited to Gd negative population. The applicant agrees with the fact that patients with Gd positive lesions failed to fulfill disease control criteria but argued that, irrespective of keeping their Gd enhancement status, all patients showed stable or reduced GIS. Similarly, the applicant considers that other results could be seen as a sign of leriglitazone impact on disease control such as lack of significant lesion growth (similar to Gd negative patients), lesion volume, Loes Score, NfL stability. Additionally, all patients remained clinically stable although they were expected to deteriorate in the timeframe of the clinical study (as per NH comparative evolution) and additional effects that were demonstrated in plasma biomarker and advanced MRI.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant presents data pointing to the similarity of lesion growth rate of patients treated with leriglitazone with those treated with either HSCT or GT. Results on Advanced MRI techniques are compared to those observed post-GT intervention or in transplanted patients, pointing to a similar therapeutic impact to other treatment options. Additionally, the applicant argued that similarly to the Gd enhancement profile observed post-GT intervention or in transplanted patients, Gd enhancement persistence after treatment with leriglitazone does not clearly correlate with disease progression. Although this might be the case for other treatments, it still lacks confirmation in patients treated with leriglitazone and warrants further investigation to demonstrate these assumptions. Further CU data is also presented but subject to great variability. The applicant further elaborates on the possible role of leriglitazone for those patients with Gd positive lesions, assigned to HSCT as per standard of care, as an immediately available treatment for those not eligible to transplant or still with a significant period of time to prepare HSCT, with leriglitazone potentially having a role in the meanwhile as a brain lesion/disease progression halt treatment. The generated evidence is still considered scarce, in the actual context of corroborating this claim. Overall current evidence was still considered limited to substantiate clinical in patients with Gd positive lesions. The applicant supported its reasoning on the expectation that Gd enhancement resolution would occur with more follow-up time, but this still needs proper demonstration.

In the OE, the applicant further elaborated on their reasoning to claim for an indication in Gd positive patients. The arguments were once again based on lesion growth arrest and clinical stability together with GIS being kept at a minimum level as a surrogate of BBB stability and possible progression to Gd-resolution. The possible impact of leriglitazone on lower lesion burdens and better clinical status was once again reiterated as having a potential impact on HSCT outcome for those patients, which was confirmed by the statement of clinical experts present at the OE. Despite the arguments that were provided, the existing evidence to support an indication in Gd-positive patients still remained questionable to grant an indication.

Results on exploratory plasma biomarkers and advanced MRI imaging markers (DTI and DSC MRI) support the radiological findings, indicating lesion stabilisation. Plasma NfL concentrations, a biomarker of axonal damage, stabilised in most subjects, following a profile parallel to the change in lesion volume. Plasma MMP-9 concentrations, a marker related to BBB disruption in cALD patients, decreased in subjects with higher baseline levels and stayed stable for the remaining subjects. Furthermore, MRI-based markers indicated preserved tissue integrity and improved microvascular function in the brain, both in lesional and distant white matter, suggesting reduction in lesional inflammatory activity contributes to lesion stability, and supporting the anti-inflammatory and neuroprotective effect of leriglitazone.

The observed reductions in radiological disease progression translated to clinical stability. All subjects remained clinically stable for the entire study follow-up, all subjects had changes in NFS ≤1 and remained free of MFD. Additionally, the estimated probability of remaining MFD-free for subjects on leriglitazone was 100% at all timepoints, compared to approximately 80%, 70%, and 55% chances of remaining MFD-free at Weeks 24, 48, and 96 for untreated natural history patients, showing the positive effect of leriglitazone on the MFD-free survival.

No subjects died while on treatment with leriglitazone, including those with follow-up beyond 96 weeks, while survival probability for untreated natural history patients was approximately 80% at W96.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Although not part of the endpoints of the study, data supporting clinical and radiological stability beyond Week 96 is also provided for 5 subjects (up to Week 120) and 2 beyond Week 120. Different periods of additional follow-up information were shared ranging from 24 to 48 weeks, with one patient data up to W240 post-treatment. Overall, there was a stabilization of Loes, Gis and NFS in these patients except for the patient with the higher disease burden at enrolment there was a progressive worsening of the disease.

## Comparison with NH cohorts

The Applicant presented the NH cohort population for the assessment of efficacy. The Mallack et al. (2021) data represents the most comparable known NH dataset to the population recruited within MT-2-02. This dataset was made available during the conduct of NEXUS study. Initially the external control arm was planned to be based on a database at the University of Minnesota however during the study it was changed to natural history data generated by Mallack et al (2021) as it was thought to be more robust and similar to the NEXUS population. The decision was taken prior to database lock. However, it appears that different sets were used for different endpoints and selection of these sets is not clear. Overall, external control arm cannot be considered sufficiently pre-specified as there is no pre-approved protocol specifying data sources, eligibility criteria, matching variables, endpoints and statistical methods.

Based on the submitted documentation, the NH external arm was employed for comparison for the OS, MFD, lesion volume analyses, Loe's scores and other exploratory endpoints.

A higher Loes stabilisation was observed in NEXUS compared to NH with a difference estimated to be 31.0% with stable Loes score (95% CI -2.5, 64.5%, p=0.0473), although the applicant highlights a potential important caveat as the follow-up was shorter in the NH group, leading to a higher percentage of patients with missing classification and potentially under-estimating the stabilization rate in the NH group.

The observed improvement in the primary endpoint of arrested disease was limited to the Gdnegative subgroup. The applicant argued that leriglitazone interfered with Gd negative lesion progression to Gd positive lesions, and that this should be regarded as a therapeutic effect, considering the rate of progression seen on the NH cohort within the same time frame of the trial. From a clinical point of view, this non (or slower) progression of Gd neg lesions could be regarded as surrogate for disease stabilization. The applicant elaborated on the expected progression rate of Gd negative lesions of NH patients matching the inclusion criteria of the NEXUS study and conclude that the reported lesion volume growth in NH was of +26%/month, way above the rate seen in the NEXUS study. Almost all (94%) NH patients with Gd negative lesion evolved to Gd positive lesions on average within 20 months. However, uncertainty remains with regards to baseline differences between the Nexus population and NH. Mallack et al 2021 included patients that subsequently were confirmed to have progressive disease based on two consecutive MRI scans while Nexus Gd-negative participants did not have such confirmation. To address these concerns the applicant argued that enrolment of Gd- negative patients into the Mallack et all (2021) cohort was not guided by their progressive status and various analyses were provided to ensure comparability between the two cohorts. This was accepted.

For the MFD-free survival analysis, individual patient-level data for the comparator arm were reconstructed from Raymond et al. (2019), and survival outcomes were compared with those from MIN-102 using Kaplan-Meier curves and a log-rank test. However, the representativeness of this comparator cohort for the NEXUS study population remains uncertain due to limited description of the natural history (NH) group, which comprised 72 untreated patients. For overall survival, the NH baseline was defined as the value closest to the date of cALD diagnosis, which differs from the baseline definition used in the NEXUS study. The NH cohort included 72 untreated boys with cALD

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

aged 3-15 years, of whom 25 (39%) had a baseline Loes score &gt;9, 38 (74.5%) were MFD-free, and 21 (29%) were Gd+ at baseline, indicating a more severe disease profile than the NEXUS population. Patients were included if they had been followed for at least 2 years after diagnosis or until death. For analyses of change from baseline, baseline was defined as the value closest to the date of cALD diagnosis, within a window of 6 months before to 3 months after diagnosis. As individual patient data from Raymond et al were not available, only descriptive cross-study comparisons could be made between the NH cohort and the MT-2-02 (NEXUS) study population.

The applicant acknowledging this limitation proposed a restriction of the indication to patients without Major Functional Disability (MFD = 0). It can be agreed that patients with MFD were not enrolled in the NEXUS study and that no evidence was generated to support the indication. Nevertheless, the less severe disease status of the NEXUS vs NH population (and subsequent doubts about full spectrum of disease severity representation in the NEXUS study) and the effect observed in patients in NEXUS leads to questioning whether an effect of leriglitazone has been demonstrated in patients with a disease severity even below the timepoint of MFD development. The applicant proposed the use of CU data (patients with higher NFS score) to allow extrapolation to other patients with a broader NFS range whilst still MFD-free. But this CU data is uncontrolled and rather variable and can only be seen as merely supportive and not definitively demonstrating a clinical effect.

## ADVANCE study (part 1 and 2/OLE)

The Advance study (MT-2-01) was submitted as a supportive study. It was a phase 2/3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in 116 adult male patients with adrenomyeloneuropathy (AMN), who received either leriglitazone (77) or placebo (39). The design of this study was generally supported in scientific advice. Participants with either a normal brain MRI or a type-1 through type-5 pattern MRI abnormality in which the abnormality did not show presence of inflammation were included while patients with gadolinium-enhancing lesions were excluded from the main study.

The study failed to prove the primary endpoint, but the sponsor gathered data on some of the study pre-defined secondary endpoints to support the cALD indication. These endpoints included cerebral lesion progression based on cerebral MRI (Incidence of Gd positive lesions; Growth of existing Gd negative lesions since Screening or Baseline; Occurrence of new Gd negative lesions after Screening or Baseline). Clinical endpoints related to cALD were also assessed, such as EDSS, Clinical Global Impression-Improvement (CGI-I), CGI-Severity (CGI-S), Patient Global Impression-Improvement (PGI-I), and European Quality of Life 5 Dimensions (EQ-5D-5L). Progressive cALD was reported as a serious adverse event (SAE) having been captured as such. Exploratory objectives explored the effects of leriglitazone on various biochemical markers in plasma such as NfL and MMP-9. Overall, the endpoints are not aligned with those that were assessed as part of the NEXUS study.

Of the 116 randomised patients, thirty-one (31) had Gd negative lesions at baseline (18 in the leriglitazone group, 13 in the placebo group). In addition, although patients with Gd positive lesions were meant to be ineligible, 2 patients were erroneously enrolled (1 in each treatment group).

A total of 11 patients showed cerebral lesion progression during the double-blind phase of the study. In the placebo arm, this affected 8 out of the 39 patients (20.5%) while in the active arm, it only affected 3 out of 77 patients (3.9%). Only patients in the placebo arm developed post-baseline Gd positive lesions (4 patients; 10.2%). However, it needs to be noted that placebo patients had longer disease duration and worse MRI scores and clinical score at baseline.

The differences observed for cerebral lesion progression are aligned with the results in cerebral lesion progression in terms of Loes scoring. The patients in the placebo group showed a higher mean (SE) increase in Loes score (0.696 [0.202]) when compared with patients treated with leriglitazone (0.114

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

[0.151]). Although not having significant expression, of the 2 enrolled patients with Gd positive lesions, only the one on placebo showed disease evolution measured by Loes score evolution.

Although not reaching significance a trend toward superiority of leriglitazone treated patients over placebo was claimed on several outcomes assessing clinical improvement and functionality.

Progressive cALD was reported as an SAE, if clinically diagnosed by the investigator during the study and was not a predefined endpoint, and as such subject to less accuracy of assessment and reporting. Only 6 patients (all placebo treated) were reported with progressive cALD which the sponsor defends as representing a meaningful difference among treatment arms (p=0.001; two-sided Fisher's exact test). The discrepancy of theses 6 patients with those described to have cerebral lesion growth in the RMI is justified by the fact that all lesion growth was reported even if such changes were small.

Plasma NfL levels and MMP-9 changes from baseline reflected the higher incidence of disease evolution in placebo when compared to leriglitazone treated patients.

In order to further explore the effect of treatment on patients with brain lesions compared to patients with no lesions, an additional subgroup analysis was conducted on patients from ADVANCE namely by stratifying patients by lesion status at baseline. Descriptive results are presented for the efficacy endpoints. Leriglitazone receiving groups had lower number of patients showing cerebral lesion progression (MRI central readers) in both subgroups. In the subgroup with brain lesions, the incidence of cerebral lesion progression assessed by the central reader was 15.8% leriglitazone vs 35.7% (placebo). Clinical diagnosis of progressive cALD was only made in patients receiving placebo. Under placebo, 12% patients who had no visible lesions at baseline (non-cALD) and 21% who had brain lesions at baseline (cALD) were diagnosed with progressive cALD. In both subgroups, the Loes score increase was larger in the placebo arm driven by patients developing progressive cALD. For NfL, placebo patients showed an increase of levels at Week 96 in both subgroups whereas for leriglitazone treated patients NfL was stable. Although the results are indicative of leriglitazone effect, uncertainties related the study design and included population prevent convincing conclusions about efficacy. ADVANCE OLE (MT-2-01 Part 2) was an open-label treatment extension study that could enrol all patients completing Part 1 and started immediately after the day of last treatment in Part 1 at V6 (after 96 weeks of double-blind treatment). Patients were evaluated with 144 weeks of daily administration of study medication. Two groups were set: those keeping leriglitazone in part 1 and 2 (LL) and those switching to leriglitazone in part 2 after having been treated with placebo in part 1 (PL).

Patients in the LL group showed an annualised incidence of cerebral lesion progression of 1.7 patients per year (3.0%) while for patients in the P group in Part 1 this was of 4.0 patients per year (10.3%).

The incidence per year of cerebral lesion progression was broadly similar (around 2 to 3%) in patients following treatment in leriglitazone in Part 1 (L), and those treated with leriglitazone in Part 2 (LL and PL).

During the OLE for the LL group there was no significant worsening in the annualised LS mean (SE) change of Loes score from baseline. In contrast, for the PL group, significant changes were observed in both the double-blind phase and the OLE, although the increase in the OLE was less than in the double-blind phase.

In EDSS step during the OLE, there was a parallel progression in both arms consistent with leriglitazone having a disease-modifying effect. This points out also to the benefit of early treatment as a higher improvement can be expected before disease installation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Adding to 6 cases reported in part 1, after 5 years of follow-up including the double-blind phase and the 3 years of OLE, only 2 additional cases of progressive cALD have been reported - one in PL and one in LL, none with Gd enhancement.

The Open-label part of the ADVANCE study further demonstrated the disease progresses with time, nevertheless, at a lower rate in patients treated with leriglitazone, either since part 1 (3% vs 10.3% in the placebo arm in part 1) or when switching from placebo in the OL study part (around 2-3%). The results also seem to stress the interest of early treatment initiation. NfL and MMP-9 plasma levels evolution were in accordance with clinical and MRI assessed cerebral lesion evolution.

The Applicant claimed that the results from ADVANCE complement those from NEXUS in paediatric patients and support the indication in adult patients as they provide evidence of drug efficacy in adult patients with cALD at earlier stages of lesion evolution (i.e., those with Gd negative lesions) and provide evidence of efficacy in patients without visible brain lesions (outside the scope of the target MAA indication).

Nevertheless, study limitations can be pointed out to this study that compromise the robustness of the generated data and impair a clear comparison with NEXUS study results and the implementation of the proposed extrapolation strategy.

The ADVANCE only included patients with Gd negative cerebral lesions, as per the exclusion criteria, patients with Gd positive had to be excluded. Two patients with Gd positive lesions were wrongly included in the study. They were assigned one to each treatment arm and the patient on leriglitazone had a more favourable evolution, but the results do not allow for clear conclusions in this subset of patients. The impact of lack of controlled data generated in adult patients with Gd positive cerebral lesions was addressed with the Applicant proposing a restriction of the indication to adult patients with Gd negative lesions. The ADVANCE study lacks a clear cALD clinical status targeted endpoint. So, even meaningful impact on LOES score seen in leriglitazone treated patients with Gd negative cerebral lesions has no clear clinical correlate assessed in the trial which might impair the clinical meaningfulness assessment of the treatment despite the results in LOES.

The endpoints in the ADVANCE study are not aligned to the ones used in the NEXUS study. The applicant acknowledged the discrepancy of endpoints between the studies which are justified by the different study designs and methodological strategies. IN NEXUS the outcome was focused on determining patients showing arrested disease (clinically and radiologically), in ADVANCE the outcome was focused on determining patients showing progression.

The applicant considers that the radiological criteria can be reasonably compared among the two studies and that the diagnosis of progressive cALD, collected as an SAE in ADVANCE, provides an additional surrogate for clinical and radiological deterioration in those patients.

There are still some concerns related to the different way these radiological endpoints were collected between the two studies. All lesion growth in ADVANCE study was considered relevant, as opposite to the way this lesion growth was assessed in the NEXUS study that only considered as progressive lesions those with growth above the threshold expected as part of the NH. If the ADVANCE's study criteria for lesion growth were used in the NEXUS study, probably the rate of arrested disease would be lower than the one reported in NEXUS. Both studies used the Loes score as a secondary outcome, and in both a treatment effect was observed on this measure of lesion load. Similarly, in both studies there was an assessment of plasma NfL, a biomarker of axonal injury, and in both studies results on NfL paralleled those of radiological measures of lesion load and further supported a treatment effect.

Different clinical and PRO tools were used in the two studies but assessing similar concepts.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

NEXUS results demonstrate an increase in arrested disease rates for patients treated with leriglitazone, and ADVANCE results demonstrate a reduced progression on patients treated with leriglitazone. The applicant considers that despite the difference's, key endpoints such as Loes score and NfL are directly comparable across the two studies.

Contrary to NEXUS study, in the ADVANCE study progressive cALD was reported as an SAE, only if clinically diagnosed by the investigator during the study and it was not a predefined endpoint, subject to less accuracy of assessment and reporting.

It is agreed that conceptually the two studies may provide information regarding disease stabilization, but direct comparison is not possible for many of the assessed endpoints. As each of the study intents to support indication in each of the population age cohort this might not be a limitation. On the other side, trying to combine all the information from the two studies in an extrapolation exercise to cover the missing age cohorts (see discussion on adolescent population and extrapolation exercise below) might be impaired.

It was clarified that in the ADVANCE study MRI assessments were conducted at specific pre-defined time-points as for NEXUS study.

When assessing the baseline population of the ADVANCE study, it is stated that there were differences between the placebo and leriglitazone treated group, namely on Loes (slightly higher baseline Loes observed in the placebo group compared to the leriglitazone group). The applicant justifies the baseline placebo group higher Loes score with an outlier patient. The applicant could not provide data supporting the expected NH progression rate in adult patients to assess if the ADVANCE population would be representative of the NH cohort, as per progression in placebo arm patients.

The placebo group clinical deterioration rate (15%) is below the expected by natural history in adults (30-40%) raising concerns on true representation of the target population. The applicant has not provided convincing evidence to support that ADVANCE population is representative of the adult NH population considering the different placebo group deterioration rate. This impairs data interpretation of the ADVANCE and generalization to the adult population.

Only the baseline Loes score data is available for both paediatric and adult studies. Loes score was higher in the adults with Gd negative lesions in ADVANCE compared to children with Gd negative lesions in NEXUS (5.23 vs 1.78). Adults tend to have a higher degree of atrophy which results in higher Loes Score, as this score takes into account both lesions and atrophy. Also, Adults have higher disease arrest rates and as such a higher lesion load is expected at enrolment. ADVANCE study allowed enrolment irrespective of Loes Score which were therefore higher in the adult study. Paediatric patients were diagnosed at an earlier stage of the disease and had lower Loes Score. It seems that ADVANCE and NEXUS populations clearly enrolled patients with different levels of disease severity.

Addressing the possible limitations of the representativeness of ADVANCE study data, the Applicant considered that the outcome of the different secondary endpoints in the ADVANCE study related to cALD could support a treatment effect in adults. Granting an indication merely based on fulfilment of secondary endpoints of a study not initially designed for that purpose is questioned. This, together with the not yet fully clarified differences between the ADVANCE and NH population makes the whole strategy questionable. The applicant decided to withdraw the adult indication.

## COMPASSIONATE USE (CU)

The global CU cohort includes a large and diverse set of patients covering all ages groups, Gd negative and Gd positive lesions and patients with early disease (low lesion load /mild symptoms) and advanced disease (high lesion load and/or advanced symptoms). Follow-up MRI of ≥3 months is available for 67 patients (30 adults, 37 boys), which is the basis for the assessment of lesion stabilization. Lesion stabilization has been reported by the investigators for the majority of patients with post-baseline data. Moreover, radiological stabilization was generally accompanied by clinical stability in patients with low lesion load.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Benefits have been described not only in lesion control but also in progression of disease impact and survival with clear higher impact in those patients treated at earlier stages of the condition. Data from a subgroup of 13 adult male patients with post-baseline data from the French cohort revealed radiologic stabilization and even Gd enhancement improvement with decision to delay HSCT.

Of the 36 participants in the Paediatric FAS (pFAS), 17 were classified as having Early-Stage disease at baseline (Loes score &gt;0 and ≤10, MFD-free) and 19 as having Advanced-Stage disease (Loes score&gt;10 or &gt;0 MFDs). Patients had a higher disease burden at treatment initiation and were older compared to the NEXUS population. Due to the start of patient enrolment to the NEXUS study, the subset of patients with early-stage disease getting access to treatment via CU and feeding these results diminished interfering with the long-term data collection regarding this subset of patients. Benefits have also been described in children regarding lesion control, progression of disability and survival with a trend toward higher impact in those patients treated at earlier stages of the condition.

Results showed slower disease progression in early-stage patients compared to advanced stage but is considered as supportive only.

Regarding the results from CU, considerable differences exist in the baseline population status, endpoints that were assessed, time of assessment, MRI frequency which impairs full comparison of CU data with the data derived from the 2 clinical studies. The fact that most of the paediatric and adult patients have been treated under approved protocols with data captured in an eCRF and in treatment centres that were involved in the clinical studies is seen as a strength of the CU collected data. As such, CU data may be regarded as supportive, but due to methodological limitations, direct comparison of data and outcomes to the data derived from clinical studies is not regarded as valid.

The applicant acknowledged the broader spectrum of patients treated in the context of CU compared to the ones enrolled in clinical studies. Additional data assessment matching baseline characteristics to the NEXUS population was made.

Adolescent patients have been included in the CUP. Clustered information regarding treatment of adolescents within CU program, stratifying by disease severity at enrolment and matching the NEXUS study baseline characteristics, overall, shown disease and lesion stability in those adolescent patients. Gd negative lesions seem to have a different behaviour with age and adolescents seem to have a more similar to adult than children profile.

The submitted data on CU reflects the data cut-off of 15 October 2024. The applicant updated this information upon request with a total of 22 new patients (19 paediatrics and 3 adults) which started the compassionate use treatment from the previous data cut-off date (15 October 2024) and additional follow-up information for previously treated patients. A consistent trend was seen regarding progression which is slower in the Early-stage group as compared to the Advanced-stage group in both the paediatric and adult populations. An updated follow-up summary of the results per disease severity and age (children, adolescents and adults) was presented. Regarding patients with high lesion / mild symptoms they are mostly all Gd positive patients and the rate of stabilization is around 30% for children and adults and 100% for adolescents.

## Extrapolation strategy for patients 13 to 17 years of age

Patients above 13 years of age are not fully covered by the clinical programme, so the trial population is not fully representative of the target population. The Applicant suggested a disease progression model to support the extrapolation of available data from children aged 2-12 years and adults to adolescent cALD patients aged 13-17 years. Radiological data from the natural history were used to develop the model which was compared with radiological changes observed in leriglitazone treated patients from NEXUS and the compassionate use programme (CUP). The model showed that disease progression is similar between paediatric, adolescent and adult progressive patients when patients with the same baseline radiological disease severity are compared. The validity of the extrapolation strategy was challenged considering the discrepancies between adult and paediatric clinical studies that generated the existing efficacy data (disease status/burden at enrolment, assessed endpoints and timing of assessment) and the known different disease progression rates in adult vs children. Data suggest that Gd-negative adolescents are more similar to adults than children. As such, extrapolation from children to adolescents was therefore questioned. The Applicant acknowledged the comments from CHMP and proposed an extrapolation exercise from adults to adolescents with the information of only 5 adolescents from CU. The exercise could only be considered if data from adult (ADVANCE study) were considered valid which is not the case. Because of the decision to drop the adult indication, the applicant has also decided to drop the adolescent indication.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Disease evolution, HSCT need and impact of better fit status at the time of HSCT

Data from the different clinical studies and compassionate use demonstrates that this is an evolving condition even with the proposed treatment. Although the pace of disease progression seems to be positively impacted by leriglitazone, even the treated patients, irrespective of their age, baseline clinical or cerebral lesion status have shown progression. Data from the OLE in adults also showed this progression. As such, reaching the point where a HSCT is needed seems to be a matter of time for all patients with cALD. The possibility to delay such a procedure, and even more, of reaching the time of HSCT in a better fit clinical status, one of leriglitazone therapeutic benefit claims, is considered a significant clinical outcome as it is anticipated to impact positively the outcome and/or final survival to the HSCT. This better clinical fitness at the time of HSCT has been demonstrated for leriglitazone treated patients, specifically for paediatric patients. The lack of information on clinical outcome of HSCT in leriglitazone treated patients compared to those not treated with leriglitazone (literature), taking into consideration disease status at the time of transplant decision, impairs confirmation of the improving HSCT outcomes claim following therapy with leriglitazone.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3.10.2. CHMP conclusions on the clinical efficacy

The efficacy of leriglitazone in cALD is only considered established for the 2 - 12 years old Gdnegative patients.

The observed improvement in the primary endpoint of arrested disease was limited to the Gd negative subgroup, despite the uncertainties whether Gd-negative participants would have progressed without leriglitazone treatment during the study period.

The applicant argued that similarly to the NEXUS study, enrolment of Gd-negative patients into the Mallack cohort was not guided by their progressive status. A new sensitive analysis was conducted to assess the possible impact of different time from diagnosis in the NEXUS population, but comparability of progression risk between cohorts remains uncertain. The Applicant has provided relevant additional analyses that reduce concerns regarding the impact of baseline definition and MRI frequency on the lesion-volume comparison, however, these analyses do not fully resolve uncertainty for the Gd-enhancement and Loes-stabilisation comparisons, and residual confounding remains possible, particularly due to the imbalance in baseline lesion volume in the time-to-Gd-enhancement analysis and missing Loes-stabilisation classifications in the NH cohort. Therefore, the external control comparisons should be interpreted with caution. The applicant provided responses trying to address this outstanding issue. The newly provided data added new information that was further discussed during an oral explanation (OE).

- a) In this OE the applicant further elaborated on their reasoning to support the Nexus and NH cohorts similarity, namely by stressing the similarity of age range, disease stage and standard of care of both populations together with a similar methodology on MRI assessment and central readers to determine outcomes
- b) Reinforcing that both cohorts enrolled patients without confirming their progression status
- c) Presenting the arguments that would resolve the concerns on a potential impact of an imbalance in baseline lesion volume in the time-to-Gd-enhancement analysis:
- a. Baseline lesion volume was higher in NEXUS group, so if acting as confounder it would disfavor treatment arm
- b. A new analysis was conducted excluding lesion volume from weighting model with a better overall balance (age SMD = 0.038, loes score SMD = 0.045, lesion volume SMD = 0.164) and similar treatment effect (p=0.0332)
- d) Trying to resolve the concerns of a potential impact of missing Loes-stabilisation classifications in the NH cohort:
- a. The results of a tipping point analysis were discussed assessing all possible scenarios regarding combinations of the missing classifications in both NH and NEXUS cohorts. The only ones that would favour NH group would be biologically highly implausible scenarios. In the most conservative plausible scenario, there was still a 19.5% point estimate in favour of NEXUS
- b. Considering that all patients with missing Loes-stabilisation classifications were Gd positive and as such most possibly not stable, the resulting scenario would result in an expected Loes stabilization difference of 43.5% in NEXUS vs. 12.0% in NH.

The applicant adequately justified the chosen definition of disease arrest rate (10%) and elaborated on the external control comparison for each of the endpoints. The possible (scarce) comparisons, considering available valid control data are acknowledged.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the OE, the applicant, once again, took the opportunity to elaborate on the results from NEXUS study, namely reiterating the consistency of primary and secondary endpoints fulfilness, all pointing to a magnitude of treatment effect above the observed/expected for the NH cohort for the Gd negative patients.

The applicant also reiterated that lesion growth arrest was demonstrated in 6/9, 66.7% (95% CI 29.9%-100%) leriglitazone treated Gd negative patients, with sensitive analysis controlling for possible different MRIs and timings of MRIs. Importantly, all 6 patients remained Gd-negative and clinically stable after 28 to 57 months. The clinical impact of lesion growth arrest was further discussed with the input from clinical experts present at the OE reinforcing that this represents a major improvement in disease control.

With all this in mind and following the discussion and clarifications that resulted from the OE it was agreed that the existing evidence could support an indication for Gd negative cALD patients under exceptional circumstances (to be discussed below under final indication).

The applicant argued that despite no impact has been demonstrated in the primary endpoint of arrested disease for Gd positive population, other results could be seen as a sign of leriglitazone impact on disease control such as lack of significant lesion growth (similar to Gd negative patients), lesion volume, Loes Score, NfL stability. Clinical stability was also seen contrary to what would be expected in the same study timeframe in the NH cohort. The applicant further elaborates on the possible role of leriglitazone for those patients with Gd positive lesions, assigned to HSCT as per standard of care, as an immediately available treatment for those not eligible to transplant or still with a significant period of time to prepare HSCT, with leriglitazone potentially having a role in the meanwhile as a brain lesion/disease progression halt treatment.

The lack of information on clinical outcome of HSCT in leriglitazone treated patients compared to those not treated with leriglitazone (literature), taking into consideration disease status at the time of transplant decision impairs confirmation of the improving HSCT outcomes claim following therapy with leriglitazone.

Overall current evidence was still considered limited to substantiate a clinical benefit in patients with Gd positive lesions. The applicant supported its reasoning on the expectation that Gd enhancement resolution would occur with more follow-up time, but this still needs proper demonstration. Irrespective of this, it is agreed that lesion growth arrest and clinical stability (compared to the expected progression in non-treated Gd positive patients) seem to be improved in leriglitazone Gd positive treated patients. The applicant provided responses trying to address this outstanding issue. The new discussion focused on the impact of leriglitazone on clinical stability based on lesion stability (reduced lesion growth and increased Loes stabilization) and preservation of low GIS for most of patients as a basis to support BBB preservation and potential Gd resolution with more Follow-up time as seen in CU patients. For these CU patients it was not demonstrated that this correlation between BBB preservation/GIS stabilization and Gd resolution.

The applicant argued that leriglitazone may provide a treatment option between cerebral lesion identification and HSCT procedures initiation. Although the rationale for this potential effect might be recognized, as discussed in previous rounds, clear benefit from the HSCT outcome in leriglitazone treated vs non-treated patients has not been demonstrated even if evidence supports that outcomes following HSCT are more favourable when transplantation is performed at lower lesion burden and better clinical status. In order to be an option for those patients non eligible to HSCT a clear benefit had to be demonstrated which is not the case.

In an OE, the applicant further elaborated on their reasoning to claim for an indication in Gd positive patients. The arguments were once again based on lesion growth arrest and clinical stability together with GIS being kept at a minimum level as a surrogate of BBB stability and possible progression to Gd-resolution. The possible impact of leriglitazone on lower lesion burden and better clinical status was once again reiterated as having a potential impact on HSCT outcome for those patients, which was confirmed by the statement of clinical experts present at the OE. Despite the arguments that were provided, the existing evidence to support an indication in Gd-positive patients still remained questionable to grant an indication.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Addressing the possible limitations of the representativeness of ADVANCE study data, the Applicant considered that the outcome of the different secondary endpoints in the ADVANCE study related to cALD could support a treatment effect in adults. Granting an indication merely based on fulfilment of secondary endpoints of a study not initially designed for that purpose is questioned. This, together with the not yet fully clarified differences between the ADVANCE and NH population made the whole strategy questionable.

The Applicant acknowledged the comments from CHMP and proposed an extrapolation exercise from adult to adolescents with the information of only 5 adolescents from CU. After consideration, the applicant decided to withdraw the adult and consequently the adolescent indications.

Regarding the final wording of the proposed indication:

Following the discussion on the use of MFD free criterion possibly covering too broad a spectrum of patients in terms of clinical status at baseline when compared with NEXUS, the Applicant proposed to narrow the indication to Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 independent of Gadolinium enhancement status in brain MRI and Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1.

The proposed restricted indication in children could only be considered acceptable provided full clarification and resolution of the objections on efficacy of both Gd negative and positive patients. Following the OE, it was considered that the doubts related to the Gd negative patients were resolved but the same was not the case for Gd positive patients.

In conclusion, efficacy of leriglitazone in paediatric cALD patients could be considered demonstrated for Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with non Gadolinium (Gd) enhancement lesions (i.e., GD-negative) in brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), with Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1 under exceptional circumstances.

## 5.4. Clinical safety

## 5.4.1. Safety data collection

The safety profile of leriglitazone for the treatment of cALD is primarily based on 5 completed clinical studies:

- Two Phase I: MT-1-01 and MT-1-03;
- Two Phase II/III: NEXUS, the pivotal study (MT-2-02) and ADVANCE (MT-2-01 Part 1);
- One open-label extension: ADVANCE OLE (MT-2-01 Part 2).

Supportive safety data is provided by a Phase II/III clinical trial in patients with Friedreich's ataxia (FRDA) (FRAMES, MT-2-03) and by accumulated experience from compassionate use (CU). CU data are presented for the French and German cohorts. For these cohorts, a descriptive safety analysis was performed based on treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs), including those related to discontinuation, treatment-related AEs, and serious AEs (SAEs).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Across the clinical studies, safety was assessed based on adverse events (AEs), clinical laboratory evaluations, vital signs, physical examinations, and 12-lead electrocardiograms (ECGs). In addition, urine cytology and echocardiograms (ECHOs) were performed in the pivotal NEXUS study (MT-2-02), in ADVANCE (MT-2-01 Part 1), in ADVANCE OLE (MT-2-01 Part 2), and in FRAMES (MT-2-03).

No integrated analysis of safety was conducted.

In NEXUS, 2 patients discontinued treatment with leriglitazone to undergo gene therapy. While gene therapy is not specifically listed in the PIP among the predefined early discontinuation criteria, such cases fall within standard ICH-GCP-compliant reasons for discontinuation (e.g., withdrawal of consent, introduction of alternative therapy, or investigator decision in the patient's best interest). These events reflected external clinical decisions unrelated to the study and did not imply any modification of the study design, conduct, or predefined stopping rules.

Overall, these instances of early discontinuation attributable to the initiation of gene therapy do not constitute deviations impacting compliance with the PIP. They did not affect the fulfilment of the agreed sample size requirement, nor did they compromise the integrity, operational conduct, or scientific validity of the NEXUS study.

It is considered that NEXUS was performed in accordance with the agreed PIP, that all required obligations were met, and that the early discontinuations observed-whether explicitly foreseen in the PIP (e.g., HSCT) or arising from standard GCP-compliant clinical decisions (e.g., commencement of gene therapy)-do not represent meaningful deviations from the PIP nor affect the interpretation of study results.

## 5.4.2. Patient exposure

The overall safety database encompasses 293 subjects exposed to leriglitazone, including 227 ALD patients (133 from clinical studies and 94 from compassionate use), 40 healthy male volunteers, and 26 patients with Friedrich's ataxia. The overall global exposure in all indications is 572 patient-years (456 patient years for adults and 116 patient years for paediatrics) and healthy volunteer studies (104 patients with &gt; 2 years and 37 patients with &gt; 5 years).

The overall exposure in ALD is 529 patient years (97 patients with &gt; 2 years and 37 patients with &gt;5 years), with a total adult ALD exposure of 442 patient years and total paediatric ALD exposure of 87 patient years.

## Study MT-1-03

A total of 12 healthy male volunteers in two parallel groups were exposed to leriglitazone. In Part A (n=6), subjects received a single oral dose of 180 mg 14C-leriglitazone hydrochloride as an oral suspension; the actual dose of radioactivity that was administered to the subjects ranged between 3.81 MBq and 3.85 MBq.

In Part B, six (n=6) subjects received a single oral dose of 180 mg leriglitazone hydrochloride as an oral suspension, followed by a 15-minute IV infusion of approximately 100 μg 14C-leriglitazone hydrochloride. The actual dose of radioactivity that was administered to the subjects ranged between 0.111 MBq and 0.113 MBq.

SAS was used for all patients who took at least 1 dose (partial or complete) of leriglitazone.

HSCT: hematopoietic stem cell transplantation, Max: maximum, Min: minimum, N: number of subject in the analysis set; n: number of subjects contributing to the summary, SD: standard deviation. Treatment Duration = Last study drug return date - Date of first administration of study drug. If last study drug return date was unknown, the last known visit prior to HSCT was used.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the NEXUS study, paediatric patients were dosed using a concentration-based approach designed to achieve a predefined target exposure, and no protocol-defined dose reductions were implemented. Consequently, all treated paediatric patients were exposed at the intended target exposure throughout the duration of treatment.

Mean systemic exposure in NEXUS was consistent with the target AUC of approximately 170 µg·h/mL ± 20%, as shown in the MT-2-02 CSR. PopPK simulations indicate that dosing tables accounting for age, weight and height perform similarly, ensuring that most paediatric patients remain within the target range, with 98.8% expected to exceed 100 µg·h/mL.

Accordingly, in NEXUS, duration of exposure directly reflects duration of treatment at the target exposure, being an exposure of 35,17 years (430,69 months).

max: maximum; Min: minimum; n: number of patients in category; N: total number of patients; SD: standard deviation.

Duration (days) = date of last study dose – date of first dose + 1.

Duration (weeks) = (date of last study dose – date of first dose)/7.

In the ADVANCE study, all adult patients initiated treatment with leriglitazone at 10 mL (150 mg), followed after approximately 4 weeks by PK-guided individualisation to achieve predefined target exposure, resulting in an average recommended dose of approximately 12 mL. Protocol-defined dose reductions were prospectively allowed as a safety risk-mitigation measure.

For patients who did not tolerate the target plasma exposure, the Investigator could reduce the study drug dose at any time point. The dose was reduced from the previously administered dose in terms of the corresponding volume rounded to the nearest 0.5 mL. Majority of patients received dose reductions of 30% from the previous dose and a few patients with up to 50% reduction. Patients who did not tolerate the minimum dose (in terms of corresponding volume) were discontinued from the study.

Predicted median and 90% prediction interval (from the 5th percentile to the 95thpercentile) Ctrough and AUC at steady state in adult male patients receiving daily doses of 150 mg (10mL) or 180 mg (12 mL) leriglitazone.

Predictions obtained by simulating with the popPK model (run702n; 20MIN0107\\_Update).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

Despite protocol-defined dose reductions in ADVANCE, the majority of patients maintained systemic exposure within the predicted exposure range for the proposed dose of 10 or 12 mL (150 mg or 180 mg) leriglitazone daily. Specifically, 96% of patients had median trough concentrations up to Week 96 within the 90% prediction intervals, and 92% of patients remained within this range at Week 96, supporting the proposed dose regimen.

Dose adjustments were made in 78 patients overall (88.6%) during Part 2 of the study; 30 patients (90.9%) in the PL group and 48 patients (87.3%) in the LL group. Overall, 10 patients (11.4%) did not experience a dose adjustment during Part 2 (3 patients [9.1%] in the PL group, 7 [12.7%] in the LL group). Of the 23 patients enrolled in the US, 19 (82.6%) experienced a temporary dose adjustment to 0 mL to comply with a US clinical hold between 08-Oct-2021 and 16-Nov-2021.

In ADVANCE study (including the double-blind and open-label extension periods), the long-term exposure within the target exposure range corresponds to 374.83 patient-years of exposure. This represents a 3.9% reduction compared with the total exposure presented in the MAA.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Supportive safety data

## FRAMES (MT-2-03)

FRAMES (MT-2-03) was a double-blind, placebo-controlled study on the effects of leriglitazone on biochemical, imaging, neurophysiological, and clinical markers in male and female patients aged ≥12 and ≤60 years with Friedreich's ataxia. Thirty-nine (39) eligible patients were randomised to treatment for 48 weeks with leriglitazone or placebo in a 2:1 ratio. A total of 39 patients were allocated to a treatment arm, 26 to receive leriglitazone and 13 to placebo. Twenty-two (22) patients were males, and 17 patients were females. Fourteen (14) patients out of the 39 (35.9%) were between 12 and 17 years old, of which 10 patients were assigned to the leriglitazone group. In addition, 25 patients between 19 and 55 years were randomised, of which 16 patients were assigned to leriglitazone. This study completed on 14 September 2020 (last observation) (CSR MT-2-03, dated 29 May 2021).

## Compassionate Use

The global CU programme includes a large and diverse set of patients covering all ages groups, Gd negative and Gd positive lesions and patients with early disease (low lesion load /mild symptoms) and advanced disease (high lesion load and/or advanced symptoms) from centres in France, Spain, Germany, Italy, United Kingdom, Netherlands, Argentina, and Israel. As of a data cut-off of 15 October 2024, 94 male cALD patients (40 adult, 54 boys) have been treated with leriglitazone. Results were reported descriptively by investigators and focussed primarily on efficacy. Safety reporting was requested with provision of forms for reporting however, similar to a post-marketing scenario few reports were received.

## French and German CU cohorts

Safety data was collected from French and German CU cohorts that were subsets of the global CU programme and followed pre-specified treatment protocols approved by the respective national agencies (ANSM and BfArM). Data from these patients were collected in an eCRF thus allowing more in-depth analyses and included more formal safety reporting. Out of the 94 male cALD patients in the global CU programme, data from 71 patients (35 adults, 36 boys; 47 in France [24 adults, 23 boys] and 24 in Germany [11 adults, 13 boys]) have been collected in eCRFs and analysed according to the pre-specified protocol.

Notes: Percentages are based on the number of patients in the safety set as displayed in column header \"N\". An ongoing AE was defined as an AE that started before first administration of study medication in Part 2 and which was ongoing at the first administration of study medication in Part 2. A TEAE was defined as an AE that commenced on or after the first dose of study medication in Part 2. If the AE was present at first dose of study medication in Part 2, then an increase in severity or frequency was considered a TEAE. Patients who reported more than 1 AE within each category were only counted once. If the relationship to study medication was missing for an ongoing AE, then the relationship was taken as \"Related\". If the severity was missing for an ongoing AE, then the severity was taken as \"Severe (Grade 3)\". Follow-up is ongoing for the SAEs of cALD for Patient 102011 and Patient 102018.

This table includes Drug-related Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events with Start Date Greater or Equal to Date of Visit 6. AEs were coded using MedDRA version 23.0.

## 5.4.3.1. Adverse drug reactions

The ADR table proposed for inclusion in the SmPC was derived from the complete dataset of the leriglitazone clinical development programme.

In line with the SmPC Guideline (2009), ADRs are generally defined as AEs for which a causal relationship with the medicinal product is at least a reasonable possibility. In the present application, the SmPC ADR table includes all AEs assessed as at least possibly related to leriglitazone, as well as AEs for which the causality assessment was considered not assessable.

The inclusion of AEs with not assessable causality represents a conservative approach with the intention of ensuring comprehensive and transparent communication of the safety profile.
