## Gender

No specific analysis for gender has been conducted, as leriglitazone is only intended for male cALD patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For the FRAMES trial (MT-2-03), safety and PK data are available for 11 female patients with Friedreich's ataxia treated with leriglitazone for up to 48 weeks, supportive of similar safety profile in women and men. No specific safety differences have been identified between the sexes.

## Extrinsic factors

The safety of leriglitazone with respect to extrinsic factors (e.g., smoking, diet) has not yet been evaluated in clinical studies.

## Pregnancy and Lactation

The use of leriglitazone during pregnancy and lactation has not been evaluated in clinical studies. Nonclinical studies have been conducted to investigate the effects of leriglitazone on embryofoetal development in pregnant rats and rabbits. Leriglitazone was not teratogenic at exposures that exceed those proposed clinically; however, reductions in foetal weight were observed at clinically relevant exposures. These findings are in line with that observed with pioglitazone. The indication currently being applied for concerns male subjects only.

Leriglitazone does not pose a genotoxic risk if used as indicated. In a juvenile rat study, effects on the reproductive tract were noted in females (decreased number of corpora lutea and asynchrony of cycling) but not in males at exposures that were slightly higher than those proposed clinically.

In a fertility and early embryonic development study in female rats, adverse effects on female fertility and early embryonic development were limited to the maximum dose of 50 mg/kg/day, as demonstrated by fewer live embryos on Day 13 of gestation due to a reduction in the mean number of corpora lutea and slight increases in the extent of pre- and post-implantation loss. In a nonclinical fertility study in male rats, no adverse effects for male fertility and male mediated effects on early embryonic development were observed at any dose evaluated.

It is not known whether leriglitazone is present in the milk of lactating women who are taking the drug.

## Overdose

In NEXUS (Study MT-2-02) and ADVANCE (Study MT-2-01) studies, no cases of overdose have occurred with leriglitazone. In FRAMES (Study MT-2-03), one patient was reported to have taken leriglitazone at higher than the recommended highest dose of 150-180 mg daily. This was an 8-fold overdose (1,200 mg/day for one day) as the first dose in a controlled clinical study. Except for mild transient headaches, hot flush, and decreased appetite, no clinically relevant findings were reported. Blood glucose remained within normal range, and there were no ECG changes. Based on this experience, there are no clinically relevant acute symptoms requiring intervention, and monitoring measures of general condition and vital signs are considered appropriate and sufficient.

In the event of overdose, appropriate supportive treatment should be initiated according to the patient's clinical signs and symptoms.

## Drug Abuse

Leriglitazone is not expected to have abuse potential.

## Withdrawal and Rebound

No effects of withdrawal or rebound have been observed in clinical studies with leriglitazone.

Effects on Ability to Drive or Operate Machinery or Impairment of Mental Ability The effect of the treatment with leriglitazone on ability to drive or operate machinery has not been evaluated. However, leriglitazone may have a minor influence on the ability to drive and use machines, as increased lacrimation as well as oedema/swelling of the eyelid causing potential visual disturbances may occur following administration of leriglitazone. Until a patient knows how leriglitazone will affect his sensory, motor, coordination, and cognitive function, he should use caution when performing hazardous tasks such as driving or operating machinery.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.7. Immunological events

Not applicable.

## 5.4.8. Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

Drug interactions with leriglitazone have not yet been evaluated in clinical studies thus, drug-drug interaction (DDI) data are currently lacking. One DDI study has recently been initiated and designed based on the feedback received during the previous MAA procedure, with results expected by mid2026. This study includes the assessment of food effect on administration of the commercial formulation in the DDI study.

A risk assessment for potential DDIs at the level of the transporter between leriglitazone (either as victim or perpetrator) and other medicinal products was performed. In vitro studies identified a potential risk for leriglitazone which was shown to be an inhibitor of hepatic transporters OATP1B1 and OATP1B3. However, application of the PBPK model to assess the extent by which leriglitazone affects the systemic exposures of the OATP1B1/OATP1B3 substrate, rosuvastatin suggests no clinically relevant interaction when the two drugs are administered concomitantly. However, as a precaution, additional assessment of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 inhibition will be obtained by evaluating the effect of leriglitazone on coproporphyrin I/III levels, an endogenous substrate of these transporters, in human samples.

Approximately 64% of leriglitazone is metabolised via cytochrome P450 (CYP)2C8, 30% via CYP3A4 and 6% via other undefined enzymes. As leriglitazone is predominantly metabolised via CYP2C8 and CYP3A4, the safety is unknown in patients who could concomitantly take leriglitazone and drugs that are CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 inhibitors or CYP3A4 inducers, due to the increased or decreased exposure of leriglitazone.

## 5.4.9. Vital signs and laboratory findings

## Vital signs, physical findings, and other observations related to safety

## Vital signs

In MT-1-01, MT-1-03, NEXUS (MT-2-02), ADVANCE (MT-2-01 Part 1) and ADVANCE OLE (MT-2-01 Part 2) studies although several individual changes from baseline were observed, blood pressure, pulse rate, respiratory rate, and body temperature showed no trends or clinically relevant changes.

Weight changes were already discussed in Section 5.4.4. 'AEs of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events'.

## Blood spot glucose test

Patients with diabetes were excluded for participating in the Phase II/III clinical trials, since these persons are likely to be receiving blood-glucose-lowering treatments that, combined with leriglitazone treatment, would predispose them to hypoglycaemia.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## NEXUS (MT-2-02)

One event of hypoglycaemia was reported in one patient. The event was considered to be unrelated to treatment, was of mild severity and was non-serious.

## ADVANCE (MT-2-01 Part 1) and ADVANCE OLE (MT-2-01 Part 2)

There were no reports of hypoglycaemia during the study. For patients in the US only, blood spot glucose tests were performed at all visits (except Visit 1 and Follow-up Visit in Part 1 and Visits 3, 5, 6 and Follow-up Visit). The checks did not yield significant findings.

## Clinical laboratory evaluations, Urine cytology and Electrocardiograms and Echocardiograms

Discussed in Section 4.4. 'AEs of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events'.

## Physical Examinations

## ADVANCE OLE (MT-2-01 Part 2)

One patient in PL group had a clinically relevant abnormal abdomen finding at Week 192, and subsequently an SAE and Grade 3 event of ascites was reported, leading to study discontinuation. No new therapeutic measures were reported as a result of the event. The event was assessed as not related to the study medication but due to chronic underlying liver disease (liver cirrhosis).

Clinically relevant abnormal findings for extremities were reported at all timepoints and were mostly related to the presence of peripheral oedema. Clinically relevant abnormal findings for skin were reported at the majority of time points and mostly concerned the presence of oedema.

## 5.4.10. Post-marketing experience

Leriglitazone is not yet marketed in any country. Compassionate use safety data are described in above sections.

## 5.4.11. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for safety

Not applicable.

## 5.4.12. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.12.1. Discussion

## 5.4.12.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

## Safety data collection

The safety database includes 293 subjects exposed to leriglitazone, including: 227 ALD patients (139 from clinical studies: 23 paediatric cALD patients from the pivotal NEXUS study and 116 adult ALD patients with adrenomyeloneuropathy (AMN) (including 33 cALD adults) from ADVANCE (Study MT2-01) Part 1, 88 who subsequently participated in the open-label extension (OLE) of ADVANCE OLE (Part 2) and 94 from compassionate use in global CU programmes (including 71 patients in French and German cohorts), 40 healthy male volunteers from two Phase I studies and 26 patients with Friedrich's ataxia from one Phase IIa study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall safety database is adequate for cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD), a rare phenotype of adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD).

The applicant presents NEXUS as the pivotal study in paediatric patients and data on adult patients are from the ADVANCE study.

Safety assessment of leriglitazone is primarily based on:

The main sources for the safety evaluation of leriglitazone are:

- NEXUS (defined as the pivotal study by the applicant), a phase II/III, multicentre, open-label, uncontrolled study to assess the efficacy, safety and PK of leriglitazone over 96 weeks in 23 paediatric male patients with cALD. Confirmatory analysis of this study was completed on 12th November 2024 (last observation, data-cut data);

- ADVANCE (part 1), a phase II/III multicentre, double-blind, randomised, placebo-controlled study to assess the efficacy, safety and PK of leriglitazone over 96 weeks, in 116 adult male ALD patients with or without Gd-brain lesions (double-blind part of the study was completed on 16 October 2020 (last observation);

- ADVANCE (part 2), an open-label extension of ADVANCE part 1, to assess the long-term safety, tolerability and efficacy of the leriglitazone over 144 weeks in 88 adult male ALD patients with or without Gd-brain lesions (data cut-off 15 October 2024);

## NEXUS (pivotal study in paediatric patients)

## Patient exposure

In NEXUS, 23 paediatric patients with cALD received at least one partial or complete dose of leriglitazone. For subjects in the safety analysis set, mean (SD) time on treatment with leriglitazone was 76.2 (48.91) weeks, ranging from 5.4 to 192.1 weeks at the time of data cut-off. 9 (39.1%) have at least 96 weeks follow up, but 14 (60.9%) did not have 96 weeks follow up. No subjects discontinued the study for any reason other than undergoing hematopoietic stem-cell transplantation (HSCT) or gene therapy (GT). There no dose modification was required in this study.

Safety findings from the pivotal open label uncontrolled study NEXUS in paediatric population are limited due to limited treatment duration (20 evaluable patients on week 24, only 9 patients have at least 96 weeks of follow up).

According to the Paediatric investigation plan (PIP) EMEA-002106-PIP01-16-M03 at least 13 male patients must be included in the study, treatment duration- 96 weeks, but treatment may be discontinued early according to the PIP predefined criteria, including HSCT, but not including gene therapy. Although PIP predefined criteria did not include gene therapy, the early discontinuation of treatment with leriglitazone to undergo gene therapy of participants 1030-03 and 1030-07 is compliant with applicable GCP regulatory requirements (e.g., ICH E6 (R3) Guideline for good clinical practice (GCP)). The significant impact on safety results of early discontinuation of treatment due to this reason is not expected.

The mean age of subjects at screening in the study NEXUS was 7.7 (SD 2.93) years. Only 1 boy in this study was 3 years old. The Applicant has provided assessment to justify extrapolation of safety data from the NEXUS study population (predominantly ≥4 years) to the target population aged 2-3 years in accordance with the ICH consensus guideline E11A Pediatric Extrapolation recommendations. The justification for safety data extrapolation to paediatric population aged 2-3 years is based on the evaluation of non- clinical data, disease similarity, pharmacology of the medicinal product, expected response to treatment, as well as assessment of age-specific safety considerations. Overall evidence presented by the Applicant allows to extrapolate available safety data to children in age group 2-3 years.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Concomitant diseases were reported in all study patients and were consistent with those expected for the patient population. The most common diseases after ALD were adrenal insufficiency, melanoderma, and asthenia, asthma, and sleep disorder. It should be noted that some of these conditions, e.g., adrenal insufficiency, melanoderma could be ALD related.

All 23 patients in the safety analyses set reported the use of at least 1 prior or concomitant medication.

The overall exposure was presented for all subjects and for ALD patients exposed to leriglitazone. Despite protocol-defined dose reductions in ADVANCE, the majority of patients maintained systemic exposure within the predicted exposure range for the proposed dose of 10 or 12 mL (150 mg or 180 mg) leriglitazone daily. Specifically, 96% of patients had median trough concentrations up to Week 96 within the 90% prediction intervals, and 92% of patients remained within this range at Week 96, supporting the proposed dose regimen.

## Adverse events

The most frequent treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) (occurring in ≥2 subjects) were nasopharyngitis (13 subjects (56,5%)); headache (11 subjects (47.8%)); influenza and abdominal pain (6 subjects each (26.1%)); vomiting, cough, and pyrexia (5 subjects each (21.7%); oropharyngeal pain (4 subjects); gastroenteritis, rhinitis, upper abdominal pain, diarrhoea, nausea, SARS-CoV-2 test positive, acute adrenocortical insufficiency, and decreased vitamin D (3 subjects each); and increased weight, appendicitis, pharyngitis, pneumonia, epistaxis, rhinorrhoea, fatigue, anaemia, leukopenia, neutropenia, eyelid oedema, depression, stress, proteinuria, arthralgia, and pain in extremity (2 subjects each).

A majority of reported TEAEs were mild (265 events) or moderate (22 events). 3 severe TEAEs occurred (appendicitis, vomiting and vascular device infection). All 3 of these severe events were considered TESAEs and required hospitalisation. Overall, 8 subjects were hospitalised. 3 subjects were hospitalised due to the 3 severe TESAEs (appendicitis, vomiting and vascular device infection), but 5 subjects were hospitalised for other TESAEs which were moderate or mild, but were reported as serious on the basis of hospitalisation. None of TESAEs were related to the investigational medicinal product.

Nine (39.1%) subjects experienced 19 TEAEs that were assessed as related to study medicinal product. The treatment related TEAEs were eyelid oedema, weight increased, leukopenia and neutropenia (2 patients each), and oedema peripheral, swelling face, differential white blood cell count abnormal, N-terminal prohormone brain natriuretic peptide increased, prothrombin level increased, international normalised ratio increased, headache, anaemia, lymphopenia, increased appetite and night sweats (1 subject each). The treatment related TEAEs in paediatric population with ADL are similar as observed in adults with ADL. None of these TEAEs required a change in dose of leriglitazone, and 1 event (headache) required administration of a concomitant medication. One treatment related TEAE was considered moderate in severity (night sweats). All other events were assessed as mild.

No deaths were reported in the study NEXUS.

Adverse events of special interest (AESIs) were defined post-hoc, a limitation that was addressed by the applicant. AESIs were identified as events relating to oedema, weight gain, lacrimation, cardiac safety, endocrine events, bone fractures, abnormal urine cytology, safety laboratory events. The mechanism of action is presented as the rationale behind the selection of each AESI. Each AESI was defined using MedDRA version 23.0 preferred terms (PTs) grouped under their corresponding SOCs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

AESI categories were derived to capture adverse events potentially related to the pharmacological class of PPARγ agonists.

The most commonly occurring adverse events of special interest (AESIs) in this study were eyelid oedema and increased weight, which were reported by 2 subjects (8.7%) each. Face swelling and peripheral oedema occurred in 1 subject each (4.3%). No events of lacrimation were reported in NEXUS. No events related to cardiac dysfunction were reported in NEXUS.

In NEXUS, 4 endocrine events were reported - 3 events of acute adrenocortical insufficiency and 1 event of Addison's disease. Two events of adrenocortical insufficiency were considered serious adverse events. All events were not considered to be related to study medicinal product. It should be noted that ADL is often accompanied by adrenocortical insufficiency or Addison's disease.

Weight gain was seen from longitudinal tracking on growth charts, there were no concerns for reduced height gain or excessive height gain during the study.

In NEXUS wrist fracture in 1 patient (4.3 %) observed. This was assessed as not causally related to study medication.

In NEXUS, no clinically significant abnormalities on cytological examination of urothelial cells have been reported.

No clinically relevant changes in laboratory values were reported in NEXUS. Anaemia, leukopenia, neutropenia was reported in 2 patients (8.7%) each.

One event of hypoglycaemia was reported in one patient in NEXUS. The event was considered to be unrelated to treatment, was of mild severity.

There were no withdrawals due to adverse events in the NEXUS.

## ADVANCE

## Patient exposure

In ADVANCE and ADVANCE OLE 110 patients have been exposed to leriglitazone; 77 patients during the placebo-controlled main phase and 33 patients switching from placebo to leriglitazone in the OLE phase.

In ADVANCE total of 96 of 116 subjects completed the placebo-controlled main phase (62/77 [80.5%] in the leriglitazone arm and 34/39 [87.2%] in the placebo arm).

In ADVANCE 20 patients (17.2%) prematurely discontinued from treatment; 15 (19.5%) of these patients were in the leriglitazone group and 5 (12.8%) in the placebo group. The most frequent reasons for premature treatment discontinuation were adverse events (AEs), reported for 8 patients (10.4%) in the leriglitazone group, and 2 patients (5.1%) in the placebo group, and withdrawal of consent, reported with 6 patients (7.8%) in the leriglitazone group, and for 3 patients (7.7%) in the placebo group. For one patient in the leriglitazone group, the reason was reported as 'other' without further information.

TEAEs leading to permanent, or temporary withdrawal from study medication and TEAEs leading to study medication dose adjustment were all more frequent in the leriglitazone group (11.7%, 10.4%, and 44.2% respectively) than in the placebo group (5.1%, 2.6%, and 5.1% respectively).

According to information in the clinical documentation the majority of patients in ADVANCE started at a leriglitazone dose of 150 mg, corresponding to a volume of 10 mL of leriglitazone oral suspension, received dose reductions of 30% from the previous dose and a few patients with up to 50% reduction. TEAEs leading to study medication dose adjustment in the ADVANCE in the leriglitazone group were in 34 patients (44.2%). Despite dose reductions, the subject median leriglitazone trough concentrations up to Week 96 in the ADVANCE study showed that the majority of the patients (96%) remained within the 90% predicted intervals for Ctrough in adult male patients receiving the proposed daily dose of 10 or 12 mL (150 or 180 mg) leriglitazone, supporting the proposed dose regimen. Comparable results were obtained with leriglitazone through concentrations at Week 96, with 92% of patients remaining within the predicted range.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Long-term exposure in the ADVANCE study (including the double-blind and open-label extension periods) is estimated at approximately 374.83 patient-years (4,497.89 months) for patients within the exposure range expected for the proposed dosage regimen. This represents a 3.9% reduction compared with the total exposure presented in the MAA.

Baseline characteristics and demographics (e.g., age, ethnicity, height, weight, and BMI) are in general balanced between the treatment groups.

There was a higher frequency of Addison's disease in the placebo group vs. the leriglitazone group (20.5% vs. 11.7%, respectively), hypercholesterolaemia in the leriglitazone group vs. placebo (13.0% vs. 2.6%, respectively). According to CSR higher frequency of nervous system disorders was in placebo group 30% vs 18,2 % in the leriglitazone group, musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders 33.3% in placebo group vs 13.0% in leriglitazone group. Higher frequency of immune system disorders (allergies) was in leriglitazone group 13.0% vs 2.6% in placebo group. These imbalances may lead to imbalances in adverse event reporting.

It should be noted that certain conditions (e.g., adrenal insufficiency, Addison's disease, erectile dysfunction) which are mentioned as concomitant diseases can be considered as part of the ADL.

Concomitant medications were reported for 114/116 patients (98.3%) in the safety population overall. The most common concomitant medications were corticosteroids for systemic use (65.5%), analgesics (50.9%) and urologicals (48.3%). A larger difference was noted for diuretics (2.6% of patients in the placebo group and 20.8% in the leriglitazone group).

## Adverse events

Overall, 112/116 patients (96.6%) reported at least 1 TEAE with a slightly higher frequency in the leriglitazone group vs. placebo (76 patients (98.7%) vs. 36 patients (92.3%)).

The most common adverse events that were reported more frequently with leriglitazone treatment (&gt;10% difference with placebo) in the leriglitazone group vs. placebo: weight increased (70.1% vs. 23.1%), oedema peripheral (63.6% vs. 17.9%), nasopharyngitis (22.1% vs. 5.1%), lacrimation increased (18.2% vs. 0.0%), oedema (14.3% vs. 0.0%) and anaemia (13.0% vs. 2.6%).

The applicant presented all TEAEs that were considered treatment-related and occurring in ≥ 2% of all patients. The most frequently reported adverse drug reactions (&gt;10%) by PT in the leriglitazone group compared to placebo were increased weight (64.9% vs. 15.4%), peripheral oedema (62.3 % vs. 15.4%) and increased lacrimation (15.6% vs. 0%). Although increased weight and oedema are known treatment-related events for PPARγ agonists, increased lacrimation is not listed for pioglitazone.

The majority of TEAEs reported were mild (28.4% overall) or moderate (53.4%) in severity (Grade 1 or 2). Fewer severe TEAEs (Grade 3) were reported in in the leriglitazone group: 8 patients (10.4%) in the leriglitazone group and 8 patients (20.5%) in the placebo group. Severe TEAEs reported for more than one patient in a treatment group were ankle fracture (two patients in the leriglitazone group), both post-traumatic and assessed by the investigator as not related to study drug, and adrenoleukodystrophy (four patients in the placebo group). PT adrenoleukodystrophy was used for encoding reported AE terms related to progression of cALD. One patient in the leriglitazone group was reported with a life threatening (Grade 4) TEAE (anaphylactic shock, caused by food allergy and exercise) which was deemed not related to study drug by the Investigator, which can be agreed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No deaths were reported in the study ADVANCE.

It was observed that a higher frequency of ADRs occurs during the first six months of leriglitazone treatment. The SmPC includes specific monitoring recommendations for this initial treatment period.

Adverse events of special interest (AESIs) were defined post-hoc and were identified as events relating to oedema, weight gain, lacrimation, cardiac safety, endocrine events, bone fractures, abnormal urine cytology, safety laboratory events.

Weight gain and peripheral oedema were reported in a high percentage of patients treated with leriglitazone (70.1% and 63.6%, respectively).

The total number of patients having oedema-related events in the double-blind phase of ADVANCE was 68 (60 patients on leriglitazone group and 8 in placebo group). Of these events, oedema peripheral was the most frequently occurring event, reported in 49 patients (63.6%) in the leriglitazone group and in 7 patients (17.9%) in the placebo group. Patients in both treatment groups were treated with concomitant corticosteroids, which are also known to cause oedema thus could explain the oedema increase in placebo group.

Mean increase in weight from baseline was 5.8 kg for the leriglitazone group vs 1.3 kg for the placebo group. Maximum increase in weight was comparable between both groups, 14.6 kg vs. 14.0 kg for the leriglitazone and the placebo group, respectively. Maximum loss of weight was also comparable between both treatment groups with a reduction of up to 5.0 vs. 5.5 kg for the leriglitazone and the placebo group, respectively. However, according to final reports of the study 34 (44.2%) patients reported that the weight increased had not recovered/not resolved.

Dose reductions were not shown to be effective in changing of the weight increase in the study. It should be noted that 5 leriglitazone-treated patients (6.5%) dropped out of the study due to oedema or weight gain.

As mentioned, oedema was one of the most reported ADR and managed in ADVANCE study with dose reductions and diuretics. The applicant presented and discussed the data supporting the recommendation not to reduce the dose or use diuretics.

In ADVANCE (MT-2-01 Part 1, Safety Set) study, the concomitant use of diuretics differed significantly between the placebo and leriglitazone arms (2,6% and 20,8% respectively). The applicant does not recommend using diuretics to manage oedema because they can decompensate patients who are already receiving hormone replacement treatment for adrenal insufficiency. This recommendation was discussed and supported by data. To date, no instances of severe oedema have been reported with leriglitazone treatment. Oedema events observed in the ALD clinical programme have been classified as mild or moderate in severity. Consequently, there is currently no clinical experience in managing severe oedema events, so the applicant recommends discontinuing leriglitazone treatment in such cases.

Lacrimation was reported only in patients treated with leriglitazone (14 patients (18.2%)) and not in patients in the placebo group. All events were determined as non-serious and mild in severity, except three events which were assessed as moderate in severity. One patient discontinued the study drug due to mild lacrimation two months after the event onset. The possible mechanism of action of increased lacrimation by leriglitazone/thiazolidinediones based on literature data is described by the applicant.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Acute adrenocortical insufficiency was reported more often in the placebo (10.3%) than in the leriglitazone (2.6%) group. It is not considered treatment-related but anticipated as progression of the disease. Cardiac-related events were deemed isolated events, and the development of oedema/weight gain did not lead to an increase the risk of cardiac failure in this population. Palpitations (3.9%) and tachycardia (2.6%) were the most frequently observed cardiovascular AEs and only reported in the leriglitazone group. Palpitations are included in the SmPC section 4.8. None of cardiac events were considered be related to treatment in ADVANCE OLE. In ADVANCE by Week 48, five patients developed a QTc interval &gt;450 msec and ≤480 msec, of these, four patients (5.2%) were in the leriglitazone group and one patient in the placebo group. In all cases there were concomitant medications that are known to be associated with QTc prolongation. There was a case of a patient that reported mild raised jugular venous pressure and mild hepatomegaly, which led to study drug adjustment. The Investigator considered the events of 'raised jugular venous pressure' and 'hepatomegaly' to be mild in severity and related to study drug. The Investigator confirmed the relatedness of AEs mild hepatomegaly and mild raised JVP to the study drug as consequences of fluid retention and oedema only after starting the leriglitazone. The applicant detailed the physical evaluation measures for monitoring fluid retention in the SmPC, which include: a) assessment of jugular venous pressure; and b) physical examination for hepatomegaly. There was a higher incidence of bone fractures with leriglitazone treatment compared to controls in ADVANCE: 9 events of fractures occurred in 8 patients; 6/8 patients were in the leriglitazone group and 2/8 patients in the placebo group. Four of the 9 events (1 in placebo and 3 in leriglitazone group) were reported as SAEs. In ADVANCE OLE 14 events in 9 patients from the leriglitazone group during double-blind part, and 7 events in 5 patients from the placebo group during double-blind part) have been reported. All events were considered to be not related to treatment. Three events (1 ankle fracture and 2 femoral neck fractures) were of Grade 3 severity. Bone fracture as adverse reaction and risk of fractures is listed for pioglitazone. The risk of bone fractures as class effect of PPARγ agonists has been included in the SmPC section 4.4. The PL has been updated accordingly. However, it was further recommended to amend the proposed text for the risk of bone fractures as class effect of PPARγ agonists in the SmPC section 4.4. The incidence of urine cytology abnormal is comparable between the placebo and the leriglitazone group: 12.8% vs 10.4%. In ADVANCE OLE, abnormal urine cytology findings were reported as TEAEs in 10 patients (11.4%). Three of these events (all from patients initially randomised to placebo) were considered treatment related. There were no findings in the study suspicious of bladder cell malignancies. Risk of bladder cancer is included in the SmPC. In ADVANCE changes in haematological parameters from baseline over the 96-week treatment period were generally small and assessed as not clinically significant by investigators. Anaemia was reported most frequently in the leriglitazone group (leriglitazone: 10 patients (13.0%), placebo: 1 patient (2.6%)), followed by macrocytosis (5.2% in the leriglitazone group), decreased haemoglobin, leukopenia, and pancytopenia (each 3.9% in the leriglitazone group), versus no such findings in the placebo group. Haematological events are included in the SmPC as adverse reactions.

No events of hypoglycaemia were reported in ADVANCE. However, in the proposed SmPC the applicant states that 'Leriglitazone should not be used in patients with blood-glucose-lowering treatments, due to the increased risk of hypoglycaemia.'. This recommendation was precautionary, considering the lack of clinical data in diabetic patients and the theoretical risk of hypoglycaemia when leriglitazone is combined with blood-glucose-lowering agents.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In ADVANCE vital signs were generally comparable between the two treatment groups. One SAE related to the study medication was reported in ADVANCE study. This refers to a patient who was diagnosed with Gilbert's syndrome during the study. Liver enzymes levels normalised, and the patient continued treatment at a reduced dose without further transaminase increases, subsequently taking part in the OLE study. No other SAEs related to IMP were reported and no pattern emerged in the reported SAEs.

In clinical development there were 5 deaths (1 in 3-year OLE and 4 during the long-term follow-up phase [after the 3 year OLE]), none related to IMP. Four patients died after diagnosis of progressive cALD. One patient had a history of depression prior enrolled the study and was found dead at home following suicide.

## ADVANCE OLE

In ADVANCE OLE 50 of the 88 (Placebo- Leraglitazone N=33, Leraglitazone- Leraglitazone N= 55 patients) (56.8%)) completed the 144-week open-label extension. At the cut-off date 1 patient was ongoing in the ADVANCE OLE.

Demographics, medical history at baseline, concomitant diseases were generally aligned with those reported in Part 1 of the study. Concomitant medications reported during ADVANCE OLE were generally aligned with those reported in Part 1 of the study, apart from vaccines which were reported in 60.2% patients overall, balanced between the treatment groups. The increased number of patients having received vaccines was explained as likely due to receipt of the COVID-19 vaccination.

Overall, 83/88 patients (94.3%) reported at least one TEAE. Generally, the distribution was similar between groups; however, a greater proportion (&gt;10% difference) of patients in the placebo-toleriglitazone group vs. leriglitazone-to-leriglitazone group reported: oedema peripheral (54.5% vs. 21.8%); weight increased (45.5% vs. 14.5%); lacrimation increased (30.3% vs. 12.7%); urinary tract infection (18.2% vs. 5.5%); and anaemia (18.2% vs. 3.6%). A greater number of patients in the leriglitazone-to-leriglitazone group (32.7%) reported a COVID-19 compared to the placebo-toleriglitazone group (21.2%). The most frequently reported treatment-related TEAEs were similar those reported in Part 1 of the study: oedema peripheral (34.1% patients), weight increased (25.0% patients) and lacrimation increased (18.2% patients).

In ADVANCE Part 2 (3-year OLE) there was 1 death. After the 3 years OLE, during the long-term follow-up phase, there were 4 deaths. None of them are related to leriglitazone.

Overall, and in accordance with Scientific Advisory Group meeting, convened under the scope of the re-examination procedure from a prior submission, and from a patient perspective, these adverse drug reactions are not disregarded but are considered to be proportional considering to the severity of the condition. This position is endorsed.

## Safety in special populations

## Paediatric patients

Safety data in children (&gt;2 to ≤12 years) from study NEXUS has been compared to safety data in adults (≥ 18 years) from ADVANCE. Adolescents (&gt;13 to &lt;17 years) data are provided from FRAMES study (MT-2-03, in patients with Friedreich's ataxia) (N=5, in addition 5 enrolled into leriglitazone arm in 48 week study)), compassionate use (N=11 at treatment initiation), additionally from NEXUS (N=4) becoming &gt;13 years during study. Available evidence on safety data shows that there no significant differences in treatment related adverse events (oedema, weight gain was commonly reported), their severity across age groups (children, adolescents, adults) of patients with ALD, but there are differences in frequency. There were no age-specific safety events identified. However, there are limited information to make conclusions on long-term safety in the different subgroups of the paediatric population. Long-term effects of treatment in children can include impacts on development, growth, and/or maturation of organ/system function.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Gender

All subjects included in NEXUS, ADVANCE, ADVANCE OLE were male patients; therefore, no specific analysis by gender was possible.

## Extrinsic factors

The safety of leriglitazone with respect to extrinsic factors (e.g., smoking, diet) has not been evaluated in clinical studies.

Pregnancy, lactation and reproductive potential in women of childbearing age have not been evaluated in clinical studies with leriglitazone. Leriglitazone is not indicated for use in women.

No cases of overdose have occurred with leriglitazone in studies NEXUS, ADVANCE. Leriglitazone is not expected to have abuse potential.

No effects of withdrawal or rebound have been observed in clinical studies with leriglitazone.

The effect of the treatment with leriglitazone on ability to drive or operate machinery has not been evaluated.

## Safety in elderly, in patients with renal, hepatic impairment

This has not been discussed by the applicant, however, relevant safety information in these populations is included in the SmPC.

## Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

Drug interactions with leriglitazone have not been evaluated in clinical studies. As leriglitazone is predominantly metabolised via CYP2C8 and CYP3A4, the safety of concomitant use of leriglitazone with CYP2C8 and CYP3A4 inhibitors or CYP3A4 inducers, due to the increased or decreased exposure of leriglitazone, is not known. According to the applicant one DDI study has recently been initiated and results expected by mid-2026. A drug interaction study should be submitted as soon as available and, based on results of this study, the product information should be amended accordingly. The applicant stated in the SmPC that no DDI studies are available to date, and therefore concomitant use of leriglitazone with CYP2C8 and CYP3A inhibitors is not recommended.

## SmPC

The applicant presented the methodology used to identify the adverse drug reactions (ADRs) included in the SmPC. The SmPC ADR table includes all AEs that were assessed as at least possibly related to leriglitazone, as well as AEs for which the causality assessment was considered not assessable. Given the observed differences in the incidence of certain ADRs between paediatric and adult populations, separate ADR tables are presented to provide clearer and more appropriate safety information.

## 5.4.12.1.2. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the SmPC

The ADRs proposed by the applicant for inclusion in the SmPC are described in section 5.4.3.1 above.

The CHMP does not recommend including any additional ADRs in the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.12.2. CHMP conclusions on clinical safety

Overall safety database (293 subjects exposed to leriglitazone) is adequate for cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD), a rare phenotype of adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD).

The applicant presents NEXUS as the pivotal study in paediatric patients and data on adult patients are from the ADVANCE study.

Safety findings from the pivotal open label uncontrolled study NEXUS in paediatric population are limited due to limited treatment duration. There are concerns on long term safety in the different subgroups of paediatric population. In study ADVANCE was high incidence of weight gain and oedema. Adverse events leading to permanent, or temporary withdrawal from study medication and leading to study medication dose adjustment were very frequent in the leriglitazone group. Additionally, large proportion of patients treated with leriglitazone were not exposed to the target dose for proposed duration of the treatment- 96 weeks, therefore safety data on long term use in proposed target dosage is limited. Therefore, the RMP addresses the clinical consequences of fluid retention and long-term safety.

Overall, and in line with scientific advice and feedback from patient associations and healthcare professionals, the safety profile of leriglitazone is acceptable for a rare and severe disease.

## 6. Risk management plan

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

The applicant proposed the following summary of safety concerns in the RMP:

Table 45: Summary of safety concerns in the proposed RMP

| Summary of safety concerns   | Summary of safety concerns                                                      |
|------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Important identified risks   | None                                                                            |
| Important potential risks    | Serious clinical consequences of fluid retention (for example, severe oe- dema) |
| Missing information          | Long-term safety, including bone safety, in the approved population             |

## 6.1.2. Discussion on proposed safety specification

After discussion the CHMP proposed to the applicant to consider the application to be approved under exceptional circumstances, in accordance with Article 14(8) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004.

To support the marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances (MAUEC) the applicant proposed two SOBs that are expected to gather more data on long-term safety of leriglitazone in the intended population, which is currently a safety concern, and more data on the effectiveness of leriglitazone.

The first SOB (SOB1) concerned a PASS with a duration of 10 years, with annual safety reports and a final report. The design, objectives, milestones and safety concerns are set out in the RMP but required some amendments especially considering the indication is restricted to children 2 to 12 years and some of the safety concerns were adjusted accordingly.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 6.2. Pharmacovigilance plan

## 6.2.1. Proposed pharmacovigilance plan.

## Routine pharmacovigilance activities

Routine pharmacovigilance activities beyond adverse reactions reporting and signal detection:

There are no other routine pharmacovigilance activities beyond adverse drug reaction reporting and signal detection.

## Additional pharmacovigilance activities

In addition, the applicant has proposed the following additional pharmacovigilance activities:

## Study short name and title:

MT-4-01 Post-authorisation safety study (PASS)

An observational study to evaluate the safety of leriglitazone in male patients with cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) treated with leriglitazone compared with an untreated population of patients.

## Rationale and study objectives:

The purpose of the PASS is to evaluate the safety of leriglitazone in relation to the serious consequences of fluid retention (for example oedema), and long-term safety, including bone safety.

Primary objective: To evaluate the incidence of serious consequences of fluid retention, including oedema, and long-term safety, including bone safety, in male patients with cALD treated with leriglitazone compared with an untreated population of patients.

## Study population:

The study population for the PASS will be patients with cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) according to the leriglitazone indication, including both patients treated with leriglitazone and untreated patients. The leriglitazone exposed patients will include:

- Patients newly initiated on leriglitazone
- Prevalent user initiated on leriglitazone during participation in the clinical development programme or compassionate use programme.

Safety outcomes will be compared with an historical or contemporaneous cohort of patients with cALD not treated with leriglitazone, selected based on the evaluation of on-going disease registries, as most appropriate and feasible.

For the most suitable setting, a feasibility assessment of existing disease registries will be conducted to determine the quality and completeness of available data, and/or the ability to collect additional data, required to meet the objectives of this PASS as well as the number of patients that are eligible to be included. The final approach will be determined based on the outcome of the feasibility assessment, e.g. existing disease registry-based approach, a de novo registry approach, or a hybrid approach.

The current estimated sample size is approximately 100 patients treated with leriglitazone. The final target sample size and associated precision estimates for primary study outcomes will be provided in the study protocol.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The PASS will be implemented through a shared operational infrastructure with the post-authorisation effectiveness registry, including common participating centres, electronic data capture (eCRFs), study coordination and data management, while addressing distinct regulatory objectives.

Protocol submission: three months after feasibility report.

End of data collection: to be determined and defined in the study protocol

Final study report: to be determined and defined in the study protocol (within 12 months after end of data collection)

## 6.2.2. Discussion on the pharmacovigilance plan

## 6.2.2.1. Routine pharmacovigilance activities

No routine pharmacovigilance activities beyond adverse reporting and signal detection are proposed. For the time being this is agreed.

## 6.2.2.2. Additional pharmacovigilance activities

The applicant proposed to conduct a non-interventional post-authorisation safety study (NI PASS) as category 3 study to address all safety concerns. However, with the applicant´s request for a MAUEC the PASS has been included in the RMP as category 2 study (SOB).

Upon the CHMP request to amend the safety concerns (delete 'cardiac failure' in the important potential risk; revise the missing information to 'Long-term safety, including bone safety, in the approved population') the applicant adapted the addressed safety concerns in the PASS presentation accordingly.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

With the MAUEC request and the addition of a PAES, the applicant deleted the effectiveness part of the PASS (exploratory objectives).

The full study protocol will be submitted for assessment 3 months after the feasibility report. The PASS duration has not been determined yet but will be defined in the study protocol. Final study report shall be submitted within 12 months after finalization of study data collection.

## 6.3. Plans for post-authorisation efficacy studies

The Marketing Authorisation under Exceptional Circumstances requires continued collection of longitudinal effectiveness data to support the CHMP's annual reassessment of the benefit-risk balance. Given the ultra-rare nature of cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD), this assessment will rely on the continued collection of effectiveness data during long-term follow-up through an open-ended registry. To fulfil this specific obligation, the MAH will establish a multinational, multicentre, non-interventional disease registry designed to collect long-term real-world effectiveness data on leriglitazone in the same patients included in PASS in the indicated population.

The operational implementation of the effectiveness registry, including the final operational model, data sources, long-term follow-up strategy and comparator approach, will be informed by the registry feasibility assessment undertaken for the PASS. The same scientific principles discussed during the PRAC assessment of the PASS will be applied to the effectiveness registry.

Study short name: Multinational, multicentric, non-interventional disease registry to collect long-term effectiveness data on leriglitazone in the same patients included in the MT-4-01 PASS in the indicated population.

Rationale and study objectives: This study will collect long-term effectiveness data on leriglitazone.

The patients enrolled into the effectiveness disease registry will be the same patients as in PASS and this will be described in the final protocols.

Primary objective: To collect long-term effectiveness data on leriglitazone in routine clinical practice, to support the CHMP's annual reassessment of the benefit-risk balance under the marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances.

Secondary objective: To describe treatment utilisation, including dosing, adherence, and persistence.

Study Design: The registry will follow an observational, non-interventional design and will be implemented through participating prescribing centres according to the operational model resulting from the registry feasibility assessment (as per the PASS). Patients treated according to the authorised indication will constitute the primary study population. An appropriate control of untreated patients will be determined following the registry feasibility assessment.

## Study Population:

Patients with a confirmed diagnosis meeting the approved indication: Treatment of Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with non Gadolinium (Gd) enhancing lesions (i.e. Gd-negative) in brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) and Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1; who are either treated with leriglitazone or untreated in routine clinical practice will be eligible for inclusion, with no exclusion criteria beyond those specified in the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Enrolment and data collection will be continuous throughout the registry's duration.

Data collection will include:

- Demographics (age, height and weight)
- Baseline disease characteristics
- Treatment initiation, dosing, dose modifications, discontinuation
- Clinical outcomes. The following clinical data will be collected:

| Clinical data collected for annual B/R assessment        | Clinical relevance                                                                                                                               | Contribution to the annual B/R assessment                                                      |
|----------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| MRI assessment (date + scheduled/unscheduled)            | Establishes the chronological framework for radiological follow-up.                                                                              | Enables longitudinal interpretation of all MRI-derived efficacy data.                          |
| Gadolinium enhancement status                            | Assessment of blood-brain barrier integrity.                                                                                                     | Documents the presence, resolution or persistence of blood–brain barrier disruption over time. |
| Lesion stable (Yes/No)                                   | Assessment of disease progression.                                                                                                               | Documents progression or stability of cerebral lesions between consecutive MRI assessments.    |
| Loes Score                                               | Assessment of disease progression                                                                                                                | Documents progression or stability of cerebral lesions between consecutive MRI assessments.    |
| Neurological Function Score (NFS)                        | Assessment of neurological progression.                                                                                                          | Documents longitudinal changes in neurological function.                                       |
| Major Functional Disabilities (MFD) (new onset Yes/No)   | Assessment of clinically meaningful functional deterioration.                                                                                    | Documents development of major functional impairment during follow- up.                        |
| HSCT (Yes/No + date)                                     | Major therapeutic intervention reflecting disease evolution.                                                                                     | Provides a key milestone in disease progression and the patient treatment pathway.             |
| Treatment status (ongoing/ discontinued + date + reason) | Assessment of treatment persistence.                                                                                                             | Supports interpretation of long-term effectiveness and tolerability.                           |
| Outcome of HSCT procedures (Clinical and MRI)            | Evaluates safety and effectiveness of treatment prior to HSCT procedures on HSCT outcomes as per standard of care assessments for cALD post-HSCT | Enable evaluation of post-HSCT clinical and MRI outcomes                                       |
| Vital status (alive/dead + date of death)                | Ultimate long-term clinical outcome.                                                                                                             | Supports the overall annual assessment of the benefit-risk balance.                            |

Effectiveness data collection will continue after HSCT to enable evaluation of post-HSCT clinical and MRI outcomes. The duration, scope and operational implementation of post-HSCT follow-up will be determined following the registry feasibility assessment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Data will be sourced from treating physicians and hospital medical records.

Data management and monitoring procedures will follow predefined quality standards, including validation rules and data completeness metrics. The registry will comply fully with GDPR and national data protection requirements, ensuring secure data handling, pseudonymisation and appropriate informed consent processes. The effectiveness registry will use the same participating centres, electronic case report forms (eCRFs), study coordination, monitoring processes and data management infrastructure established for the PASS, thereby minimising operational burden while ensuring consistency of long-term data collection.

Statistical analyses will include descriptive and longitudinal evaluations of the efficacy outcomes collected; no formal hypothesis testing is planned.

After discussions with the CHMP, the applicant was requested to consider its application to be approved under exceptional circumstances, in accordance with Article 14(8) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004.

To support the MAUEC the applicant proposed two SOBs that are expected to gather more data on long-term safety of leriglitazone in the intended population, which is currently a safety concern (SOB1), and more data on the effectiveness of leriglitazone (SOB2).

The second SOB (SOB2) is proposed to be open-ended, and intends to a be an effectiveness registry, i.e., collection of RWD in untreated and leriglitazone treated patients in the approved indication, with annual submission of available data to support the requirements of a MAUEC. The applicant presented the data to be collected from patients treated and included in the registry namely, demographics, base line characteristics, treatment initiation, dosing, dose modifications, discontinuations as well as clinical outcomes.

The effectiveness data collection will continue after HSCT to enable evaluation of post-HSCT clinical and MRI outcomes. The duration, scope and operational implementation of post-HSCT follow-up will be determined following the registry feasibility assessment. Considering the size of the population to be treated is limited, both SOBs will be using the same population to ensure the feasibility of both studies and the relevance of the collected data for post-approval assessment of the safety and efficacy of Nezglyal. Consequently, milestones and due dates are aligned for both PAES and PASS studies. The due dates when the feasibility assessment and the protocol of PAES (and PASS) are to be submitted to EMA to support MAUEC, are set to be: three months for feasibility study and a protocol will be submitted three months later. Considering this is a marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances a 3rd SOB is being requested/included in product information Annex II so that any available data on exposure to leriglitazone will be collected and submitted for assessment.

## 8. Product information

## 8.1. Summary of product characteristics (SmPC)

## 8.1.1. SmPC section 4.1 justification

The applicant applied for the following indication: Nezglyal is indicated for the treatment of male adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) patients 2 years and older with cerebral ALD (cALD). After assessment of the data made available during the procedure it was concluded that efficacy of leriglitazone in paediatric cALD patients could only be considered demonstrated for Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 years with non-Gadolinium (Gd) enhancement lesions (i.e., GD-negative) in brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), with Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1 under exceptional circumstances.

## 8.1.2. SmPC section 5.1 justification

Taking into account that the final indication proposed for approval was restricted to children 2-12 years with non-Gadolinium enhancing lesions in brain MRI, information from population 2 from the NEXUS study (i.e., Gd-positive children 2-12 years) and from the ADVANCE study was removed from this section. The text was also streamlined regarding the outcome of secondary endpoints.
