Pursuant to Article 23(1) of Regulation No (EU) 726/2004, Nezglyal is included in the additional monitoring list since it is approved under exceptional circumstances REG Art 14(8) of Regulation 726/2004.

Therefore, the summary of product characteristics and the package leaflet include a statement that this medicinal product is subject to additional monitoring and will allow quick identification of new safety information. The statement is preceded by an inverted equilateral black triangle.

## 9. Benefit-risk assessment

## 9.1. Therapeutic context

## 9.1.1. Disease or condition, proposed therapeutic indication

Cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) is a rare and devastating phenotype of adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD), a rare genetic disorder linked to the X-chromosome characterised by the presence of brain lesions that, once progressive, can lead to a rapid neurological decline, vegetative state and death.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

cALD is characterised by the presence of brain lesions that, once progressive, can lead to a rapid neurological decline, vegetative state and death. All male ALD patients older than 2 years are at risk for developing cALD. The pathophysiology of cALD is associated with increasing inflammation, along with demyelination, axonal damage and BBB disrupt, which is detected through magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), the only marker for disease progression. Currently, there are no validated tests or biomarkers that can predict cALD onset in ALD patients, with MRI detection of brain lesions contributing to establish the diagnosis.

## 9.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

For a detailed description, please see section 2.1 of this document.

Currently no pharmacological therapies are approved for cALD patients to reduce and/or prevent the onset of progressive disease. In the EU, haematopoietic stem-cell transplantation (HSCT) is the only potential treatment option for progressive cALD (typically with Gd positive lesions), which may halt disease progression in children. However, the window of opportunity to treat patients and the outcome is poor in advanced disease (Engelen 2022), along with limitation of suitable donors, engraftment failure and severe side effects (Raymond 2019) and high mortality.

There is an unmet medical need for the treatment of this condition.

## 9.2. Main clinical studies

For a detailed description of the main clinical studies supporting this application, please refer to section 5.3.2 of this document.

The efficacy data is derived from two clinical studies, NEXUS and ADVANCE, in paediatric and adult patients respectively, and data collected in the compassionate use programme in adults and children with most of the patients treated under ANSM and BfArM approved protocols.

NEXUS is the pivotal, Phase II/III, open label study evaluating leriglitazone in children (2-12 years) with either Gd negative brain lesions (Population 1) or Gd positive brain lesions (Population 2). Patients received leriglitazone in addition to the option of SoC HSCT, which was possible at any timepoint during the study when meeting predefined criteria. The patients were dosed with daily leriglitazone. The primary objective of NEXUS was to evaluate whether leriglitazone can arrest progression of cALD in boys prior to HSCT. The primary efficacy endpoint was proportion of patients with clinically and radiologically arrested disease at Week 96 or at the visit prior to HSCT. A total of 23 boys with cALD were enrolled in NEXUS, of which, 9 had Gd negative brain lesions (Population 1) and 14 had Gd positive lesions (Population 2) at baseline. Three patients were excluded from the evaluable set resulting in the evaluable set comprising 20 patients (9 from Population 1 and 11 from Population 2).

ADVANCE was a Phase II/III placebo-controlled study (2:1) designed to assess the efficacy and safety of leriglitazone in 116 adult male ALD patients with evidence of spinal cord involvement (AMN) and without brain lesions or with Gd negative brain lesions (i.e., cALD). The primary objective was to evaluate the effect of leriglitazone on AMN based on 6MWT (primary endpoint). The study also included endpoints relevant to cALD that are the base of the assessed data for the current target indication that included: Cerebral lesion progression (MRI) and Loes score; EDSS, Global impressions and QoL; and plasma biomarkers. Patients were initially dosed with 150 mg leriglitazone hydrochloride administered once daily, with possible adjustments on an individual patient basis, based on PK data obtained up to 12 weeks after first dose, to achieve leriglitazone target plasma exposure of 200 µg·h/mL. Following the 2-year double-blind and placebo-controlled part of the study Part 1), patients could participate in the open-label extension (Part 2; OLE), which was designed to evaluate the long-term safety and efficacy of leriglitazone.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Compassionate Use data of leriglitazone in boys and adults with cALD is also submitted to support the indication. The global CU cohort includes a large and diverse set of patients covering all ages groups, Gd negative and Gd positive lesions and patients with early disease (low lesion load /mild symptoms) and advanced disease (high lesion load and/or advanced symptoms). Follow-up MRI of ≥3 months is available for 67 patients (30 adults, 37 boys). French and German cohorts from the global CU cohort followed prespecified treatment protocols within CU programmes which were approved by the respective national agencies (ANSM and BfArM). Data from these patients were collected in an eCRF thus allowing more in-depth analyses. Additional data assessment matching baseline characteristics to the NEXUS population was made. Clustered information regarding treatment of adolescents within CU program, stratifying by disease severity at enrolment and matching the NEXUS study baseline characteristics, overall, shown disease and lesion stability in those adolescent patients. Gd negative lesions seem to have a different behavior with age and adolescents seem to have a more similar to adult than children's profile.

## 9.3. Favourable effects

## NEXUS study

1. Seven of the 20 patients (35%) of the evaluable patient population showed no significant lesion growth and no Gd enhancement 'arrested disease', including one patient who was initially Gd positive and no longer showed Gd enhancement. The primary endpoint of the study was met, with 35% of subjects meeting 'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT, representing a statistically significant improvement over the 10% rate of patients with disease arrest expected in natural history patients.
2. The primary endpoint was met in each of the sensitivity analyses performed, reflecting a statistically significant improvement over the 10% rate of patients with disease arrest expected in natural history patients.
3. All the 20 patients (100%) of the evaluable patient population remained clinically stable with a change of ≤ 1 from baseline in NFS and were free of MFD;
4. Fourteen of the 20 patients (70%) of the evaluable patient population had no significant lesion growth.
5. At W24 and W96, the 6 patients from Pop1 were Gd-. Three patients in Pop 1 undergone HSCT because of Gd conversion (2 at W24 and 1 at W36).
6. Subjects meeting 'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 continued to remain clinically and radiologically stable at all study visits beyond Week 96.
7. Eleven (91,7%) of the 12 evaluable subjects that completed the Visit Prior to HSCT met the HSCT criteria at the visit prior to HSCT, 3 from Population 1 and 8 from Population 2. Nine (75.0%) subjects met the criteria due to persistent gadolinium enhancement but did not show significant lesion growth at two consecutive visits. Only 2 (16.7%) subjects met both components of the HSCT criteria, 1 in each population. 1 patient from Population 2 was transplanted without meeting HSCT criteria, which means this patient remained clinically stable and, lesions were stable and Gd negative. All patients who underwent HSCT remained clinically stable at the Visit Prior to HSCT.

## ADVANCE Study (part 1 and part 2 OLE)

1. Of the 116 randomized patients, 11 showed cerebral lesion progression during the double-blind phase of the study - 8 out of the 39 (20.5%) patients on placebo; 3 out of 77 patients (3.9%)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

patients on active arm (proportion difference of -0.17, Newcombe-Wilson CI of proportion difference -0.32 to -0.05; p=0.007, two-sided Fisher's exact test).

2. Only patients in the placebo arm developed post-baseline Gd positive lesions (4 patients; 10.2%).
3. The patients in the placebo group showed a higher mean (SE) increase in Loes score (0.696 [0.202]) when compared with patients treated with leriglitazone (0.114 [0.151]) and the difference was nominally significant (LS means difference [SE] of -0.582 [0.254]; 95% CI 1.088, - 0.077; p=0.024, analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) with baseline Loes as covariate).
4. Leriglitazone showed favourable trends when compared to placebo regarding; i) neurological disability as measured by EDSS (LS mean difference -0.34; 95% CI -0.72, 0.05; p=0.085, ANCOVA); ii) Clinician-based Assessment of Global Improvement (proportion difference 6.5%; 95% CI -0.13, 0.25; p=0.166, Fisher's exact test).
5. During the OLE:
- a. Patients in the LL group (leriglitazone in part 1&amp;2) showed an annualised incidence of cerebral lesion progression of 1.7 patients per year (3.0%) while for patients in the PL group (placebo in part 1 and leriglitazone in part 2), in Part 1 this was of 4.0 patients per year (10.3%).
- b. The incidence per year of cerebral lesion progression was broadly similar (around 2 to 3%) in patients following treated in leriglitazone in Part 1 (L), and those treated with leriglitazone in Part 2 (LL and PL)
- c. For the LL group there was no significant worsening in the annualised LS mean (SE) change of Loes score from baseline. For the PL group, significant changes were observed in both the double-blind phase and the OLE, although the increase in the OLE was less than in the double-blind phase. Worsening of Loes score appeared to stabilise during OLE of the study, with smaller annualised mean increases from baseline.

## COMPASSIONATE USE

1. In adult and paediatric patients treated with leriglitazone under CU, the disease progression was slower in the Early-Stage group as compared to the Advanced-Stage group.
2. Clustered information regarding treatment of adolescents within CU program, stratifying by disease severity at enrolment and matching the NEXUS study baseline characteristics, overall, shown disease and lesion stability in those adolescent patients.
3. Overall, lesion stabilisation was reported for most patients with post-baseline data in the global CU cohort, which aligns with the data from the NEXUS and ADVANCE clinical studies. The results further indicated that the most meaningful benefits were observed when treatment was initiated early in disease (particularly in adults), which is similar to the HSCT experience.

## 9.3.1. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

The observed improvement in the primary endpoint of arrested disease was limited to the Gd negative subgroup.

The chosen rate of disease arrest expected in natural history patients was further justified and is considered acceptable for Gd negative lesions but not to Gd positive lesions.

There were some doubts regarding the characteristics of NEXUS Gd negative population, as it was unclear if the apparent effect would be attributable to leriglitazone.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant argued that similarly to the NEXUS study, enrolment of Gd- patients into the Mallack cohort was not guided by their progressive status. The Applicant has provided relevant additional analyses that reduce concerns regarding the impact of baseline definition and MRI frequency on the lesion-volume comparison, however, these analyses did not fully resolve uncertainty for the Gdenhancement and Loes-stabilisation comparisons, and residual confounding remained possible, particularly due to the imbalance in baseline lesion volume in the time-to-Gd-enhancement analysis and missing Loes-stabilisation classifications in the NH cohort. Therefore, the external control comparisons should be interpreted with caution. The applicant provided responses trying to address this outstanding issue.

The provided data added new information to the discussion, but there were still some doubts that the magnitude of the information generated from assumptions and different subsequent theoretical analysis (with all the uncertainty that is added) required to compensate for the gaps in the generated evidence could impair granting the proposed restricted paediatric indication.

During the subsequent Oral Explanation in front of the CHMP the applicant further elaborated on their reasoning to support the comparability of the NEXUS and NH populations namely by:

- a) Stressing the similarity of age range, disease stage and standard of care of both populations together with a similar methodology on MRI assessment and central readers to determine outcomes
- b) Reinforcing that both cohorts enrolled patients without confirming their progression status
- c) Presenting the arguments that would resolve the concerns on a potential impact of an imbalance in baseline lesion volume in the time-to-Gd-enhancement analysis:
- a. Baseline lesion volume was higher in NEXUS group, so if acting as confounder it would disfavor treatment arm
- b. A new analysis was conducted excluding lesion volume from weighting model with a better overall balance (age SMD = 0.038, loes score SMD = 0.045, lesion volume SMD = 0.164) and similar treatment effect (p=0.0332)
- d) Trying to resolve the concerns of a potential impact of missing Loes-stabilisation classifications in the NH cohort:
- a. The results of a tipping point analysis were discussed assessing all possible scenarios regarding combinations of the missing classifications in both NH and NEXUS cohorts. The only ones that would favour NH group would be biologically highly implausible scenarios. In the most conservative plausible scenario there was still a 19.5% point estimate in favour of NEXUS
- b. Considering that all patients with missing Loes-stabilisation classifications were Gd positive and as such most possibly not stable, the resulting scenario would result in an expected Loes stabilization difference of 43.5% in NEXUS vs. 12.0% in NH.
- e) The applicant, once again, took the opportunity to elaborate on the results from NEXUS study regarding Gd negative patients. Following the discussion and clarifications at the OE it was agreed that the existing evidence could support an indication for Gd negative cALD patients under exceptional circumstances (discussed below under final indication).

No treatment effect was demonstrated in the Gd positive population, which represents patients with active, progressive disease (only 1 paediatric patient with Gd positive lesion had arrested disease). The applicant argued that despite no impact has been demonstrated in the primary endpoint of arrested disease for Gd positive population, other results could be seen as a sign of leriglitazone impact on disease control such as lack of significant lesion growth (similar to Gd negative patients), lesion volume, Loes Score, NfL stability. Clinical stability was also seen contrary to what would be expected in the same study timeframe in the NH cohort. Overall current evidence was still considered limited to substantiate a clinical benefit in patients with Gd positive lesions. The applicant supported their reasoning on the expectation that Gd enhancement resolution would occur with more follow-up time, but this still needs proper demonstration. Irrespective of this, it is agreed that lesion growth arrest and clinical stability (compared to the expected progression in non-treated Gd positive patients) seem to be improved in leriglitazone Gd positive treated patients. These findings are, nevertheless, still considered scarce to support an indication. The applicant provided responses to further address this outstanding issue. The new discussion focussed on the impact of leriglitazone on clinical stability based on lesion stability (reduced lesion growth and increased Loes stabilization) and preservation of low GIS for most of patients as a basis to support BBB preservation and potential Gd resolution with more follow-up time as seen in CU patients. For these CU patients this correlation between BBB preservation/GIS stabilization and Gd resolution was not demonstrated.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant argued that leriglitazone may provide a treatment option between cerebral lesion identification and HSCT procedures initiation. Although the rationale for this potential effect might be recognized, as discussed during the assessment, a clear benefit from the HSCT outcome in leriglitazone treated versus non-treated patients has not been demonstrated even if evidence supports that outcomes following HSCT are more favourable when transplantation is performed at lower lesion burdens and better clinical status. In order to be an option for those patients non eligible to HSCT a clear benefit had to be demonstrated which was not the case.

In the OE, the applicant further elaborated on their reasoning to claim for an indication in Gd positive patients. The arguments were once again based on lesion growth arrest and clinical stability together with GIS being kept at a minimum level as a surrogate of BBB stability and possible progression to Gd-resolution. The possible impact of leriglitazone on lower lesion burdens and better clinical status was once again reiterated as having a potential impact on HSCT outcome for those patients, which was confirmed by the statement of clinical experts present at the OE. Despite the arguments that were provided, the existing evidence to support an indication in Gd-positive patients still remained questionable to grant an indication.

The fact that ADVANCE study was not primarily designed to assess leriglitazone impact on cALD patients resulted in several issues with potential impact on data validity to support the adult indication. Following CHMP position on the lack of clinical evidence that has been generated in adults the applicant proposed not to pursue the indication in adults.

Compassionate use data was presented. Although supportive, results are from a combination of considerable different patients in terms of disease burden, time of disease evolution that impairs its true validity to support an indication per se.

The proposed extrapolation strategy for adolescents was questioned. Data on adolescent exposure only exists in the context of CU. The Applicant acknowledged the comments from CHMP and proposed an extrapolation exercise from adult to adolescents with the information of only 5 adolescents from CU. After assessment, the applicant decided to withdraw also the adolescent indication.

Following the discussion on the use of MFD free criterion, possibly covering too broad spectrum of patients in terms of clinical status at baseline when compared with NEXUS, the Applicant proposed to narrow the indication to 'Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 independent of Gadolinium enhancement status in brain MRI and Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1'. The proposed restricted indication in children could have only been considered acceptable provided full clarification and resolution of the objections on efficacy of both Gd negative and positive patients. Following the OE, the doubts related to the Gd negative patients were considered resolved but the same was not the case for Gd positive patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In conclusion, efficacy of leriglitazone in paediatric cALD patients could only be considered for Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with non Gadolinium (Gd) enhancement (i.e., Gd-negative) in brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), with a Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1, under exceptional circumstances.

## 9.4. Unfavourable effects

The safety database includes a total of 293 subjects exposed to leriglitazone. This comprises 227 patients with adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD)-including 23 paediatric cALD patients from the pivotal NEXUS study, and 116 adult ALD patients (including 33 with cALD) from the ADVANCE (MT-2-01) Part 1 study. Of these, 88 patients continued treatment in the open-label extension (Part 2), and 94 patients received leriglitazone under compassionate-use programmes (71 from French and German cohorts). In addition, 40 healthy male volunteers were included from two Phase I studies, and 26 patients with Friedreich's ataxia participated in one Phase IIa study.

Overall safety database is adequate for cerebral adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD), a rare phenotype of adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD).

The overall safety profile of leriglitazone is consistent between clinical studies (including supportive data) and with the therapeutic class effect. The most common adverse events were weight increased, oedema (multiple forms), haematological events and lacrimation and were mild or moderate. It was observed that a higher frequency of ADRs occurs during the first six months of leriglitazone treatment.

In children (2-&lt;12 years) and adolescents (12-&lt;18 years), the most frequently reported adverse reactions were weight increased, eyelid oedema, leukopenia, and neutropenia (each in 8.7% of patients). These data are derived from the open-label NEXUS (MT-2-02) study, which included 23 patients treated to a target exposure of 170 µg·h/mL (±20%).

In adults, evaluated in 77 patients receiving an average daily dose of 12 mL to achieve a target exposure of 200 µg·h/mL (±20%), the most common adverse reactions were oedema (81.8%), weight increased (70.1%), increased lacrimation (18.2%), and anaemia (10.3%).

Oedema (including peripheral, facial, and eyelid oedema) occurred in 17.4% of paediatric patients, typically within the first 24 weeks of treatment, and was generally mild to moderate in severity. Weight increase was reported in 8.7% of paediatric patients, also mainly within the first 24 weeks. In adults, the mean body weight increase at Week 96 was 5.8 kg. Early weight gain may reflect fluid retention, while later stages may be associated with fat accumulation. Anaemia (including iron deficiency anaemia) was observed in 4.3% of paediatric patients, mostly mild and asymptomatic.

## 9.4.1. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

Safety findings from the pivotal open label uncontrolled study NEXUS in paediatric population are limited due to limited treatment duration. There are concerns on long term safety in the different subgroups of paediatric population.

Furthermore, there are concerns raised regarding risk of bone fractures. Adverse events leading to permanent, or temporary withdrawal from study medication and leading to study medication dose adjustment were very frequent in the leriglitazone group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 9.6. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 9.6.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

The supporting data for the current MAA relies on the results of 2 studies:

a) NEXUS is the pivotal open-label study with cALD paediatric patients being treated with leriglitazone. The primary endpoint of the study was met in each of the sensitivity analyses, with 35% of subjects meeting 'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 or the Visit Prior to HSCT, representing a statistically significant improvement over the 10% rate of patients with disease arrest expected in natural history patients. The observed improvement in the primary endpoint of arrested disease was limited to the Gd negative subgroup, in which, given the characteristics of this population, the apparent effect was unclear whether it was attributable to leriglitazone. Further arguments were presented and discussed in an OE trying to address the concerns of NEXUS and NH population comparability. It was agreed that the existing evidence could support the claim of population comparability and an indication for Gd-negative cALD patients under exceptional circumstances (discussed below under final indication).

No treatment effect was demonstrated in the Gd positive population, which represents presymptomatic patients with active, progressive disease (only 1 patient with Gd positive lesion had arrested disease). Limited support comes from secondary endpoints such as Loes score due to a small number of evaluable patients and lack of comparison arm. New supportive data has been provided regarding BBB preservation and preservation of low GIS potential hint that Gd resolution would occur with more follow-up time as seen in CU patients. For CU patients, correlation between BBB preservation/GIS stabilization and Gd resolution was not demonstrated. The findings were still considered scarce to support an indication in Gd-positive patients.

The decision of the applicant not to have a placebo arm increases the importance of the quality of NH data that was used to conclude on leriglitazone efficacy. Based on the data provided, it is not clear the degree of similarity of the baseline data of this reference population to the NEXUS population.

The disease severity status at baseline for all the patients enrolled in NEXUS study was low and there were doubts regarding extrapolation to more severely impaired patients. Therefore the indication is restricted to those patients without MFD.

All patients of the evaluable patient population remained clinically stable with a change of ≤ 1 from baseline in NFS and were free of MFD, and 14 of the 20 patients (70%) of the evaluable patient population had no significant lesion growth as per predefined criteria. Compared to the expected clinical evolution in the NH cohort in the same period of time, this can be seen as a significant clinical improvement. However, the less severe stage of the NEXUS population vs NH cohort and the limited number of patients questioned the true effect of leriglitazone in the favourable outcome observed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Subjects meeting 'arrested disease' criteria at Week 96 continued to remain clinically and radiologically stable at all study visits beyond Week 96.

Eleven (91,7%) of the 12 evaluable subjects that completed the Visit Prior to HSCT met the HSCT criteria at the visit prior to HSCT. All patients who underwent HSCT remained clinically stable at the Visit Prior to HSCT. These results suggest a leriglitazone effect in the clinical stabilization of patients in pre-transplant. Nevertheless, the lack of information on clinical outcome of HSCT in leriglitazone treated patients compared to those not treated with leriglitazone (literature), taking into consideration disease status at the time of transplant decision impairs confirmation of the improving HSCT outcomes claim following therapy with leriglitazone.

b) ADVANCE was a placebo-controlled study (randomisation 2:1) designed to assess the efficacy and safety of leriglitazone in adult male ALD patients with evidence of spinal cord involvement

(adrenomyeloneuropathy) and with or without brain lesions (i.e., cALD) but without evidence of progressive cALD at baseline. Uncertainties exist regarding baseline similarity of the placebo and actively treated groups namely on Loes (slightly higher baseline Loes observed in the placebo group compared to the leriglitazone group) which question the relevance of the effects observed. Also, the placebo group clinical deterioration rate (15%) is below the expected by natural history in adults (30-40%) raising concerns on true representation of the target population in this study.

This, together with the fact that the ADVANCE the study was not primarily designed to assess leriglitazone impact on cALD makes the whole strategy questionable.

Compassionate use data, although supportive, results from a combination of considerable different patients in terms of disease burden, time of disease evolution that impairs its true validity to support an indication per se.

Despite convergence of CU data and the one obtained from the clinical studies, the information can only be considered supportive due to the methodological differences on how data was generated.

Data on adolescent exposure only exists in the context of CU. Even controlling the data and assessing it as per similar to clinical studies, baseline characteristics and methodological limitations impair reaching meaningful conclusions.

The safety profile is well characterized and is as expected for the therapeutic class and mainly includes weight gain and oedema related to fluid retention. In adults, oedema and weight increase were very common, while in children they appeared less frequently but with the same pattern. In many cases, AEs were mostly mild to moderate and were managed based on dose reduction or the use of diuretics, but they may become relevant in long-term treatment, especially for paediatric patients. These measures are not recommended in the SmPC, and this position was supported by an extensive discussion. Other reported adverse events such as anaemia, leukopenia, neutropenia and increased lacrimation were mild and reversible.

Regarding the final wording of the proposed indication:

The proposed restricted Gadolinium enhancement independent indication in children 2-12 could have only been considered acceptable provided full resolution of the objections on efficacy of both Gd negative and positive patients. Following the OE, the doubts related to the Gd negative patients were considered resolved which was not the case for Gd positive patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In conclusion, efficacy of leriglitazone in paediatric cALD patients could only be considered confirmed for Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with no Gadolinium enhancement status in brain MRI and Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1 under exceptional circumstances. The unfavourable effects are mostly manageable but frequent.

Considering the lack of robust efficacy evidence in all types of patients, the overall B/R of leriglitazone is considered positive for Gd-negative patients but negative for Gd-positive patients.

## 9.6.2. Balance of benefits and risks

In the pivotal (NEXUS) study, the primary endpoint was met, with 35% of subjects achieving \"arrested disease\" at Week 96 or prior to hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT), compared to a 10% rate expected in natural history (NH) populations. This improvement was statistically significant and remained consistent across various sensitivity analyses. However, the robustness of this effect was most apparent for Gd-negative patients and the evidence was deemed sufficient to support a claim of efficacy in this population. The clinical relevance is high for patients who achieve stabilization, as 70% of the evaluable population showed no significant lesion growth and all remained clinically stable (NFS change ≤ 1). On the opposite, no definitive treatment effect was demonstrated regarding the primary endpoint for Gd-positive patients (only 1 patient achieved arrested disease), and data from secondary endpoints (Loes score) were limited by small sample sizes and the absence of a comparison arm.

The safety profile is well-characterized and consistent with the therapeutic class. The primary risks include fluid retention (with frequent weight gain and oedema) and mild and reversible cases of anaemia, leukopenia, neutropenia, and increased lacrimation. Most adverse events are mild to moderate and manageable via dose reduction or diuretics, although the long-term relevance of these effects in paediatric patients remains a concern.

In conclusion, the benefit/risk balance is considered positive under exceptional circumstances in Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with non Gadolinium (Gd) enhancement lesions (i.e., Gd-negative) in brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), with a Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1.

## 9.6.2.1. Marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances

## 9.6.2.1.1. Applicant's request for a marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances:

The applicant requested consideration of its application for a marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances, upon proposal from CHMP, in accordance with Article 14(8) of Regulation (EC) No 726/2004 based on the following:

The benefit-risk balance is positive in the following indication: Treatment of Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with non Gadolinium (Gd) enhancing lesions (i.e., Gd-negative) in brain Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), with a Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1.

It is not likely that the applicant will be able to provide comprehensive data. The applicant claimed that comprehensive efficacy and safety data under normal conditions of use cannot be generated for this indication, for objective and verifiable reasons inherent to the rarity of the disease and to medical ethics.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

cALD is an ultra-rare, rapidly progressive and life-threatening demyelinating disease. Untreated, the inflammatory cerebral form leads to progressive neurological deterioration, major functional disability and death.

Boys with Gd-negative cerebral lesions are managed through close MRI surveillance; haematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT), the current disease-modifying standard of care, is typically considered upon appearance of gadolinium enhancement. There is therefore no authorised pharmacological treatment at the Gd-negative stage, despite this being the therapeutic window in which preventing inflammatory progression is most likely to deliver the greatest long-term benefit.

Leriglitazone would provide the first pharmacological treatment option for patients at this stage. The medicine therefore addresses a critical unmet need in a serious, ultra-rare paediatric disease for which no satisfactory alternative is currently available.

The inability to provide comprehensive data is intrinsic and unavoidable, and rests on the ground recognised in the legislation: the rarity of the indication. The second ground, the state of scientific knowledge, is not separately relied upon: the disease mechanism, the relevant endpoints and the imaging markers are established. However, the volume and breadth of comparative, confirmatory efficacy evidence that a standard marketing authorisation would normally require - specifically, a substantially larger comparative dataset in Gd-negative boys aged 2 to 12, confirmatory evidence from multiple adequately powered controlled studies, and extensive long-term comparative data cannot be provided. Although the limitation is not a qualitative deficiency in the evidence there is a considerable level of uncertainty that could only be resolved with improved scientific knowledge sourced from additional studies

## Rarity of the condition

cALD is the most severe neurological manifestation of ALD and it is ultra-rare. At the time of orphan designation, adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) affected less than 0.4 in 10,000 people in the European Union - fewer than 21,000 individuals - well below the orphan ceiling of 5 in 10,000 (EU orphan designation EU/3/16/1770, granted 18 November 2016 to Minoryx Therapeutics). The cerebral form (cALD) is a subset of this population; patients with Gd-negative lesions is a smaller subset within those with cALD; and the eligible Gd-negative population aged 2 to 12 -even smaller. Orphan status alone is not sufficient to justify the inability to provide comprehensive data, but in this case it is presented in support of the rarity ground together with the quantified epidemiology (Wiesinger et al., 2015; Engelen et al., 2012).

Specific data for prevalence of cALD are not available. Therefore, prevalence can be derived from incidence data in regions where newborn screening (NBS) is established and inferred for the European population as there are no known geographical differences in incidence (Wiesinger et al, 2015; Cappa et al., 2023). The incidence of ALD from recent publications considering NBS from Priestly, et al. (2022) gives an incidence of 1 in 10,500 newborn males and another from Videbaek et al., (2023) with the longest duration datasets with incidences of 1 in 15,400 and 1 in 13,000 in males. It is described that about one third of male patients with ALD will develop cALD (Engelen et al.2012). Using the incidences from the above publications the estimated number of boys aged 2-12 years with cALD in Europe is of less than 600 patients. Furthermore, there are no specific data for the proportion of Gd negative cALD in this population, therefore using the proportions of Gd negative from the Nexus study (9 out of 23 recruited patients) it can be estimated that there are about 235 Gd negative patients aged 2-12 years in Europe.

## Limitations of clinical development

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The Applicant conducted an international, multicentre programme designed to maximise the evidence obtainable in this population: the pivotal NEXUS study (which enrolled 23 boys with childhood cALD, one of the largest prospective datasets generated in this disease), the supportive adults ADVANCE study, and compassionate-use experience.

An adequately powered confirmatory or comparative trial restricted to Gd-negative boys aged 2 to 12 cannot be conducted: the eligible population is too small to recruit such a study, even as a large cooperative multicentre effort across the limited number of specialist centres, and alternative or exploratory designs cannot overcome this constraint. These limitations are inherent to the disease and are not expected to be overcome within a reasonable timeframe, even with continued development and international collaboration.

To conduct a placebo controlled clinical trial in the indicated population the study would need with onesided 5% level test 50 subjects for an 80% power to detect an improvement in arrested disease rate from 10% on Standard of care/placebo to 40% on Nezglyal. The feasibility to recruit 50 subjects from the assumed pool of prevalent patients would be very difficult considering:

- Patient identification is difficult with very little new-born screening for ALD being performed globally therefore patients are identified sporadically and often with Gd-positive lesions and/or clinical signs which would be an exclusion;
- The importance of the associated acute anxiety for the patient and family at the time of the diagnosis and agreeing to management steps and therefore consideration of clinical trial participation is less likely;
- Exceedingly long recruitment period. As a reference, to recruit 9 Gd negative patients into the Nexus study required a period of over 3 years in a multicentre, multinational and global referral setting.

## Medical ethics

cALD is a rare, rapidly progressive, and life-threatening paediatric condition in which early intervention is critical to preserving neurological function. The Gd negative stage represents an important therapeutic window, as progression to Gd positive disease marks the point at which HSCT becomes the indicated intervention, where feasible. However, HSCT depends on donor availability and is not accessible to all patients.

In this context, once leriglitazone is approved, withholding treatment during the Gd negative stage would expose children to a foreseeable risk of disease progression during a phase when intervention may be most beneficial. This raises important considerations in relation to established principles of medical ethics, including the obligation to minimise harm and to provide access to available therapies.

Furthermore, allowing preventable progression in order to maintain a placebo comparison could increase the likelihood that patients require HSCT under less favourable circumstances or may no longer be eligible. Where HSCT is indicated and a suitable donor is available, delaying transplantation for trial purposes would likewise be difficult to align with appropriate clinical care.

On this basis, a placebo-controlled design is considered challenging to justify in this population. The pivotal NEXUS study, as agreed with PDCO, was therefore conducted as a single arm phase 2/3 trial with a natural history external control, ensuring that patients were not unnecessarily deprived of timely treatment.

The external natural-history control was adopted as the maximum comparative design that is acceptable in this setting; it was discussed and agreed with the Paediatric Committee (PDCO) in the context of the agreed Paediatric Investigation Plan (P/0330/2024).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

These considerations do not diminish over time. For as long as leriglitazone remains available for the indicated population, a placebo-controlled trial would raise the same considerations, and would in addition be difficult to recruit to, since parents and clinicians are unlikely to consent to placebo allocation once an authorised alternative exists. This constraint is therefore not expected to be overcome within a reasonable timeframe, and comprehensive comparative efficacy data cannot be generated, before or after authorisation.

## 9.6.2.1.2. Discussion on comprehensiveness of data in the context of a marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances:

In regard to the request for Marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances the CHMP consider that the following is to be accounted in terms of comprehensiveness of the dossier:

The CHMP concluded that the B/R of Nezglyal could only be established for the treatment of Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with Gd-negative lesions in brain MRI. This conclusion has been made based on limited efficacy data and missing longterm data. This is explained by the recognition that cALD by itself is an ultra-rare disorder (&lt;0.4 in 10,000 people in the EU) and the restricted indication (patients with Gd-negative lesions and aged 2 to 12 years old) which even more limits the number of patients to be treated (as per applicant's calculation of the prevalence derived from incidence in regions where cALD is included in NBS, the population in Europe to be treated may be 235 patients).

This is a rapidly progressive and life-threatening demyelinating disease; when untreated, the inflammatory cerebral form leads to progressive neurological deterioration, major functional disability and death. The available data indicates that leriglitazone induces a stabilization of the disease in the patients now included in the indication allowing them to wait for other therapeutic approaches such as HSCT or gene therapy, as no satisfactory alternative is currently available. The high unmet medical need is therefore established. It is also clear that for this disease and population to be treated it will never be possible to gather data that fulfils the comprehensiveness of the data required for a full MA.

Throughout the assessment, CHMP recognized the non-comprehensiveness of the data, which is mainly due to still existing uncertainties regarding the disease, the population, the relevant biomarkers and endpoints that could be selected for a potential RCT, which would have provided more data and consequently more evidence of the efficacy of leriglitazone in the treatment of these patients.

## 9.6.2.1.3. Conclusions and recommendation on marketing authorisation under exceptional circumstances

The CHMP has determined that Nezglyal (leriglitazone) provides a favorable benefit-risk profile for a highly specific group of male patients aged 2-12 with cALD who exhibit Gd-negative brain MRI lesions and a Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1. Due to the ultra-rare nature of the disease and ethical constraints, the applicant was unable to provide comprehensive long-term efficacy and safety data, and a randomized controlled trial was deemed unfeasible. Nevertheless, because cALD is a lifethreatening condition with no satisfactory alternatives, the available evidence suggests that Nezglyal can stabilize the disease, serving as a critical bridge for patients awaiting further interventions such as gene therapy or hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT).

The MAUEC will be granted with three specific obligations (SOBs):

1. A Post-Authorisation Safety Study (PASS) with annual safety reports.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

2. An open-ended PAES post-authorisation effectiveness registry to collect real-world data (RWD) in the same population as the PASS (untreated and leriglitazone treated patients in the approved population), with annual submissions of available data.
3. Collection and annual submission of any data available on the exposure to leriglitazone.

In conclusion, the MAUEC is supported as well as the proposed SOBs to gather further knowledge on the effectiveness and long-term safety of Nezglyal in the approved indication.

## 9.6.2.2. Third party intervention(s)

A third-party intervention was received after final round of assessment of the procedure.

The third-party intervention included a report based on a survey conducted among participants of the Advance and Nexus clinical trials, as well as patients who received Leriglitazone for compassionate use. The information from this third-party intervention was taken into consideration by CHMP.

## 9.7. Benefit-risk conclusions

## 9.7.1. At Day 210 - CHMP conclusions

The benefit/risk of Leriglitazone is positive for treatment of Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2-12 with Gd-negative lesions in brain MRI and Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1.

## 10. Appendix

## 10.1. Divergent position(s) to the majority recommendation

The undersigned members of the CHMP did not agree with the CHMP's positive opinion recommending the refusal of the granting of the marketing authorisation of Nezglyal indicated for 'Cerebral Adrenoleukodystrophy (cALD) in males with Adrenoleukodystrophy (ALD) aged 2 to 12 years of age with Gadolinium (Gd) negative lesions in brain MRI, with a Neurological Functional Score (NFS) of 0 or 1'.

The reasons for divergent opinion were the following:

Support for the proposed indication is based primarily on a single-arm study. Although the pivotal Nexus/MT-2-02 study met the predefined primary endpoint of arrested disease, the result is associated with several uncertainties.

Firstly, this result was driven by a very small number of subjects (arrest disease in 6 out of 9 boys with gadolinium non-enhancing (Gd-negative) lesions).

Secondly, while most patients with cALD are likely to progress over time, it is unclear how many of the Gd-negative patients in Nexus study would have shown lesion growth, developed new Gd-negative lesions, or progressed to Gd-positive disease without treatment during the study period.

The external natural history comparison for Gd negative subgroup was based on Mallack et al., 2021 and included 13 boys with Gd-negative lesions, 12 of whom were classified as having progressive disease based on two consecutive MRI assessments. Comparable confirmation of progression was not obtained in Nexus before leriglitazone treatment was initiated. The external comparison therefore does not provide robust data to support a treatment effect of leriglitazone in the proposed indication.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Therefore, the undersigning members are of the opinion that the benefit-risk balance for the targeted patients with cALD is negative.
